He seldom spoke to her more than a sentence at a time, but he did like to have her near him, and I suppose held, through the bare presence, some kind of dull one-sided communication with her; what did a woman know about business? and what did he know about except business? It is true he had a rudimentary pleasure in music—and would sometimes ask her to play to him, when he would listen, and after his fashion enjoy. But although here was a gift that might be developed until his soul could echo the music of the spheres, the embodied souls of Handel or Mendelssohn were to him but clouds of sound wrapped about kernels—let me say of stock or bonds.
===Chinese===
每逢星期日黄昏，登普斯特太太本是很乐意再上一趟教堂的——哪怕只为从丈夫在场所给她的那份沉甸甸的压迫下溜出去片刻也好，尽管她从不肯向自己承认这也是理由之一。他一次对她说的话难得超过一句，可他着实喜欢有她在身旁；我想，单凭这同处一室，他便算与她之间维系着某种沉闷而单向的往来；女人家懂得什么生意？而除了生意，他又懂得什么？诚然，他对音乐有一种初萌的趣味——间或请她弹给他听，他便听着，并按自己的方式享受一二。然而，虽说这是一份尽可发展、直至他的灵魂能与诸天之乐相和鸣的天赋，可韩德尔（Handel）或门德尔松（Mendelssohn）那凝聚为声的灵魄，在他听来也不过是裹着核儿的声之云雾——容我说，那核儿是股票或公债。

===Original===
For a year or so after their marriage it had been the custom that, the first thing after breakfast on Monday morning, she should bring him her account-book, that they might together go over her week’s expenses. She must cultivate the business habits in which, he said, he found her more than deficient. How could he endure in a wife what would have been preposterous in a clerk, and would have led to his immediate dismissal? It was in his eyes necessary that the same strict record of receipt and expenditure should be kept in the household as in the office; how else was one to know in what direction things were going? he said. He required of his wife, therefore, that every individual thing that cost money, even to what she spent upon her own person, should be entered in her book. She had no money of her own, neither did he allow her any special sum for her private needs; but he made her a tolerably liberal weekly allowance, from which she had to pay everything except house-rent and taxes, an arrangement which I cannot believe a good one, as it will inevitably lead some conscientious wives to self-denial severer than necessary, and on the other hand will tempt the vulgar nature to make a purse for herself by mean savings off everybody else. It was especially distasteful to Mrs. Dempster to have to set down every little article of personal requirement that she bought. It would probably have seemed to her but a trifle had they both been young when they married, and had there been that tenderness of love between them which so soon sets everything more than right; but as it was, she could never get over the feeling that the man was strange to her. As it was she would have got over this. But there was in her a certain constitutional lack of precision, combined with a want of energy and a weakness of will, that rendered her more than careless where her liking was not interested. Hence, while she would have been horrified at playing a wrong note or singing out of tune, she not only had no anxiety, for the thing’s own sake, to have her accounts correct, but shrunk from every effort in that direction. Now I can perfectly understand her recoil from the whole affair, with her added dislike to the smallness of the thing required of her; but seeing she did begin with doing it after a fashion, it is not so easy to understand why, doing it, she should not make a consolation of doing it with absolute exactness. Not even her dread of her husband’s dissatisfaction—which was by no means small—could prevail to make her, instead of still trusting a memory that constantly played her false, put down a thing at once, nor postpone it to a far less convenient season. Hence it came that her accounts, though never much out, never balanced; and the weekly audit, while it grew more and more irksome to the one, grew more and more unsatisfactory to the other. For to Mr. Dempster’s dusty eyes exactitude wore the robe of rectitude, and before long, precisely and merely from the continued unsatisfactory condition of her accounts, he began, in a hidden corner of his righteous soul, to reflect on the moral condition of his wife herself as unsatisfactory. Now such it certainly was, but he was not the man to judge it correctly, or to perceive the true significance of her failing. In business, while scrupulous as to the requirements of custom and recognized right, he nevertheless did things from which her soul would have recoiled like “the tender horns of cockled snails;” yet it was to him not merely a strange and inexplicable fact that she should never be able to show to a penny, nay, often not to a shilling or eighteenpence, how the week’s allowance went, but a painful one as indicating something beyond perverseness. And truly it was no very hard task he required of her, for, seeing they had no children, only three servants, and saw little company, her housekeeping could not be a very heavy or involved affair. Perhaps if it had been more difficult she would have done it better, but anyhow she hated the whole thing, procrastinated, and setting down several things together, was sure to forget some article or mistake some price; yet not one atom more would she distrust her memory the next time she was tempted. But it was a small fault at worst, and if her husband had loved her enough to understand the bearings of it in relation to her mental and moral condition he would have tried to content himself that at least she did not exceed her allowance; and would of all things have avoided making such a matter a burden upon the consciousness of one so differently educated, if not constituted, from himself. It is but fair to add on the other side that, if she had loved him after anything like a wifely ideal, which I confess was not yet possible to her, it would not have been many weeks before she had a first correct account to show him. Convinced, at length, that accuracy was not to be had from her, and satisfying himself with dissatisfaction, he one morning threw from him the little ruled book, and declared, in a wrath which he sought to smother into dignified but hopeless rebuke, that he would trouble himself with her no further. She burst into tears, took up the book, left the room, cried a little, resolved to astonish him the next Monday, and never set down another item. When it came, and breakfast was over, he gave her the usual cheque, and left at once for town. Nor had the accounts ever again been alluded to between them.
===Chinese===
婚后约莫一年光景，他们之间曾有这样一个规矩：每星期一早晨头一件事，便是她把自家的账本呈给他，好让两人一同把她这一周的花销过上一遍。她说他发觉她在"生意人的习惯"上头大有欠缺，她便非得把这些习惯培养起来不可。一个伙计若有此等情形便是荒唐，必立遭辞退，他又怎能在妻子身上忍受同样的事呢？在他看来，家里头的收支也必须像办公间里那样逐笔记下，严明无误；否则怎晓得事情的走向呢？他说。于是他要求妻子，凡花钱买下的每一样物件，哪怕是她花在自家身上的，都要一一登入账本。她自己并无钱财，他也不曾给她一笔专作私用的款项；他每周给她一份还算宽裕的零用，房租和税款除外，其余一应开销俱要从中支付——这样的安排，我难以认为是妥当的，因为它势必会引得某些有良心的妻子过于克己、自苦得太甚，另一方面又会诱使庸俗之辈从人人头上卑劣地克扣下来，给自己攒下私房。要登普斯特太太把所买的每件贴身用的小物件都逐一记下，尤为令她难堪。倘若他俩成亲时都还年轻，彼此之间又有那种柔情蜜意——这柔情总能在转眼间使一切不止于妥帖——她大约也只觉这是桩微不足道的小事；然而既已如此，她便始终过不去那一道坎，总觉这男子于她是个陌生人。倘若仅此而已，她原也过得去。可她天性里就缺了一种精确，兼之气力不足、意志薄弱，但凡心所不系之处，便不止于疏忽二字了。是以她若弹错一音、唱走一调，必惊惶失措；可账目之事，她不仅不为其自身之故而务求其准，反倒对这方面的一切努力都退避三舍。她这桩事整个地令她厌恶，加之她讨厌所被要求的事的琐碎，我完全能理解她的退却；只是既然她到底开了个头、勉为其难地做了起来，那么做的时候，为何不索性以绝对的精确为自慰呢，这便不那么好懂了。纵然她对丈夫不满的畏惧——这畏惧并不算小——也压不住她，使她不再依赖那一再欺骗她的记忆力、当下便记下一笔，或至少不把事情拖到更不便的时候。于是她的账目虽从未大错，却从未轧得平；每星期的对账，于一方愈发成了苦差，于另一方则愈发不能令人满意。因为在登普斯特先生那双蒙尘的眼里，精确正穿着端正的外袍；没多久，单凭她账上一连串令人不满的状况，他便在自己那颗端正灵魂的隐秘一角，开始把妻子本人的道德状况也一并视作不能令人满意的了。她的状况诚然如此，但他却不是那种能正确评断她、能看出她这短处之真切分量的人。在生意场上，对于习俗与公认之权利的要求，他虽然一丝不苟，却会做出一些事来——她的灵魂见了，定要如"蜗牛怯生生的触角"那样瑟缩回去；然而在她竟永远不能精确到一便士、往往甚至不能到一先令或十八便士地说出那一周的津贴是怎么花的，于他不仅是桩古怪难解的事，更是桩令人痛心的事，因为它所指的，已不止于执拗二字。他所要求于她的，也实在算不得什么难事，因为他们既无儿女、只有三个仆人，又少有客人往来，她的家计料理原不至于多么繁重纠结。也许事情愈难她反倒做得愈好；但无论如何，她讨厌这一桩事，一拖再拖，把好几样东西一并记下时，又总会忘了某件物品或记错了某个价钱；可到了下一次受诱惑时，她对自家记忆力的信赖，却依旧分毫未减。但往最坏里说，这也不过是桩小过；倘若她丈夫爱她爱到足以明白这一过处在她的心智与道德状况中所处的位置，他便会勉强自己知足——至少她没有超出那笔津贴；而最要紧的是，他必会竭力避免把这样一桩事，压在一个在所受教育、甚至天性与他都如此不同的人的良心上。另一面也公允地补一句：倘若她是照着为人妻者那种理想去爱他的——我承认这于她那时还不可能——不出几个星期，她便能拿出一本头一份分毫不差的账来给他看了。终于，他确信从她那里再也得不着精确，便以不满来聊以自慰；某日早晨，他把那本画了格的小账本掷到一旁，在一腔他竭力压成庄严却无望的训斥的怒气里，宣告他再也不愿为这事费神了。她泪如雨下，捡起账本，退出房去，哭了一阵，暗下决心下个星期一要叫他大吃一惊——却再也没有登过一笔账。那一日到了，早膳过后，他如往常一样把支票递给她，便立刻进城去了。此后，那些账目他俩再不曾提起过。

===Original===
Now this might have been very well, or at least not very ill, if both had done tolerably well thereafter—that is, if the one had continued to attend to her expenditure as well as before, and the other, when he threw away the account-book, had dismissed from his mind the whole matter. But Dempster was one of those dangerous men—more dangerous, however, to themselves than to others—who never forget, that is, get over, an offence or disappointment. They respect themselves so much, and, out of their respect for themselves, build so much upon success, set so much by never being defeated but always gaining their point, that when they are driven to confess themselves foiled, the confession is made from the “poor dumb mouth” of a wound that cannot be healed. It is there forever—will be there at least until they find another God to worship than their own paltry selves. Hence it came that the bourn between the two spiritual estates yawned a little wider at one point, and a mist of dissatisfaction would not unfrequently rise from a certain stagnant pool in its hollow. The cause was paltry in one sense, but nothing to which belongs the name of “Cause” can fail to mingle the element of awfulness even with its paltriness. Its worst effect was that it hindered approximation in other parts of their marching natures.
===Chinese===
那么，这本可以很好，至少也不至于太糟——只要此后双方各自照旧过得去——也就是说，只要这一方继续像从前那样留心着自己的开销，而那一方在扔掉账本的同时，也把整桩事从心头一并抛开。然而登普斯特正是那种危险的人物——不过对他们自己比对旁人更危险——他们从不遗忘，亦即从不释怀，一桩冒犯或一场失望。他们那样地自尊，因着这份自尊，那样地看重成功、那样地以从不落败、总能如愿以偿自许，以至于一旦被逼得承认自己受挫，那供认便是从一个无法愈合的伤口那"可怜的、哑了的嘴"里说出来的。那伤便永远在那里——至少要等他们找到一个别的神祇来崇拜、不再供奉自家那点渺小的自我时，方才会消。于是乎，这两份心灵疆域之间的界河，便在某一点上又裂得宽了些；一团不满的薄雾，也不时从那河底一方死水潭里升起。这缘由在某种意义上固然渺小，但凡配得上"因"之名的物事，都不免要在这渺小里掺进一缕可畏。它最坏的影响在于：它妨碍了两人在各自前行的天性的其他部分上彼此靠近。

===Original===
And as to Mrs. Dempster, I am sorry for the apparent justification which what I have to confess concerning her must give to the severe whims of such husbands as hers: from that very Monday morning she began to grow a little careless about her expenditure—which she had never been before. By degrees bill after bill was allowed to filch from the provision of the following week, and when that was devoured, then from that of the week after. It was not that she was in the least more expensive upon herself, or that she consciously wasted anything; but, altogether averse to housekeeping, she ceased to exercise the same outlook upon the expenditure of the house, did not keep her horses together, left the management more and more to her cook; while the consciousness that she was not doing her duty made her more and more uncomfortable, and the knowledge that things were going farther and farther wrong, made her hate the idea of accounts worse and worse, until she came at length to regard them with such a loathing as might have fitted some extreme of moral evil. The bills which were supposed by her husband to be regularly settled every week were at last months behind, and the week’s money spent in meeting the most pressing of its demands, while what it could no longer cover was cast upon the growing heap of evil for the time to come.
===Chinese===
至于登普斯特太太，关于她我不得不承认的种种，似乎恰给她丈夫那种严厉的怪癖作了辩白，对此我深感遗憾：正从那个星期一的早晨起，她开始对自己的开销稍显疏忽了——这是她从前不曾有过的。渐渐地，一纸又一纸账单，得以从下一周的用度里偷取，那一周被吃空之后，又开始再偷下一周的。倒不是说她在自己身上多花了分毫，也不是说她有意识地浪费过什么；只是她本就厌烦家政，便不再像从前那样留心家用，不再把自家的马拢在一处（意谓把家中的开销拢在一处），把打理之事愈来愈多地交给了厨娘；与此同时，自觉未尽本分的良心令她愈发不安，而事情愈走愈歪的知觉，又令她愈发痛恨账目这一念头，直至她终于怀着一种厌恶来看待它们——那种厌恶，原只配加诸某种登峰造极的道德之恶。她丈夫以为每星期都在按规矩结清的那些账单，到末了竟拖了几个月之久；当周的款项都拿去应付最紧迫的催讨，而它再也遮掩不住的部分，便被抛到日积月累、日益壮大的那堆恶账之上，留给往后的时日。

===Original===
I must say this for her, however, that there was a small sum of money she expected on the death of a crazy aunt, which, if she could but lay hold of it without her husband’s knowledge, she meant to devote to the clearing off of everything, when she vowed to herself to do better in the time to come.
===Chinese===
不过，我须为她补上一句：有一笔为数不多的钱，是她的一位疯癫姨妈死后她指望着到手的；这笔钱她若能瞒着丈夫弄到手，便打算拿它把一切欠账尽数清偿，那时她暗自发誓，往后的日子定要做得好些。

===Original===
The worst thing in it all was that her fear of her husband kept increasing, and that she felt more and more uncomfortable in his presence. Hence that troubled look in her eye, always more marked when her husband sat dozing in his chair of a Sunday afternoon.
===Chinese===
这整件事里最糟糕的，是她对丈夫的畏惧与时俱增，在他面前也愈发不自在。她眼底那抹不安的神情，便由此而来——每逢星期日下午她丈夫在椅上打盹时，这神情便格外分明。

===Original===
It was natural, too, that, although they never quarrelled, their intercourse should not grow of a more tender character. Seldom was there a salient point in their few scattered sentences of conversation, except, indeed, it were some piece of news either had to communicate. Occasionally the wife read something from the newspaper, but never except at her husband’s request. In general he enjoyed his newspaper over a chop at his office. Two or three times since their marriage—now eight years—he had made a transient resolve pointing at the improvement of her mind, and to that end had taken from his great glass-armoured bookcase some standard work—invariably, I believe, upon party-politics—from which he had made her read him a chapter. But, unhappily, she had always got to the end of it without gaining the slightest glimmer of a true notion of what the author was driving at.
===Chinese===
自然，虽则他们从不争吵，彼此之间的往来也不会变得更为温存。两人之间零星散落的几句交谈，难得有什么出彩之处——除非是某一方有什么新闻要告知。间或妻子也会从报上读上几段，但从不曾离了丈夫的吩咐。多半时候，他在办公间里就着一客排骨享用他的报纸。成亲至今——已有八年——他曾有两三回兴起一阵转瞬即逝的决心，意在使她的心智有所长进；为此，他曾从他那座大号玻璃门书柜里取出某部"权威"之作——我相信无一例外都是关于党派政治的——命她读上一章给他听。可惜的是，她每每读到末了，对于作者究竟想说什么，仍是一丝真切的影子也摸不着。

===Original===
It almost moves me to pity to think of the vagueness of that rudimentary humanity in Mr. Dempster which made him dream of doing something to improve his wife’s mind. What did he ever do to improve his own? It is hard to understand how horses find themselves so comfortable in their stables that, be the day ever so fine, the country ever so lovely, the air ever so exhilarating, they are always rejoiced to get back into their dull twilight: it is harder to me to understand how Mr. Dempster could be so comfortable in his own mind that he never wanted to get out of it, even at the risk of being beside himself; but no doubt the dimness of its twilight had a good deal to do with his content. And then there is that in every human mind which no man’s neighbour, nay, no man himself, can understand. My neighbour may in his turn be regarding my mind as a gloomy place to live in, while I find it no undesirable residence—though chiefly because of the number of windows it affords me for looking out of it. Still, if Dempster’s dingy office in the City was not altogether a sufficing type of the mind that used it, I consider it a very fairly good one.
===Chinese===
每每想到登普斯特先生身上那份初萌的人性竟模糊到这般田地，竟使他做起梦来，想要做点什么以增进妻子的心智，我几乎要生出怜悯。他自己又曾做过什么来增进自己的心智呢？马儿在自家马厩里竟觉着那般自在，任凭天色再好、乡野再美、空气再令人振奋，它们总是一心欢欣地要回到那昏沉的暮色里去——这一点颇难理解：而登普斯特先生竟能在自家的心里那般自在，以至从不肯踏出它一步、哪怕冒着一阵失神的风险也在所不惜，这于我则更难理解；不过毫无疑问，他那心里的暮色之昏沉，与他这份知足大有干系。再说，每一颗人心里都有那么一些，是任何旁人、不、连他自家都摸不透的。我的邻人反过来或许也正把我的心看作一处阴暗的居所，我却觉它住着不坏——虽说这主要是因为它给了我许多扇窗子，可供朝外张望。然而纵使如此，倘若城里那间昏暗的办公间尚不足以完全充当使用它的那颗心的写照，我却也觉得，它已是一份相当贴切的写照了。

===Original===
But wherein was Mrs. Dempster so very different from her husband as I rudely fancy some of my readers imagining her? Whatever may have been her reasons for marrying him—one would suppose they must have been weighty—to do so she must have been in a very undeveloped condition, and in that condition she still remained. I do not mean that she was less developed than ninety-nine out of the hundred: most women affect me only as valuable crude material out of which precious things are making. How much they might be, must be, shall be! For now they stand like so many Lot’s-wives—so many rough-hewn marble blocks, rather, of which a Divinity is shaping the ends. Mrs. Dempster had all the making of a lovely woman, but notwithstanding her grace, her beauty, her sweetness, her lark-like ballading too, she was a very ordinary woman in that region of her which knew what she meant when she said “I.” Of this fact she had hardly a suspicion, however; for until aspiration brings humility, people are generally pretty well satisfied with themselves, having no idea what poor creatures they are. She saw in her mirror a superior woman, regarded herself as one of the finer works of creation. The worst was that from the first she had counted herself superior to her husband, and in marrying him had felt not merely that she was conferring a favour, which every husband would allow, but that she was lowering herself without elevating him. Now it is true that she was pleasanter to look at, that her manners were sweeter, and her notions of the becoming far less easily satisfied than his; also that she was a little less deficient in vague reverence for certain forms of the higher than he. But I know of nothing in her to determine her classification as of greater value than he, except indeed that she was on the whole rather more honest. She read novels and he did not; she passed shallow judgment, where he scorned to judge; she read all the middling poetry that came in her way, and copied books full of it; but she could no more have appreciated one of Milton’s or Shakespeare’s smallest poems than she could have laughed over a page of Chinese. She liked to hear this and that popular preacher, and when her husband called his sermons humbug, she heard it with a shocked countenance; but was she better or worse than her husband when, admiring them as she did, she permitted them to have no more influence upon her conduct than if they had been the merest humbug ever uttered by ambitious demagogue? In truth, I cannot see that in the matter of worth there was much as yet to choose between them.
===Chinese===
然而登普斯特太太究竟与她丈夫如何地大不相同，竟如我不客气地揣想某些读者所想象的那般？无论她嫁给他的缘由是什么——想来总该是举足轻重的缘由——她能这么做，必是当时还处在一种尚未开化的状态里，而那状态她至今仍停留其中。我并非说她比百人中之九十九更为不开化：在我看来，大多数女子不过是珍贵的粗坯，正待从中琢磨出珍品来。她们可以成为多少、必将多少、终将多少啊！因为如今她们站立着，恰如一尊尊罗得之妻——不，恰如一尊尊粗粗凿就的大理石块，一位神祇正在雕琢其终极的模样。登普斯特太太具有成为一位可爱女子的一切资质，然而尽管她优雅、美丽、温柔，还能如云雀一般唱出歌谣，可是在她那晓得自己说"我"时所指为何的那一处，她却是个再寻常不过的女子。不过，对于这一事实，她几乎毫无察觉；因为除非志向带来谦卑，人大抵都对自己相当满意，全然不晓得自己是何等可悲的造物。她在镜中看到的是一位出众的女子，把自己看作造化中较为精巧的一件作品。最糟的是，她从一开始便把自己算作高于丈夫一等；嫁给他的那一刻，她不仅觉得自己是在施恩——这原是每位丈夫都肯承认的——更觉得自己是在自降身份，却并未把他抬高半分。她看着确比他悦目，她的举止比他甜美，她对于何为得体的标准远比他难于满足，这些都是实情；再者，对于某些更高之物的形式，她那模糊的敬畏，也比他略少欠缺些许。然而除了她整体上要诚实一些之外，我寻不出她身上有什么可以判定她比他更有价值的依据。她读小说，他不读；在他不屑置评之处，她草草下些肤浅的判语；凡到她手里的平平之作，她都读了，还抄了满满几大本；可她若能品赏弥尔顿（Milton）或莎士比亚（Shakespeare）最短小的一首诗，便大约也能对着半页中文笑出声来。她爱听这位那位风头正盛的布道师讲道；丈夫称那些讲章为骗局时，她面露愠色；可她一边赞赏着这些讲章，一边任凭它们对自己的行为毫无影响，仿佛它们恰是某个野心勃勃的煽动家所发出的最最彻头彻尾的骗局一般——这般情形之下，她又比她丈夫好上几分、坏上几分呢？说到底，在"价值"这一桩上，我看他俩之间，尚没有多少高下可选。

===Original===
It is hardly necessary, then, to say that there was little appreciable approximation of any kind going on between them. If only they would have read Dickens together! Who knows what might have come of it! But this dull close animal proximity, without the smallest conscious nearness of heart or mind or soul—and so little chance, from very lack of wants, for showing each other kindnesses—surely it is a killing sort of thing! And yet, and yet, there is always a something—call it habit, or any poorest name you please—grows up between two who are much together, at least when they neither quarrel nor thwart each other’s designs, which, tending with its roots towards the deeper human, blossoms into—a wretched little flower indeed, yet afar off partaking of the nature of love. The Something seldom reveals its existence until they are parted. I suspect that with not a few, Death is the love-messenger at the stroke of whose dart the stream of love first begins to flow in the selfish bosom.
===Chinese===
那么，他们之间几乎没有什么可见的彼此靠近可言，便也毋庸多说了。倘若他俩能一同读一读狄更斯（Dickens）该多好！谁能料想那会带来怎样的结果！然而这般沉闷而贴近的兽性的比邻，心、智、灵之间却无一丝有意识的相近——又因彼此全然无所需求，而几无机会互施体恤——这实在是一种叫人发死的事啊！然而，然而，两人之间——至少当他们既不争吵、又不互挫彼此的打算之时——长长久久相处下来，总会生出一种什么——你尽可以叫它习惯，或随你叫它一个再卑微不过的名字——它的根朝着更深的人性扎下去，开出一朵花来——诚然是朵可怜巴巴的小花，却也远远地沾着几分爱的本性。这"某种东西"鲜少显露其存在，直到两人分离之时。我疑心，在相当一些人那里，死亡便是那爱的信使；他那一镖击中的刹那，爱的溪流才头一遭在那自私的胸臆里开始流淌。

===Original===
It is now necessary to mention a little break in the monotony of Mrs. Dempster’s life, which, but for what came afterwards, could claim no record. One morning her page announced Major Strong, and possibly she received the gentleman who entered with a brighter face than she had ever shown her husband. The major had just arrived from India. He had been much at her father’s house while she was yet a mere girl, being then engaged to one of her sisters, who died after he went abroad, and before he could return to marry her. He was now a widower, a fine-looking, frank, manly fellow. The expression of his countenance was little altered, and the sight of him revived in the memory of Mrs. Dempster many recollections of a happy girlhood, when the prospect of such a life as she now led with tolerable content would have seemed simply unendurable. When her husband came home she told him as much as he cared to hear of the visitor she had had, and he made no objection to her asking him to dine the next Sunday. When he arrived Mr. Dempster saw a man of his own age, bronzed and big, with not much waist left, but a good carriage and pleasant face. He made himself agreeable at dinner, appreciated his host’s wine, and told good stories that pleased the business man as showing that he knew “what was what.” He accorded him his more particular approval, speaking to his wife, on the ground that he was a man of the world, with none of the army slang about him. Mr. Dempster was not aware that he had himself more business peculiarities than any officer in her majesty’s service had military ones.
===Chinese===
这里有必要提一提登普斯特太太生活中一段小小的波澜，打破了她的单调——若非后来发生的事，它本不配在此留下一笔。某日上午，她家的小厮通报斯特朗少校（Major Strong）来访；这位先生进来时，她迎候的脸色，许是比她向丈夫展露过的任何一次都更为明朗。少校刚从印度（India）归来。她还不过是个小女孩时，他常到她父亲家里走动，那时他与她的一个姐姐订了婚；他出国之后、未及归来成婚之前，那位姐姐便已死去。如今他是个鳏夫，一位相貌堂堂、坦率而颇具丈夫气的人。他面容的神情几乎没有改变，他这一出现，便在登普斯特太太的记忆里唤起了许许多多快乐少女时代的往事——那时，像她如今这般心满意足地过着的日子，在当年的她看来简直不堪忍受。丈夫归家后，她便把来访者的种种，拣他愿听的那么一点告诉了他；他对她请那人下个星期日来吃饭一事，并无异议。来客到时，登普斯特先生见到的是一个与他同龄的男子，肤色黝黑、身材魁梧，腰身已所剩无几，却风度翩翩、面容和悦。席间他颇善应酬，赏识主人的酒，讲些颇见世面的好故事，博得了这位生意人的欢心——这些故事正显出他"懂得门道"。登普斯特先生对妻子谈起他时，给了他格外特别的称许，理由是他是个见过世面的人，身上没有半点军中腔调。登普斯特先生却未觉察，他自己身上的生意人做派，比女王陛下麾下任何一位军官身上的军人做派都要多。

===Original===
After this Major Strong frequently called upon Mrs. Dempster. They were good friends, and did each other no harm whatever, and the husband neither showed nor felt the least jealousy. They sang together, occasionally went out shopping, and three or four times went together to the play. Mr. Dempster, so long as he had his usual comforts, did not pine in his wife’s absence, but did show a little more pleasure when she came home to him than usually when he came home to her. This lasted for a few months. Then the major went back to India, and for a time the lady missed him a good deal, which, considering the dullness of her life, was not very surprising or reprehensible.
===Chinese===
自那以后，斯特朗少校便常来登普斯特太太府上走动。他们是交情不错的朋友，彼此谁也不曾给对方带来半点损害；做丈夫的既未流露、也不曾感到一丝嫉妒。他们一同唱歌，偶尔一同出门购物，还三四次结伴去看戏。登普斯特先生，只要他那日常的安逸照旧，妻子不在身边并不觉孤寂；不过她回到他身边时，他确是比平常他回到她身边时，多流露出几分欢喜。这般光景持续了几个月。后来少校回了印度，有一阵子这位太太相当惦记他——念及她生活的乏味，这也算不得怎么出奇，也不算怎么该受指摘。

## I The Mountain Stream / 一 山涧溪流

===Original===
Once upon a time, there lived in a valley in Scotland, a boy about twelve years of age, the son of a shepherd. His mother was dead, and he had no sister or brother. His father was out all day on the hills with his sheep; but when he came home at night, he was as sure of finding the cottage neat and clean, the floor swept, a bright fire, and his supper waiting for him, as if he had had wife and daughter to look after his household, instead of only a boy. Therefore, although Colin could only read and write, and knew nothing of figures, he was ten times wiser, and more capable of learning anything, than if he had been at school all his days. He was never at a loss when anything had to be done. Somehow, he always blundered into the straight road to his end, while another would be putting on his shoes to look for it. And yet all the time that he was busiest working, he was busiest building castles in the air. I think the two ought always to go together.

And so Colin was never overworked, but had plenty of time to himself. In winter he spent it in reading by the fireside, or carving pieces of wood with his pocket knife; and in summer he always went out for a ramble. His great delight was in a little stream which ran down the valley from the mountains above. Up this burn he would wander every afternoon, with his hands in his pockets. He never got far, however﻿—he was so absorbed in watching its antics. Sometimes he would sit on a rock, staring at the water as it hurried through the stones, scolding, expostulating, muttering, and always having its own way. Sometimes he would stop by a deep pool, and watch the crimson-spotted trouts, darting about as if their thoughts and not their tails sent them where they wanted to go. And when he stopped at the little cascade, tumbling smooth and shining over a hollowed rock, he seldom got beyond it.

===Chinese===
从前，在苏格兰的一个山谷里，住着一个约莫十二岁的男孩，是一个牧羊人的儿子。他的母亲已经去世，他既无姐妹，也无兄弟。父亲整天在山上照看羊群；可每到夜里回家，小屋里总是收拾得整整齐齐——地板扫得干净，炉火旺亮，晚饭也替他备好等着，仿佛操持这个家的不是孤零零一个男孩，而是有妻有女一般。因此，虽说科林（Colin）不过略识读写，对算术一窍不通，可他比起那些整日在学堂里待着的孩子，反倒要聪明十倍，学什么都更上手。但凡有事要做，他从不犯难。说来奇怪，他总能误打误撞地走上抵达目标的正道，而旁人却还在慢条斯理地系鞋带，准备动身去寻呢。然而，他手头最忙的时候，也正是他心里最忙着搭空中楼阁的时候。我以为这两桩事本就该形影不离。

正因如此，科林从不至于劳累过度，反倒有大把属于自己的时光。冬天，他便在炉边读书，或是用折刀削刻木块；到了夏天，他总爱出门闲逛。他最钟爱的，是那条从山间奔下、穿过山谷的小溪。每天午后，他总要把双手插在口袋里，顺着这条溪水（burn，苏格兰语“溪流”）往上游信步漫游。然而他总走不了多远——他太沉迷于观看溪水的种种把戏了。有时他坐在一块岩石上，凝望水流急匆匆地穿过乱石，一路责骂、争辩、嘟囔，却始终我行我素。有时他会在一处深潭边停下，看那身上缀着红斑的鳟鱼往来穿梭，仿佛送它们到心仪之处的不是尾巴，倒是它们的念头。而当他走到那道小瀑布前——水流光润闪亮地翻过一块凹空的岩石倾泻而下——他极少能再往前走。

===Original===
But there was one thing which always troubled him. It was, that when the stream came near the cottage, it could find no other way than through the little yard where stood the cowhouse and the pigsty; and there, not finding a suitable channel, spread abroad in a disconsolate manner, becoming rather a puddle than a brook, all defiled with the treading of the cloven feet of the cow and the pigs. In fact, it looked quite lost and ruined; so that even after it had, with much labour, got out of the yard again, it took a long time to gather itself together, and not quite succeeding, slipped away as if ashamed, with spent forces and poverty-stricken speed; till at length, meeting the friendly help of a rivulet coming straight from the hills, it gathered heart and bounded on afresh.

“It can’t be all that the cow drinks that makes the difference,” said Colin to himself. “The pigs don’t care about it. I do believe it’s affronted at being dashed about. The cow isn’t dirty, but she’s rather stupid and inconsiderate. The pigs are dirty. Something must be done. Let me see.”

===Chinese===
可有一桩事总叫他揪心。那就是：溪水流到小屋附近时，除了穿过那片立着牛棚和猪圈的小院，再也找不到别的出路；在那里，它寻不见一条像样的河床，只得灰心丧气地四散漫流，与其说是溪，不如说成了一洼泥潭，全被牛和猪那偶蹄践踏得污浊不堪。说实在的，它看上去彻底迷失了、毁了；以至于即便费尽气力再钻出院子，也要好半天才能重新收拢自己，却始终没能完全聚拢，只好像羞愧似的，拖着耗尽的水势、以一副穷酸潦倒的步子悄悄溜走；直到最后，遇见一条从山里直奔而来的友邻小溪伸出援手，它才重又振作，欢腾着向前奔去。

“造成这差别，总不会只是因为牛喝了那点水吧，”科林自言自语道，“猪根本不在乎。我倒相信，它是觉得自己受了辱——被人这样泼溅搅弄。牛不算脏，可它又蠢又不顾旁人。猪倒是真脏。总得想个法子。让我想想。”

===Original===
He reconnoitred the whole ground. Upon the other side of the house all was rock, through which he could not cut; and he was forced to the conclusion that the only other course for the stream to take lay right through the cottage.

To most engineers this would have appeared the one course to be avoided; but Colin’s heart danced at the thought of having his dear burn running right through the house. How cool it would be all the summer! How convenient for cooking; and how handy at meals! And then the music of it! How it would tell him stories, and sing him to sleep at night! What a companion it would be when his father was away! And then he could bathe in it when he liked. In winter﻿—ah!﻿—to be sure! But winter was a long way off.

===Chinese===
他把四周的地势细细察看了一遍。屋子另一侧全是岩石，他根本凿不开；他不得不得出结论：溪水若要另寻出路，唯一的路径，只能径直穿过这座小屋。

对绝大多数工程师而言，这恰恰是唯一该避之不及的一条路径；可一想到能让心爱的溪水径直从屋里淌过，科林的心便欢跳起来。整个夏天该有多清凉啊！烧饭该有多方便；吃饭时取水又有多顺手！再说那水声呢！它会如何给他讲故事，如何在夜里唱着歌哄他入睡！父亲不在家时，它会是多么好的一个伴！况且他高兴时，随时都能在里头洗个澡。至于冬天——哎呀！——那是当然要费点事的！可冬天还远着呢。

===Original===
The very next day his father went to the fair. So Colin set to work at once.

It was not such a very difficult undertaking; for the walls of the cottage, and the floor as well, were of clay﻿—the former nearly sun-dried into a brick, and the latter trampled hard; but still both assailable by pickaxe and spade. He cut through the walls, and dug a channel along the floor, letting in stones in the bottom and sides. After it got out of the cottage and through the small garden in front, it should find its own way to the channel below, for here the hill was very steep.

===Chinese===
就在第二天，父亲赶集市去了。科林便立马动手干了起来。

这工程倒也并非多么艰难；因为小屋的墙和地都是黏土筑成的——前者差不多已被日头晒成了砖，后者则被踩踏得坚硬结实；但二者终究还敌不过鹤嘴锄和铁锹。他在墙上凿开缺口，沿地板挖出一条沟渠，在底和两侧嵌进石块。等溪水钻出小屋、穿过屋前的小花园，自然能寻得通往下方旧河道的去路，因为这一段的山势极陡。

===Original===
The same evening his father came home.

“What have you been about, Colin?” he asked, in great surprise, when he saw the trench in the floor.

“Wait a minute, father,” said Colin, “till I have got your supper, and then I’ll tell you.”

So when his father was seated at the table, Colin darted out, and hurrying up to the stream, broke through the bank just in the place whence a natural hollow led straight to the cottage. The stream dashed out like a wild creature from a cage, faster than he could follow, and shot through the wall of the cottage. His father gave a shout; and when Colin went in, he found him sitting with his spoon halfway to his mouth, and his eyes fixed on the muddy water which rushed foaming through his floor.

===Chinese===
就在当天傍晚，父亲回了家。

“你这是在折腾什么呀，科林？”他一眼瞧见地板上那条沟，十分诧异地问。

“等一会儿，爸，”科林说，“等我把您的晚饭端来，再讲给您听。”

等父亲在桌前坐定，科林便一溜烟冲了出去，三步并作两步赶到溪边，正对着那一处天然的凹地——它笔直地通向小屋——把溪岸掘开了。溪水像一头挣脱囚笼的野兽般奔涌而出，快得连他都追不上，一头射穿了小屋的墙。父亲惊呼一声；等科林再进屋时，只见父亲坐在那里，汤匙举到半道，两眼直直地瞪着那浑浊的水正泛着白沫，从他脚下的地板上奔涌而过。

===Original===
“It will soon be clean, father,” said Colin, “and then it will be so nice!”

His father made no answer, but continued staring.

Colin went on with a long list of the advantages of having a brook running through your house. At length his father smiled and said:﻿—

“You are a curious creature, Colin. But why shouldn’t you have your fancies as well as older people? We’ll try it awhile, and then we’ll see about it.”

===Chinese===
“它一会儿就清了，爸，”科林说，“到那时候，可就好极啦！”

父亲没有作声，只是一个劲儿地盯着。

科林接着滔滔不绝地数说起让一条溪水从屋里淌过的种种好处来。末了，父亲笑了笑，说道——

“你这孩子真是古怪，科林。可年纪大的人有自己的一套念想，你又为何不能有你的一套呢？咱们先试一阵子，到时候再看吧。”

===Original===
The fact was, Colin’s father had often thought what a lonely life the boy’s was. And it seemed hard to take from him any pleasure he could have. So out rushed Colin at the front, to see how the brook would take the shortest way headlong down the hill to its old channel. And to see it go tumbling down that hill was a sight worth living for.

“It is a mercy,” said Colin, “it has no neck to break or it would break twenty times in a minute. It flings itself from rock to rock right down, just as I should like to do, if it weren’t for my neck.”

===Chinese===
说到底，科林的父亲常在心里念叨：这孩子的日子过得未免太孤单了。要从他手里夺走哪怕一丝欢愉，似乎都太狠心了。于是科林一阵风似的冲出门前，去看那溪水怎样寻最短的路径，一头扑下山坡，奔向它旧日的河道。而眼看它那样一路翻滚着冲下山坡，实在是一桩值得为此活一场的景象。

“幸而，”科林说，“它没有脖子可摔断，不然一分钟之内怕是要摔上二十回。它就那么一块岩石接一块岩石地往下扑，直直地落——若不是顾忌我这脖子，我也正想这么干呢。”

===Original===
All that evening he was out and in without a moment’s rest; now up to the beginning of the cut, now following the stream down to the cottage; then through the cottage, and out again at the front door to see it dart across the garden, and dash itself down the hill.

At length his father told him he must go to bed. He took one more peep at the water which was running quite clear now, and obeyed. His father followed him presently.

===Chinese===
整整一个傍晚，他进出不停，片刻不得歇：一会儿跑到沟渠的源头，一会儿顺着溪水折回小屋；接着穿过小屋，再从前门蹿出去，看它飞掠过花园，一头冲下山坡。

终于，父亲吩咐他必须上床睡觉了。他又朝那溪水多瞥了一眼——此刻它已流得十分清澈了——便乖乖听命。父亲随后也跟了进来。

## The Cruel Painter / 残忍的画师

===Original===
Among the young men assembled at the University of Prague, in the year 159‒, was one called Karl von Wolkenlicht. A somewhat careless student, he yet held a fair position in the estimation of both professors and men, because he could hardly look at a proposition without understanding it. Where such proposition, however, had to do with anything relating to the deeper insights of the nature, he was quite content that, for him, it should remain a proposition; which, however, he laid up in one of his mental cabinets, and was ready to reproduce at a moment’s notice. This mental agility was more than matched by the corresponding corporeal excellence, and both aided in producing results in which his remarkable strength was equally apparent. In all games depending upon the combination of muscle and skill, he had scarce rivalry enough to keep him in practice. His strength, however, was embodied in such a softness of muscular outline, such a rare Greek-like style of beauty, and associated with such a gentleness of manner and behaviour, that, partly from the truth of the resemblance, partly from the absurdity of the contrast, he was known throughout the university by the diminutive of the feminine form of his name, and was always called Lottchen.

===Chinese===
一五九‒年间，聚于布拉格大学（University of Prague）的青年学子之中，有一人名叫卡尔·冯·沃尔肯利希特（Karl von Wolkenlicht）。他虽是个颇为疏懒的学生，在师长与同窗眼里却颇有几分地位，因为他几乎看一眼便能领会一个命题。然而若这命题涉及对人性更深处的洞察，他便十分乐意让它对自己始终停留在"命题"的阶段；不过他会将其妥帖安放在心中某一格抽屉里，随时可一呼便取。这份心智的敏捷，更有相应的体魄之美与之相称，二者相辅相成，往往结出种种令他那出众力量同样昭然可见的果实。凡需筋骨与技巧并用的竞技，他几乎寻不到足够的对手来使自己常保操练。然而他的力量是寓于那样柔和的肌肉轮廓、那样罕见的希腊式的美之中，又与那样温文的举止气度相伴，以至于——一半因其相似之真切，一半因其反差之荒谬——全校上下都以其名字的女性指小名称呼他，总唤他洛特臣（Lottchen）。

===Original===
“I say, Lottchen,” said one of his fellow-students, called Richter, across the table in a wine-cellar they were in the habit of frequenting, “do you know, Heinrich Höllenrachen here says that he saw this morning, with mortal eyes, whom do you think?﻿—Lilith.”
“Adam’s first wife?” asked Lottchen, with an attempt at carelessness, while his face flushed like a maiden’s.
“None of your chaff!” said Richter. “Your face is honester than your tongue, and confesses what you cannot deny, that you would give your chance of salvation﻿—a small one to be sure, but all you’ve got﻿—for one peep at Lilith. Wouldn’t you now, Lottchen?”
“Go to the devil!” was all Lottchen’s answer to his tormentor; but he turned to Heinrich, to whom the students had given the surname above mentioned, because of the enormous width of his jaws, and said with eagerness and envy, disguising them as well as he could, under the appearance of curiosity﻿—
“You don’t mean it, Heinrich? You’ve been taking the beggar in! Confess now.”
“Not I. I saw her with my two eyes.”
“Notwithstanding the different planes of their orbits,” suggested Richter.
“Yes, notwithstanding the fact that I can get a parallax to any of the fixed stars in a moment, with only the breadth of my nose for the base,” answered Heinrich, responding at once to the fun, and careless of the personal defect insinuated. “She was near enough for even me to see her perfectly.”
“When? Where? How?” asked Lottchen.
“Two hours ago. In the churchyard of St. Stephen’s. By a lucky chance. Any more little questions, my child?” answered Höllenrachen.
“What could have taken her there, who is seen nowhere?” said Richter.
“She was seated on a grave. After she left, I went to the place; but it was a new-made grave. There was no stone up. I asked the sexton about her. He said he supposed she was the daughter of the woman buried there last Thursday week. I knew it was Lilith.”
“Her mother dead!” said Lottchen, musingly. Then he thought with himself﻿—“She will be going there again, then!” But he took care that this ghost-thought should wander unembodied. “But how did you know her, Heinrich? You never saw her before.”
“How do you come to be over head and ears in love with her, Lottchen, and you haven’t seen her at all?” interposed Richter.
“Will you or will you not go to the devil?” rejoined Lottchen, with a comic crescendo; to which the other replied with a laugh.
“No one could miss knowing her,” said Heinrich.
“Is she so very like, then?”
“It is always herself, her very self.”

===Chinese===
"我说，洛特臣，"一个名叫里希特（Richter）的同学说道——他们正坐在一家惯常光顾的酒窖里，隔着桌子开口，"你可知道，这位海因里希·赫伦拉亨（Heinrich Höllenrachen）说，他今早竟以肉眼看见了——你猜是谁？——莉莉丝（Lilith）。"
"亚当的原配夫人？"洛特臣问，竭力装出一副漫不经心的样子，脸上却像少女一般飞起了红晕。
"少来打哈哈！"里希特说，"你的脸可比你的舌头诚实，它招出了你无法否认的事——你愿拿自己得救的那点指望（虽则微乎其微，却是你的全部家当）去换莉莉丝的一瞥。是不是这样呢，洛特臣？"
"见鬼去吧！"洛特臣对这位促狭鬼只回了这一句；但他转向海因里希——同学们因其下颌宽得出奇而给他起了上文那个绰号（Höllenrachen，意为"地狱之口"）——尽量在好奇的神情之下掩住自己的急切与艳羡，说道——
"你不是当真的吧，海因里希？你是在蒙我们这些穷光蛋吧！快招来。"
"我才不。我亲眼看见了她。"
"尽管两只眼眶不在同一平面上。"里希特插话道。
"是的，尽管我只消以鼻梁之宽为基线，眨眼之间就能测出任何一颗恒星的视差。"海因里希应声接住这玩笑，毫不介意话里暗讽的那点生理缺陷，"她离我够近，连我都能看得一清二楚。"
"什么时候？在哪里？怎么碰上的？"洛特臣一连串追问。
"两个钟头前。在圣斯德望堂（St. Stephen's）的墓地里。凑巧撞见的。还有什么小问题要问吗，我的孩子？"赫伦拉肯答道。
"她这样一个四处都不露面的人，怎会到那儿去呢？"里希特说。
"她坐在一座坟上。她走后，我去了那个地方；那是一座新坟。还没有立碑。我向教堂司事打听她。他说他猜她就是上上个星期四安葬在那儿的那位妇人的女儿。我就知道那是莉莉丝。"
"她的母亲死了！"洛特臣若有所思地说。继而他在心里想——"那么她还会再到那里去！"不过他小心地让这个幽灵般的念头不露形迹地飘过。"可你是怎么认出她来的，海因里希？你从前并未见过她呀。"
"你又怎么会对她一往情深、神魂颠倒呢，洛特臣，你可是连见都还没见过她一面？"里希特插了进来。
"你到底去不去见鬼？"洛特臣以一连串滑稽渐强的声调回敬道；对方只以一笑作答。
"没有人会认不出她来的。"海因里希说。
"那么她真是那么相像么？"
"她总是她自己，千真万确的他自己。"

===Original===
A fresh flask of wine, turning out to be not up to the mark, brought the current of conversation against itself; not much to the dissatisfaction of Lottchen, who had already resolved to be in the churchyard of St. Stephen’s at sundown the following day, in the hope that he too might be favoured with a vision of Lilith.
This resolution he carried out. Seated in a porch of the church, not knowing in what direction to look for the apparition he hoped to see, and desirous as well of not seeming to be on the watch for one, he was gazing at the fallen rose-leaves of the sunset, withering away upon the sky; when, glancing aside by an involuntary movement, he saw a woman seated upon a new-made grave, not many yards from where he sat, with her face buried in her hands, and apparently weeping bitterly. Karl was in the shadow of the porch, and could see her perfectly, without much danger of being discovered by her; so he sat and watched her. She raised her head for a moment, and the rose-flush of the west fell over it, shining on the tears with which it was wet, and giving the whole a bloom which did not belong to it, for it was always pale, and now pale as death. It was indeed the face of Lilith, the most celebrated beauty of Prague.
Again she buried her face in her hands; and Karl sat with a strange feeling of helplessness, which grew as he sat; and the longing to help her whom he could not help, drew his heart towards her with a trembling reverence which was quite new to him. She wept on. The western roses withered slowly away, and the clouds blended with the sky, and the stars gathered like drops of glory sinking through the vault of night, and the trees about the churchyard grew black, and Lilith almost vanished in the wide darkness. At length she lifted her head, and seeing the night around her, gave a little broken cry of dismay. The minutes had swept over her head, not through her mind, and she did not know that the dark had come.

===Chinese===
这时新送来的一壶酒竟不合口味，话头便转去数落这酒；这倒颇合洛特臣的心意，因为他早已下定决心：次日日落时分要守在圣斯德望堂的墓地里，盼着自己也能有幸一睹莉莉丝的容颜。
他果然依计而行。他坐在教堂门廊里，既不知该朝哪个方向去寻觅他所期盼的那道幻影，又不愿显得像在守候什么，便索性凝望着天际残阳那纷纷飘落的玫瑰瓣，看它们在天幕上一寸寸地枯萎散尽；忽然，他不经意地一侧目，只见一个女子坐在离他不几步远的一座新坟上，双手掩面，似在痛哭。卡尔正隐在门廊的阴影里，将她看得清清楚楚，又不致被她发觉；他便坐着，凝视她。她片刻抬起头来，西方的玫瑰色霞光倾泻其上，映着她满脸的泪痕熠熠生辉，给整张面容笼上一层原本不属于它的红润——因为它一向苍白，此刻更苍白如死。那正是莉莉丝的面容——布拉格最负盛名的美人。
她重又将脸埋进双手；卡尔坐着，心头涌起一种奇异的无力之感，且愈坐愈浓；那想要相助却又无从相助的渴望，以一种他全然陌生的、战栗的虔敬，将他的心引向了她。她仍在哭泣。西方的玫瑰缓缓凋尽，云气融入天穹，群星如一滴一滴的荣光，透过夜的穹顶沉降而下，墓地四周的树影渐渐黝黑，莉莉丝的身影几乎消融在那一片广漠的幽暗之中。她终于抬起头来，发觉夜已四合，便发出一声惊惶的、断断续续的轻呼。时光之流只是从她头顶掠过，并未穿过她的心，她竟不知黑暗已经降临。

===Original===
Hearing her cry, Karl rose and approached her. She heard his footsteps, and started to her feet. Karl spoke﻿—
“Do not be frightened,” he said. “Let me see you home. I will walk behind you.”
“Who are you?” she rejoined.
“Karl Wolkenlicht.”
“I have heard of you. Thank you. I can go home alone.”
Yet, as if in a half-dreamy, half-unconscious mood, she accepted his offered hand to lead her through the graves, and allowed him to walk beside her, till, reaching the corner of a narrow street, she suddenly bade him good night and vanished. He thought it better not to follow her, so he returned her good night and went home.
How to see her again was his first thought the next day; as, in fact, how to see her at all had been his first thought for many days. She went nowhere that ever he heard of; she knew nobody that he knew; she was never seen at church, or at market; never seen in the street. Her home had a dreary, desolate aspect. It looked as if no one ever went out or in. It was like a place on which decay had fallen because there was no indwelling spirit. The mud of years was baked upon its door, and no faces looked out of its dusty windows.

===Chinese===
听见她的呼喊，卡尔起身向她走去。她听见他的脚步声，猛地站了起来。卡尔开口道——
"别怕，"他说，"让我送你回家吧。我在你后面跟着走。"
"你是谁？"她反问。
"卡尔·沃尔肯利希特。"
"我听说过你。谢谢你。我能独自回家。"
然而她仿佛怀着半是迷蒙、半是无意识的心绪，竟接过他伸出的手，任他引她穿过那些坟茔，又任他伴在身侧同行，直到走到一条窄街的拐角，她忽然道了声晚安，便消失了踪影。他觉得还是不跟为好，于是回了一声晚安，独自归去。
次日他头一个念头便是：怎样才能再见到她；事实上，许多天来他头一个念头一直是如何才能见她一面。他从没听说她到过什么地方；她所认识的人，没有一个是他认识的；教堂里、集市上，从来不见她的身影；街头巷尾，也从未见过她。她的家宅一副阴森荒凉的模样，仿佛从来无人进出。那地方像是因了没有居留其中的生气，衰败之气便落了上来。多年的泥垢已在其门上结成了硬壳，蒙尘的窗后也不见半张面孔探望。

===Original===
How then could she be the most celebrated beauty of Prague? How then was it that Heinrich Höllenrachen knew her the moment he saw her? Above all, how was it that Karl Wolkenlicht had, in fact, fallen in love with her before ever he saw her? It was thus﻿—
Her father was a painter. Belonging thus to the public, it had taken the liberty of renaming him. Everyone called him Teufelsbürst, or Devilsbrush. It was a name with which, to judge from the nature of his representations, he could hardly fail to be pleased. For, not as a nightmare dream, which may alternate with the loveliest visions, but as his ordinary everyday work, he delighted to represent human suffering.
Not an aspect of human woe or torture, as expressed in countenance or limb, came before his willing imagination, but he bore it straightway to his easel. In the moments that precede sleep, when the black space before the eyes of the poet teems with lovely faces, or dawns into a spirit-landscape, face after face of suffering, in all varieties of expression, would crowd, as if compelled by the accompanying fiends, to present themselves, in awful levee, before the inner eye of the expectant master. Then he would rise, light his lamp, and, with rapid hand, make notes of his visions; recording, with swift successive sweeps of his pencil, every individual face which had rejoiced his evil fancy. Then he would return to his couch, and, well satisfied, fall asleep to dream yet further embodiments of human ill.
What wrong could man or mankind have done him, to be thus fearfully pursued by the vengeance of the artist’s hate?

===Chinese===
那么她又怎能是布拉格最负盛名的美人呢？那么海因里希·赫伦拉肯又怎会一见她便认得？更有甚者，卡尔·沃尔肯利希特事实上竟是在尚未见过她之前，就已爱上了她——这又是为何？原来如此——
她的父亲是个画师。既是画师，便属于公众；公众便擅自给他另起了名字。人人都唤他托伊费尔斯比尔斯特（Teufelsbürst），亦即"魔鬼之笔"（Devilsbrush）。从他笔下所绘种种看来，这名字倒叫他不悦也难。因为——并非如一场噩梦，尚可与最美的幻象交替而来——他以描绘人世的苦痛为日常的功课、平生的乐事。
凡人之哀愁苦刑形诸于面目四肢的种种情状，只要一浮现于他那欣然迎受的想象之前，他便立时将其搬到画架上去。在入睡之前那片刻——诗人在那眼前漆黑的空间里，或见佳丽如云，或见一片心灵的风景渐次破晓——而于他，则是一张又一张苦痛的脸，神情各异，仿佛被相伴的群魔所驱，一齐挤到这位翘首以待的主人的心眼之前，列队谒见，气象森然。于是他便起身，点上灯，以飞快的手笔记下他的幻象；铅笔一笔接一笔地掠过，逐一录下每一张叫他邪恶的趣味为之欢悦的面孔。然后他才回到榻上，心满意足地睡去，去梦见更多更多人之苦痛的化身。
人或人类究竟做了什么对不起他的事，要这样被这位画师满腔仇恨的报复穷追不舍、追到如此可怖的地步？

===Original===
Another characteristic of the faces and form which he drew was, that they were all beautiful in the original idea. The lines of each face, however distorted by pain, would have been, in rest, absolutely beautiful; and the whole of the execution bore witness to the fact that upon this original beauty the painter had directed the artillery of anguish to bring down the sky-soaring heights of its divinity to the level of a hated existence. To do this, he worked in perfect accordance with artistic law, falsifying no line of the original forms. It was the suffering, rather than his pencil, that wrought the change. The latter was the willing instrument to record what the imagination conceived with a cruelty composed enough to be correct.
To enhance the beauty he had thus distorted, and so to enhance yet further the suffering that produced the distortion, he would often represent attendant demons, whom he made as ugly as his imagination could compass; avoiding, however, all grotesqueness beyond what was sufficient to indicate that they were demons, and not men. Their ugliness rose from hate, envy, and all evil passions; amongst which he especially delighted to represent a gloating exultation over human distress. And often in the midst of his clouds of demon faces, would someone who knew him recognise the painter’s own likeness, such as the mirror might have presented it to him when he was busiest over the incarnation of some exquisite torture.
But apparently with the wish to avoid being supposed to choose such representations for their own sakes, he always found a story, often in the histories of the church, whose name he gave to the painting, and which he pretended to have inspired the pictorial conception. No one, however, who looked upon his suffering martyrs, could suppose for a moment that he honoured their martyrdom. They were but the vehicles for his hate of humanity. He was the torturer, and not Diocletian or Nero.

===Chinese===
他所绘的面容与形体还有另一桩特征：它们在最初的构想里无一不是美的。每一张脸的线条，无论如何为痛苦所扭曲，若在宁静之中本该是绝对之美；而整幅笔墨的处置，都在为一桩事实作证——画师正是对着这份本原的美，调来了苦难的炮火，要把它那直冲云霄的神性高峰，轰到他所憎恶的生存的平地上来。为达成此事，他完全依照艺术的法则来下笔，对原初形体的任何一线都不作歪曲。是那苦痛，而非他的画笔，造成了种种改变。后者不过是一件甘心情愿的工具，忠实地录下想象所构思的一切——那份想象之残忍，从容到了精准无误的地步。
为了使他所扭曲的这份美更形焕发，从而也使造成这扭曲的苦痛更形深烈，他常在画中添上侍立的群魔，把他们画得想象力所能及的那样丑陋；不过他避免一切过分的怪诞，只保留足以点明他们是魔而非人的那一点。他们的丑来自仇恨、嫉妒和一切恶念；其中他尤其爱描绘一种对人之苦难幸灾乐祸的狂喜。而常常，在他那一团团魔脸的云雾之中，认识他的人会认出画师自己的容貌——正仿佛他在最专心致志地描摹某种精妙的酷刑之时，镜子里照给他的那副模样。
但显然是怕别人以为他纯粹为描绘而描绘这类景象，他总要从某一段故事里——往往取自教会史——给画作找一个由头，以那段故事命名，并伪称是它启发了这幅画的构思。然而，但凡凝视过他所画那些受难的殉道者的人，决不会有一刻以为他是在敬重他们的殉道。他们不过是他厌恨人类的载体。真正的施刑者是他，而不是戴克里先（Diocletian）或尼禄（Nero）。

===Original===
But, stranger yet to tell, there was no picture, whatever its subject, into which he did not introduce one form of placid and harmonious loveliness. In this, however, his fierceness was only more fully displayed. For in no case did this form manifest any relation either to the actors or the endurers in the picture. Hence its very loveliness became almost hateful to those who beheld it. Not a shade crossed the still sky of that brow, not a ripple disturbed the still sea of that cheek. She did not hate, she did not love the sufferers: the painter would not have her hate, for that would be to the injury of her loveliness: would not have her love, for he hated. Sometimes she floated above, as a still, unobservant angel, her gaze turned upward, dreaming along, careless as a white summer cloud, across the blue. If she looked down on the scene below, it was only that the beholder might see that she saw and did not care﻿—that not a feather of her outspread pinions would quiver at the sight. Sometimes she would stand in the crowd, as if she had been copied there from another picture, and had nothing to do with this one, nor any right to be in it at all. Or when the red blood was trickling drop by drop from the crushed limb, she might be seen standing nearest, smiling over a primrose or the bloom on a peach. Some had said that she was the painter’s wife; that she had been false to him; that he had killed her; and, finding that that was no sufficing revenge, thus half in love, and half in deepest hate, immortalised his vengeance. But it was now universally understood that it was his daughter, of whose loveliness extravagant reports went abroad; though all said, doubtless reading this from her father’s pictures, that she was a beauty without a heart. Strange theories of something else supplying its place were rife among the anatomical students. With the girl in the pictures, the wild imagination of Lottchen, probably in part from her apparently absolute unattainableness and her undisputed heartlessness, had fallen in love, as far as the mere imagination can fall in love.

===Chinese===
然而还有更奇的事：无论画作主题为何，他没有一幅画里不安排进一个宁静和谐的美的形象。可在这一点上，他的狠厉只不过是表露得更充分罢了。因为这个形象在任何画中都既不与画中的施为者、也不与受苦者发生任何瓜葛。正因如此，她那一份美在观者眼里几乎变得可憎。那一额的天庭不曾有一丝阴翳掠过，那一面的颊颐不曾有一纹波纹扰动。她不恨那些受苦的人，也不爱他们：画师不让她恨，因为恨会伤损她的美；也不让她爱，因为他自己怀着恨。有时她飘浮在上，像一位安宁而不察的天使，目光上仰，悠悠梦行而过，如一朵夏日的白云横过碧空般无忧无虑。她若俯瞰下面的景象，那也只是为叫观者看见——她看见了，却全不在意；她那展开的双翼，连一根翎羽也不会为此一颤。有时她又立在人群之中，仿佛是从另一幅画里临摹过来，与这幅画毫无干系，本不该出现在这里。又或者，当殷红的血正一滴一滴地从被碾碎的肢体上淌下，她可以被瞧见站在离得最近的地方，对着一朵樱草或桃子上的粉霜微笑。有人说过，她是画师的妻子；说她曾对他不忠；说他杀死了她；而又发觉这报复还不够，便这样一半是爱、一半是最深的恨，将自己的报复化为不朽。但如今人们已普遍明白，那是他的女儿，关于她的美，外面流传着种种夸大的说法；虽说众人也都依照从她父亲画中读出来的那一套，断定她是一位没有心的美人。至于有什么别的东西替代了心的位置，解剖学的学生之间颇有种种离奇的议论。至于画中的这位少女，洛特臣狂放的想象已爱上了她——多半是因了她那显而易见、无可企及的姿态，和她那份无可争辩的冷漠——爱到了单凭想象所能爱到的地步。

===Original===
But again, how was he to see her? He haunted the house night after night. Those blue eyes never met his. No step responsive to his came from that door. It seemed to have been so long unopened that it had grown as fixed and hard as the stones that held its bolts in their passive clasp. He dared not watch in the daytime, and with all his watching at night, he never saw father or daughter or domestic cross the threshold. Little he thought that, from a shot-window near the door, a pair of blue eyes, like Lilith’s, but paler and colder, were watching him just as a spider watches the fly that is likely ere long to fall into his toils. And into those toils Karl soon fell. For her form darkened the page; her form stood on the threshold of sleep; and when, overcome with watching, he did enter its precincts, her form entered with him, and walked by his side. He must find her; or the world might go to the bottomless pit for him. But how?
Yes. He would be a painter. Teufelsbürst would receive him as a humble apprentice. He would grind his colours, and Teufelsbürst would teach him the mysteries of the science which is the handmaiden of art. Then he might see her, and that was all his ambition.

===Chinese===
可话说回来，他又要怎样才能见到她？他一夜一夜在那宅子前徘徊。那双蓝眼睛从不与他的目光相遇。那扇门里，从没有过应和他脚步的足音。那门似乎久不开启，已变得和那些被动地箍住门闩的石头一样僵死、一样坚硬。他不敢在白日守候，而尽管夜里屡屡守候，他从没看见父女或仆佣跨过那道门槛。他万万想不到，从门旁一扇狭小的窗洞里，有一双酷似莉莉丝、却更苍白更冷的蓝眼睛正在窥伺着他，正如蜘蛛窥伺着那只眼看就要落网的苍蝇。卡尔不久便落入了这网罗。因为她的身影遮暗了他面前的书页；她的身影伫立在睡乡的门槛上；当他守候得筋疲力尽、终于踏入那片境地之时，她的身影便随他一同进入，与他并肩而行。他非找到她不可；否则整个世界尽可为他坠入无底坑。但怎么找？
对。他要做个画师。托伊费尔斯比尔斯特会收他做个谦卑的学徒。他愿为他研磨颜料，而托伊费尔斯比尔斯特会教给他那门作为艺术侍婢的学问中的奥秘。那样他或许就能见到她——而这便是他全部的抱负。

===Original===
In the clear morning light of a day in autumn, when the leaves were beginning to fall seared from the hand of that Death which has his dance in the chapels of nature as well as in the cathedral aisles of men﻿—he walked up and knocked at the dingy door. The spider painter opened it himself. He was a little man, meagre and pallid, with those faded blue eyes, a low nose in three distinct divisions, and thin, curveless, cruel lips. He wore no hair on his face; but long grey locks, long as a woman’s, were scattered over his shoulders, and hung down on his breast. When Wolkenlicht had explained his errand, he smiled a smile in which hypocrisy could not hide the cunning, and, after many difficulties, consented to receive him as a pupil, on condition that he would become an inmate of his house. Wolkenlicht’s heart bounded with delight, which he tried to hide: the second smile of Teufelsbürst might have shown him that he had ill succeeded. The fact that he was not a native of Prague, but coming from a distant part of the country, was entirely his own master in the city, rendered this condition perfectly easy to fulfil; and that very afternoon he entered the studio of Teufelsbürst as his scholar and servant.

===Chinese===
那是一个秋日清晨，晨光澄澈，落叶开始从那位死亡的手中焦黄地飘落——那死亡既在人所建的教堂甬道之中起舞，也在自然的幽深祭所里蹁跹——他便走上前去，叩响了那扇灰暗的门。那位蛛网画师亲自来开门。他是个矮小的人，瘦削而苍白，一双褪色的蓝眼睛，一只低矮的鼻子分作界限分明的三段，两片薄薄的、毫无曲线的、残忍的嘴唇。他脸上不留须；但一头长长的灰发，长得如同女人的头发，披散在他双肩之上，垂落胸前。沃尔肯利希特把来意说明之后，他微微一笑，那笑容里虚伪掩不住狡黠；几番刁难之后，总算答应收他为学生，条件是他得住进自己家里。沃尔肯利希特心花怒放，却极力掩饰：而托伊费尔斯比尔斯特的第二个微笑本可让他看出，他掩饰得并不成功。他并非布拉格本地人，而是从国内一处遥远的地方而来，在这城里完全自主，这一条件于他毫无为难之处；就在当天下午，他便作为学生兼仆从，踏进了托伊费尔斯比尔斯特的画室。

===Original===
It was a great room, filled with the appliances and results of art. Many pictures, festooned with cobwebs, were hung carelessly on the dirty walls. Others, half finished, leaned against them, on the floor. Several, in different stages of progress, stood upon easels. But all spoke the cruel bent of the artist’s genius. In one corner a lay figure was extended on a couch, covered with a pall of black velvet. Through its folds, the form beneath was easily discernible; and one hand and forearm protruded from beneath it, at right angles to the rest of the frame. Lottchen could not help shuddering when he saw it. Although he overcame the feeling in a moment, he felt a great repugnance to seating himself with his back towards it, as the arrangement of an easel, at which Teufelsbürst wished him to draw, rendered necessary. He contrived to edge himself round, so that when he lifted his eyes he should see the figure, and be sure that it could not rise without his being aware of it. But his master saw and understood his altered position; and under some pretence about the light, compelled him to resume the position in which he had placed him at first; after which he sat watching, over the top of his picture, the expression of his countenance as he tried to draw; reading in it the horrid fancy that the figure under the pall had risen, and was stealthily approaching to look over his shoulder. But Lottchen resisted the feeling, and, being already no contemptible draughtsman, was soon interested enough to forget it. And then, any moment *she* might enter.

===Chinese===
那是一间大屋，满陈着艺术的器具与成品。许多画作，缠挂着蛛丝，潦潦草草地挂在肮脏的墙上。另一些画了一半的，则倚墙斜立在地板上。还有几幅进度各异的，立在画架之上。但无一不在诉说这位艺术家天才之中残忍的偏向。一个角落里，一具人体模型摊在卧榻之上，覆着一块黑丝绒的棺衣。透过那布的褶皱，底下的人形清晰可辨；一只手和前臂从衣下伸出来，与身体其余部分成直角。洛特臣一见之下，禁不住一个寒噤。他虽在一瞬间压下了这种感觉，可他对于背朝着那东西坐下，仍怀着极大的反感——而画架的摆设正要他非如此不可，托伊费尔斯比尔斯特原是要他就在那儿作画。他设法挪了挪身，使自己一抬眼便能瞧见那模型，确信它若要起来，自己定能察觉。可他的师父看在眼里、也明白他这改动；便借着光的借口，强使他回到最初摆布他的那个姿势；随后便越过自己画幅的顶端，坐看这少年作画时脸上的神情，从中读出那可怕的遐想——棺衣下的那东西已经起身，正蹑手蹑脚地挨过来，要从他肩后探看。然而洛特臣抗住了这种感觉，而且他本已是位不可小觑的画手，不久便被手头的活计吸引得把这事忘了。况且，下一刻，*她*说不定就会进来。

===Original===
Now began a system of slow torture, for the chance of which the painter had been long on the watch﻿—especially since he had first seen Karl lingering about the house. His opportunities of seeing physical suffering were nearly enough even for the diseased necessities of his art; but now he had one in his power, on whom, his own will fettering him, he could try any experiments he pleased for the production of a kind of suffering, in the observation of which he did not consider that he had yet sufficient experience. He would hold the very heart of the youth in his hand, and wring it and torture it to his own content. And lest Karl should be strong enough to prevent those expressions of pain for which he lay on the watch, he would make use of further means, known to himself, and known to few besides.

===Chinese===
于是一套缓慢的酷刑就此开场——这位画师为着这样一个时机，已经窥伺许久——尤其是自他头一回看见卡尔在那宅子附近徘徊之日起。他从前观摩肉身苦痛的机会，即便为他那病态的艺术所需而言，也已近乎足够；可如今他手里握着一个人，这人被自己的意志所缚，他便大可拿他任意做种种试验，去炮制一种他自认尚未充分观察够的苦痛。他要将这少年的心整个攥在手心，随自己的意拧它、折磨它。而为了防止卡尔刚强到能压住他所窥伺的那些痛楚神情，他还要动用进一步的手段——那些手段只他一人知道，此外鲜有人知。

===Original===
All that day Karl saw nothing of Lilith; but he heard her voice once﻿—and that was enough for one day. The next, she was sitting to her father the greater part of the day, and he could see her as often as he dared glance up from his drawing. She had looked at him when she entered, but had shown no sign of recognition; and all day long she took no further notice of him. He hoped, at first, that this came of the intelligence of love; but he soon began to doubt it. For he saw that, with the holy shadow of sorrow, all that distinguished the expression of her countenance from that which the painter so constantly reproduced, had vanished likewise. It was the very face of the unheeding angel whom, as often as he lifted his eyes higher than hers, he saw on the wall above her, playing on a psaltery in the smoke of the torment ascending forever from burning Babylon.﻿—The power of the painter had not merely wrought for the representation of the woman of his imagination; it had had scope as well in realising her.
Karl soon began to see that communication, other than of the eyes, was all but hopeless; and to any attempt in that way she seemed altogether indisposed to respond. Nor if she had wished it, would it have been safe; for as often as he glanced towards her, instead of hers, he met the blue eyes of the painter gleaming upon him like winter lightning. His tones, his gestures, his words, seemed kind: his glance and his smile refused to be disguised.

===Chinese===
那一整日卡尔都没见到莉莉丝；但他听见过她一次声音——而这一日的所求，便已够了。次日，她大半天都在给父亲做模特，他只要敢从画稿上抬眼，便能看见她。她进来时曾看过他一眼，却没有流露半点相识的神色；一整天里，她再不曾对他多看一眼。他起先还盼着这是爱情的心照不宣；但不久他便生出疑窦。因为他看见，随着那神圣的哀愁之影，所有使她的神情有别于画师笔下一再重复的那副神情的东西，也都一同消逝了。那正是不闻不问的天使的面容——每当他把目光抬得比她更高，便看见她身后墙上那一位，拨着索尔特里琴，置身于永世从燃烧的巴比伦升腾而上的折磨之烟里。——这位画师的力量，不仅已为他想象中那位女子的再现而施展；更已在将她化为真实这一点上，大显了身手。
卡尔不久便明白，除目光之外的其他交流，几乎全然无望；而对于那方面的任何尝试，她似乎全无回应的意思。便算她有意，那也是不安全的；因为每当他向她投去一瞥，迎着的不是她的目光，而是画师那双蓝眼睛，如冬日的闪电般在他身上闪烁。他的声调、他的手势、他的言语，似乎都很和善：可他的目光与他的微笑，却拒绝伪装。

===Original===
The first day he dined alone in the studio, waited upon by an old woman; the next he was admitted to the family table, with Teufelsbürst and Lilith. The room offered a strange contrast to the study. As far as handicraft, directed by a sumptuous taste, could construct a house-paradise, this was one. But it seemed rather a paradise of demons; for the walls were covered with Teufelsbürst’s paintings. During the dinner, Lilith’s gaze scarcely met that of Wolkenlicht; and once or twice, when their eyes did meet, her glance was so perfectly unconcerned, that Karl wished he might look at her forever without the fear of her looking at him again. She seemed like one whose love had rushed out glowing with seraphic fire, to be frozen to death in a more than wintry cold: she now walked lonely without her love. In the evenings, he was expected to continue his drawing by lamplight; and at night he was conducted by Teufelsbürst to his chamber. Not once did he allow him to proceed thither alone, and not once did he leave him there without locking and bolting the door on the outside. But he felt nothing except the coldness of Lilith.

===Chinese===
头一天，他独自在画室里用餐，由一个老妇人伺候；次日他便被允许上自家的饭桌，与托伊费尔斯比尔斯特和莉莉丝同席。这屋子与画室形成一种奇异的对照。但凡精巧的手艺在一派富丽趣味指引之下所能营造的家中乐园，这间便是了。然而它更像一个群魔的乐园；因为四面墙上挂满了托伊费尔斯比尔斯特的画。席间，莉莉丝的目光几乎不曾与沃尔肯利希特的相遇；有那么一两次他们的目光碰在一处，她那一瞥又是那样全然漠然，以至于卡尔宁愿永生永世这样看着她、再不必担心她会回看自己一眼。她仿佛是这样一个人：她的爱曾炽燃着炽天使之火奔涌而出，却在一场比严冬更冷的风中冻死；如今她孤身踽行，已没有了自己的爱。到了傍晚，他要奉命在灯下继续作画；到了夜里，则由托伊费尔斯比尔斯特亲自送他回房。他没有一次容他独自走过去，也没有一次不在外头把门落锁加闩。然而除了莉莉丝的冷淡之外，他什么也觉不出来。

===Original===
Day after day she sat to her father, in every variety of costume that could best show the variety of her beauty. How much greater that beauty might be, if it ever blossomed into a beauty of soul, Wolkenlicht never imagined; for he soon loved her enough to attribute to her all the possibilities of her face as actual possessions of her being. To account for everything that seemed to contradict this perfection, his brain was prolific in inventions; till he was compelled at last to see that she was in the condition of a rosebud, which, on the point of blossoming, had been chilled into a changeless bud by the cold of an untimely frost. For one day, after the father and daughter had become a little more accustomed to his silent presence, a conversation began between them, which went on until he saw that Teufelsbürst believed in nothing except his art. How much of his feeling for that could be dignified by the name of belief, seeing its objects were such as they were, might have been questioned. It seemed to Wolkenlicht to amount only to this: that, amidst a thousand distastes, it was a pleasant thing to reproduce on the canvas the forms he beheld around him, modifying them to express the prevailing feelings of his own mind.

===Chinese===
她日复一日地给父亲做模特，换上种种最能为她的种种美增色的衣裳。这份美倘若能有朝一日绽出灵魂之美，又将宏大到何等地步——这一点沃尔肯利希特从未想象过；因为他不久便爱她爱到了这般田地：把她容颜所显示的一切可能性，都当作了她实实在在的禀赋。为了替一切似乎与此种圆满相抵触的表象找出解释，他的脑子花样百出；直到他末了不得不认清：她正像一枝含苞的玫瑰，正当欲绽之际，被一场不合时令的霜寒冻成了一朵永不更变的骨朵。原来有一日，父女俩稍稍习惯了他那沉默的在侧之后，彼此之间谈起话来，谈着谈着，他便看出托伊费尔斯比尔斯特除了自己的艺术之外，什么都不信。可他对于艺术的那份情感，究竟能否当得起"信"这个字眼——既然它所对的对象是那样一些对象——倒也大可质疑。在沃尔肯利希特看来，那不过是这般模样：在千百种厌恶之中，把周遭所见的形形色色复现在画布之上，并将其加以改易，以抒发自己心头的主导之情，这于他尚算一桩快事罢了。

===Original===
A more desolate communication between souls than that which then passed between father and daughter could hardly be imagined. The father spoke of humanity and all its experiences in a tone of the bitterest scorn. He despised men, and himself amongst them; and rejoiced to think that the generations rose and vanished, brood after brood, as the crops of corn grew and disappeared. Lilith, who listened to it all unmoved, taking only an intellectual interest in the question, remarked that even the corn had more life than that; for, after its death, it rose again in the new crop. Whether she meant that the corn was therefore superior to man, forgetting that the superior can produce being without losing its own, or only advanced an objection to her father’s argument, Wolkenlicht could not tell. But Teufelsbürst laughed like the sound of a saw, and said: “Follow out the analogy, my Lilith, and you will see that man is like the corn that springs again after it is buried; but unfortunately the only result we know of is a vampire.”
Wolkenlicht looked up, and saw a shudder pass through the frame, and over the pale thin face of the painter. This he could not account for. But Teufelsbürst could have explained it, for there were strange whispers abroad, and they had reached his ear; and his philosophy was not quite enough for them. But the laugh with which Lilith met this frightful attempt at wit, grated dreadfully on Wolkenlicht’s feeling. With her, too, however, a reaction seemed to follow. For, turning round a moment after, and looking at the picture on which her father was working, the tears rose in her eyes, and she said: “Oh! father, how like my mother you have made me this time!” “Child!” retorted the painter with a cold fierceness, “you have no mother. That which is gone out is gone out. Put no name in my hearing on that which is not. Where no substance is, how can there be a name?”

===Chinese===
那时父女之间所通的信息，其荒凉几乎难以想象。父亲以最刻薄的轻蔑语气谈论人类及人类的一切遭际。他鄙视人，也鄙视其中他自己；想到一代代人生了又灭，一茬接一茬，如同庄稼长了又消失，他便满心欢喜。莉莉丝听这一切都无动于衷，仅以一种知性的兴味对待这个问题，她指出，便是庄稼也比这更有生气些；因为庄稼死后，还会在新一茬里复生。她这话究竟是意指庄稼因此胜过人——却忘了那更高者能有所生成而不失自身——抑或只是对父亲的论调提一则异议，沃尔肯利希特无从判断。但托伊费尔斯比尔斯特笑了起来，那笑声像锯子拉过的声响，说道："把这层类比追下去吧，我的莉莉丝，你就会看见人就像那埋下后又重新长出来的庄稼；只可惜我们所知的唯一结果，是个吸血鬼。"
沃尔肯利希特抬起眼，看见一阵战栗传过画师的身躯、掠过他苍白瘦削的脸。这一点他无从解释。可托伊费尔斯比尔斯特本可以解释的，因为外头有些奇怪的耳语，已传到了他的耳中；而他的那点哲学还不足以应对它们。可莉莉丝听了这可怕的卖弄机智的话，竟也报以一笑，那笑声在沃尔肯利希特的心上刺耳极了。然而在她似乎也随之而来一阵反激。因为片刻之后她转过身去，望着父亲正画着的那幅画，眼里涌上了泪，说道："啊！父亲，这一回你把我画得多像我的母亲啊！""孩子！"画师以一种冷冷的凶狠回嘴道，"你没有母亲。出去了的就是出去了。在我面前，不要给那并不存在的东西安上名字。既然并无实体，又何来名字？"

===Original===
Lilith rose and left the room. Wolkenlicht now understood that Lilith was a frozen bud, and could not blossom into a rose. But pure love lives by faith. It loves the vaguely beheld and unrealised ideal. It dares believe that the loved is not all that she ever seemed. It is in virtue of this that love loves on. And it was in virtue of this, that Wolkenlicht loved Lilith yet more after he discovered what a grave of misery her unbelief was digging for her within her own soul. For her sake he would bear anything﻿—bear even with calmness the torments of his own love; he would stay on, hoping and hoping.﻿—The text, that we know not what a day may bring forth, is just as true of good things as of evil things; and out of Time’s womb the facts must come.
But with the birth of this resolution to endure, his suffering abated; his face grew more calm; his love, no less earnest, was less imperious; and he did not look up so often from his work when Lilith was present. The master could see that his pupil was more at ease, and that he was making rapid progress in his art. This did not suit his designs, and he would betake himself to his further schemes.

===Chinese===
莉莉丝起身离开了房间。沃尔肯利希特这才明白，莉莉丝是一朵冻僵的骨朵，开不成一朵玫瑰了。然而纯洁的爱靠信而活。它爱的是那隐隐约约望见、尚未成真的理想。它敢于相信，所爱之人并非她一向显现出来的那副全貌。爱之所以能爱下去，正是凭这一点。也正是凭这一点，沃尔肯利希特在发觉莉莉丝的不信正如何在她自己的灵魂里为她掘出一座苦痛的坟之后，反而更爱她了。为了她，他什么都愿忍——甚至愿以平静去忍受自己这份爱的折磨；他要留下来，盼着，盼着。——"我们不知道一日将生出何事来"，这句话对好事与对坏事一样真切；而那些事实，迟早要从时间的子宫里生出来。
可随着这忍受的决心一旦下定，他的苦楚也减轻了些；他的面色愈发沉静；他的爱并未稍减诚挚，却少了那份暴烈；莉莉丝在场之时，他也较少从活计上抬眼了。师父看得出徒弟安定了些，艺术上正飞速长进。这却不合他的算盘，他便要另动更深的脑筋。

===Original===
For this purpose he proceeded first to simulate a friendship for Wolkenlicht, the manifestations of which he gradually increased, until, after a day or two, he asked him to drink wine with him in the evening. Karl readily agreed. The painter produced some of his best; but took care not to allow Lilith to taste it; for he had cunningly prepared and mingled with it a decoction of certain herbs and other ingredients, exercising specific actions upon the brain, and tending to the inordinate excitement of those portions of it which are principally under the rule of the imagination. By the reaction of the brain during the operation of these stimulants, the imagination is filled with suggestions and images. The nature of these is determined by the prevailing mood of the time. They are such as the imagination would produce of itself, but increased in number and intensity. Teufelsbürst, without philosophising about it, called his preparation simply a love-philtre, a concoction well known by name, but the composition of which was the secret of only a few. Wolkenlicht had, of course, not the least suspicion of the treatment to which he was subjected.

===Chinese===
为达此目的，他首先假装对沃尔肯利希特生出一段友谊，并渐渐把这番做作的友好显露得愈来愈浓，过了两日，便邀他晚间共饮葡萄酒。卡尔欣然应允。画师拿出几瓶自己收藏的上品；却小心着不让莉莉丝沾唇；因为他在酒里狡猾地配入了一剂由若干草药及其他配料煎熬的汤液，那东西专能作用于脑，并会过度兴奋脑中那些主要受想象力支配的部分。在这些兴奋剂作用之时，大脑的反应会使想象力被种种暗示与意象所充斥。这些暗示与意象的性质，由当下主导的情绪所决定。它们原是想象力自发会生出的那一类，只是数量与强度都被放大了。托伊费尔斯比尔斯特并不对这事做什么哲学思辨，只把自己的配制物称作一剂春药（love-philtre）——这东西名字众所周知，可其配方只有少数几人知晓。沃尔肯利希特当然对自己所受的款待毫无疑心。

===Original===
Teufelsbürst was, however, doomed to fresh disappointment. Not that his potion failed in the anticipated effect, for now Karl’s real sufferings began; but that such was the strength of Karl’s will, and his fear of doing anything that might give a pretext for banishing him from the presence of Lilith, that he was able to conceal his feelings far too successfully for the satisfaction of Teufelsbürst’s art. Yet he had to fetter himself with all the restraints that self-exhortation could load him with, to refrain from falling at the feet of Lilith and kissing the hem of her garment. For that, as the lowliest part of all that surrounded her, itself kissing the earth, seemed to come nearest within the reach of his ambition, and therefore to draw him the most.
No doubt the painter had experience and penetration enough to perceive that he was suffering intensely; but he wanted to see the suffering embodied in outward signs, bringing it within the region over which his pencil held sway. He kept on, therefore, trying one thing after another, and rousing the poor youth to agony; till to his other sufferings were added, at length, those of failing health; a fact which notified itself evidently enough even for Teufelsbürst, though its signs were not of the sort he chiefly desired. But Karl endured all bravely.
Meantime, for various reasons, he scarcely ever left the house.

===Chinese===
然而托伊费尔斯比尔斯特注定要再失望一回。并非他的药剂未能奏效，因为卡尔的真正苦楚由此开始；而是卡尔的意志如此之坚、他如此害怕做出任何可能招致自己被逐出莉莉丝面前的事，以至于他能把自己的情思掩藏得过分手到擒来——远到不能叫托伊费尔斯比尔斯特的艺术心满意足。然而他不得不给自己套上自劝自勉所能加诸的一切羁绊，才约束住自己不扑到莉莉丝脚下去亲吻她衣裳的裙边。因为那裙边，作为环绕她的一切之中最卑微的一角、本身又亲吻着大地的裙边，似乎最接近他那份抱负所能触及的边缘，因而也最能牵引他。
画师无疑有足够的阅历与洞察，能看出他正在剧烈受苦；可他要的是这苦楚化成外在的征象呈现出来，落进他那画笔所统辖的疆域之内。于是他不断一试再试，一桩接一桩地做去，把这可怜的少年逼到痛苦难当；到末了，又添上了健康衰退之苦——这一事实即便对托伊费尔斯比尔斯特也显露得够明白了，虽然其征象并非他最渴望的那一类。但卡尔都勇敢地忍了下来。
与此同时，由于种种缘故，他几乎从不迈出这宅子一步。

===Original===
I must now interrupt the course of my story to introduce another element.
A few years before the period of my tale, a certain shoemaker of the city had died under circumstances more than suggestive of suicide. He was buried, however, with such precautions, that six weeks elapsed before the rumour of the facts broke out; upon which rumour, not before, the most fearful reports began to be circulated, supported by what seemed to the people of Prague incontestable evidence.﻿—A *spectrum* of the deceased appeared to multitudes of persons, playing horrible pranks, and occasioning indescribable consternation throughout the whole town. This went on till at last, about eight months after his burial, the magistrates caused his body to be dug up; when it was found in just the condition of the bodies of those who in the eastern countries of Europe are called “vampires.” They buried the corpse under the gallows; but neither the digging up nor the reburying were of avail to banish the spectre. Again the spade and pickaxe were set to work, and the dead man being found considerably improved in *condition* since his last interment, was, with various horrible indignities, burnt to ashes, “after which the *spectrum* was never seen more.”
And a second epidemic of the same nature had broken out a little before the period to which I have brought my story.

===Chinese===
此刻我不得不打断故事的进程，另行引入一桩因由。
在我所述之事发生之前几年，城里有一位鞋匠死时的情形颇令人疑为自尽。然而他的下葬处置得极为审慎，直到六个星期之后，实情的传言才泄露出来；正是从这传言——而非更早——开始，种种最骇人的说法流传开来，且有布拉格百姓眼中无可辩驳的佐证为之撑腰。——死者的*幻影*（spectrum）在许多人面前现身，做出种种骇人的恶作剧，给全城带来难以言喻的惊惶。这种情形持续下去，直到最后，在他下葬约八个月之后，市政长官命人将他的尸首挖出；只见那尸首正处在欧洲东部诸国被称为"吸血鬼"（vampires）之人的那种状态。他们把那尸首葬在绞架之下；可无论是挖出还是重葬，都未能驱逐那幽灵。于是铲和镐再度动手，那死人被发现自上次入土以来状况又"好转"了不少，便在种种可怖的侮辱之中，被焚为灰烬，"此后那*幻影*再未出现过"。
而在我所推至此处的故事发生之前不久，第二场同类的瘟疫又爆发了。

===Original===
About midnight, after a calm frosty day, for it was now winter, a terrible storm of wind and snow came on. The tempest howled frightfully about the house of the painter, and Wolkenlicht found some solace in listening to the uproar, for his troubled thoughts would not allow him to sleep. It raged on all the next three days, till about noon on the fourth day, when it suddenly fell, and all was calm. The following night, Wolkenlicht, lying awake, heard unaccountable noises in the next house, as of things thrown about, of kicking and fighting horses, and of opening and shutting gates. Flinging wide his lattice and looking out, the noise of howling dogs came to him from every quarter of the town. The moon was bright and the air was still. In a little while he heard the sounds of a horse going at full gallop round the house, so that it shook as if it would fall; and flashes of light shone into his room. How much of this may have been owing to the effect of the drugs on poor Lottchen’s brain, I leave my readers to determine. But when the family met at breakfast in the morning, Teufelsbürst, who had been already out of doors, reported that he had found the marks of strange feet in the snow, all about the house and through the garden at the back; stating, as his belief, that the tracks must be continued over the roofs, for there was no passage otherwise. There was a wicked gleam in his eye as he spoke; and Lilith believed that he was only trying an experiment on Karl’s nerves. He persisted that he had never seen any footprints of the sort before. Karl informed him of his experiences during the night; upon which Teufelsbürst looked a little graver still, and proceeded to tell them that the storm, whose snow was still covering the ground, had arisen the very moment that their next door neighbour died, and had ceased as suddenly the moment he was buried, though it had raved furiously all the time of the funeral, so that “it made men’s bodies quake and their teeth chatter in their heads.” Karl had heard that the man, whose name was John Kuntz, was dead and buried. He knew that he had been a very wealthy, and therefore most respectable, alderman of the town; that he had been very fond of horses; and that he had died in consequence of a kick received from one of his own, as he was looking at his hoof. But he had not heard that, just before he died, a black cat “opened the casement with her nails, ran to his bed, and violently scratched his face and the bolster, as if she endeavoured by force to remove him out of the place where he lay. But the cat afterwards was suddenly gone, and she was no sooner gone, but he breathed his last.”

===Chinese===
那是个无风的霜天，因为已是冬天了，约莫半夜时分，一场可怕的风雪骤然而至。暴风在画师的宅子四周凄厉地呼啸，沃尔肯利希特听着这一片喧嚣，竟得了几分慰藉，因为他纷乱的心绪不容他安眠。狂风暴雪又接连肆虐了三日，到第四日约近正午，忽地停了，四下归于寂静。次日夜间，沃尔肯利希特躺着睡不着，听见隔壁宅子里传来种种难解的响动，仿佛是什么东西被摔来掷去，又像马匹踢打厮斗、门户开阖之声。他一把推开窗扉往外探看，城中四面八方都传来群犬的哀嚎。月色明亮，空气纹丝不动。不多时他听见一匹马绕着那宅子全速奔驰的声音，那宅子被震得仿佛就要倾倒；又有道道闪光射进他的房里。这其中有多少要归因于那些药剂对可怜的洛特臣大脑的作用，我留给我的读者去判定。可等到清晨一家人坐到早餐桌前，已经出过门的托伊费尔斯比尔斯特报告说，他在雪地里发现了一些古怪的脚印，遍布宅子四周、穿过后面的花园；他还说据他所见，那些足迹必是延续过了屋顶，因为再无别路可通。他说话时眼里闪着一道恶意的光；莉莉丝相信他不过是在拿卡尔的神经做试验。他却一口咬定自己从没见过这等模样的脚印。卡尔把自己夜里的经历告诉了他；托伊费尔斯比尔斯特闻言面色愈发凝重了几分，便告诉他们：那场至今仍覆着地的雪之风暴，正是在隔壁邻人咽气的一瞬间发作起来的，又在他下葬的一瞬间同样突兀地止歇——尽管下葬的全过程中它都疯狂地咆哮着，以至于"教人浑身打颤，牙齿在脑袋里格格作响"。卡尔曾听说那个人，名叫约翰·孔茨（John Kuntz），已经死了、且已入土。他知道此人是城中一位极富、因而也极体面的参事；平素极爱马；死是因为查看自家一匹马的蹄子时被踢了一脚所致。可他未曾听说，此人临终之前，一只黑猫"用爪子拨开了窗扉，跑到他床上，拼命抓挠他的脸和枕头，仿佛要硬生生把他从他躺着的地方挪开。可那猫随后忽然就不见了，它前脚一走，他后脚就咽了气。"

===Original===
So said Teufelsbürst, as the reporter of the town talk. Lilith looked very pale and terrified; and it was perhaps owing to this that the painter brought no more tales home with him. There were plenty to bring, but he heard them all and said nothing. The fact was that the philosopher himself could not resist the infection of the fear that was literally raging in the city; and perhaps the reports that he himself had sold himself to the devil had sufficient response from his own evil conscience to add to the influence of the epidemic upon him. The whole place was infested with the presence of the dead Kuntz, till scarce a man or woman would dare to be alone. He strangled old men; insulted women; squeezed children to death; knocked out the brains of dogs against the ground; pulled up posts; turned milk into blood; nearly killed a worthy clergyman by breathing upon him the intolerable airs of the grave, cold and malignant and noisome; and, in short, filled the city with a perfect madness of fear, so that every report was believed without the smallest doubt or investigation.

===Chinese===
托伊费尔斯比尔斯特转述城中流言时如是说。莉莉丝面色苍白、神情惊恐；也许正因为此，画师再没把更多的传闻带回家来。可带的传闻其实多得很，他全听了去，却一言不发。事实是，这位哲人自己也抵不住那简直在城中如瘟疫般肆虐的恐惧的传染；况且也许那些说他已把自己卖给了魔鬼的传言，在他自己那恶念丛生的良心里得到了足够的回响，从而加重了这场瘟疫对他的影响。整座城被那死去的孔茨的阴魂所侵扰，几乎没有哪个男女敢独处。他扼杀老人；凌辱妇女；把孩童活活捏死；把狗往地上摔出脑浆；拔起柱子；把牛奶化作鲜血；险些用坟墓里那不可忍受的、阴冷、恶毒而腥臭的气息，把一位可敬的牧师吹得一命呜呼；总而言之，使全城陷入了一种十足的疯狂恐惧之中，以至于每一则传闻都不容一丝置疑、不经过一丝查究便被信以为真。

===Original===
Though Teufelsbürst brought home no more of the town talk, the old servant was a faithful purveyor, and frequented the news-mart assiduously. Indeed she had some nightmare experiences of her own that she was proud to add to the stock of horrors which the city enjoyed with such a hearty community of goods. For those regions were not far removed from the birthplace and home of the vampire. The belief in vampires is the quintessential concentration and embodiment of all the passion of fear in Hungary and the adjacent regions. Nor, of all the other inventions of the human imagination, has there ever been one so perfect in crawling terror as this. Lilith and Karl were quite familiar with the popular ideas on the subject. It did not require to be explained to them, that a vampire was a body retaining a kind of animal life after the soul had departed. If any relation existed between it and the vanished ghost, it was only sufficient to make it restless in its grave. Possessed of vitality enough to keep it uncorrupted and pliant, its only instinct was a blind hunger for the sole food which could keep its awful life persistent﻿—living human blood. Hence it, or, if not it, a sort of semi-material exhalation or essence of it, retaining its form and material relations, crept from its tomb, and went roaming about till it found someone asleep, towards whom it had an attraction, founded on old affection. It sucked the blood of this unhappy being, transferring so much of its life to itself as a vampire could assimilate. Death was the certain consequence. If suspicion conjectured aright, and they opened the proper grave, the body of the vampire would be found perfectly fresh and plump, sometimes indeed of rather florid complexion;﻿—with grown hair, eyes half open, and the stains of recent blood about its greedy, leech-like lips. Nothing remained but to consume the corpse to ashes, upon which the vampire would show itself no more. But what added infinitely to the horror was the certainty that whoever died from the mouth of the vampire, wrinkled grandsire or delicate maiden, must in turn rise from the grave, and go forth a vampire, to suck the blood of the dearest left behind. This was the generation of the vampire brood. Lilith trembled at the very name of the creature. Karl was too much in love to be afraid of anything. Yet the evident fear of the unbelieving painter took a hold of his imagination; and, under the influence of the potions of which he still partook unwittingly, when he was not thinking about Lilith, he was thinking about the vampire.

===Chinese===
尽管托伊费尔斯比尔斯特不再把城中流言带回家来，那位老仆人却是个忠实的供应商，仍殷勤地常去那个新闻集市。她甚至还有几桩自家的噩梦般的经历，颇为得意地添进城中那一库百姓所"有福同享"的恐怖积储之中。因为这些地方离吸血鬼的故乡和老巢本就不远。对吸血鬼的信仰，是匈牙利及其邻近地区一切恐惧之情的精髓凝聚与化身。而在人类想象的一切发明之中，也再没有哪一样像它这样，在令人毛骨悚然这一点上如此臻于完美。莉莉丝和卡尔对民间关于此物的种种说法都很熟悉。他们不消谁来解释便知道，吸血鬼是一具灵魂既去之后仍保留着某种畜生般生命的尸身。它若与那已消散的幽灵还有什么瓜葛，那瓜葛也只够叫它在坟中不得安息。它保有着足以使自身不腐不僵的一点生机，而它唯一的本能，是一股盲目的饥渴——只求那唯一能维持它那可怖生命的食物：活人的鲜血。于是它——或者若非它本身，也是一种半具半质、由它呼出的精气，保留着它的形貌与物质瓜葛——从墓中爬出，四处游荡，直到寻见一个睡着的人，而这人是它凭着旧日的情分而心有所向的。它便吮吸这不幸之人的血，把一个吸血鬼所能吸收的那点生命转移到自己身上。死亡是必然的下场。若疑心猜得准、掘开了那座该掘的坟，吸血鬼的尸身会被发现十分新鲜饱满，有时甚至面色颇为红润；——头发长了，眼睛半睁，贪婪如水蛭的唇上带着新血的斑渍。除了把那尸首焚为灰烬之外别无他法，如此那吸血鬼便再不出现。然而使恐怖无限加甚的是这一桩确定：凡死于吸血鬼之口的人，无论是满脸皱纹的老祖父还是娇弱的少女，都必反过来从坟中起身，外出作吸血鬼，去吸他所留下来的至亲骨肉的鲜血。这便是吸血鬼一族的繁衍。莉莉丝一听见这东西的名字便发抖。卡尔则爱得正深，什么都不怕了。然而那位不信鬼神的画师那显而易见的恐惧，却攫住了他的想象；再加上他仍在不知不觉中饮用的那些药剂的威力，他只要不在想莉莉丝，便是在想那吸血鬼。

===Original===
Meantime, the condition of things in the painter’s household continued much the same for Wolkenlicht﻿—work all day; no communication between the young people; the dinner and the wine; silent reading when work was done, with stolen glances many over the top of the book, glances that were never returned; the cold good night; the locking of the door; the wakeful night and the drowsy morning. But at length a change came, and sooner than any of the party had expected. For, whether it was that the impatience of Teufelsbürst had urged him to yet more dangerous experiments, or that the continuance of those he had been so long employing had overcome at length the vitality of Wolkenlicht﻿—one afternoon, as he was sitting at his work, he suddenly dropped from his chair, and his master hurrying to him in some alarm, found him rigid and apparently lifeless. Lilith was not in the study when this took place. In justice to Teufelsbürst, it must be confessed that he employed all the skill he was master of, which for beneficent purposes was not very great, to restore the youth; but without avail. At last, hearing the footsteps of Lilith, he desisted in some consternation; and that she might escape being shocked by the sight of a dead body where she had been accustomed to see a living one, he removed the lay figure from the couch, and laid Karl in its place, covering him with a black velvet pall. He was just in time. She started at seeing no one in Karl’s place and said﻿—
“Where is your pupil, father?”
“Gone home,” he answered, with a kind of convulsive grin.

===Chinese===
