“Oh no, certainly not,” answered the lark, with condescension, and then, bursting into his Jubilate, he sprang aloft, clapping his wings like a clock running down.

“Tell us where﻿—” began Buffy-Bob.

But the lark was out of sight. His song was all that was left of him. That was everywhere, and he was nowhere.

“Selfish bird!” said Buffy. “It’s all very well for larks to go hunting the sun, but they have no business to despise their neighbours, for all that.”

===Chinese===
唱到这里，云雀已经站在巢边，望着孩子们。

"可怜的小东西！你们不会飞。"云雀说。

"是啊；可我们会仰望呀。"崔克西说。

"哎，你们可不知道，亲眼看见太阳最最开头的那一缕，是什么滋味。"

"可我们知道等他到来是什么滋味。他不会因为你先看见他就变得差一点吧，是不是？"

"哦，不，当然不会。"云雀带着几分居高临下地答道，接着便迸发出他的《欢颂》，一跃而起，双翼拍打得像一座走完发条的钟。

"告诉我们哪儿——"胖墩鲍勃刚开口。

可云雀已经不见了踪影。他的歌声是他留下的全部。那歌声无处不在，他的人却无处可寻。

"自私的鸟！"胖墩说，"云雀爱去追太阳，随他们去；可他们也没有理由因此就看不起邻居呀。"

===Original===
“Can I be of any use to you?” said a sweet bird-voice out of the nest.

This was the lark’s wife, who stayed at home with the young larks while her husband went to church.

“Oh! thank you. If you please,” answered Tricksey-Wee.

And up popped a pretty brown head; and then up came a brown feathery body; and last of all came the slender legs on to the edge of the nest. There she turned, and, looking down into the nest, from which came a whole litany of chirpings for breakfast, said, “Lie still, little ones.” Then she turned to the children.

“My husband is King of the Larks,” she said.

Buffy-Bob took off his cap, and Tricksey-Wee courtesied very low.

“Oh, it’s not me,” said the bird, looking very shy. “I am only his wife. It’s my husband.” And she looked up after him into the sky, whence his song was still falling like a shower of musical hailstones. Perhaps *she* could see him.

“He’s a splendid bird,” said Buffy-Bob; “only you know he *will* get up a little too early.”

===Chinese===
"我能帮你们点什么忙吗？"巢里传出一个甜甜的鸟声。

说话的是云雀的妻子，丈夫去"做礼拜"的时候，她便在家陪着小云雀们。

"哦！谢谢您。劳驾您了。"小淘气崔克西答道。

随即，一颗漂亮的褐色小脑袋探了出来；接着探出一个褐色羽毛的身躯；最后，一双纤细的腿也跨上了巢边。她在那儿转过身，朝巢里望下去——巢里正传出一整串讨早餐的啁啾声——说道："乖乖躺着，小家伙们。"然后她转向孩子们。

"我丈夫是云雀之王。"她说。

胖墩鲍勃摘下帽子，小淘气崔克西深深行了个屈膝礼。

"哦，不是我，"那鸟儿说，神情十分腼腆，"我不过是他的妻子。是我丈夫。"说着她仰望他飞去的方向，他的歌声仍像一阵音乐的冰雹从天上洒落。也许，*她*能瞧见他。

"他是一只了不起的鸟儿。"胖墩鲍勃说，"只不过，您知道，他总爱起得稍早一点。"

===Original===
“Oh, no! he doesn’t. It’s only his way, you know. But tell me what I can do for you.”

“Tell us, please, Lady Lark, where the she-eagle lives that sits on Giant Thunderthump’s heart.”

“Oh! that is a secret.”

“Did you promise not to tell?”

“No; but larks ought to be discreet. They see more than other birds.”

“But you don’t fly up high like your husband, do you?”

“Not often. But it’s no matter. I come to know things for all that.”

“Do tell me, and I will sing you a song,” said Tricksey-Wee.

“Can you sing too?﻿—You have got no wings!”

“Yes. And I will sing you a song I learned the other day about a lark and his wife.”

“Please do,” said the lark’s wife. “Be quiet, children, and listen.”

Tricksey-Wee was very glad she happened to know a song which would please the lark’s wife, at least, whatever the lark himself might have thought of it, if he had heard it. So she sang﻿

===Chinese===
"哦，不！他才没有呢。您知道，那不过是他的性子。可您倒是说说，我能为你们做些什么。"

"云雀夫人，劳驾您告诉我们，那只蹲在巨人雷霆步心上的母鹰，住在哪儿？"

"哎呀！那可是个秘密。"

"您答应过人家不说吗？"

"那倒没有；不过云雀应当谨慎。我们比别的鸟儿看得多。"

"可您不像您丈夫那样飞得那么高，是吧？"

"不常飞。可那不要紧。尽管如此，我还是能知道不少事儿。"

"请您告诉我吧，我给您唱一支歌。"小淘气崔克西说。

"你们也会唱歌？——你们可没有翅膀！"

"会。我给您唱一支我前些日子学来的歌，唱的是一只云雀和他的妻子。"

"那就请唱吧。"云雀的妻子说，"安静，孩子们，听着。"

小淘气崔克西十分庆幸，自己恰好会唱一首能让云雀夫人——至少是夫人——喜欢的歌，至于云雀本人听了会怎么想，那就顾不上了。于是她唱道

===Original===
> “ ‘Good morrow, my lord!’ in the sky alone,
> Sang the lark, as the sun ascended his throne.
> ‘Shine on me, my lord; I only am come,
> Of all your servants, to welcome you home.
> I have flown a whole hour, right up, I swear,
> To catch the first shine of your golden hair!’
>
> “ ‘Must I thank you, then,’ said the king, ‘Sir Lark,
> For flying so high, and hating the dark?
> You ask a full cup for half a thirst:
> Half is love of me, and half love to be first.
> There’s many a bird that makes no haste,
> But waits till I come. That’s as much to my taste.
>
> “And the king hid his head in a turban of cloud;
> And the lark stopped singing, quite vexed and cowed.
> But he flew up higher, and thought, ‘Anon,
> The wrath of the king will be over and gone,
> And his crown, shining out of its cloudy fold,
> Will change my brown feathers to a glory of gold.’
>
> “So he flew, with the strength of a lark he flew.
> But as he rose, the cloud rose too;
> And not a gleam of the golden hair
> Came through the depth of the misty air;
> Till, weary with flying, with sighing sore,
> The strong sun-seeker could do no more.
>
> “His wings had had no chrism of gold,
> And his feathers felt withered and worn and old;
> So he quivered and sank, and dropped like a stone.
> And there on his nest, where he left her, alone,
> Sat his little wife on her little eggs,
> Keeping them warm with wings and legs.
>
> “Did I say alone? Ah, no such thing!
> Full in her face was shining the king.
> ‘Welcome, Sir Lark! You look tired,’ said he.
> ‘*Up* is not always the best way to me.
> While you have been singing so high and away,
> I’ve been shining to your little wife all day.’
>
> “He had set his crown all about the nest,
> And out of the midst shone her little brown breast;
> And so glorious was she in russet gold,
> That for wonder and awe Sir Lark grew cold.
> He popped his head under her wing, and lay
> As still as a stone, till the king was away.”

===Chinese===
> "『早安，主公！』在空旷的天庭，
> 云雀高歌，正值太阳登上宝座。
> 『照着我吧，主公；唯独我一人，
> 赶在所有仆从之前迎您归家。
> 我整整飞了一个钟头，笔直向上，我发誓，
> 只为最先沐浴您金发的光华！』
>
> "『那么我该谢你吗，』国王说道，『云雀爵士，
> 因为你飞得那样高，又那样憎恨黑暗？
> 不过半是口渴，你却讨要满杯：
> 一半是爱我，一半是爱争先。
> 有许多鸟儿并不匆忙，
> 只静静等我前来——那才合我胃口。
>
> "于是国王把头藏进一顶云的头巾里；
> 云雀停了歌声，又气又怕。
> 可他飞得更高，心想，『不一会儿，
> 国王的怒气就要过去、消散，
> 他的王冠，从云的褶皱里闪耀而出，
> 会把我褐色的羽毛化作金色的荣光。』
>
> "于是他飞，竭尽一只云雀之力地飞。
> 可他升高一寸，那云也升高一寸；
> 那金发没有一丝光芒
> 透过迷雾深重的空气；
> 终于，飞得倦了，叹得深了，
> 这强壮的追日者再也无力向上。
>
> "他的双翼不曾受过金膏的涂抹，
> 他的羽毛只觉枯萎、破旧、苍老；
> 于是他颤抖、下沉，像石头一般坠落。
> 而在巢里，在他离开她的地方，
> 孤零零地坐着他的小妻子，守着她小小的蛋，
> 用翅膀和腿为它们保暖。
>
> "我说孤零零吗？啊，绝非如此！
> 国王正满面光辉地照着她。
> 『欢迎，云雀爵士！你看上去累了，』他说，
> 『向上并不总是通向我的最佳道路。
> 你在高空、远方一路高歌的时候，
> 我一整天都在照耀你的小妻子。』
>
> "他已把王冠安放在巢的四周，
> 从正中映出她小小褐色的胸脯；
> 她在栗金色中如此辉煌，
> 惊异与敬畏之下，云雀爵士凉了半截。
> 他把头钻到她的翅膀底下，躺着，
> 像石头一样一动不动，直到国王离去。"

===Original===
As soon as Tricksey-Wee had finished her song, the lark’s wife began a low, sweet, modest little song of her own; and after she had piped away for two or three minutes, she said﻿

“You dear children, what can I do for you?”

“Tell us where the she-eagle lives, please,” said Tricksey-Wee.

“Well, I don’t think there can be much harm in telling such wise, good children,” said Lady Lark; “I am sure you don’t want to do any mischief.”

“Oh, no; quite the contrary,” said Buffy-Bob.

“Then I’ll tell you. She lives on the very topmost peak of Mount Skycrack; and the only way to get up is to climb on the spiders’ webs that cover it from top to bottom.”

“That’s rather serious,” said Tricksey-Wee.

“But you don’t want to go up, you foolish little thing! You can’t go. And what do you want to go up for?”

“That is a secret,” said Tricksey-Wee.

“Well, it’s no business of mine,” rejoined Lady Lark, a little offended, and quite vexed that she had told them. So she flew away to find some breakfast for her little ones, who by this time were chirping very impatiently. The children looked at each other, joined hands, and walked off.

===Chinese===
小淘气崔克西的歌刚一唱完，云雀夫人便开始唱起一支属于她自己的、低低、甜美、谦逊的小调；她啭了两三分钟，说道——

"亲爱的孩子们，我能为你们做点什么呢？"

"请告诉我们那只母鹰住在哪儿吧。"小淘气崔克西说。

"这个嘛，我想，告诉你们这样又聪明又乖巧的孩子，应该没什么大碍。"云雀夫人说，"我肯定你们不是要去干坏事。"

"哦，不；正相反呢。"胖墩鲍勃说。

"那我就告诉你们吧。她住在裂天峰最最高的那座峰顶上；要上去，唯一的办法就是攀着从山顶到山脚层层覆盖着它的蜘蛛网往上爬。"

"那可够呛。"小淘气崔克西说。

"可你们并不想上去呀，你这傻乎乎的小东西！你们上不去的。再说，你们上去要做什么？"

"那是个秘密。"小淘气崔克西说。

"那是，那不关我的事。"云雀夫人答道，有点不悦，也懊恼自己竟告诉了他们。于是她飞走了，去给小家伙们找些早点——这会儿他们已经等得不耐烦，叫得正欢。孩子们对视一眼，牵起手，向前走去。

===Original===
In a minute more the sun was up, and they soon reached the outside of the tree. The bark was so knobby and rough, and full of twigs, that they managed to get down, though not without great difficulty. Then, far away to the north, they saw a huge peak, like the spire of a church, going right up into the sky. They thought this must be Mount Skycrack, and turned their faces towards it. As they went on, they saw a giant or two, now and then, striding about the fields or through the woods, but they kept out of their way. Nor were they in much danger; for it was only one or two of the border giants that were so very fond of children.

At last they came to the foot of Mount Skycrack. It stood in a plain alone, and shot right up, I don’t know how many thousand feet, into the air, a long, narrow, spearlike mountain. The whole face of it, from top to bottom, was covered with a network of spiders’ webs, with threads of various sizes, from that of silk to that of whipcord. The webs shook and quivered, and waved in the sun, glittering like silver. All about ran huge greedy spiders, catching huge silly flies, and devouring them.

===Chinese===
没过多久太阳便升起来了，他们很快来到了树外。树皮疙里疙瘩、十分粗糙，又满是细枝，他们好歹攀着爬了下来——尽管费了老大劲。然后，远远的北方，他们瞧见一座巨大的山峰，像教堂的尖顶，直插云霄。他们想这一定就是裂天峰了，便朝它转过脸去。一路上，他们不时瞥见一两个巨人，在田野间大步流星，或穿过树林，但他们都远远避开。其实他们也没多大危险；因为边境上的巨人里，只有那么一两个格外嗜吃小孩。

他们终于来到裂天峰脚下。那山孤零零地立在一片平原上，笔直地向天上射去——我说不准有几万英尺高——又长又窄，像一杆长矛。山的整面，从顶到底，都罩着一层蛛网，蛛丝粗细不一，从蚕丝那么细到鞭绳那么粗。蛛网在阳光下颤动、摇曳、起伏，闪闪发亮，像银子一般。四处爬着贪婪的巨蛛，捉住一只只愚蠢的巨蝇，把它们吞下肚去。

===Original===
Here they sat down to consider what could be done. The spiders did not heed them, but ate away at the flies.﻿—Now, at the foot of the mountain, and all round it, was a ring of water, not very broad, but very deep. As they sat watching them, one of the spiders, whose web was woven across this water, somehow or other lost his hold, and fell in on his back. Tricksey-Wee and Buffy-Bob ran to his assistance, and laying hold each of one of his legs, succeeded, with the help of the other legs, which struggled spiderfully, in getting him out upon dry land. As soon as he had shaken himself, and dried himself a little, the spider turned to the children, saying﻿

“And now, what can I do for you?”

“Tell us, please,” said they, “how we can get up the mountain to the she-eagle’s nest.”

“Nothing is easier,” answered the spider. “Just run up there, and tell them all I sent you, and nobody will mind you.”

“But we haven’t got claws like you, Mr. Spider,” said Buffy.

“Ah! no more you have, poor unprovided creatures! Still, I think we can manage it. Come home with me.”

“You won’t eat us, will you?” said Buffy.

“My dear child,” answered the spider, in a tone of injured dignity, “I eat nothing but what is mischievous or useless. You have helped me, and now I will help you.”

===Chinese===
他们坐下来，琢磨该怎么办。蜘蛛们并不理睬他们，只顾埋头吃苍蝇。——原来，在山脚下，绕山一圈，有道环形的水带，不很宽，却很深。他们正坐着看那些蜘蛛的时候，有一只蛛网横跨水面的蜘蛛，不知怎的失了手，仰面朝天跌进了水里。小淘气崔克西和胖墩鲍勃赶忙跑去相救，一人抓住他一条腿，靠他其余那些腿大力地扑腾挣扎着帮忙，总算把他拽上了干地。蜘蛛抖了抖身子，又稍稍晾干了一点，便转向孩子们说——

"那么，我能为你们做点什么？"

"劳驾您告诉我们，"他们说，"我们怎样才能爬上那座山，到母鹰的窝里去。"

"再容易不过了。"蜘蛛答道，"只管跑上去，告诉他们是我派你们来的，谁也不会管你们。"

"可我们没有您那样的爪子呀，蜘蛛先生。"胖墩说。

"啊！你们确实没有，可怜的、什么装备都没有的小生灵！不过，我想咱们能想法子。跟我回家吧。"

"您不会吃了我们吧，会吗？"胖墩问。

"亲爱的孩子，"蜘蛛答道，那口吻带着受了委屈的尊严，"我只吃那些有害的、或一无是处的东西。你们帮过我，如今我也来帮你们。"

===Original===
The children rose at once, and climbing as well as they could, reached the spider’s nest in the centre of the web. Nor did they find it very difficult; for whenever too great a gap came, the spider spinning a strong cord stretched it just where they would have chosen to put their feet next. He left them in his nest, after bringing them two enormous honey-bags, taken from bees that he had caught; but presently about six of the wisest of the spiders came back with him. It was rather horrible to look up and see them all round the mouth of the nest, looking down on them in contemplation, as if wondering whether they would be nice eating. At length one of them said﻿—“Tell us truly what you want with the eagle, and we will try to help you.”

Then Tricksey-Wee told them that there was a giant on the borders who treated little children no better than radishes, and that they had narrowly escaped being eaten by him; that they had found out that the great she-eagle of Mount Skycrack was at present sitting on his heart; and that, if they could only get hold of the heart, they would soon teach the giant better behaviour.

“But,” said their host, “if you get at the heart of the giant, you will find it as large as one of your elephants. What can you do with it?”

“The least scratch will kill it,” replied Buffy-Bob.

“Ah! but you might do better than that,” said the spider.﻿—“Now we have resolved to help you. Here is a little bag of spider-juice. The giants cannot bear spiders, and this juice is dreadful poison to them. We are all ready to go up with you, and drive the eagle away. Then you must put the heart into this other bag, and bring it down with you; for then the giant will be in your power.”

===Chinese===
孩子们立刻起身，尽力攀爬，来到了蛛网正中的蜘蛛巢里。他们倒也没觉得多困难；因为每当缺口太大，蜘蛛便吐出一根结实的蛛丝，正好横在他们下一步想落脚的地方。他把两只巨大的蜜囊——取自他逮到的蜜蜂——送到他们手里，便把他们留在巢中；可不久，大约六只最聪明的蜘蛛跟着他回来了。抬头一瞧，他们围在巢口，若有所思地俯瞰着下面那两个孩子，似乎在琢磨他们好不好吃——这场面相当骇人。终于，其中一只说道——"老老实实告诉我们，你们找那只鹰要做什么，我们就尽力帮你们。"

于是小淘气崔克西告诉他们，边境上有个巨人，把小孩子当萝卜看待，他们俩差一点儿就被他吃了；他们又打听到，裂天峰那只大母鹰如今正蹲在他的心上；只要能把那颗心弄到手，他们就能很快教会那巨人学点规矩。

"可是，"招待他们的主人说，"你们要是找到巨人的心，会发现它有你们那儿一头大象那么大。你们拿它怎么办？"

"只要划破一点点皮就能杀死它。"胖墩鲍勃答道。

"啊！不过你们可以做得更漂亮。"蜘蛛说，——"我们已经决定帮你们。这里有一小袋蛛汁。巨人受不了蜘蛛，这汁对他们来说是剧毒。我们都准备好跟你们一起上去，把那只鹰赶走。然后你们要把那颗心装进另一只袋子里，带它下来；这样一来，巨人就落在你们手里了。"

===Original===
“But how can we do that?” said Buffy. “The bag is not much bigger than a pudding-bag.”

“But it is as large as you will be able to carry.”

“Yes; but what are we to do with the heart?”

“Put it in the bag, to be sure. Only, first, you must squeeze a drop out of the other bag upon it. You will see what will happen.”

“Very well; we will do as you tell us,” said Tricksey-Wee. “And now, if you please, how shall we go?”

“Oh, that’s our business,” said the first spider. “You come with me, and my grandfather will take your brother. Get up.”

===Chinese===
"可那怎么成呢？"胖墩说，"那袋子也不比装布丁的袋子大多少。"

"但它已经大得你们勉强能拎动了。"

"是呀；可那颗心怎么办？"

"当然装进袋子里呀。只不过，得先从另一只袋子里挤一滴汁水到它身上。到时候你们就知道了。"

"很好；我们听您的。"小淘气崔克西说，"那么，劳驾，我们怎么个走法？"

"哦，那是我们的事。"头一只蜘蛛说，"你跟我来，我爷爷带你弟弟。上来吧。"

===Original===
So Tricksey-Wee mounted on the narrow part of the spider’s back, and held fast. And Buffy-Bob got on the grandfather’s back. And up they scrambled, over one web after another, up and up﻿—so fast! And every spider followed; so that, when Tricksey-Wee looked back, she saw a whole army of spiders scrambling after them.

“What can we want with so many?” she thought; but she said nothing.

The moon was now up, and it was a splendid sight below and around them. All Giantland was spread out under them, with its great hills, lakes, trees, and animals. And all above them was the clear heaven, and Mount Skycrack rising into it, with its endless ladders of spiderwebs, glittering like cords made of moonbeams. And up the moonbeams went, crawling, and scrambling, and racing, a huge army of huge spiders.

===Chinese===
于是小淘气崔克西骑上蜘蛛背部窄窄的地方，紧紧抓住。胖墩鲍勃则爬上了那爷爷蜘蛛的背。他们一网一网地往上爬，越爬越高——快极了！每一只蜘蛛都跟在后面；因此，当小淘气崔克西回头看时，只见一整支蜘蛛大军在他们身后攀爬。

"要这么多蜘蛛做什么呢？"她心想；可她什么也没说。

此时月亮已经升起，他们脚下和四周是一片壮丽的景象。整个巨人国在下面铺展开来，大山、大湖、巨树、巨兽尽收眼底。头顶则是澄澈的天穹，裂天峰直插其间，那无尽的蛛网梯子，亮得像一根根月光搓成的绳索。而沿着这些"月光绳索"，爬的爬，攀的攀，赛的赛，正是一支巨蛛组成的大军。

===Original===
At length they reached all but the very summit, where they stopped. Tricksey-Wee and Buffy-Bob could see above them a great globe of feathers, that finished off the mountain like an ornamental knob.

“But how shall we drive her off?” said Buffy.

“We’ll soon manage that,” answered the grandfather-spider. “Come on you, down there.”

Up rushed the whole army, past the children, over the edge of the nest, on to the she-eagle, and buried themselves in her feathers. In a moment she became very restless, and went pecking about with her beak. All at once she spread out her wings, with a sound like a whirlwind, and flew off to bathe in the sea; and then the spiders began to drop from her in all directions on their gossamer wings. The children had to hold fast to keep the wind of the eagle’s flight from blowing them off. As soon as it was over, they looked into the nest, and there lay the giant’s heart﻿—an awful and ugly thing.

“Make haste, child!” said Tricksey’s spider.

So Tricksey took her bag, and squeezed a drop out of it upon the heart. She thought she heard the giant give a far-off roar of pain, and she nearly fell from her seat with terror. The heart instantly began to shrink. It shrunk and shrivelled till it was nearly gone; and Buffy-Bob caught it up and put it into his bag. Then the two spiders turned and went down again as fast as they could. Before they got to the bottom, they heard the shrieks of the she-eagle over the loss of her egg; but the spiders told them not to be alarmed, for her eyes were too big to see them.﻿—By the time they reached the foot of the mountain, all the spiders had got home, and were busy again catching flies, as if nothing had happened.

===Chinese===
他们终于爬到了离山顶只差一点儿的地方，停了下来。小淘气崔克西和胖墩鲍勃看见头顶上方有一个巨大的羽毛球，像一颗装饰用的圆球，扣在山顶上收了尾。

"可我们怎么把她赶走呢？"胖墩问。

"这事马上就办。"爷爷蜘蛛答道，"下面的，上。"

整支蜘蛛大军一拥而上，越过孩子们，翻过巢边，扑到母鹰身上，钻进了她的羽毛里。她一下子变得十分焦躁，用喙四下乱啄。突然，她展开双翼，发出一阵旋风般的响声，飞走了，去海里沐浴；于是蜘蛛们便纷纷撑开他们的游丝翅，从她身上朝四面八方飘落。孩子们只得紧紧抓住，免得被鹰翼掀起的气流吹下去。风一过，他们朝巢里望去——巨人的心就躺在那里——一个又可怖又丑陋的东西。

"快，孩子！"崔克西的蜘蛛说。

于是崔克西拿起她的袋子，从里面挤了一滴汁水到那颗心上。她好像听见巨人远远地发出一声痛苦的咆哮，吓得差点从坐处跌下去。那颗心立刻开始缩小。它越缩越瘪，几乎要缩没了；胖墩鲍勃一把抓起它，塞进了自己的袋子。接着两只蜘蛛转身，尽快往山下赶。还没下到山脚，他们就听见母鹰因丢了"蛋"而发出的尖叫；可蜘蛛们叫他们别怕，因为她的眼睛太大，根本瞧不见他们。——等他们到达山脚时，所有的蜘蛛都已回了家，又忙着捉苍蝇，仿佛什么事也没发生过。

===Original===
After renewed thanks to their friends, the children set off, carrying the giant’s heart with them.

“If you should find it at all troublesome, just give it a little more spider-juice directly,” said the grandfather, as they took their leave.

Now, the giant had given an awful roar of pain the moment they anointed his heart, and had fallen down in a fit, in which he lay so long that all the boys might have escaped if they had not been so fat. One did, and got home in safety. For days the giant was unable to speak. The first words he uttered were﻿

“Oh, my heart! my heart!”

“Your heart is safe enough, dear Thunderstump,” said his wife. “Really, a man of your size ought not to be so nervous and apprehensive. I am ashamed of you.”

“You have no heart, Doodlem,” answered he. “I assure you that at this moment mine is in the greatest danger. It has fallen into the hands of foes, though who they are I cannot tell.”

Here he fainted again; for Tricksey-Wee, finding the heart begin to swell a little, had given it the least touch of spider-juice.

===Chinese===
孩子们再次向朋友们道谢，便带着巨人的心出发了。

"要是它闹起别扭来，就立刻再给它来一点蛛汁。"他们告别时，爷爷蜘蛛叮嘱道。

原来，他们给他的心涂上蛛汁的那一刻，巨人就发出了一声骇人的痛吼，随即倒下昏死过去。他这一昏厥，时间长得足够让所有男孩都逃跑——要不是他们太胖的话。还真有一个逃了出去，安然无恙地回到了家。一连好几天巨人都说不出话来。他吐出的头一句话是——

"哎呀，我的心啊！我的心啊！"

"您的心平安无事呢，亲爱的雷霆桩（Thunderstump）。"他妻子说，"说真的，您这么大块头的男人，不该这么神经兮兮、大惊小怪。我都替您难为情。"

"你才没有心呢，杜德伦。"他答道，"我向您保证，这会儿我的心正处在最大的危险里。它已经落入仇敌之手，至于他们是谁，我说不上来。"

说到这里他又昏了过去；因为小淘气崔克西发觉那颗心稍稍有点胀，便又给它轻轻点了一点蛛汁。

===Original===
Again he recovered, and said﻿

“Dear Doodlem, my heart is coming back to me. It is coming nearer and nearer.”

After lying silent for hours, he exclaimed﻿

“It is in the house, I know!”

And he jumped up and walked about, looking in every corner.

As he rose, Tricksey-Wee and Buffy-Bob came out of the hole in the tree-root, and through the cat-hole in the door, and walked boldly towards the giant. Both kept their eyes busy watching him. Led by the love of his own heart, the giant soon spied them, and staggered furiously towards them.

===Chinese===
他再度醒转，说道——

"亲爱的杜德伦，我的心正朝我回来呢。它越来越近了。"

静默地躺了几个钟头之后，他叫道——

"它已经进了屋，我知道！"

他一跃而起，四处走动，每个角落都翻了一遍。

就在他起身的同时，小淘气崔克西和胖墩鲍勃从树根的洞口钻了出来，穿过门上的猫洞，大摇大摆地朝巨人走去。两人都目不转睛地盯着他。被对自己那颗心的热爱牵引着，巨人很快发现了他们，踉踉跄跄、怒气冲冲地朝他们扑来。

===Original===
“I will eat you, you vermin!” he cried. “Here with my heart!”

Tricksey gave the heart a sharp pinch. Down fell the giant on his knees, blubbering, and crying, and begging for his heart.

“You shall have it, if you behave yourself properly,” said Tricksey.

“How shall I behave myself properly?” asked he, whimpering.

“Take all those boys and girls, and carry them home at once.”

“I’m not able; I’m too ill. I should fall down.”

“Take them up directly.”

“I can’t, till you give me my heart.”

“Very well!” said Tricksey; and she gave the heart another pinch.

The giant jumped to his feet, and catching up all the children, thrust some into his waistcoat pockets, some into his breast pocket, put two or three into his hat, and took a bundle of them under each arm. Then he staggered to the door.

All this time poor Doodlem was sitting in her armchair, crying, and mending a white stocking.

===Chinese===
"我要吃了你们，你们这两个害人精！"他吼道，"把我的心还来！"

崔克西在心上狠狠一掐。巨人扑通跪倒在地，又哭又号，连连讨要他的心。

"你乖乖听话，就还给你。"崔克西说。

"我怎么个乖乖听话法？"他抽抽搭搭地问。

"把那些男孩女孩全都带上，立刻把他们送回家去。"

"我做不到；我病得太重了。我会栽跟头的。"

"立刻把他们抱起来。"

"我办不到，你得先把心还给我。"

"那好！"崔克西说着，又掐了那心一下。

巨人一蹦跳起来，把孩子们一股脑儿抓起，有的塞进马甲口袋，有的塞进胸前口袋，两三个塞进帽子里，每边腋下再夹上一捆。然后他踉踉跄跄地朝门口走去。

这一会儿工夫，可怜的杜德伦一直坐在她的扶手椅里抹眼泪，手里补着一只白袜子。

===Original===
The giant led the way to the borders. He could not go so fast but that Buffy and Tricksey managed to keep up with him. When they reached the borders, they thought it would be safer to let the children find their own way home. So they told him to set them down. He obeyed.

“Have you put them all down, Mr. Thunderthump?” asked Tricksey-Wee.

“Yes,” said the giant.

“That’s a lie!” squeaked a little voice; and out came a head from his waistcoat pocket.

Tricksey-Wee pinched the heart till the giant roared with pain.

“You’re not a gentleman. You tell stories,” she said.

“He was the thinnest of the lot,” said Thunderthump, crying.

“Are you all there now, children?” asked Tricksey.

“Yes, ma’am,” returned they, after counting themselves very carefully, and with some difficulty; for they were all stupid children.

===Chinese===
巨人在前面带路，朝边境走去。他走得不算太快，胖墩和崔克西还能勉强跟上。到了边境，他们觉得让孩子们自己摸回家去更稳妥些，便叫他把孩子们放下。他照办了。

"您把他们全都放下了吗，雷霆步先生？"小淘气崔克西问。

"是的。"巨人说。

"您撒谎！"一个细细的声音尖叫道；跟着从他的马甲口袋里探出一颗脑袋来。

小淘气崔克西一掐那颗心，痛得巨人直吼。

"您不是个君子。您撒谎。"她说。

"他是这一伙里最瘦的一个。"雷霆步哭丧着说。

"现在你们都到齐了吗，孩子们？"崔克西问。

"到齐了，小姐。"他们答道——这是他们仔仔细细、颇费气力地点过数之后才说的；因为他们都是些笨孩子。

===Original===
“Now,” said Tricksey-Wee to the giant, “will you promise to carry off no more children, and never to eat a child again all you life?”

“Yes, yes! I promise,” answered Thunderthump, sobbing.

“And you will never cross the borders of Giantland?”

“Never.”

“And you shall never again wear white stockings on a Sunday, all your life long.﻿—Do you promise?”

The giant hesitated at this, and began to expostulate; but Tricksey-Wee, believing it would be good for his morals, insisted; and the giant promised.

Then she required of him, that, when she gave him back his heart, he should give it to his wife to take care of for him forever after.

The poor giant fell on his knees, and began again to beg. But Tricksey-Wee giving the heart a slight pinch, he bawled out﻿

“Yes, yes! Doodlem shall have it, I swear. Only she must not put it in the flour-barrel, or in the dust-hole.”

“Certainly not. Make your own bargain with her.﻿—And you promise not to interfere with my brother and me, or to take any revenge for what we have done?”

“Yes, yes, my dear children; I promise everything. Do, pray, make haste and give me back my poor heart.”

“Wait there, then, till I bring it to you.”

“Yes, yes. Only make haste, for I feel very faint.”

===Chinese===
"现在，"小淘气崔克西对巨人说，"您肯不肯答应，再也不拐走孩子，这辈子再也不吃一个小孩？"

"肯，肯！我答应。"雷霆步抽抽搭搭地答道。

"您也绝不再跨出巨人国的边境？"

"绝不。"

"还有，您这辈子再也不在星期天穿白袜子。——您答应吗？"

对此巨人犹豫起来，开始辩解；可小淘气崔克西认为这对他大有教益，坚持不让；巨人便答应了。

接着她又要求：等她把心还给他，他得把它交给妻子，从此永远由她替他照管。

可怜的巨人又跪倒在地，重新央求起来。可小淘气崔克西轻轻一掐那颗心，他便大声嚎叫——

"肯，肯！杜德伦可以拥有它，我发誓。只要她别把它放进面粉桶，也别扔进垃圾洞就行。"

"那当然。剩下的您自个儿跟她商量去。——还有，您答应不来招惹我哥哥和我，也不为我们做的事报复？"

"答应，答应，亲爱的孩子们；我什么都答应。求求你们，快点，把我那可怜的心还给我吧。"

"那您就在那儿等着，我把它给您送过来。"

"是，是。只是请快点，我晕得厉害。"

===Original===
Tricksey-Wee began to undo the mouth of the bag. But Buffy-Bob, who had got very knowing on his travels, took out his knife with the pretence of cutting the string; but, in reality, to be prepared for any emergency.

No sooner was the heart out of the bag, than it expanded to the size of a bullock; and the giant, with a yell of rage and vengeance, rushed on the two children, who had stepped sideways from the terrible heart. But Buffy-Bob was too quick for Thunderthump. He sprang to the heart, and buried his knife in it, up to the hilt. A fountain of blood spouted from it; and with a dreadful groan the giant fell dead at the feet of little Tricksey-Wee, who could not help being sorry for him after all.

===Chinese===
小淘气崔克西动手去解袋口。可胖墩鲍勃这一路已学得机灵，便掏出他的小刀，装作要割断扎口的绳子；实际上，是为防万一。

那颗心一出袋子，便一下子胀成一头阉牛那么大；巨人发出一声狂怒与复仇的嘶吼，朝两个孩子扑来——他俩早已侧身闪开了那颗可怕的心。可胖墩鲍勃比雷霆步更快一步。他一个箭步扑到心上，把小刀一直没入刀柄地扎进了它里面。一股鲜血像喷泉似的涌了出来；巨人发出一声可怖的呻吟，倒地身亡，倒在小淘气崔克西的脚边——尽管如此，她到底还是忍不住为他难过。

### I / 一

===Original===
“My hearers, we grow old,” said the preacher. “Be it summer or be it spring with us now, autumn will soon settle down into winter, that winter whose snow melts only in the grave. The wind of the world sets for the tomb. Some of us rejoice to be swept along on its swift wings, and hear it bellowing in the hollows of earth and sky; but it will grow a terror to the man of trembling limb and withered brain, until at length he will long for the shelter of the tomb to escape its roaring and buffeting. Happy the man who shall then be able to believe that old age itself, with its pitiable decays and sad dreams of youth, is the chastening of the Lord, a sure sign of his love and his fatherhood.”
It was the first Sunday in Advent; but “the chastening of the Lord” came into almost every sermon that man preached.

===Chinese===
“我的听众们，我们都在老去。”传道人说，“且不论此刻是夏是春，秋日很快便会沉落为冬——那冬日的积雪，唯有在坟墓之中才会消融。世上的风，皆吹向坟茔。我们当中有些人，乐于被这疾风鼓荡的翅翼卷着前行，乐于听它在天地间的空谷中咆哮；可对于四肢战栗、心力枯萎的人，这风终将成为一种恐惧，直到末了，他会渴望坟墓的庇护，好躲开它的呼啸与冲击。那时，倘若一个人仍能深信：老迈本身——连同它可悲的衰朽与对青春凄然的旧梦——正是主的管教，是他慈爱与为父之心的确凿凭证——这样的人，是有福的。”
那是降临节的第一个星期日；然而“主的管教”一语，几乎出现在那人讲的每一篇道里。

===Original===
“Eloquent! But after all, *can* this kind of thing be true?” said to himself a man of about thirty, who sat decorously listening. For many years he had thought he believed this kind of thing﻿—but of late he was not so sure.
Beside him sat his wife, in her new winter bonnet, her pretty face turned up toward the preacher; but her eyes﻿—nothing else﻿—revealed that she was not listening. She was much younger than her husband﻿—hardly twenty, indeed.
In the upper corner of the pew sat a pale-faced child about five, sucking her thumb, and staring at the preacher.

===Chinese===
“说得动听！可归根结底，这类话*当真*可信么？”一个约莫三十岁的男人暗自说道；他正端坐着，规规矩矩地听着。多年以来，他自以为相信这一套——可近来，他没那么有把握了。
他身旁坐着他的妻子，戴着新置的冬日女帽，那张俏脸仰向着传道人；然而她的眼神——唯有她的眼神——泄露了她并未在听。她比丈夫年轻得多——其实，几乎还不满二十。
在这排座席的上首角落里，坐着一个约莫五岁、面色苍白的孩子，吮着拇指，直瞪瞪地盯着传道人。

===Original===
The sermon over, they walked home in proximity. The husband looked gloomy, and his eyes sought the ground. The wife looked more smiling than cheerful, and her pretty eyes went hither and thither. Behind them walked the child﻿—steadily, “with level-fronting eyelids.”
It was a late-built region of large, commonplace houses, and at one of them they stopped and entered. The door of the dining-room was open, showing the table laid for their Sunday dinner. The gentleman passed on to the library behind it, the lady went up to her bedroom, and the child a stage higher to the nursery.
It wanted half an hour to dinner. Mr. Greatorex sat down, drummed with his fingers on the arm of his easy-chair, took up a book of arctic exploration, threw it again on the table, got up, and went to the smoking-room. He had built it for his wife’s sake, but was often glad of it for his own. Again he seated himself, took a cigar, and smoked gloomily.

===Chinese===
讲道既毕，他们三人挨近着走回家去。做丈夫的面色阴沉，眼睛只顾盯着地面。妻子的神情，与其说是欣然，不如说是带着笑意，那双秀目四下流转。孩子走在他们身后——步履沉静，“双目平视前方”。
这一带是新辟的街区，尽是一幢幢宽大而平庸的宅子，他们在其中一幢前停下，走了进去。餐室的门敞着，看得到里面已为主日的午餐摆好了席。那位先生径直穿过餐室，走进它后面的书房；那位太太上楼进了自己的卧室；孩子则再上一层，去了育儿室。
离午餐还有半小时。格雷特雷克斯先生（Mr. Greatorex）坐下来，用手指在扶手椅的扶手上敲着鼓点，拿起一本北极探险的书，又把它扔回桌上，起身去了吸烟室。这间吸烟室本是为他太太添盖的，可他也常为自己能有这样一处而暗自庆幸。他又坐下，取一支雪茄，闷闷地抽着。

===Original===
Having reached her bedroom, Mrs. Greatorex took off her bonnet, and stood for ten minutes turning it round and round. Earnestly she regarded it﻿—now gave a twist to the wire-stem of a flower, then spread wider the loop of a bow. She was meditating what it lacked of perfection rather than brooding over its merits: she was keen in bonnets.
Little Sophy﻿—or, as she called herself by a transposition of consonant sounds common with children, Phosy﻿—found her nurse Alice in the nursery. But she was lost in the pages of a certain London weekly, which had found her in a mood open to its influences, and did not even look up when the child entered. With some effort Phosy drew off her gloves, and with more difficulty untied her hat. Then she took off her jacket, smoothed her hair, and retreated to a corner. There a large shabby doll lay upon her little chair: she took it up, disposed it gently upon the bed, seated herself in its place, got a little book from where she had left it under the chair, smoothed down her skirts, and began simultaneously to read and suck her thumb. The book was an unhealthy one, a cup filled to the brim with a poverty-stricken and selfish religion: such are always breaking out like an eruption here and there over the body of the Church, doing their part, doubtless, in carrying off the evil humours generated by poverty of blood, or the congestion of self-preservation. It is wonderful out of what spoiled fruit some children will suck sweetness.

===Chinese===
格雷特雷克斯太太一进卧室，便摘下女帽，足足有十分钟站在那里把帽子翻来覆去地端详。她看得那样专注——时而拧一拧一朵花的铁丝梗，时而把一只蝴蝶结的环圈拉得更开些。她琢磨的是这帽子还缺了什么才算完美，而不是沾沾自喜于它的好处：在女帽一道上，她是极有眼力的。
小索菲（Sophy）——或者，照孩子们常有的那种辅音颠倒的习惯，她把自己叫作福西（Phosy）——在育儿室里找到了她的保姆爱丽丝（Alice）。可爱丽丝正沉迷于一份伦敦周刊的字里行间，那刊物恰逢她心境易受浸染之时寻上了她，孩子进来时她连头也没抬。福西费了点劲才扯下手套，又更费力地解开帽子。随后她脱去外套，抚平头发，退到一个角落。那里，一只又大又破的布娃娃正躺在她的小椅子上：她把它抱起，轻轻安置到床上，自己在它的位置坐下，从椅子底下原先搁着的地方摸出一本小书，抚平裙摆，便同时开始读书和吮拇指。那是本不太健康的书，一只斟得满满、盛着一种贫乏而自私的宗教的杯子：这类东西总像皮疹一样，在教会的肌体上这里那里地冒出来，无可置疑地起着某种作用——把那因血气贫乏或自我保全的郁结所滋生的浊气排将出去。有些孩子竟会从如此败坏的果实里吮出甜味来，真是一件奇事。

===Original===
But she did not read far: her thoughts went back to a phrase which had haunted her ever since first she went to church: “Whom the Lord loveth, he chasteneth.”
“I wish he would chasten me,” she thought for the hundredth time.
The small Christian had no suspicion that her whole life had been a period of chastening﻿—that few children indeed had to live in such a sunless atmosphere as hers.

===Chinese===
可她没读多远：心思又转回到一句自她头一回去教堂便一直萦绕心头的话——“主所爱的，他必管教。”
“巴不得他也来管教管教我。”她第一百回这样想。
这小小的基督徒丝毫没有察觉，她的一生其实一直都在受着管教——能活在像她那样不见天日的氛围里的孩子，实在没有几个。

===Original===
Alice threw down the newspaper, gazed from the window into the backyard of the next house, saw nothing but an elderly manservant brushing a garment, and turned upon Sophy.
“Why don’t you hang up your jacket, miss?” she said, sharply.
The little one rose, opened the wardrobe-door wide, carried a chair to it, fetched her jacket from the bed, clambered up on the chair, and, leaning far forward to reach a peg, tumbled right into the bottom of the wardrobe.
“You clumsy!” exclaimed the nurse angrily, and pulling her out by the arm, shook her.
Alice was not generally rough to her, but there were reasons today.
Phosy crept back to her seat, pale, frightened, and a little hurt. Alice hung up the jacket, closed the wardrobe, and, turning, contemplated her own pretty face and neat figure in the glass opposite. The dinner-bell rang.
“There, I declare!” she cried, and wheeled round on Phosy. “And your hair not brushed yet, miss! Will you ever learn to do a thing without being told it? Thank goodness, I shan’t be plagued with you long! But I pity her as comes after me: I do!”
“If the Lord would but chasten me!” said the child to herself, as she rose and laid down her book with a sigh.

===Chinese===
爱丽丝把报纸一扔，从窗口望进隔壁房子的后院，只看见一个上了年纪的男仆在刷一件衣裳，便转过身来冲着索菲。
“你怎么不把外套挂起来，小姐？”她尖刻地说。
小家伙站起来，把衣橱的门大开，搬过一把椅子，又从床上取来外套，爬上椅子，探身去够衣钩，结果一头栽进了衣橱的最里头。
“你这个笨手笨脚的！”保姆怒声嚷道，一把抓住她的胳膊把她拽出来，狠狠摇了几下。
爱丽丝平日对她倒不至于这么粗鲁，可今天自有缘故。
福西爬回自己的位子，脸色苍白，受了惊，也受了一点伤。爱丽丝挂好外套，关上橱门，一转身，对着对面的镜子端详起自己俏丽的脸庞和齐整的身段来。开饭铃响了。
“好哇，真有你的！”她嚷着，猛地转向福西。“你的头发还没梳呢，小姐！你几时才能学着自己做事、不用人催？谢天谢地，我不久就不用再受你这份罪啦！不过我倒是可怜接我班的那个：真的！”
“巴不得主来管教管教我！”孩子一边起身，一边叹着气放下书，暗自说道。

===Original===
The maid seized her roughly by the arm, and brushed her hair with an angry haste that made the child’s eyes water, and herself feel a little ashamed at the sight of them.
“How could anybody love such a troublesome chit?” she said, seeking the comfort of justification from the child herself.
Another sigh was the poor little damsel’s only answer. She looked very white and solemn as she entered the dining-room.

===Chinese===
女佣一把抓住她的胳膊，气冲冲地替她梳头，那份急火把孩子梳得眼里涌出泪来，女佣自己瞧见了，也不禁有点惭愧。
“这么个讨人嫌的小丫头，谁还能爱得起来？”她说着，竟想从孩子本人嘴里讨一份自我开脱的安慰。
那可怜的小姑娘唯一的回答，又是一声叹息。她走进餐室时，神色煞白而庄重。

===Original===
Mr. Greatorex was a merchant in the City. But he was more of a man than a merchant, which all merchants are not. Also, he was more scrupulous in his dealings than some merchants in the same line of business, who yet stood as well with the world as he; but, on the other hand, he had the meanness to pride himself upon it as if it had been something he might have done without and yet held up his head.
Some six years before, he had married to please his parents; and a year before, he had married to please himself. His first wife had intellect, education, and heart, but little individuality﻿—not enough to reflect the individuality of her husband. The consequence was, he found her uninteresting. He was kind and indulgent however, and not even her best friend blamed him much for manifesting nothing beyond the average devotion of husbands. But in truth his wife had great capabilities, only they had never ripened, and when she died, a fortnight after giving birth to Sophy, her husband had not a suspicion of the large amount of undeveloped power that had passed away with her.

===Chinese===
格雷特雷克斯先生是伦敦城（the City，即伦敦金融区）的一位商人。不过，比起商人的身份，他首先是一个人——这一点，并非所有商人都做得到。再者，比起同业中一些在世界眼里名声并不逊于他的人，他做事更讲信义；可另一方面，他又有那种小气，竟以此自矜，仿佛这份正直是他本可弃之不顾、却仍能昂首挺胸的东西似的。
约莫六年前，他为了顺从父母而结了婚；而一年以前，他为了遂自己的心愿又结了一次婚。他的前妻有才智、有教养、也有心肠，却少个性——少到不足以映照出她丈夫的个性。其结果是，他觉得她乏味。然而他待她宽厚纵容，所以即便是她最要好的女友，也没怎么责怪他只表现出为人夫者那份平均水平的尽心。但事实上，他的妻子大有潜能，只是从未成熟；待她生下索菲两周后辞世之时，她丈夫丝毫没有察觉，有多么大一份未曾展开的力量，已随她一同消逝了。

===Original===
Her child was so like her both in countenance and manner that he was too constantly reminded of her unlamented mother; and he loved neither enough to discover that, in a sense as true as marvellous, the child was the very flower-bud of her mother’s nature, in which her retarded blossom had yet a chance of being slowly carried to perfection. Love alone gives insight, and the father took her merely for a miniature edition of the volume which he seemed to have laid aside forever in the dust of the earth’s lumber-room. Instead, therefore, of watering the roots of his little human slip from the well of his affections, he had scarcely as yet perceived more in relation to her than that he was legally accountable for her existence, and bound to give her shelter and food. If he had questioned himself on the matter, he would have replied that love was not wanting, only waiting upon her growth, and the development of something to interest him.
Little right as he had had to expect anything from his first marriage, he had yet cherished some hopes therein﻿—tolerably vague, it is true, yet hardly faint enough, it would seem, for he was disappointed in them. When its bonds fell from him, however, he flattered himself that he had not worn them in vain, but had through them arrived at a knowledge of women as rare as profound. But whatever the reach of this knowledge, it was not sufficient to prevent him from harbouring the presumptuous hope of so choosing and so fashioning the heart and mind of a woman that they should be as concave mirrors to his own. I do not mean that he would have admitted the figure, but such was really the end he blindly sought. I wonder how many of those who have been disappointed in such an attempt have been thereby aroused to the perception of what a frightful failure their success would have been on both sides. It was bad enough that Augustus Greatorex’s theories had cramped his own development; it would have been tenfold worse had they been operative to the stunting of another soul.

===Chinese===
她的孩子，无论容貌还是举止，都与她生母如出一辙，以至于他无时无刻不被勾起对那位不曾被他哀悼的母亲的记忆；而他对这母女二人，都爱得不够深，否则便会发现：以一种既真切又奇妙的意义而言，这孩子正是她母亲天性中那枚花蕾——在其中，那朵迟迟未放的花，仍有机会被缓缓地带向圆满。唯有爱才能赋予人洞察的眼力，而这位父亲只把她当作一卷袖珍本——那卷书，他似乎已永远搁置在尘世这间堆放杂物之屋的尘埃里了。因此，他非但没有从自己情思的井中汲水，去浇灌这株小人儿幼根，反倒至今在与她的关系上，几乎只意识到一点：在法律上他要为她的存在负责，必须给她衣食庇护。倘若他就此事拷问自己，他定会答道：爱并非欠缺，只是要等她长大，等她身上长出一些能让他感到趣味的东西来。
尽管他从第一次婚姻中几乎无权指望得到什么，他却仍怀抱着一些希望——诚然相当模糊，却似乎又不算太微弱，因为他到底在其中感到了失望。然而当那婚姻的枷锁从他身上脱落之时，他竟自我恭维起来，以为这枷锁戴得并不冤枉，说他正是借由它，才获得了一份既稀罕又深刻的女人学问。可无论这份学识多么深远，都还不足以阻止他怀揣起那份僭妄的希望——他要亲手挑选、亲手塑就一个女人的心灵与头脑，使之成为映照他自己的一面凹面镜。我并不是说他会承认这个比喻，可那确确实实是他盲目追逐的终点。我常好奇，在这样一次尝试里栽过跟头的人当中，究竟有几位，会因此猛然觉悟：倘若他们当真成了功，那将是一场何等可怕的、两败俱伤的失败。奥古斯都·格雷特雷克斯（Augustus Greatorex）的种种理论，已钳制了他自己的成长，这已够糟；倘若这些理论再进而压抑、扭曲了另一个灵魂，那便要糟上十倍。

===Original===
Letty Merewether was the daughter of a bishop *in partibus*. She had been born tolerably innocent, had grown up more than tolerably pretty, and was, when she came to England at the age of sixteen, as nearly a genuine example of Locke’s sheet of white paper as could well have fallen to the hand of such an experimenter as Greatorex would fain become.
In his suit he had prospered﻿—perhaps too easily. He loved the girl, or at least loved the modified reflection of her in his own mind; while she, thoroughly admiring the dignity, good looks, and accomplishments of the man whose attentions flattered her self-opinion, accorded him deference enough to encourage his vainest hopes. Although she knew little, fluttering over the merest surfaces of existence, she had sense enough to know that he talked sense to her, and foolishness enough to put it down to her own credit, while for the sense itself she cared little or nothing. And Greatorex, without even knowing what she was rough-hewn for, would take upon him to shape her ends!﻿—an ambition the Divinity never permits to succeed: he who fancies himself the carver finds himself but the chisel, or indeed perhaps only the mallet, in the hand of the true workman.

===Chinese===
莱蒂·梅里韦瑟（Letty Merewether）是一位名义主教（bishop *in partibus*，即名义上驻于异教地区、并无实际教区的主教）的女儿。她生下来时算得上相当天真无邪，长大后又出落得相当俏丽；待她十六岁来到英格兰时，简直是一张再地道不过的洛克所谓的白纸，恰好落入格雷特雷克斯这般一心想做实验者的人手中。
他的求婚颇为顺利——也许太过顺利了。他爱这姑娘，或者至少爱她在自己心目中那个经过修饰的投影；而她呢，对这位殷勤讨好、遂使她自视更佳的男子的气度、仪表与才情都钦慕备至，给予他足够的尊崇，恰恰鼓舞了他最虚荣的指望。她虽然所知甚少，只在生活的最浅表层上扑腾，却还有那么点见识，能听出他对她讲的是有道理的话；而她又愚蠢到把它记到自己功劳账上，至于那些道理本身，她倒几乎毫不在乎。而格雷特雷克斯，连她这块木料天生粗坯是要派什么用场的都还不知道，竟就要着手去“削整她的端末”！——这是一种神明从不许其得逞的野心：那自以为是雕刻师的人，到头来发现自己不过是真匠人手中的凿子，甚至也许只是那把木槌。

===Original===
During the days of his courtship, then, Letty listened and smiled, or answered with what he took for a spiritual response, when it was merely a brain-echo. Looking down into the pond of her being, whose surface was, not yet ruffled by any bubbling of springs from below, he saw the reflection of himself and was satisfied. An able man on his hobby looks a centaur of wisdom and folly; but if he be at all a wise man, the beast will one day or other show him the jade’s favour of unseating him. Meantime Augustus Greatorex was fooled, not by poor little Letty, who was not capable of fooling him, but by himself. Letty had made no pretences; had been interested, and had shown her interest; had understood, or seemed to understand, what he said to her, and forgotten it the next moment﻿—had no pocket to put it in, did not know what to do with it, and let it drop into the Limbo of Vanity. They had not been married many days before the scouts of advancing disappointment were upon them. Augustus resisted manfully for a time. But the truth was each of the two had to become a great deal more than either was, before any approach to unity was possible. He tried to interest her in one subject after another﻿—tried her first, I am ashamed to say, with political economy. In that instance, when he came home to dinner he found that she had not got beyond the first page of the book he had left with her. But she had the best of excuses, namely, that of that page she had not understood a sentence. He saw his mistake, and tried her with poetry. But Milton, with whom unfortunately he commenced his approaches, was to her, if not equally unintelligible, equally uninteresting. He tried her next with the elements of science, but with no better success. He returned to poetry, and read some of the Faerie Queene with her: she was, or seemed to be, interested in all his talk about it, and inclined to go on with it in his absence, but found the first stanza she tried more than enough without him to give life to it. She could give it none, and therefore it gave her none. I believe she read a chapter of the Bible every day, but the only books she read with any real interest were novels of a sort that Augustus despised. It never occurred to him that he ought at once to have made friends of this Momus of unrighteousness, for by them he might have found entrance to the sealed chamber. He ought to have read with her the books she did like, for by them only could he make her think, and from them alone could he lead her to better. It is but from the very step upon which one stands that one can move to the next. Besides these books, there was nothing in her scheme of the universe but fashion, dress, calls, the park, other-peopledom, concerts, plays, churchgoing﻿—whatever could show itself on the frosted glass of her *camera obscura*﻿—make an interest of motion and colour in her darkened chamber. Without these, her bosom’s mistress would have found life unendurable, for not yet had she ascended her throne, but lay on the floor of her nursery, surrounded with toys that imitated life.

===Chinese===
于是，在求偶的那些日子里，莱蒂只是听着、笑着，或以他当作心灵回响的话语作答——其实那不过是脑子里的一个回声。他俯身望进她那口生命之潭——潭面尚未被任何自下涌出的泉水激起涟漪——他看见了自己的倒影，便心满意足了。一个有能耐的人骑上自己的嗜好之马，远看就像一匹半人马，一半是智慧，一半是愚蠢；可他若还算个智者，那畜生迟早要赏他一记劣马的“恩惠”——把他掀下马来。此时此刻，被愚弄的是奥古斯都·格雷特雷克斯；愚弄他的不是可怜的小莱蒂——她还无能到愚弄不了他——而是他自己。莱蒂并没作过任何伪装；她是真有兴趣，也表露了她的兴致；她懂了，或像是懂了他对她讲的话，转眼却又忘个干净——她没有口袋可装下它，也不知拿它如何是好，便随它落进了虚荣的灵薄狱（Limbo of Vanity）。他们成亲没几天，那支悄然逼近的失望的斥候便已找上了他们。奥古斯都一度硬着头皮、像个男子汉似的抵抗了一阵。但事实是：这两人都得比各自当下所是的，成长得更多，才有可能走向彼此合一。他一样接一样地想勾起她的兴致——说来惭愧，他最先拿去试探她的，是政治经济学。那一回，他回家吃午饭时发现，她连他留给她的那本书的第一页都没翻过去。可她有再充分不过的借口：那一页上的每一句，她都没看懂。他看出自己走错了棋，便改用诗歌去试她。可惜他不幸从弥尔顿下手，而弥尔顿于她，纵非同样费解，至少是同样无趣。接着他又拿科学的入门去试，结果也没好到哪儿去。他重新回到诗歌，陪她读了一些《仙后》（Faerie Queene）：对他就这部诗所发的一切议论，她似乎都很有兴致，也愿意在他不在时自己接着往下读；可她试着独自读第一段，便觉得没了他在旁给它注入生气，实在受不了。她给不了它生气，于是它也还不出半点生气给她。我相信她每天读一章《圣经》，可她真正带着兴趣去读的，只有那种奥古斯都瞧不上眼的小说。他从未想到，自己原该立刻与这“不义之莫穆斯”（Momus，希腊神话中专司嘲弄与非难之神）交个朋友——因为借由它们，他或许能找到通往那间上了封的密室的入口。他本该陪她读她所喜欢的那类书；因为唯有通过它们，他才能让她开始思考，也唯有从它们出发，他才能领她走向更好。人只能从脚下的这一级，迈向下一级。除这些书之外，在她的宇宙图景里再没有别的，只有时尚、衣裳、拜客、逛公园、他人之境、音乐会、看戏、上教堂——凡是能映上她那架暗箱（camera obscura）毛玻璃的种种——在她那间幽暗的内室里，化作运动与色彩的一点兴味。少了这些，她心坎里的那位女主人便会觉得日子难熬；因为那时她尚未登上自己的宝座，只是躺在育儿室的地板上，被一堆模仿着活物样子的玩具团团围着。

===Original===
It was no wonder, therefore, that Augustus was at length compelled to allow himself disappointed. That it was the fault of his self-confidence made the thing no whit better. He was too much of a man not to cherish a certain tenderness for her, but he soon found to his dismay that it had begun to be mingled with a shadow of contempt. Against this he struggled, but with fluctuating success. He stopped later and later at business, and when he came home spent more and more of his time in the smoking-room, where by and by he had bookshelves put up. Occasionally he would accept an invitation to dinner and accompany his wife, but he detested evening parties, and when Letty, who never refused an invitation if she could help it, went to one, he remained at home with his books. But his power of reading began to diminish. He became restless and irritable. Something kept gnawing at his heart. There was a sore spot in it. The spot grew larger and larger, and by degrees the centre of his consciousness came to be a soreness: his cherished idea had been fooled; he had taken a silly girl for a woman of undeveloped wealth;﻿—a bubble, a surface whereon fair colours chased each other, for a hearted crystal.
On her part, Letty too had her grief, which, unlike Augustus, she did not keep to herself, receiving in return from more than one of her friends the soothing assurance that Augustus was only like all other men; that women were but their toys, which they cast away when weary of them. Letty did not see that she was herself making a toy of her life, or that Augustus was right in refusing to play with such a costly and delicate thing. Neither did Augustus see that, having, by his own blunder, married a mere child, he was bound to deal with her as one, and not let the child suffer for his fault more than what could not be helped. It is not by pressing our insights upon them, but by bathing the sealed eyelids of the human kittens, that we can help them.

===Chinese===
因此，到末了，奥古斯都不得不承认自己失望了，这也就不足为奇了。这要归咎于他的自负，可这一点并未让事情好上半分。他到底还算个男子汉，对她仍怀有一份柔情；但他很快便不安地发觉，这份柔情里已开始掺进一丝轻蔑的阴影。他与这种心绪搏斗着，却时胜时败。他在行里耽搁得越来越晚；回到家中，待在吸烟室里的时间也越来越多，后来他又让人在那儿装上了书架。偶尔他会应下某次晚宴的邀约，陪妻子同往，可他厌恶晚间的聚会；而莱蒂只要能赴约便从不推辞，每逢她出门赴会，他便留在家里与书为伴。可他读书的能力开始衰减。他变得心神不宁、易怒烦躁。有什么东西老在啃啮他的心。那心上有一处溃疡。这溃疡越烂越大，渐渐地，他意识的中心本身竟成了一处溃疡：他所珍视的那个念头被人耍了；他把一个傻丫头，错当作了内里尚未展开其富足的女人；——把一个肥皂泡，一面任绚丽色彩相互追逐的薄膜，错当作了一颗有内核的水晶。
至于莱蒂，她也有自己的苦楚；与奥古斯都不同，她并不把这些苦楚闷在心里，而作为回报，她从不止一位女友那里得到了那份宽慰人心的保证：奥古斯都不过和天底下所有男人一样；女人不过是他们的玩物，玩腻了便随手丢弃。莱蒂没有看出，她自己正在把生命当作一件玩物来消遣；也没有看出，奥古斯都拒绝拿这么一件昂贵又精巧的东西来玩耍，原是对的。而奥古斯都同样没有看出：既然是他自己的失察，娶回来的不过是个孩子，那他就该把她当作孩子来待，不该让这孩子为他的过错承受超出无可奈何之外的苦。要帮助那些小人儿，靠的不是把我们的见识硬塞给他们，而是替这些人类的小猫咪，去润洗他们那紧闭的眼睑。

===Original===
And all the time poor little Phosy was left to the care of Alice, a clever, careless, good-hearted, self-satisfied damsel, who, although seldom so rough in her behaviour as we have just seen her, abandoned the child almost entirely to her own resources. It was often she sat alone in the nursery, wishing the Lord would chasten her﻿—because then he would love her.
The first course was nearly over ere Augustus had brought himself to ask﻿—
“What did you think of the sermon today, Letty?”

===Chinese===
而与此同时，可怜的小福西被丢给了爱丽丝照管——这是个机灵、马虎、心肠不坏又自满的姑娘；虽说平日里难得像我们方才所见的那样粗暴，她却几乎把这孩子整个儿扔给她自己去打发。这孩子常常独自坐在育儿室里，巴望着主来管教她——因为那样，主就是爱她了。
头道菜快上完时，奥古斯都才终于鼓起勇气问——
“莱蒂，你觉得今天这篇讲道如何？”

===Original===
“Not much,” answered Letty. “I am not fond of finery. I prefer simplicity.”
Augustus held his peace bitterly. For it was just finery in a sermon, without knowing it, that Letty was fond of: what seemed to him a flimsy syllabub of sacred things, beaten up with the whisk of composition, was charming to Letty; while, on the contrary, if a man such as they had been listening to was carried away by the thoughts that struggled in him for utterance, the result, to her judgment, was finery, and the object display. In excuse it must be remembered that she had been used to her father’s style, which no one could have aspersed with lack of sobriety. Presently she spoke again.
“Gus, dear, couldn’t you make up your mind for once to go with me to Lady Ashdaile’s tomorrow? I am getting quite ashamed of appearing so often without you.”

===Chinese===
“不怎么样。”莱蒂答道，“我不爱花哨。我喜欢素净。”
奥古斯都满腹苦涩地沉默了。因为莱蒂所爱的，恰恰正是讲道中的花哨——只是她自己并不知晓罢了：在他看来，那不过是用作文的打蛋器，把一桩桩神圣之物搅打出来的一盘轻飘飘的奶冻；可在莱蒂眼里，那却迷人之极。反之，倘若一位像他们方才听过的那样的传道人，被那些在他心中挣扎着要冲口而出的念头带离了自己，那么在她看来，那结果便是花哨，其用心便是卖弄。为她开脱一句，得记得她向来习惯的是她父亲的那套风格——而那套风格，谁也不能指摘它少了庄重。过了一会儿，她又开口了。
“加斯，亲爱的，明天你就不能打定主意，破例陪我去一趟阿什代尔夫人（Lady Ashdaile）家么？老是我一个人抛头露面，我都快不好意思了。”

===Original===
“There is another way of avoiding that unpleasantness,” remarked her husband drily.
“You cruel creature!” returned Letty playfully. “But I must go this once, for I promised Mrs. Holden.”
“You know, Letty,” said her husband, after a little pause, “it gets of more and more consequence that you should not fatigue yourself. By keeping such late hours in such stifling rooms you are endangering two lives﻿—remember that, Letty. It you stay at home tomorrow, I will come home early, and read to you all the evening.”
“Gussy, that *would* be charming. You *know* there is nothing in the world I should enjoy so much. But this time I really mustn’t.”
She launched into a list of all the great nobodies and small somebodies who were to be there, and whom she positively must see: it might be her only chance.
Those last words quenched a sarcasm on Augustus’ lips. He was kinder than usual the rest of the evening, and read her to sleep with the Pilgrim’s Progress.

===Chinese===
“还有一种法子，可以免去那桩不痛快。”她的丈夫冷冷地说。
“你这个狠心的人儿！”莱蒂娇嗔地回道。“可这一回我非去不可，因为我已经答应了霍尔顿夫人（Mrs. Holden）。”
“你知道的，莱蒂，”她丈夫顿了顿，说道，“你不能再累着自己，这事越发要紧了。在那样气闷的屋子里熬到那么晚，你是在拿两条性命冒险——记住啊，莱蒂。明天你要是肯留在家里，我就早点回来，整整一晚上读书给你听。”
“加西，那*可*太好了。你*知道*，天底下再没有比这更让我开心的事了。可这一回，我是真的不能不去了。”
她便滔滔不绝地数起所有那些要去赴会的要人中的小角色、小角色中的要人，说她非见他们不可：这也许是她唯一的机会了。
这最后几个字，噎住了奥古斯都唇边一句讥诮。那天晚上余下的时光，他比平日更加温存，读着《天路历程》（Pilgrim’s Progress），一直读到她睡熟。

===Original===
Phosy sat in a corner, listened, and understood. Or where she misunderstood, it was an honest misunderstanding, which never does much hurt. Neither father nor mother spoke to her till they bade her good night. Neither saw the hungry heart under the mask of the still face. The father never imagined her already fit for the modelling she was better without, and the stepmother had to become a mother before she could value her.
Phosy went to bed to dream of the Valley of Humiliation.

===Chinese===
福西坐在角落里，听着，也听懂了。即便有听不懂的地方，那也是老实的误解，而老实的误解从来为害不大。父亲和母亲，直到跟她道晚安之前，谁也没跟她说过一句话。谁也没看见那张沉静面孔之下，那一颗饥渴的心。父亲从没想过，她早已是可以拿来捏塑的料了——尽管这捏塑她宁可不要；而这位继母，也得等到自己也成了母亲，才能懂得她的价值。
福西上床睡觉，梦里去到了谦卑谷（Valley of Humiliation）。

## The Golden Key / 金钥匙

===Original===
There was a boy who used to sit in the twilight and listen to his great-aunt’s stories.
She told him that if he could reach the place where the end of the rainbow stands he would find there a golden key.
“And what is the key for?” the boy would ask. “What is it the key of? What will it open?”
“That nobody knows,” his aunt would reply. “He has to find that out.”
“I suppose, being gold,” the boy once said, thoughtfully, “that I could get a good deal of money for it if I sold it.”
“Better never find it than sell it,” returned his aunt. And then the boy went to bed and dreamed about the golden key.
Now, all that his great-aunt told the boy about the golden key would have been nonsense, had it not been that their little house stood on the borders of Fairyland. For it is perfectly well known that out of Fairyland nobody ever can find where the rainbow stands. The creature takes such good care of its golden key, always flitting from place to place, lest anyone should find it! But in Fairyland it is quite different. Things that look real in this country look very thin indeed in Fairyland, while some of the things that here cannot stand still for a moment, will not move there. So it was not in the least absurd of the old lady to tell her nephew such things about the golden key.

===Chinese===
从前有个男孩，常常坐在暮色里，听他姑奶奶讲故事。
姑奶奶告诉他，只要他能走到彩虹落脚的地方，就能在那里找到一把金钥匙。
"那钥匙是做什么用的呢？"男孩总问，"它能开什么？又是什么东西的钥匙？"
"这谁也不知道，"姑奶奶答道，"这事得他自己去找出来。"
"我猜，既然是金的，"有一回男孩若有所思地说，"我要是把它卖了，准能换不少钱。"
"那宁可永远别找着它，也别卖它。"姑奶奶回他。于是男孩上了床，整夜梦见那把金钥匙。
话说回来，要不是他们那间小屋正巧坐落在仙境（Fairyland）的边陲，姑奶奶对男孩说的这一番关于金钥匙的话，便全是无稽之谈了。因为人尽皆知，出了仙境，谁也寻不见彩虹究竟立在哪里。那生灵把自己的金钥匙看得可紧啦，总是一处一处地飞来飞去，生怕有人寻着！可是在仙境里头，情形就大不相同。在凡间看着结结实实的东西，到了仙境里其实薄得很；而有些在凡间一刻也静不下来的东西，到了那里却一动也不动。所以这位老太太对她侄儿讲的那些关于金钥匙的话，一点也不荒唐。

===Original===
“Did you ever know anybody find it?” he asked one evening.
“Yes. Your father, I believe, found it.”
“And what did he do with it, can you tell me?”
“He never told me.”
“What was it like?”
“He never showed it to me.”
“How does a new key come there always?”
“I don’t know. There it is.”
“Perhaps it is the rainbow’s egg.”
“Perhaps it is. You will be a happy boy if you find the nest.”
“Perhaps it comes tumbling down the rainbow from the sky.”
“Perhaps it does.”

===Chinese===
"您可曾知道有谁找到过它吗？"一天傍晚他问。
"知道。你父亲，我想，就找到过。"
"他拿它怎么用了，您能告诉我吗？"
"他从未告诉过我。"
"它是什么样子的？"
"他也从未拿出来给我看过。"
"那里怎么总会有一把新钥匙呢？"
"我不知道。它就是在那儿。"
"说不定它是彩虹下的蛋。"
"说不定是的。你要是能找到那窝，可就是个有福的孩子了。"
"说不定它是顺着彩虹从天上滚下来的。"
"说不定是那样。"

===Original===
One evening, in summer, he went into his own room, and stood at the lattice-window, and gazed into the forest which fringed the outskirts of Fairyland. It came close up to his great-aunt’s garden, and, indeed, sent some straggling trees into it. The forest lay to the east, and the sun, which was setting behind the cottage, looked straight into the dark wood with his level red eye. The trees were all old, and had few branches below, so that the sun could see a great way into the forest; and the boy, being keen-sighted, could see almost as far as the sun. The trunks stood like rows of red columns in the shine of the red sun, and he could see down aisle after aisle in the vanishing distance. And as he gazed into the forest he began to feel as if the trees were all waiting for him, and had something they could not go on with till he came to them. But he was hungry, and wanted his supper. So he lingered.

===Chinese===
夏日的一个傍晚，他走进自己的小房间，站在花格窗前，凝望着那片镶在仙境边陲的森林。那林子一直延伸到姑奶奶的花园边，甚至还有几棵歪歪斜斜的树伸进了园子里。森林在东边，正在小屋背后落山的太阳，用它那一只平平的红眼睛直直地望进幽暗的林子。树都是些老树，下头的枝杈稀稀落落，因此太阳望得见森林的深处；而那男孩眼力又尖，几乎能望得和太阳一般远。在红日的辉光里，一根根树干立着，如同排排红色的廊柱，他可以顺着一条又一条甬道望向渐次隐没的深处。他凝望着那片森林，渐渐觉着，那些树仿佛全都在等着他，有一桩什么事，非等他来到它们跟前才能继续做下去。可他肚子饿了，想吃晚饭。于是他迟迟没有动。

===Original===
Suddenly, far among the trees, as far as the sun could shine, he saw a glorious thing. It was the end of a rainbow, large and brilliant. He could count all the seven colours, and could see shade after shade beyond the violet; while before the red stood a colour more gorgeous and mysterious still. It was a colour he had never seen before. Only the spring of the rainbow-arch was visible. He could see nothing of it above the trees.
“The golden key!” he said to himself, and darted out of the house, and into the wood.
He had not gone far before the sun set. But the rainbow only glowed the brighter: for the rainbow of Fairyland is not dependent upon the sun as ours is. The trees welcomed him. The bushes made way for him. The rainbow grew larger and brighter; and at length he found himself within two trees of it.

===Chinese===
猛然间，在林子深处，在那太阳光所能照到的地方，他看见了一样光辉灿烂的东西。那是彩虹的一端，又大又亮。彩虹的七色他一种一种数得清楚，还能看见紫色之外一层又一层更深的色阶；而在红色之前，还有一色，比哪一色都更绚烂，也更神秘。那是一种他从未见过的颜色。他只能看见彩虹拱形的根部，拱的上头在树梢之上，他一概看不见。
"金钥匙！"他自言自语，随即冲出屋子，奔进了林子。
他没走多远，太阳便落了。可彩虹反而更加明亮地燃着：因为仙境的彩虹不像我们这儿的彩虹要靠着太阳。树木迎接他。灌木为他让路。彩虹越变越大，越变越亮；终于他离它只剩两棵树之遥了。

===Original===
It was a grand sight, burning away there in silence, with its gorgeous, its lovely, its delicate colours, each distinct, all combining. He could now see a great deal more of it. It rose high into the blue heavens, but bent so little that he could not tell how high the crown of the arch must reach. It was still only a small portion of a huge bow.
He stood gazing at it till he forgot himself with delight﻿—even forgot the key which he had come to seek. And as he stood it grew more wonderful still. For in each of the colours, which was as large as the column of a church, he could faintly see beautiful forms slowly ascending as if by the steps of a winding stair. The forms appeared irregularly﻿—now one, now many, now several, now none﻿—men and women and children﻿—all different, all beautiful.
He drew nearer to the rainbow. It vanished. He started back a step in dismay. It was there again, as beautiful as ever. So he contented himself with standing as near it as he might, and watching the forms that ascended the glorious colours towards the unknown height of the arch, which did not end abruptly, but faded away in the blue air, so gradually that he could not say where it ceased.
When the thought of the golden key returned, the boy very wisely proceeded to mark out in his mind the space covered by the foundation of the rainbow, in order that he might know where to search, should the rainbow disappear. It was based chiefly upon a bed of moss.
Meantime it had grown quite dark in the wood. The rainbow alone was visible by its own light. But the moment the moon rose the rainbow vanished. Nor could any change of place restore the vision to the boy’s eyes. So he threw himself down upon the mossy bed, to wait till the sunlight would give him a chance of finding the key. There he fell fast asleep.

===Chinese===
那是何等壮观的景象啊——它就那样无声地在那里燃着，带着它那绚烂的、可爱的、纤美的颜色，一色一色各自分明，又融在一起。他现在能看见的，比先前多得多了。它高高地升入湛蓝的天穹，可是弯得极微，他实在说不出那拱顶究竟高到了何处。它仍然不过是一道巨大长弓的一小段。
他站着凝望它，直望得喜而忘我——连自己原是为寻那把钥匙来的，也忘了。他正站着，那景象越发奇了。原来在那每一种颜色里——每一种都大得像教堂的廊柱——他都隐约看得见美丽的形体，仿佛踩着一道螺旋阶梯，正缓缓地往上升。那些形体时隐时现地出现——时而一个，时而许多，时而几个，时而一个也没有——有男人，有女人，也有孩子——各不相同，却无一不美。
他又朝彩虹走近了些。彩虹不见了。他惊愕地退后一步。彩虹却又在那里了，和先前一样美。于是他只好就站在所能站到的最近的地方，望着那些顺着绚烂的色彩、朝着拱顶未知的高处拾级而上的形体；那拱顶并非戛然而止，而是在蓝色的空气里一点点淡去，淡得那样缓慢，他竟说不出它究竟消失在何处。
待那男孩想起了金钥匙，他便很明智地用心记下了彩虹底部所覆盖的那片地方，好等彩虹一旦消失，他知道该去何处找寻。那彩虹 chiefly 落在一片苔藓地上。
这当儿，林子里已经全黑了。唯有那彩虹，凭着它自己的光，还能看得见。可是月亮一升起来，彩虹便消失了。男孩无论换到何处，也无法让那景象再回到眼里。于是他扑倒在长满青苔的地面上，等着天亮，好借着日光有机会找到那把钥匙。他在那里沉沉睡去。

===Original===
When he woke in the morning the sun was looking straight into his eyes. He turned away from it, and the same moment saw a brilliant little thing lying on the moss within a foot of his face. It was the golden key. The pipe of it was of plain gold, as bright as gold could be. The handle was curiously wrought and set with sapphires. In a terror of delight he put out his hand and took it, and had it.
He lay for a while, turning it over and over, and feeding his eyes upon its beauty. Then he jumped to his feet, remembering that the pretty thing was of no use to him yet. Where was the lock to which the key belonged? It must be somewhere, for how could anybody be so silly as make a key for which there was no lock? Where should he go to look for it? He gazed about him, up into the air, down to the earth, but saw no keyhole in the clouds, in the grass, or in the trees.
Just as he began to grow disconsolate, however, he saw something glimmering in the wood. It was a mere glimmer that he saw, but he took it for a glimmer of rainbow, and went towards it.﻿—And now I will go back to the borders of the forest.

===Chinese===
早晨他醒来时，太阳正一眼不眨地直望进他的眼睛。他把脸转开，就在同一瞬间，他看见苔藓上离他不到一尺的地方，躺着一样亮晶晶的小东西。那正是金钥匙。钥匙的杆是赤金打成的，亮得不能再亮。钥匙柄錾着奇异的花样，还镶着蓝宝石。他怀着一场大欢喜里的惊恐，伸出手去，一把抓住了它，它便是他的了。
他躺了一会儿，把它翻来覆去地把玩，让两眼尽情饱餐它的美。然后他一跃而起，想了起来：这漂亮玩意儿眼下对他还毫无用处。这把钥匙所配的锁孔在哪儿呢？它总该在某处才是，因为怎么会有人傻到去做一把根本没有锁可配的钥匙呢？他该到哪儿去找呢？他四下里张望，朝天上望，朝地下望，可云里、草里、树里，都看不见半个锁孔。
然而正当他开始灰心丧气的时候，他看见林子里有什么东西在闪闪发亮。他看见的不过是一星微光，他却当作是彩虹的一星闪光，便朝它走了过去。——话分两头，这里我且退回到森林的边陲。

===Original===
Not far from the house where the boy had lived there was another house, the owner of which was a merchant, who was much away from home. He had lost his wife some years before, and had only one child, a little girl, whom he left to the charge of two servants, who were very idle and careless. So she was neglected and left untidy, and was sometimes ill-used besides.
Now, it is well known that the little creatures commonly called fairies, though there are many different kinds of fairies in Fairyland, have an exceeding dislike to untidiness. Indeed, they are quite spiteful to slovenly people. Being used to all the lovely ways of the trees and flowers, and to the neatness of the birds and all woodland creatures, it makes them feel miserable, even in their deep woods and on their grassy carpets, to think that within the same moonlight lies a dirty, uncomfortable, slovenly house. And this makes them angry with the people that live in it, and they would gladly drive them out of the world if they could. They want the whole earth nice and clean. So they pinch the maids black and blue, and play them all manner of uncomfortable tricks.
But this house was quite a shame, and the fairies in the forest could not endure it. They tried everything on the maids without effect, and at last resolved upon making a clean riddance, beginning with the child. They ought to have known that it was not her fault, but they have little principle and much mischief in them, and they thought that if they got rid of her the maids would be sure to be turned away.

===Chinese===
离男孩住的那间屋子不远，还有另一间屋子，屋主是个商人，常在外头奔波。他几年前没了妻子，膝下只有一个孩子，是个小女孩，他把孩子交给两个仆人照管，可这两个仆人又懒又马虎。于是那孩子便无人照管，蓬头垢面，有时还要挨打受气。
话说回来，人尽皆知，那些通常被唤作仙灵（fairy）的小生灵——虽说仙境里的仙灵有许多许多不同的种类——都极其厌恶不整洁。说真的，他们对邋遢的人家满怀坏心眼。他们看惯了花草树木那一切可爱的仪态，看惯了飞鸟和一切林间生灵的齐整，所以哪怕在他们幽深的林子里、在他们铺满青草的地毯上，一想到同一片月光底下竟躺着一户又脏又难受又邋遢的人家，他们心里就难受得很。这便叫他们对住在里头的人也生起气来，恨不得把他们通通赶出这世界才好。他们要这整个大地又洁净又清爽。于是他们就拧那些女仆，拧得她们青一块紫一块，还变着法子捉弄她们，叫她们不得安生。
可这一户人家实在太不成话了，林子里的仙灵们再也忍不下去。他们在两个女仆身上把什么法子都使尽了，全无效果，最后便下了决心，要来个斩草除根，先从那个孩子下手。他们本该明白这不是孩子的过错，可他们一肚子坏水，原则又少，便想着只要把孩子除掉，那两个女仆就一定会被赶走。

===Original===
So one evening, the poor little girl having been put to bed early, before the sun was down, the servants went off to the village, locking the door behind them. The child did not know she was alone, and lay contentedly looking out of her window towards the forest, of which, however, she could not see much, because of the ivy and other creeping plants which had straggled across her window. All at once she saw an ape making faces at her out of the mirror, and the heads carved upon a great old wardrobe grinning fearfully. Then two old spider-legged chairs came forward into the middle of the room, and began to dance a queer, old-fashioned dance. This set her laughing, and she forgot the ape and the grinning heads. So the fairies saw they had made a mistake, and sent the chairs back to their places. But they knew that she had been reading the story of Silverhair all day. So the next moment she heard the voices of the three bears upon the stair, big voice, middle voice, and little voice, and she heard their soft, heavy tread, as if they had had stockings over their boots, coming nearer and nearer to the door of her room, till she could bear it no longer. She did just as Silverhair did, and as the fairies wanted her to do: she darted to the window, pulled it open, got upon the ivy, and so scrambled to the ground. She then fled to the forest as fast as she could run.

===Chinese===
于是有一天傍晚，那可怜的小女孩早早地被打发上了床，太阳还没落山呢，两个仆人就到村子里去了，临走把门反锁上。孩子并不知道屋里只剩自己一个，便安安稳稳地躺在床上，朝窗外的林子望着；不过她也看不见多少，因为常春藤和别的爬藤早已乱蓬蓬地爬满了她的窗子。忽然之间，她看见镜子里有一只猿猴朝她做鬼脸，一口老旧大衣橱上雕着的几个人头也在狰狞地龇牙咧嘴。接着，两把蜘蛛腿似的老椅子挪到屋子中央，跳起了一种古里古怪的老派舞蹈。这把她逗得大笑起来，便把那猿猴和龇牙的人头都忘了。仙灵们一看，知道自己弄错了，又把椅子送回原处。可他们知道，她一整天都在读银发（Silverhair）的故事。于是下一刻，她就听见楼梯上有三只熊的声音——一个粗嗓门、一个中嗓门、一个细嗓门——她还听见它们那又轻又沉的脚步声，仿佛在靴子外头又套了袜子，一步一步，离她房间的门越来越近，近得她再也受不了啦。她便照着银发姑娘的样子做，也正是仙灵们想要她做的：她一个箭步扑到窗前，推开窗子，攀住常春藤，就这样爬到了地上。然后她拼命地朝林子飞奔而去。

===Original===
Now, although she did not know it, this was the very best way she could have gone; for nothing is ever so mischievous in its own place as it is out of it; and, besides, these mischievous creatures were only the children of Fairyland, as it were, and there are many other beings there as well; and if a wanderer gets in among them, the good ones will always help him more than the evil ones will be able to hurt him.
The sun was now set, and the darkness coming on, but the child thought of no danger but the bears behind her. If she had looked round, however, she would have seen that she was followed by a very different creature from a bear. It was a curious creature, made like a fish, but covered, instead of scales, with feathers of all colours, sparkling like those of a hummingbird. It had fins, not wings, and swam through the air as a fish does through the water. Its head was like the head of a small owl.
After running a long way, and as the last of the light was disappearing, she passed under a tree with drooping branches. It dropped its branches to the ground all about her, and caught her as in a trap. She struggled to get out, but the branches pressed her closer and closer to the trunk. She was in great terror and distress, when the air-fish, swimming into the thicket of branches, began tearing them with its beak. They loosened their hold at once, and the creature went on attacking them, till at length they let the child go. Then the air-fish came from behind her, and swam on in front, glittering and sparkling all lovely colours; and she followed.

===Chinese===
虽说孩子并不知道，可她走的这条路，恰恰是再好不过的一条路；因为任何作祟的生灵，离开了自己的地盘，便远不如在自己地盘上那般厉害；况且这些淘气的家伙，说到底不过是仙境里的孩子罢了，那里头还有许许多多别的生灵；一个迷路的旅人只要闯进他们中间，善的生灵肯帮他一把，总归胜过恶的生灵能害他几分。
这会儿太阳已经落山，夜色渐渐压下来，可孩子只想得到身后那几只熊的危险。然而她要是回头一看，就会看见跟着她的，是一个和熊全然两样的生灵。那是一个古怪的生灵，长得像一条鱼，可身上覆着的不是鳞片，而是五颜六色的羽毛，闪闪发亮，像蜂鸟的羽毛一般。它生的是鳍，不是翅膀，在空气里游着，就像鱼在水里游一样。它的头像一只小猫头鹰的头。
她跑了老远一段路，趁着最后一丝光亮也将褪尽，她从一棵垂着枝条的树下跑过。那树登时把枝条四面八方地垂到地上，把她像落进陷阱一般兜头罩住。她拼命挣扎着想脱身，可那些枝条却把她越箍越紧，直箍到树干上。她正怕得要命、苦得要命，那空气鱼（air-fish）却游进了枝条的密丛，开始用喙去啄扯它们。那些枝条一下子便松开了她，那生灵仍不住嘴地啄，直啄到最后它们把孩子放了才算。于是那空气鱼绕到她前头，在前面游着，浑身闪烁着各种可爱的颜色；孩子便跟在后头。

===Original===
It led her gently along till all at once it swam in at a cottage-door. The child followed still. There was a bright fire in the middle of the floor, upon which stood a pot without a lid, full of water that boiled and bubbled furiously. The air-fish swam straight to the pot and into the boiling water, where it lay quiet. A beautiful woman rose from the opposite side of the fire and came to meet the girl. She took her up in her arms, and said﻿—
“Ah, you are come at last! I have been looking for you a long time.”

===Chinese===
它温温和和地引着她一路前行，忽然之间，它游进了一间小屋的门里。孩子依旧跟着。屋正中的地上烧着一团旺火，火上架着一口没盖盖子的锅，锅里满是沸滚翻腾的水。那空气鱼径直游到锅边，一头扎进滚水里，静静地躺着不动了。一位美丽的女子从火的对面站起身来，迎上前来。她把孩子抱在怀里，说道——
"啊，你到底来了！我找你找了好久好久了。"

===Original===
She sat down with her on her lap, and there the girl sat staring at her. She had never seen anything so beautiful. She was tall and strong, with white arms and neck, and a delicate flush on her face. The child could not tell what was the colour of her hair, but could not help thinking it had a tinge of dark green. She had not one ornament upon her, but she looked as if she had just put off quantities of diamonds and emeralds. Yet here she was in the simplest, poorest little cottage, where she was evidently at home. She was dressed in shining green.
The girl looked at the lady, and the lady looked at the girl.
“What is your name?” asked the lady.
“The servants always call me Tangle.”
“Ah, that was because your hair was so untidy. But that was their fault, the naughty women! Still it is a pretty name, and I will call you Tangle too. You must not mind my asking you questions, for you may ask me the same questions, every one of them, and any others that you like. How old are you?”
“Ten,” answered Tangle.
“You don’t look like it,” said the lady.
“How old are you, please?” returned Tangle.
“Thousands of years old,” answered the lady.
“You don’t look like it,” said Tangle.
“Don’t I? I think I do. Don’t you see how beautiful I am?”
And her great blue eyes looked down on the little Tangle, as if all the stars in the sky were melted in them to make their brightness.
“Ah! but,” said Tangle, “when people live long they grow old. At least I always thought so.”
“I have no time to grow old,” said the lady. “I am too busy for that. It is very idle to grow old.﻿—But I cannot have my little girl so untidy. Do you know I can’t find a clean spot on your face to kiss?”
“Perhaps,” suggested Tangle, feeling ashamed, but not too much so to say a word for herself﻿—“perhaps that is because the tree made me cry so.”
“My poor darling!” said the lady, looking now as if the moon were melted in her eyes, and kissing her little face, dirty as it was, “the naughty tree must suffer for making a girl cry.”
“And what is your name, please?” asked Tangle.
“Grandmother,” answered the lady.
“Is it really?”
“Yes, indeed. I never tell stories, even in fun.”
“How good of you!”
“I couldn’t if I tried. It would come true if I said it, and then I should be punished enough.” And she smiled like the sun through a summer-shower.

===Chinese===
她坐了下来，把孩子放在自己膝上，那女孩就坐在那里，直直地望着她。她从没见过这样美丽的人。她又高又健壮，手臂和颈项洁白，脸上泛着一抹淡淡的嫣红。孩子说不出她的头发到底是什么颜色，只禁不住想，那里面似乎透着一丝暗绿。她身上一件饰物也没有，可那模样，倒像是刚刚才摘下了无数的钻石和祖母绿。然而她此刻所在的，却是一间再简陋不过、再寒酸不过的小屋，而且显然这就是她的家。她穿一身闪着光的绿色衣裳。
女孩望着这位夫人，夫人也望着女孩。
"你叫什么名字？"夫人问。
"仆人们都叫我缠儿（Tangle）。"
"啊，那是因为你的头发太乱了。可那是她们的过错，这两个坏女人！不过这名字倒也好听，那我也叫你缠儿吧。你可别介意我问你话，因为我问你的每一句，你都可以照样回问我，还可以问任何别的你想问的。你几岁了？"
"十岁。"缠儿答道。
"你看着可不像。"夫人说。
"请问您几岁了？"缠儿反问。
"几千岁了。"夫人答道。
"您看着可不像。"缠儿说。
"不像吗？我倒觉得我像的。你没瞧见我有多美吗？"
她那一双蓝色的大眼睛垂望着小小的缠儿，仿佛天上所有的星星都化在了里面，才成就了那份光辉。
"啊！可是，"缠儿说，"人活得久了，总会变老的呀。至少我一直这样以为。"
"我没有工夫变老，"夫人说，"我太忙了，忙得顾不上那个。变老，那是太闲了才会的事。——可我不能让我家的小姑娘这样不整洁。你可知我在你脸上竟找不出一块干净的地方好亲一亲吗？"
"也许，"缠儿红着脸说，可也没太害羞，还为自个儿开了一句口——"也许那是那棵树害我哭成这样的。"
"我可怜的宝贝！"夫人说。此刻她的眼神，仿佛是月亮化在了里面，她便去亲那孩子脏兮兮的小脸，"那棵坏树害得一个姑娘哭了，可要受罚呢。"
"请问您叫什么名字？"缠儿问。
"奶奶（Grandmother）。"夫人答。
"当真吗？"
"当真。我从不说谎话，连开玩笑也不说。"
"您可真好！"
"我就是想说也说不来呀。我要是说了假话，它会成真的，那我就要受够罚了。"说着，她笑了笑，那笑容像一阵夏日阵雨里透出来的阳光。

===Original===
“But now,” she went on, “I must get you washed and dressed, and then we shall have some supper.”
“Oh! I had supper long ago,” said Tangle.
“Yes, indeed you had,” answered the lady﻿—“three years ago. You don’t know that it is three years since you ran away from the bears. You are thirteen and more now.”
Tangle could only stare. She felt quite sure it was true.
“You will not be afraid of anything I do with you﻿—will you?” said the lady.
“I will try very hard not to be; but I can’t be certain, you know,” replied Tangle.
“I like your saying so, and I shall be quite satisfied,” answered the lady.
She took off the girl’s nightgown, rose with her in her arms, and going to the wall of the cottage, opened a door. Then Tangle saw a deep tank, the sides of which were filled with green plants, which had flowers of all colours. There was a roof over it like the roof of the cottage. It was filled with beautiful clear water, in which swam a multitude of such fishes as the one that had led her to the cottage. It was the light their colours gave that showed the place in which they were.

===Chinese===
"不过这会儿，"她接着说，"我得替你洗一洗、穿好衣裳，然后我们吃晚饭。"
"哎呀！我早就吃过晚饭了。"缠儿说。
"是呀，你早吃过了，"夫人答道——"那是三年以前的事了。你不知道吧，自从你从那几只熊那儿逃出来，到今天已经三年了。你如今已经十三岁出头了。"
缠儿只能干瞪眼。她心里却十分确信，这是真的。
"我待会儿对你做的种种事，你都不会害怕吧——会吗？"夫人说。
"我一定努力不害怕；可我没法打包票，您知道。"缠儿答。
"你肯这样说，我便很欢喜，也便很放心了。"夫人答。
她替女孩脱下睡袍，把她抱在怀里，走到小屋的墙边，打开了一扇门。于是缠儿看见了一口深池，池的四壁满是绿色的植物，开着各种颜色的花。池子上头有一顶棚，和小屋的屋顶一样。池子里盛满了清澈美丽的水，水里游着无数条鱼，都和引她来到这小屋的那一条同种。正是这些鱼身上的颜色所发出的光，照亮了它们所在的所在。

===Original===
The lady spoke some words Tangle could not understand, and threw her into the tank.
The fishes came crowding about her. Two or three of them got under her head and kept it up. The rest of them rubbed themselves all over her, and with their wet feathers washed her quite clean. Then the lady, who had been looking on all the time, spoke again; whereupon some thirty or forty of the fishes rose out of the water underneath Tangle, and so bore her up to the arms the lady held out to take her. She carried her back to the fire, and, having dried her well, opened a chest, and taking out the finest linen garments, smelling of grass and lavender, put them upon her, and over all a green dress, just like her own, shining like hers, and soft like hers, and going into just such lovely folds from the waist, where it was tied with a brown cord, to her bare feet.
“Won’t you give me a pair of shoes too, Grandmother?” said Tangle.
“No, my dear; no shoes. Look here. I wear no shoes.”
So saying she lifted her dress a little, and there were the loveliest white feet, but no shoes. Then Tangle was content to go without shoes too. And the lady sat down with her again, and combed her hair, and brushed it, and then left it to dry while she got the supper.

===Chinese===
夫人念了几句缠儿听不懂的话，便把她抛进了池子里。
群鱼纷纷围拢到她身边来。有两三条钻到她头底下，把她的头托出水面。其余的鱼便在她全身上下蹭来蹭去，用它们湿漉漉的羽毛，把她洗得干干净净。夫人一直在旁边看着，这时又开口说了几句话；登时有三十来条、四十来条鱼从缠儿身底下的水里浮了起来，就这样把她托上来，送进夫人伸出的双臂里。夫人把她抱回火边，把她周身擦干，打开了一口箱子，取出几件最细的亚麻内衣，散发着青草和薰衣草的香气，替她穿上，又在外面罩上一件绿色的衣裙，和她自己身上那件一模一样，和她那件一样闪光，和她那件一样柔软，从腰间系着一条棕绳的地方起，一直垂到她赤着的双脚，落下和她那件一样好看的褶子。
"奶奶，您不也给我一双鞋穿吗？"缠儿说。
"不，亲爱的；不穿鞋。你瞧。我也没有穿鞋。"
说着她把衣裙微微提起一点，只见一双再可爱不过的白净小脚，却不见鞋。缠儿便也甘心不穿鞋了。于是夫人又和她一起坐下，替她梳头、刷头，然后让头发晾着，自个儿去预备晚饭。

===Original===
First she got bread out of one hole in the wall; then milk out of another; then several kinds of fruit out of a third; and then she went to the pot on the fire, and took out the fish, now nicely cooked, and, as soon as she had pulled off its feathered skin, ready to be eaten.
“But,” exclaimed Tangle. And she stared at the fish, and could say no more.
“I know what you mean,” returned the lady. “You do not like to eat the messenger that brought you home. But it is the kindest return you can make. The creature was afraid to go until it saw me put the pot on, and heard me promise it should be boiled the moment it returned with you. Then it darted out of the door at once. You saw it go into the pot of itself the moment it entered, did you not?”
“I did,” answered Tangle, “and I thought it very strange; but then I saw you, and forgot all about the fish.”
“In Fairyland,” resumed the lady, as they sat down to the table, “the ambition of the animals is to be eaten by the people; for that is their highest end in that condition. But they are not therefore destroyed. Out of that pot comes something more than the dead fish, you will see.”

===Chinese===
她先从墙上的一个洞里取出面包，又从另一个洞里取出牛奶，再从第三个洞里取出好几种果子，然后走到火上的锅边，把那条鱼取了出来——这时鱼已炖得恰到好处，只要把那身羽毛皮一剥，便可吃了。
"可是——"缠儿喊了一声。她瞪着那条鱼，再也说不出别的话来。
"我知道你要说什么，"夫人答道，"你不情愿吃掉这位把你领回家的信使。可这却是你能给它的最厚道的回报。这生灵本来不敢走，直到它看见我把锅架上，听见我答应它，只要它把你带回来，便立刻下锅煮它。它听了才一下子冲出门去。你瞧见它一进门就自己跳进了锅里，不是吗？"
"我瞧见了，"缠儿答，"我还当这事透着古怪；可我随后就看见您，便把那条鱼忘得干干净净了。"
"在仙境里，"夫人一边说，一边和她坐到桌前，"禽兽们的心愿，就是被人们吃掉；因为在那样的境况里，那是它们最高的归宿。可它们并不会就此被毁灭。从那口锅里出来的，会比一条死掉的鱼多出一些什么，你待会儿便见。"

===Original===
Tangle now remarked that the lid was on the pot. But the lady took no further notice of it till they had eaten the fish, which Tangle found nicer than any fish she had ever tasted before. It was as white as snow, and as delicate as cream. And the moment she had swallowed a mouthful of it, a change she could not describe began to take place in her. She heard a murmuring all about her, which became more and more articulate, and at length, as she went on eating, grew intelligible. By the time she had finished her share, the sounds of all the animals in the forest came crowding through the door to her ears; for the door still stood wide open, though it was pitch dark outside; and they were no longer sounds only; they were speech, and speech that she could understand. She could tell what the insects in the cottage were saying to each other too. She had even a suspicion that the trees and flowers all about the cottage were holding midnight communications with each other; but what they said she could not hear.
As soon as the fish was eaten, the lady went to the fire and took the lid off the pot. A lovely little creature in human shape, with large white wings, rose out of it, and flew round and round the roof of the cottage; then dropped, fluttering, and nestled in the lap of the lady. She spoke to it some strange words, carried it to the door, and threw it out into the darkness. Tangle heard the flapping of its wings die away in the distance.
“Now have we done the fish any harm?” she said, returning.
“No,” answered Tangle, “I do not think we have. I should not mind eating one every day.”
“They must wait their time, like you and me too, my little Tangle.”
And she smiled a smile which the sadness in it made more lovely.
“But,” she continued, “I think we may have one for supper tomorrow.”

===Chinese===
缠儿这才留意到，锅上已经盖了盖子。可是直到她们把那条鱼吃完，夫人才又去理会那口锅。缠儿吃着，觉得这鱼比她平生吃过的任何鱼都好吃。它白得像雪，嫩得像奶油。而她刚刚咽下一口，一种她说不出的变化便在她身上生了出来。她听见四下里一片嘤嘤嗡嗡的声音，越来越清晰可辨，到最后，她越吃下去，那声音竟成了能听懂的话。等她把自己那一份吃完，整座林子里所有禽兽的声音便都穿过洞开的门，涌到她耳里来了；因为那扇门始终敞着，虽说外头已黑得伸手不见五指；而那些声响也不再只是声响，它们是话，是她能听懂的话。她连屋里那些虫子彼此在说些什么，也都能听明白了。她甚至疑心，小屋四围的树木花草，也在那里彼此进行着半夜的密谈；只是它们说些什么，她还听不见。
那条鱼一吃完，夫人便走到火边，揭开了锅盖。一个小小的人形可爱生灵，生着一对白色的大翅膀，从锅里升起，绕着小屋的屋顶飞了一圈又一圈；然后扑棱着落下来，偎进夫人的怀里。她对它说了几句听不懂的怪话，把它抱到门口，抛进了黑夜里。缠儿听见它拍翅的声音在远处一点一点地消逝。
"这一下，我们可算害了那条鱼吗？"她回过身来问道。
"没有，"缠儿答道，"我想我们没有害它。哪怕每天吃一条，我也不在意。"
"它们也得等候自己的时辰，就像你我一样啊，我的小缠儿。"
说着她微微一笑，那笑容里的一缕哀愁，反倒把它衬得更美了。
"不过，"她接着说，"我想明天的晚饭，我们可以再要一条。"

===Original===
So saying she went to the door of the tank, and spoke; and now Tangle understood her perfectly.
“I want one of you,” she said﻿—“the wisest.”
Thereupon the fishes got together in the middle of the tank, with their heads forming a circle above the water, and their tails a larger circle beneath it. They were holding a council, in which their relative wisdom should be determined. At length one of them flew up into the lady’s hand, looking lively and ready.
“You know where the rainbow stands?” she asked.
“Yes, Mother, quite well,” answered the fish.
“Bring home a young man you will find there, who does not know where to go.”
The fish was out of the door in a moment. Then the lady told Tangle it was time to go to bed; and, opening another door in the side of the cottage, showed her a little arbour, cool and green, with a bed of purple heath growing in it, upon which she threw a large wrapper made of the feathered skins of the wise fishes, shining gorgeous in the firelight.
Tangle was soon lost in the strangest, loveliest dreams. And the beautiful lady was in every one of her dreams.

===Chinese===
说着，她走到池子门口，开口说话；这会儿缠儿已把她的话听得清清楚楚。
"我要你们里头的一条，"她说——"最聪明的那一条。"
于是群鱼聚到池子中央，它们的头在水面上围成一个圈，尾巴在水底下围成一个更大的圈。它们正在开一场议事会，要从中定出哪一条最聪明。终于有一条鱼一跃跳进了夫人的手掌，看着精神十足、整装待发。
"你晓得彩虹立在哪里吧？"她问。
"晓得，母亲，再清楚不过了。"那条鱼答。
"在那儿你会找到一个年轻人，他不知该往哪里去，你把他领回家来。"
那条鱼一眨眼便冲出了门。随后夫人便告诉缠儿，该上床睡了；她打开小屋墙上的另一扇门，让她看一间小小的凉棚，绿意盎然，里面长着一张紫色的石楠花床，夫人把一件大斗篷抛在那花床上——那是用那些聪明鱼的羽毛皮拼成的，在火光里闪着华丽的光。
缠儿很快便沉入了一场又一场最离奇、最可爱的梦。而那位美丽的夫人，在她每一个梦里都出现。

===Original===
In the morning she woke to the rustling of leaves over her head, and the sound of running water. But, to her surprise, she could find no door﻿—nothing but the moss-grown wall of the cottage. So she crept through an opening in the arbour, and stood in the forest. Then she bathed in a stream that ran merrily through the trees, and felt happier; for having once been in her grandmother’s pond, she must be clean and tidy ever after; and, having put on her green dress, felt like a lady.
She spent that day in the wood, listening to the birds and beasts and creeping things. She understood all that they said, though she could not repeat a word of it; and every kind had a different language, while there was a common though more limited understanding between all the inhabitants of the forest. She saw nothing of the beautiful lady, but she felt that she was near her all the time; and she took care not to go out of sight of the cottage. It was round, like a snow-hut or a wigwam; and she could see neither door nor window in it. The fact was, it had no windows; and though it was full of doors, they all opened from the inside, and could not even be seen from the outside.
She was standing at the foot of a tree in the twilight, listening to a quarrel between a mole and a squirrel, in which the mole told the squirrel that the tail was the best of him, and the squirrel called the mole Spade-fists, when, the darkness having deepened around her, she became aware of something shining in her face, and looking round, saw that the door of the cottage was open, and the red light of the fire flowing from it like a river through the darkness. She left Mole and Squirrel to settle matters as they might, and darted off to the cottage. Entering, she found the pot boiling on the fire, and the grand, lovely lady sitting on the other side of it.
“I’ve been watching you all day,” said the lady. “You shall have something to eat by and by, but we must wait till our supper comes home.”
She took Tangle on her knee, and began to sing to her﻿—such songs as made her wish she could listen to them forever. But at length in rushed the shining fish, and snuggled down in the pot. It was followed by a youth who had outgrown his worn garments. His face was ruddy with health, and in his hand he carried a little jewel, which sparkled in the firelight.

===Chinese===
清晨她醒来，听见头顶叶子的窸窣声，还有流水声。可叫她惊奇的是，她竟找不见一扇门——眼前只有长满青苔的小屋墙壁。于是她从凉棚的一个缺口钻出去，站到了林子里。然后她在一条欢快地穿过树林的小溪里洗了个澡，心里舒畅多了；因为凡是下过奶奶那口池子的人，从此以后必定永远干净齐整了；她穿上那件绿衣裙，便觉得自己也像一位夫人了。
那一天她在林子里度过，听着鸟儿、走兽和爬行的小生灵说话。它们说的话，她句句都听得明白，只是一句也学不来；每一种都有自己的语言，而林中所有的居民彼此之间，又有一种共通的、却较为有限的理解。那位美丽的夫人她一眼也没见着，却觉得夫人始终就在她身旁；她也留心着，不走出那间小屋的视线之外。那屋子是圆的，像一座雪屋，又像一座棚屋；她既看不见门，也看不见窗。事实是，它一扇窗也没有；至于门，倒满屋都是，只是那些门都只能从里头打开，从外面连看也看不见。
暮色里，她正站在一棵树脚下，听一只鼹鼠和一只松鼠拌嘴——鼹鼠对松鼠说，那条尾巴才是它身上最好的部分，松鼠则叫鼹鼠"铲拳头"——这时她四围的夜色越发浓了，她忽然觉察到有什么光照在她脸上，回头一望，只见小屋的门开着，火的红光像一条河似的，从门里流过夜色。她便撇下鼹鼠和松鼠，任它们自个儿去把事情了结，自个儿一溜烟奔到小屋去了。一进门，她便看见火上那口锅正沸腾着，那位雍容美丽的夫人坐在锅的另一边。
"我整天都在看着你呢。"夫人说，"待会儿你就有东西吃了，可我们得等我们的晚饭自己回家来。"
