她把缠儿抱在膝上，开始唱歌给她听——那一些歌，叫她恨不得一辈子都听下去。可后来，那条闪光的鱼到底冲了进来，舒舒服服地钻进了锅里。紧跟着它进来的，是一个已经把一身旧衣裳撑得嫌小的少年。他满面红光，气色极好，手里拿着一颗小小的宝石，在火光里闪闪发亮。

===Original===
The first words the lady said were﻿—
“What is that in your hand, Mossy?”
Now Mossy was the name his companions had given him, because he had a favourite stone covered with moss, on which he used to sit whole days reading; and they said the moss had begun to grow upon him too.
Mossy held out his hand. The moment the lady saw that it was the golden key, she rose from her chair, kissed Mossy on the forehead, made him sit down on her seat, and stood before him like a servant. Mossy could not bear this, and rose at once. But the lady begged him, with tears in her beautiful eyes, to sit, and let her wait on him.
“But you are a great, splendid, beautiful lady,” said Mossy.
“Yes, I am. But I work all day long﻿—that is my pleasure; and you will have to leave me so soon!”
“How do you know that, if you please, madam?” asked Mossy.
“Because you have got the golden key.”
“But I don’t know what it is for. I can’t find the keyhole. Will you tell me what to do?”
“You must look for the keyhole. That is your work. I cannot help you. I can only tell you that if you look for it you will find it.”
“What kind of box will it open? What is there inside?”
“I do not know. I dream about it, but I know nothing.”
“Must I go at once?”
“You may stop here tonight, and have some of my supper. But you must go in the morning. All I can do for you is to give you clothes. Here is a girl called Tangle, whom you must take with you.”

===Chinese===
夫人开口的第一句话是——
"你手里拿的是什么，小苔生（Mossy）？"
原来小苔生是同伴们给他起的绰号，因为他有一块心爱的、长满青苔的石头，常常整天整天坐在上头读书；他们便说，连他身上也开始长出青苔来了。
小苔生把手伸出来。夫人一看见那是金钥匙，便从椅子上站起来，在小苔生的额头上亲了一下，让他坐到自己的座位上，自己却像个仆人似的站在他跟前。小苔生哪里受得住这个，登时就站了起来。可夫人那双美丽的眼睛里含着泪，恳求他坐着，让她伺候他。
"可您是一位了不起的、华贵的、美丽的夫人啊。"小苔生说。
"是，我是。可我整天整天地做工——那便是我的快乐；而你，很快就要离开我了！"
"请问您怎么知道这个呢，夫人？"小苔生问。
"因为你手里有了那把金钥匙。"
"可我不知道它是做什么用的。我寻不见那个锁孔。您能告诉我该怎么办吗？"
"你得去寻那锁孔。那是你的活儿。我帮不了你。我只能告诉你：你只要去找，就一定能找到。"
"它开的是什么样的匣子呢？那里头有什么？"
"我不知道。我梦见它，可我什么也不知道。"
"我必须马上就走吗？"
"今晚你可以留下来，吃一点我的晚饭。可你明天早晨就得走。我能为你做的，只有给你几件衣裳。这里有一位姑娘，叫缠儿，你得带她一道去。"

===Original===
“That *will* be nice,” said Mossy.
“No, no!” said Tangle. “I don’t want to leave you, please, Grandmother.”
“You must go with him, Tangle. I am sorry to lose you, but it will be the best thing for you. Even the fishes, you see, have to go into the pot, and then out into the dark. If you fall in with the Old Man of the Sea, mind you ask him whether he has not got some more fishes ready for me. My tank is getting thin.”
So saying, she took the fish from the pot, and put the lid on as before. They sat down and ate the fish, and then the winged creature rose from the pot, circled the roof, and settled on the lady’s lap. She talked to it, carried it to the door, and threw it out into the dark. They heard the flap of its wings die away in the distance.
The lady then showed Mossy into just such another chamber as that of Tangle; and in the morning he found a suit of clothes laid beside him. He looked very handsome in them. But the wearer of Grandmother’s clothes never thinks about how he or she looks, but thinks always how handsome other people are.
Tangle was very unwilling to go.
“Why should I leave you? I don’t know the young man,” she said to the lady.
“I am never allowed to keep my children long. You need not go with him except you please, but you must go some day; and I should like you to go with him, for he has the golden key. No girl need be afraid to go with a youth that has the golden key. You will take care of her, Mossy, will you not?”
“That I will,” said Mossy.
And Tangle cast a glance at him, and thought she should like to go with him.
“And,” said the lady, “if you should lose each other as you go through the﻿—the﻿—I never can remember the name of that country﻿—do not be afraid, but go on and on.”
She kissed Tangle on the mouth and Mossy on the forehead, led them to the door, and waved her hand eastward. Mossy and Tangle took each other’s hand and walked away into the depth of the forest. In his right hand Mossy held the golden key.

===Chinese===
"那*再好不过了*。"小苔生说。
"不，不！"缠儿说，"奶奶，我不想离开您。"
"你得跟他一道去，缠儿。我舍不得你，可这对你才是最好的。你看，连那些鱼，也得下到锅里，再出去到黑暗里。你要是路上遇见大海老人（the Old Man of the Sea），千万记着问问他，是不是又给我备下了一些鱼。我那池子快见空了。"
说着，她从锅里把鱼取出来，照旧把盖子盖上。他们坐下把鱼吃了，然后那个带翅膀的小生灵从锅里升起，绕着屋顶飞了一圈，落在夫人的膝上。她对它说了几句话，把它抱到门口，抛进了黑暗里。他们听见它拍翅的声音在远处一点一点地消逝。
随后夫人便把小苔生领进一间屋子，正和缠儿住过的那间一模一样；第二天早晨，他发觉身旁已放好了一整套衣裳。穿在身上，他显得十分英俊。但凡穿着奶奶所赐衣裳的人，从不会去想自己看着怎样，只会想着旁人是何等好看。
缠儿是一万个不愿意走。
"我为什么要离开您呢？那个少年我并不认识。"她对夫人说。
"我从来不被允许把孩子长久留在身边。你若不愿意，倒不一定要跟他同去，可你总有一天是要走的；我倒盼着你跟他一道走，因为他手里有金钥匙。凡手里有金钥匙的少年，哪个姑娘也不必怕与他同行。你会照顾她的，是吧，小苔生？"
"我一定。"小苔生说。
缠儿瞟了他一眼，心想自己倒愿意和他一道去。
"还有，"夫人说，"你们走过那——那——我总也记不住那个地方的名字——你们要是在那里头彼此走散了，可别害怕，只管一直往前走。"
她亲了缠儿的嘴，亲了小苔生的额头，把他们领到门口，朝东方挥了挥手。小苔生和缠儿彼此牵着手，走进了森林的深处。小苔生的右手里，紧紧握着那把金钥匙。

===Original===
They wandered thus a long way, with endless amusement from the talk of the animals. They soon learned enough of their language to ask them necessary questions. The squirrels were always friendly, and gave them nuts out of their own hoards; but the bees were selfish and rude, justifying themselves on the ground that Tangle and Mossy were not subjects of their queen, and charity must begin at home, though indeed they had not one drone in their poorhouse at the time. Even the blinking moles would fetch them an earthnut or a truffle now and then, talking as if their mouths, as well as their eyes and ears, were full of cotton wool, or their own velvety fur. By the time they got out of the forest they were very fond of each other, and Tangle was not in the least sorry that her grandmother had sent her away with Mossy.

===Chinese===
他们就这样走了好长一段路，一路上听着禽兽们的闲话，其乐无穷。不多久，他们便把它们的语言学了个够，能向它们问些非问不可的话。松鼠一向和气，常从自家屯的坚果里掏出些给他们；蜜蜂却既自私又无礼，还振振有词地替自己开脱，说缠儿和小苔生不是它们女王的子民，仁慈总要从自家做起——虽说那时候它们的济贫院里，其实连一只雄蜂也没有。就连那些老眨巴眼的鼹鼠，时不时也会给他们叼来一颗地果或是一块松露，说起话来却像是嘴里、眼里、耳里都塞满了棉花，要不就是塞满了它们自己那天鹅绒般的细毛。等他们走出森林的时候，彼此已十分要好，缠儿一点也不懊恼奶奶把她打发出来跟小苔生同行了。

===Original===
At length the trees grew smaller, and stood farther apart, and the ground began to rise, and it got more and more steep, till the trees were all left behind, and the two were climbing a narrow path with rocks on each side. Suddenly they came upon a rude doorway, by which they entered a narrow gallery cut in the rock. It grew darker and darker, till it was pitch-dark, and they had to feel their way. At length the light began to return, and at last they came out upon a narrow path on the face of a lofty precipice. This path went winding down the rock to a wide plain, circular in shape, and surrounded on all sides by mountains. Those opposite to them were a great way off, and towered to an awful height, shooting up sharp, blue, ice-enamelled pinnacles. An utter silence reigned where they stood. Not even the sound of water reached them.
Looking down, they could not tell whether the valley below was a grassy plain or a great still lake. They had never seen any space look like it. The way to it was difficult and dangerous, but down the narrow path they went, and reached the bottom in safety. They found it composed of smooth, light-coloured sandstone, undulating in parts, but mostly level. It was no wonder to them now that they had not been able to tell what it was, for this surface was everywhere crowded with shadows. The mass was chiefly made up of the shadows of leaves innumerable, of all lovely and imaginative forms, waving to and fro, floating and quivering in the breath of a breeze whose motion was unfelt, whose sound was unheard. No forests clothed the mountainsides, no trees were anywhere to be seen, and yet the shadows of the leaves, branches, and stems of all various trees covered the valley as far as their eyes could reach. They soon spied the shadows of flowers mingled with those of the leaves, and now and then the shadow of a bird with open beak, and throat distended with song. At times would appear the forms of strange, graceful creatures, running up and down the shadow-boles and along the branches, to disappear in the wind-tossed foliage. As they walked they waded knee-deep in the lovely lake. For the shadows were not merely lying on the surface of the ground, but heaped up above it like substantial forms of darkness, as if they had been cast upon a thousand different planes of the air. Tangle and Mossy often lifted their heads and gazed upwards to discry whence the shadows came; but they could see nothing more than a bright mist spread above them, higher than the tops of the mountains, which stood clear against it. No forests, no leaves, no birds were visible.

===Chinese===
渐渐地，树木越来越矮，彼此离得越来越远，地势开始抬升，越升越陡，直到树木全被抛在身后，两个少年正攀着一条夹在两侧巉岩间的窄路往上爬。忽然他们撞见一道粗陋的门口，便由此走进一条凿在岩石里的狭长廊道。廊道里越走越暗，到后来伸手不见五指，他们只得用手摸索着前行。终于光线又回来了，最后他们走到了一道高峻峭壁半腰上的一条窄路上。这条路顺着岩壁蜿蜒而下，通向一片广阔的平原，那平原呈圆形，四面环山。他们对面的那些山远得很，高耸入云，一座座蓝色尖峭、覆着冰釉的峰尖直插天际。他们所站的地方笼罩着一片彻彻底底的寂静。连水的声响也传不到他们耳里。
朝下一望，他们竟说不出底下那片谷地究竟是一片青草地，还是一片静悄悄的大湖。他们从未见过哪一处地方看上去像它那样。通往谷底的路又险又难，可他们仍顺着窄路走下去，平安到了底。他们发觉这底下是由一种光滑的浅色砂岩构成的，有些地方起伏不定，大体上却平展展的。这会儿他们也用不着奇怪自己方才分辨不清那是什么了，因为这片地面上到处都挤满了影子。那一大片主要是由无数树叶的影子构成的，形状可爱、富于想象，在一种感觉不到其动、听不见其声的微风的吐息里来回摇曳、漂浮、颤动。山坡上没有半片森林，哪里也看不见一棵树，可各种树木的叶子、枝桠和树干的影子，却铺满了这谷地，一直铺到目光所及之处。他们很快又窥见花朵的影子掺杂在叶影里，时不时还出现一只鸟的影子，张着喙，鼓着喉头在唱歌。有时还会冒出一些古怪而优美的生灵的形体，在那些影子树干上上下奔跑，沿着枝桠窜动，又一头钻进被风戏弄的叶丛里不见了。他们一边走，一边在那片可爱的湖里涉水而行，湖水没到膝盖。因为那些影子并不只是平铺在地面上，而是在地面之上堆叠起来，像是有分量的黑暗形体，仿佛它们是投在一千层高低不同的空气层上的。缠儿和小苔生常常抬起头，仰望着想看清那些影子究竟从何而来；可他们所能看见的，只是一片明亮的雾霭铺在头顶，比那些山顶还高，而群山的轮廓清晰地映在它上面。没有森林，没有树叶，也没有飞鸟。

===Original===
After a while, they reached more open spaces, where the shadows were thinner; and came even to portions over which shadows only flitted, leaving them clear for such as might follow. Now a wonderful form, half birdlike half human, would float across on outspread sailing pinions. Anon an exquisite shadow group of gambolling children would be followed by the loveliest female form, and that again by the grand stride of a Titanic shape, each disappearing in the surrounding press of shadowy foliage. Sometimes a profile of unspeakable beauty or grandeur would appear for a moment and vanish. Sometimes they seemed lovers that passed linked arm in arm, sometimes father and son, sometimes brothers in loving contest, sometimes sisters entwined in gracefullest community of complex form. Sometimes wild horses would tear across, free, or bestrode by noble shadows of ruling men. But some of the things which pleased them most they never knew how to describe.
About the middle of the plain they sat down to rest in the heart of a heap of shadows. After sitting for a while, each, looking up, saw the other in tears: they were each longing after the country whence the shadows fell.
“We *must* find the country from which the shadows come,” said Mossy.
“We must, dear Mossy,” responded Tangle. “What if your golden key should be the key to *it*?”
“Ah! that would be grand,” returned Mossy.﻿—“But we must rest here for a little, and then we shall be able to cross the plain before night.”
So he lay down on the ground, and about him on every side, and over his head, was the constant play of the wonderful shadows. He could look through them, and see the one behind the other, till they mixed in a mass of darkness. Tangle, too, lay admiring, and wondering, and longing after the country whence the shadows came. When they were rested they rose and pursued their journey.
How long they were in crossing this plain I cannot tell; but before night Mossy’s hair was streaked with gray, and Tangle had got wrinkles on her forehead.
As evening grew on, the shadows fell deeper and rose higher. At length they reached a place where they rose above their heads, and made all dark around them. Then they took hold of each other’s hand, and walked on in silence and in some dismay. They felt the gathering darkness, and something strangely solemn besides, and the beauty of the shadows ceased to delight them. All at once Tangle found that she had not a hold of Mossy’s hand, though when she lost it she could not tell.
“Mossy, Mossy!” she cried aloud in terror.
But no Mossy replied.
A moment after, the shadows sank to her feet, and down under her feet, and the mountains rose before her. She turned towards the gloomy region she had left, and called once more upon Mossy. There the gloom lay tossing and heaving, a dark, stormy, foamless sea of shadows, but no Mossy rose out of it, or came climbing up the hill on which she stood. She threw herself down and wept in despair.

===Chinese===
过了一阵，他们走到一些较为开阔的地方，那里的影子稀薄了些；甚至还有那么几片地段，影子只是从上一掠而过，把地面让得清清爽爽，留给随后要来的影子。这会儿，一个半似飞鸟、半似人的奇妙形体，会张开双翼，悠悠地滑翔而过。不多时，一组嬉戏着的孩子那极美的影子群，后面又跟着一个最可爱的女子形体，再后面又跟着一个泰坦般高大身影的阔步，每一个都在四围挤挤挨挨的叶影里消失了。有时，一张美得无法形容、或庄严得无法言说的侧影会显现片刻，便不见了。有时他们像是手挽着手走过的恋人，有时像是父与子，有时像是相亲相争的兄弟，有时又像是姿态最为优雅地交缠在一起的姊妹。有时，野马会呼啸着奔过，自由自在，或是被一些高贵的、为王者之影骑着。可有些最让他们心动的事物，他们却怎么也说不清楚。
走到平原中央，他们在一堆影子的当中坐下来歇息。坐了一会儿，两人各自抬头一看，都瞧见对方眼里含着泪：他们心里都渴慕着那些影子所投下的那片国土。
"我们*一定*要找到影子所来的那片国土。"小苔生说。
"我们一定要找到它，亲爱的小苔生。"缠儿应道，"要是你那把金钥匙，正是开*它*的钥匙呢？"
"啊！那可太妙了。"小苔生说。——"不过我们得在这儿歇一会儿，这样天黑前就能穿过这片平原了。"
说着他便在地上躺下，他四围、他头顶上，到处都是那些奇妙的影子不停地戏耍。他能透过它们看进去，看见一个影子背后又叠着一个，直到它们混成了一大团黑暗。缠儿也躺着，又是赞叹，又是惊奇，又是渴慕着那些影子所来的那片国土。歇够之后，他们便起来，继续赶路。
他们究竟走了多久才穿过这片平原，我说不上来；只是天黑之前，小苔生的头发已夹了灰白，缠儿的额上也起了皱纹。
暮色渐深，影子也落得越深，升得越高。终于他们走到一处，影子升到了他们头顶之上，把他们四围都罩得一片漆黑。于是他们握紧彼此的手，默默地、颇为惊惶地往前走。他们感到那愈聚愈浓的黑暗，还感到一种说不出的奇异庄严，影子的美也不再让他们欢喜了。猛然间，缠儿发觉自己并没有握着小苔生的手，至于什么时候松开的，她也说不清。
"小苔生！小苔生！"她吓得放声大喊。
可没有小苔生应她。
过了一刻，那些影子沉到了她脚下，又沉到她脚底下去了，群山便在她面前耸立起来。她转身朝着自己方才离开的那片幽冥之地，再次呼唤小苔生。那幽冥之地正如一片黑暗、翻涌、却没有泡沫的影子之海，在起伏翻腾，可没有一个小苔生从里头升起来，也没有一个爬上她所站立的山坡。她扑倒在地，绝望地哭了起来。

===Original===
Suddenly she remembered that the beautiful lady had told them, if they lost each other in a country of which she could not remember the name, they were not to be afraid, but to go straight on.
“And besides,” she said to herself, “Mossy has the golden key, and so no harm will come to him, I do believe.”
She rose from the ground, and went on.
Before long she arrived at a precipice, in the face of which a stair was cut. When she had ascended halfway, the stair ceased, and the path led straight into the mountain. She was afraid to enter, and turning again towards the stair, grew giddy at sight of the depth beneath her, and was forced to throw herself down in the mouth of the cave.
When she opened her eyes, she saw a beautiful little figure with wings standing beside her, waiting.
“I know you,” said Tangle. “You are my fish.”
“Yes. But I am a fish no longer. I am an aeranth now.”
“What is that?” asked Tangle.
“What you see I am,” answered the shape. “And I am come to lead you through the mountain.”
“Oh! thank you, dear fish﻿—aeranth, I mean,” returned Tangle, rising.
Thereupon the aeranth took to his wings, and flew on through the long, narrow passage, reminding Tangle very much of the way he had swum on before her when he was a fish. And the moment his white wings moved, they began to throw off a continuous shower of sparks of all colours, which lighted up the passage before them.﻿—All at once he vanished, and Tangle heard a low, sweet sound, quite different from the rush and crackle of his wings. Before her was an open arch, and through it came light, mixed with the sound of sea-waves.

===Chinese===
忽然她记起，那位美丽的夫人曾告诉他们：要是他们在一个她总也记不住名字的地方彼此走散了，可不要害怕，只管径直往前走。
"况且，"她自个儿说，"小苔生手里有那把金钥匙，所以我相信他不会有事的。"
她从地上站起来，继续往前走。
没走多久，她来到一堵峭壁跟前，峭壁面上凿着一段石阶。她才爬到一半，石阶便到了头，那条路笔直地通进山里。她不敢进去，转身想退回石阶那边，可一望见脚下那深不见底的所在，便一阵眩晕，只好一头扑倒在洞口。
待她睁开眼睛，只见一个长着翅膀的、美丽的小小身影，正站在她身旁等候。
"我认得你。"缠儿说，"你是我的那条鱼。"
"是。可我不再是一条鱼了。我如今是一个空中花（aeranth）了。"
"那是什么？"缠儿问。
"你看着我是的样子。"那身影答，"我来，是为引你穿过这座山。"
"哦！谢谢你，亲爱的鱼——我是说，空中花。"缠儿起身说。
于是那空中花便展翅飞起，沿着那条又长又窄的通道一路飞去，那姿态很叫缠儿想起它从前还是一条鱼时、在她前面游着引路的模样。而它那对白翅膀一动，便开始不停地洒下一阵又一阵五颜六色的火星，把他们前头的通道照得通明。——忽然之间，它不见了，缠儿听见一个低低的、甜甜的声音，和它翅膀的那一阵扑啦扑啦全然不同。她面前是一道敞开的拱门，光从那拱门里透过来，里头还掺着海浪的声音。

===Original===
She hurried out, and fell, tired and happy, upon the yellow sand of the shore. There she lay, half asleep with weariness and rest, listening to the low plash and retreat of the tiny waves, which seemed ever enticing the land to leave off being land, and become sea. And as she lay, her eyes were fixed upon the foot of a great rainbow standing far away against the sky on the other side of the sea. At length she fell fast asleep.
When she awoke, she saw an old man with long white hair down to his shoulders, leaning upon a stick covered with green buds, and so bending over her.
“What do you want here, beautiful woman?” he said.
“Am I beautiful? I am so glad!” said Tangle, rising. “My grandmother is beautiful.”
“Yes. But what do you want?” he repeated, kindly.
“I think I want you. Are not you the Old Man of the Sea?”
“I am.”
“Then Grandmother says, have you any more fishes ready for her?”
“We will go and see, my dear,” answered the old man, speaking yet more kindly than before. “And I can do something for you, can I not?”
“Yes﻿—show me the way up to the country from which the shadows fall,” said Tangle.
For there she hoped to find Mossy again.
“Ah! indeed, that would be worth doing,” said the old man. “But I cannot, for I do not know the way myself. But I will send you to the Old Man of the Earth. Perhaps he can tell you. He is much older than I am.”

===Chinese===
她连忙奔出去，又疲又喜地扑倒在海岸的黄沙上。她躺在那里，倦意和安适使她半睡半醒，听着细碎的小浪一阵阵轻轻拍上岸又退下去，仿佛在一味地诱哄着这片陆地不要再做陆地，变成大海才好。她躺着，两眼直望着远处海那头、映在天边的一道大彩虹的根部。终于她沉沉睡去。
她醒来时，看见一位白发老人，头发长得一直垂到肩头，拄着一根长满绿色嫩芽的手杖，正这样俯身望着她。
"美丽的女子，你到这里来想寻什么呢？"他说。
"我美吗？我可太高兴了！"缠儿站起身来，"我奶奶才是美的。"
"不错。可你想寻什么呢？"他和气地重复了一遍。
"我想我要寻的是您。您不就是大海老人吗？"
"我就是。"
"那么奶奶问您，您可还有更多的鱼给她备下了吗？"
"我们去看看吧，亲爱的。"老人答道，说话比先前更加和蔼，"我也能替你做点什么，是吧？"
"是——请您指给我上去那片影子所投下的国土的路。"缠儿说。
因为她希望在那里能再找到小苔生。
"啊！那可真是值得做的事。"老人说，"可我做不来，因为我自个儿也不知道那条路。不过我可以把你送到大地老人（the Old Man of the Earth）那里去。也许他能告诉你。他比我年长得多。"

===Original===
Leaning on his staff, he conducted her along the shore to a steep rock, that looked like a petrified ship turned upside down. The door of it was the rudder of a great vessel, ages ago at the bottom of the sea. Immediately within the door was a stair in the rock, down which the old man went, and Tangle followed. At the bottom the old man had his house, and there he lived.
As soon as she entered it, Tangle heard a strange noise, unlike anything she had ever heard before. She soon found that it was the fishes talking. She tried to understand what they said; but their speech was so old-fashioned, and rude, and undefined, that she could not make much of it.
“I will go and see about those fishes for my daughter,” said the Old Man of the Sea.
And moving a slide in the wall of his house, he first looked out, and then tapped upon a thick piece of crystal that filled the round opening. Tangle came up behind him, and peeping through the window into the heart of the great deep green ocean, saw the most curious creatures, some very ugly, all very odd, and with especially queer mouths, swimming about everywhere, above and below, but all coming towards the window in answer to the tap of the Old Man of the Sea. Only a few could get their mouths against the glass; but those who were floating miles away yet turned their heads towards it. The old man looked through the whole flock carefully for some minutes, and then turning to Tangle, said﻿—
“I am sorry I have not got one ready yet. I want more time than she does. But I will send some as soon as I can.”
He then shut the slide.
Presently a great noise arose in the sea. The old man opened the slide again, and tapped on the glass, whereupon the fishes were all as still as sleep.
“They were only talking about you,” he said. “And they do speak such nonsense!﻿—Tomorrow,” he continued, “I must show you the way to the Old Man of the Earth. He lives a long way from here.”

===Chinese===
他拄着手杖，领着她沿着海岸走到一块陡峭的岩石前，那岩石看上去像一只石化了的、底朝天的船。它的门，是一艘大船的船舵，千百年以前沉在海底的那艘船。一进门，便是一道凿在岩石里的石阶，老人顺着走下去，缠儿跟在后头。到了底下，便是老人的家，他就住在那里。
缠儿一进门，便听见一阵古怪的响声，是她从未听过的。很快她便发觉，那是鱼在说话。她想听懂它们说些什么，可它们的语言那样古旧、粗陋、含混，她实在听不出多少名堂。
"我去替我女儿看看那些鱼吧。"大海老人说。
他推开屋里墙上一扇小拉窗，先往外望了一眼，然后在一块嵌满圆形开口的厚水晶上敲了几下。缠儿走到他身后，从那窗口朝那幽深碧绿的大海的心脏里窥去，看见些再古怪不过的生灵，有的丑得很，全都奇形怪状，嘴巴尤其古怪，到处游来游去，上有下有，可全都朝着这扇窗游来，应着大海老人的叩击。只有几条能把嘴贴到玻璃上，可那些漂浮在几英里外的，也都把头转了过来。老人在那整群鱼里仔仔细细地看了几分钟，然后转身对缠儿说——
"真对不住，我还没有备好一条。我要的工夫比她要的多。可我会尽快送几条去。"
说罢他便关上了拉窗。
不多时，海里又掀起一阵大骚动。老人又推开拉窗，在玻璃上敲了几下，那些鱼便都静了下来，静得像睡熟了一般。
"它们不过是议论你罢了。"他说，"它们说的话可真是没头没脑！——明天，"他接着说，"我得送你去大地老人那里。他住得离这儿很远。"

===Original===
“Do let me go at once,” said Tangle.
“No. That is not possible. You must come this way first.”
He led her to a hole in the wall, which she had not observed before. It was covered with the green leaves and white blossoms of a creeping plant.
“Only white-blossoming plants can grow under the sea,” said the old man. “In there you will find a bath, in which you must lie till I call you.”
Tangle went in, and found a smaller room or cave, in the further corner of which was a great basin hollowed out of a rock, and half-full of the clearest seawater. Little streams were constantly running into it from cracks in the wall of the cavern. It was polished quite smooth inside, and had a carpet of yellow sand in the bottom of it. Large green leaves and white flowers of various plants crowded up and over it, draping and covering it almost entirely.
No sooner was she undressed and lying in the bath, than she began to feel as if the water were sinking into her, and she were receiving all the good of sleep without undergoing its forgetfulness. She felt the good coming all the time. And she grew happier and more hopeful than she had been since she lost Mossy. But she could not help thinking how very sad it was for a poor old man to live there all alone, and have to take care of a whole seaful of stupid and riotous fishes.

===Chinese===
"请您就让我去吧。"缠儿说。
"不。那可不成。你得先到这边来一趟。"
他把她领到墙上的一个洞口前，那洞口她先前没留意到。洞口上爬满了一种攀缘植物的绿叶和白花。
"在海底，只有开白花的植物才能生长。"老人说，"你到里面去，会找到一个浴池，你得在里头躺着，等我叫你。"
缠儿走了进去，发现里头是一间更小的屋子，也可说是一处洞穴，洞穴再深处的角落里，有一个凿在一块岩石里的大盆，半盆盛着最清的海水。一道道细流不断地从洞壁的裂缝里流进盆里。盆里头磨得十分光滑，底下还铺着一层黄沙的地毯。各种植物的大绿叶子和白花挤挤挨挨地从盆边涌上来、又垂下去，几乎把它整个地遮掩了。
她一脱了衣裳躺进浴池，便开始觉得那水仿佛正一寸寸渗进她身体里，而她正领受着睡眠的一切好处，却免除了睡眠的遗忘。她觉得那好处源源不断地来。她比自从失了小苔生以来任何一天都要快乐、都要心怀盼望。可她也不禁心想，一位可怜的老人独自住在这里，还要照看满满一海的又蠢又喧闹的鱼，这事多么叫人伤心啊。

===Original===
After about an hour, as she thought, she heard his voice calling her, and rose out of the bath. All the fatigue and aching of her long journey had vanished. She was as whole, and strong, and well as if she had slept for seven days.
Returning to the opening that led into the other part of the house, she started back with amazement, for through it she saw the form of a grand man, with a majestic and beautiful face, waiting for her.
“Come,” he said; “I see you are ready.”
She entered with reverence.
“Where is the Old Man of the Sea?” she asked, humbly.
“There is no one here but me,” he answered, smiling. “Some people call me the Old Man of the Sea. Others have another name for me, and are terribly frightened when they meet me taking a walk by the shore. Therefore I avoid being seen by them, for they are so afraid, that they never see what I really am. You see me now.﻿—But I must show you the way to the Old Man of the Earth.”
He led her into the cave where the bath was, and there she saw, in the opposite corner, a second opening in the rock.
“Go down that stair, and it will bring you to him,” said the Old Man of the Sea.
With humble thanks Tangle took her leave. She went down the winding stair, till she began to fear there was no end to it. Still down and down it went, rough and broken, with springs of water bursting out of the rocks and running down the steps beside her. It was quite dark about her, and yet she could see. For after being in that bath, people’s eyes always give out a light they can see by. There were no creeping things in the way. All was safe and pleasant though so dark and damp and deep.

===Chinese===
过了约莫一个钟头——她是这样觉得的——她听见老人的声音在唤她，便从浴池里起身。长途跋涉的一切疲乏和酸痛都消失了。她整个人健壮、有力、康健，仿佛已经足足睡了七天。
她走回到通往屋子另一头的那个门口，却吓得倒退一步，因为透过那门口，她看见一个庄严男子的身影，正带着一张高贵而美丽的脸，在那里等她。
"来吧，"他说，"我看你已准备好了。"
她怀着敬意走了进去。
"大海老人在哪里？"她谦卑地问。
"这里除了我，再没有别人。"他微笑着答，"有些人叫我大海老人。也有些人对我另有一个名字，一旦在岸边散步时撞见我，便吓得魂不附体。所以我才避开他们，不让他们看见，因为他们那样害怕，竟从来也看不见我真正的样子。你现在看见我了。——可我得把你送到大地老人那里去。"
他把她领进那间有浴池的洞穴，在那里，她看见对面的角落里，岩石上又开了第二个洞口。
"顺着那段石阶下去，它会把你带到他跟前。"大海老人说。
缠儿谦恭地道了谢，告辞而去。她顺着那段蜿蜒的石阶往下走，走着走着，开始害怕这段石阶永无尽头。可它仍是一直往下、往下，又崎岖又破碎，一路上还有泉水从岩壁里迸出，顺着她身边的石阶流下去。她四围一片漆黑，可她偏偏看得见。因为凡在那浴池里洗过的人，双眼从此总会发出一种光，足以照见前路。路上没有什么爬虫。一切都安稳、舒心，尽管那样黑、那样潮、那样深。

===Original===
At last there was not one step more, and she found herself in a glimmering cave. On a stone in the middle of it sat a figure with its back towards her﻿—the figure of an old man bent double with age. From behind she could see his white beard spread out on the rocky floor in front of him. He did not move as she entered, so she passed round that she might stand before him and speak to him.
The moment she looked in his face, she saw that he was a youth of marvellous beauty. He sat entranced with the delight of what he beheld in a mirror of something like silver, which lay on the floor at his feet, and which from behind she had taken for his white beard. He sat on, heedless of her presence, pale with the joy of his vision. She stood and watched him. At length, all trembling, she spoke. But her voice made no sound. Yet the youth lifted up his head. He showed no surprise, however, at seeing her﻿—only smiled a welcome.
“Are you the Old Man of the Earth?” Tangle had said.
And the youth answered, and Tangle heard him, though not with her ears:﻿—
“I am. What can I do for you?”
“Tell me the way to the country whence the shadows fall.”
“Ah! that I do not know. I only dream about it myself. I see its shadows sometimes in my mirror: the way to it I do not know. But I think the Old Man of the Fire must know. He is much older than I am. He is the oldest man of all.”
“Where does he live?”
“I will show you the way to his place. I never saw him myself.”
So saying, the young man rose, and then stood for a while gazing at Tangle.
“I wish I could see that country too,” he said. “But I must mind my work.”

===Chinese===
终于，再没有一步石阶了，她发觉自己置身于一个隐隐发亮的洞穴里。洞中央的一块石头上，坐着一个背对着她的身影——那是一位因年迈而佝偻成两截的老人。从他背后，她能看见他的白胡须铺展在他面前的岩石地面上。她进去时他一动不动，于是她绕到他跟前，好站在他面前和他说话。
她一望进他的脸，便看见他竟是一位美得惊人的少年。他正痴痴地坐在那里，沉醉于他在一面银一般的镜子里所见的景象之中——那镜子铺在他脚下的地上，她从背后看去，还当作是他的白胡须。他仍坐着，全然不理会她在场，因他所见的景象而面容苍白。她站着看他。终于，她浑身颤抖着开了口。可她的声音没有发出一点声响。然而那少年却抬起了头。不过他看见她，也并不惊奇——只是含笑相迎。
"您是大地老人吗？"缠儿这样说道。
那少年答了话，缠儿听见了——却不是用耳朵听见的：——
"我是。我能为你做什么？"
"请告诉我去影子所投下的那片国土的路。"
"啊！那条路我不知道。我自己也只是梦见过它。我有时在我的镜子里看见它的影子：那条路我却不知道。不过我想，烈火老人（the Old Man of the Fire）一定知道。他比我年长得多。他是最最年长的人。"
"他住在哪里？"
"我指给你去他那里的路。我自己从未见过他。"
说着，那少年站起身来，又站了一会儿，凝视着缠儿。
"但愿我也能看见那片国土。"他说，"可我得守着我的活儿。"

===Original===
He led her to the side of the cave, and told her to lay her ear against the wall.
“What do you hear?” he asked.
“I hear,” answered Tangle, “the sound of a great water running inside the rock.”
“That river runs down to the dwelling of the oldest man of all﻿—the Old Man of the Fire. I wish I could go to see him. But I must mind my work. That river is the only way to him.”
Then the Old Man of the Earth stooped over the floor of the cave, raised a huge stone from it, and left it leaning. It disclosed a great hole that went plumb-down.
“That is the way,” he said.
“But there are no stairs.”
“You must throw yourself in. There is no other way.”
She turned and looked him full in the face﻿—stood so for a whole minute, as she thought: it was a whole year﻿—then threw herself headlong into the hole.
When she came to herself, she found herself gliding down fast and deep. Her head was under water, but that did not signify, for, when she thought about it, she could not remember that she had breathed once since her bath in the cave of the Old Man of the Sea. When she lifted up her head a sudden and fierce heat struck her, and she sank it again instantly, and went sweeping on.
Gradually the stream grew shallower. At length she could hardly keep her head under. Then the water could carry her no farther. She rose from the channel, and went step for step down the burning descent. The water ceased altogether. The heat was terrible. She felt scorched to the bone, but it did not touch her strength. It grew hotter and hotter. She said, “I can bear it no longer.” Yet she went on.
At the long last, the stair ended at a rude archway in an all but glowing rock. Through this archway Tangle fell exhausted into a cool mossy cave. The floor and walls were covered with moss﻿—green, soft, and damp. A little stream spouted from a rent in the rock and fell into a basin of moss. She plunged her face into it and drank. Then she lifted her head and looked around. Then she rose and looked again. She saw no one in the cave. But the moment she stood upright she had a marvellous sense that she was in the secret of the earth and all its ways. Everything she had seen, or learned from books; all that her grandmother had said or sung to her; all the talk of the beasts, birds, and fishes; all that had happened to her on her journey with Mossy, and since then in the heart of the earth with the Old man and the Older man﻿—all was plain: she understood it all, and saw that everything meant the same thing, though she could not have put it into words again.

===Chinese===
他把她领到洞穴的一边，让她把耳朵贴在墙上。
"你听见什么？"他问。
"我听见，"缠儿答，"岩石里头有一股大水奔流的声音。"
"那条河，一直流到那位最最年长的人——烈火老人的住处。我恨不得能去拜望他。可我得守着我的活儿。那条河是通往他那里的唯一的路。"
于是大地老人俯身在洞底，从地上掀起一块大石，让它斜斜地倚着。石头底下露出一个直直通下去的大洞。
"那就是路。"他说。
"可是那里头没有石阶。"
"你必须纵身跳下去。此外别无他法。"
她转过身，直直地望着他的脸——这样站了足有一刻钟——她是这样觉得的：其实是一整年——然后便一头扎进了那个洞里。
等她回过神来，发觉自己正飞快地、深深地往下滑。她的头埋在水底下，可这不要紧，因为她仔细一想，竟记不起自打在大海老人的洞穴里洗过那浴之后，她曾呼吸过一回。她一抬头，一股凶猛的灼热便扑面而来，她赶紧又把头沉下去，任由自己被水一路卷着前行。
水流渐渐地浅了。终于她几乎已不能把头藏在水下。这时水也再载不动她了。她从河床里站起来，一步一步顺着那灼热的下坡往下走。水完全没有了。酷热可怕极了。她觉得自己已被烤到骨头里，可那热却丝毫伤不了她的气力。越来越热，越来越热。她说："我再也受不住了。"可她仍往前走。
直到最后最后，石阶在一块几乎通红的岩石上一道粗陋的拱门前到了头。缠儿筋疲力尽地穿过这道拱门，扑进一个凉爽、长满青苔的洞穴。地面和四壁都覆满了青苔——青青的，软软的，湿湿的。一道细细的泉水从岩缝里喷出来，落进一个青苔围成的水盆里。她把脸埋进去，喝了个够。然后抬头四下张望。然后站起来再看一遍。她在洞里看不见一个人。可她一直起身来，便有一种奇妙的感觉：她已窥见大地的奥秘和它一切行事之道。她所见过的一切，从书本上学过的一切；奶奶对她说过、唱过的一切；禽兽鱼鳖所说的一切；她和小苔生同行时发生在她身上的一切；以及自那以后、在这大地的心脏里、与那位老人和更年长的老人之间所经历的一切——全都明明白白：这一切她都懂得了，她看出来这一切意味着同一桩事，虽说她再也不能把它用话讲出来。

===Original===
The next moment she descried, in a corner of the cave, a little naked child sitting on the moss. He was playing with balls of various colours and sizes, which he disposed in strange figures upon the floor beside him. And now Tangle felt that there was something in her knowledge which was not in her understanding. For she knew there must be an infinite meaning in the change and sequence and individual forms of the figures into which the child arranged the balls, as well as in the varied harmonies of their colours, but what it all meant she could not tell.* He went on busily, tirelessly, playing his solitary game, without looking up, or seeming to know that there was a stranger in his deep-withdrawn cell. Diligently as a lace-maker shifts her bobbins, he shifted and arranged his balls. Flashes of meaning would now pass from them to Tangle, and now again all would be not merely obscure, but utterly dark. She stood looking for a long time, for there was fascination in the sight; and the longer she looked the more an indescribable vague intelligence went on rousing itself in her mind. For seven years she had stood there watching the naked child with his coloured balls, and it seemed to her like seven hours, when all at once the shape the balls took, she knew not why, reminded her of the Valley of Shadows, and she spoke:﻿—
“Where is the Old Man of the Fire?” she said.
* I think I must be indebted to Novalis for these geometrical figures.

===Chinese===
下一刻，她在洞穴的一个角落里，瞥见一个光着身子的小孩坐在青苔上。他正玩着一些大小不一、颜色各异的球，把它们在身边的地上摆成种种奇异的图形。这时缠儿觉得，她的所知之中，有一样是她的所识所悟还容纳不下的。因为她知道，那孩子把球摆成的图形的种种变化、先后次序、各异的形状，以及球的颜色那千变万化的和谐之中，必定含有无穷无尽的意义，可这一切究竟意味着什么，她却说不出来。* 他忙着、不倦地玩着他那一个人的游戏，头也不抬，似乎并不知道这个深深隐居的洞窟里来了一个生客。他像一个织花边的女工那样勤勉地挪动她的线轴，挪动着、排列着他的那些球。一闪一闪的意义时而从那些球上闪到缠儿心里来，时而又一切变得不仅模糊，简直是漆黑一片。她站了许久看着，因为这景象有叫人移不开眼的魔力；她看得越久，她心里便有一种说不出的、隐隐的智慧，越发地苏醒过来。她在那里整整站了七年，看着那个光身子的孩子和他的彩球，而她觉得不过七个钟头，忽然之间，那些球所构成的图形——她也不知是何缘故——叫她想起了影子之谷，于是她开了口：——
"烈火老人在哪里？"她说。
* 我想，这些几何图形，我大概受了诺瓦利斯（Novalis）的恩惠。

===Original===
“Here I am,” answered the child, rising and leaving his balls on the moss. “What can I do for you?”
There was such an awfulness of absolute repose on the face of the child that Tangle stood dumb before him. He had no smile, but the love in his large gray eyes was deep as the centre. And with the repose there lay on his face a shimmer as of moonlight, which seemed as if any moment it might break into such a ravishing smile as would cause the beholder to weep himself to death. But the smile never came, and the moonlight lay there unbroken. For the heart of the child was too deep for any smile to reach from it to his face.
“Are you the oldest man of all?” Tangle at length, although filled with awe, ventured to ask.
“Yes, I am. I am very, very old. I am able to help you, I know. I can help everybody.” And the child drew near and looked up in her face so that she burst into tears.
“Can you tell me the way to the country the shadows fall from?” she sobbed.
“Yes. I know the way quite well. I go there myself sometimes. But you could not go my way; you are not old enough. I will show you how you can go.”
“Do not send me out into the great heat again,” prayed Tangle.
“I will not,” answered the child.
And he reached up, and put his little cool hand on her heart.
“Now,” he said, “you can go. The fire will not burn you. Come.”

===Chinese===
"我在这儿。"那孩子答道，站起身来，把他的球留在青苔上。"我能为你做什么？"
那孩子的脸上，有一种绝对的安宁，安宁得近乎可畏，缠儿在他面前竟哑口无言地站着。他没有笑，可他那双灰色的大眼睛里的爱，深得像万物的中心。在那份安宁之上，他脸上还浮着一层如月光般的微辉，那微辉仿佛随时会绽放出那样一个令人销魂的微笑，足以叫看见的人一直哭到死。然而那微笑始终没有来，那月光就那样不曾破碎地铺在那里。因为这孩子的心太深了，深到任何微笑都无法从那里头升到他脸上。
"您是最最年长的人吗？"缠儿终于鼓起敬畏之心，大着胆子问。
"是，我是。我很老很老了。我能够帮助你，我知道。我能帮助每一个人。"那孩子走上前来，仰脸望着她，望得她一下子泪如泉涌。
"您能告诉我去影子所投下的那片国土的路吗？"她抽泣着说。
"能。那条路我清清楚楚。我自己有时也到那里去。可你不能走我那条路；你还不够老。我会指给你一条你能走的路。"
"请别再把我送到那大热里去了。"缠儿恳求道。
"我不会的。"那孩子答。
他伸出手，把他那只凉凉的小手，按在她的心上。
"现在，"他说，"你可以去了。火不会烧着你。来吧。"

===Original===
He led her from the cave, and following him through another archway, she found herself in a vast desert of sand and rock. The sky of it was of rock, lowering over them like solid thunderclouds; and the whole place was so hot that she saw, in bright rivulets, the yellow gold and white silver and red copper trickling molten from the rocks. But the heat never came near her.
When they had gone some distance, the child turned up a great stone, and took something like an egg from under it. He next drew a long curved line in the sand with his finger, and laid the egg in it. He then spoke something Tangle could not understand. The egg broke, a small snake came out, and, lying in the line in the sand, grew and grew till he filled it. The moment he was thus full-grown, he began to glide away, undulating like a sea-wave.
“Follow that serpent,” said the child. “He will lead you the right way.”
Tangle followed the serpent. But she could not go far without looking back at the marvellous child. He stood alone in the midst of the glowing desert, beside a fountain of red flame that had burst forth at his feet, his naked whiteness glimmering a pale rosy red in the torrid fire. There he stood, looking after her, till, from the lengthening distance, she could see him no more. The serpent went straight on, turning neither to the right nor left.

===Chinese===
他把她领出洞外，她跟着他穿过又一道拱门，发觉自己置身于一片广袤的沙石荒漠之中。这里头的天也是岩石做的，像凝固的雷雨云一般低低地压在他们头顶；这整个地方热得她看得见黄金、白银和赤铜化作明亮的细流，从岩石上一滴一滴熔化下来。可那热却一点也不逼近她。
他们走了一程，那孩子掀起一块大石，从石头底下拿出一个像蛋一样的东西。接着，他用手指在沙地里画了一条长长的弧线，把那枚蛋放了进去。然后他念了几句缠儿听不懂的话。那蛋破了，钻出一条小蛇，躺在沙地的那条线里，越长越大，大到把那条线填满。它一长成，便开始一扭一扭地游走了，像一道海浪。
"跟着那条蛇。"那孩子说，"它会引你走对路。"
缠儿跟着那条蛇。可她走不多远，便忍不住回头望那不可思议的孩子。他独自站在那灼灼的荒漠中央，身边是一股从他脚下喷涌而出的红火之泉，他那光洁雪白的身子，在那酷烈的烈火里，闪着淡淡的玫瑰红。他就这样站着，目送着她，直到随着距离越拉越远，她再也看不见他。那条蛇径直往前游，既不偏左，也不偏右。

===Original===
Meantime Mossy had got out of the Lake of Shadows, and, following his mournful, lonely way, had reached the seashore. It was a dark, stormy evening. The sun had set. The wind was blowing from the sea. The waves had surrounded the rock within which lay the old man’s house. A deep water rolled between it and the shore, upon which a majestic figure was walking alone.
Mossy went up to him and said﻿—
“Will you tell me where to find the Old Man of the Sea?”
“I am the Old Man of the Sea,” the figure answered.
“I see a strong kingly man of middle age,” returned Mossy.
Then the old man looked at him more intently, and said﻿—
“Your sight, young man, is better than that of most who take this way. The night is stormy: come to my house and tell me what I can do for you.”
Mossy followed him. The waves flew from before the footsteps of the Old Man of the Sea, and Mossy followed upon dry sand.
When they had reached the cave, they sat down and gazed at each other.
Now Mossy was an old man by this time. He looked much older than the Old Man of the Sea, and his feet were very weary.
After looking at him for a moment, the old man took him by the hand and led him into his inner cave. There he helped him to undress, and laid him in the bath. And he saw that one of his hands Mossy did not open.
“What have you in that hand?” he asked.
Mossy opened his hand, and there lay the golden key.
“Ah!” said the old man, “that accounts for your knowing me. And I know the way you have to go.”
“I want to find the country whence the shadows fall,” said Mossy.
“I dare say you do. So do I. But meantime, one thing is certain.﻿—What is that key for, do you think?”
“For a keyhole somewhere. But I don’t know why I keep it. I never could find the keyhole. And I have lived a good while, I believe,” said Mossy, sadly. “I’m not sure that I’m not old. I know my feet ache.”
“Do they?” said the old man, as if he really meant to ask the question; and Mossy, who was still lying in the bath, watched his feet for a moment before he replied﻿—“No, they do not. Perhaps I am not old either.”

===Chinese===
却说那一边，小苔生已从影子之湖里走了出来，一路凄凉孤单地走着，也到了海边。那是一个又黑又多风暴的傍晚。太阳已经落山。风从海面上吹来。海浪已经把藏着老人屋子的那块岩石团团围住。岩石和海岸之间隔着一片深水，海岸上，一个庄严的身影正独自走着。
小苔生走到他跟前，说道——
"您能告诉我，到哪里去找大海老人吗？"
"我就是大海老人。"那身影答。
"我看见的是一位健壮、王者气度的中年男子。"小苔生回他。
于是那老人更专注地看了他一眼，说道——
"年轻人，你的眼力比大多数走这条路的人都好。今夜有风浪：到我家里来，告诉我我能为你做什么。"
小苔生跟着他。大海老人脚步所到之处，海浪纷纷退避，小苔生便踏着干爽的沙地跟在后头。
他们到了洞里，便坐下，彼此对望。
这会儿小苔生已经是一位老人了。他看着比大海老人还要老得多，他的双脚也十分疲惫。
老人看了他一会儿，便牵起他的手，把他领进内洞。在那里，他帮他脱了衣裳，扶他躺进浴池。他看见小苔生有一只手始终攥着不肯松开。
"你那只手里拿着什么？"他问。
小苔生张开手，那把金钥匙正躺在里头。
"啊！"老人说，"难怪你认得我。我也知道你该走哪条路。"
"我想找到影子所投下的那片国土。"小苔生说。
"我料你想。我也想。不过眼下有一件事是确定的。——你想，那把钥匙是为着什么用的？"
"是为着某处的一个锁孔。可我不知道自己为何还留着它。我从没找到那个锁孔。而且我想，我已经活了挺久了。"小苔生伤感地说，"我说不准自己是不是已经老了。我只知道我的脚在痛。"
"是吗？"老人说，那语气仿佛是真真切切地在问这个问题；小苔生仍躺在浴池里，对着自己的脚端详了一会儿，才答道——"不，它们不痛了。也许我也没有老。"

===Original===
“Get up and look at yourself in the water.”
He rose and looked at himself in the water, and there was not a gray hair on his head or a wrinkle on his skin.
“You have tasted of death now,” said the old man. “Is it good?”
“It is good,” said Mossy. “It is better than life.”
“No, said the old man: it is only more life.﻿—Your feet will make no holes in the water now.”
“What do you mean?”
“I will show you that presently.”
They returned to the outer cave, and sat and talked together for a long time. At length the Old Man of the Sea rose, and said to Mossy﻿—
“Follow me.”
He led him up the stair again, and opened another door. They stood on the level of the raging sea, looking towards the east. Across the waste of waters, against the bosom of a fierce black cloud, stood the foot of a rainbow, glowing in the dark.
“This indeed is my way,” said Mossy, as soon as he saw the rainbow, and stepped out upon the sea. His feet made no holes in the water. He fought the wind, and clomb the waves, and went on towards the rainbow.
The storm died away. A lovely day and a lovelier night followed. A cool wind blew over the wide plain of the quiet ocean. And still Mossy journeyed eastward. But the rainbow had vanished with the storm.

===Chinese===
"起来，在水里照照你自己。"
他站起来，在水里照了照自己，他头上没有一根白发，皮肤上没有一条皱纹。
"你如今已尝过死亡的滋味了。"老人说，"它好吗？"
"它好。"小苔生说，"它比生命更好。"
"不，"老人说，"它只不过是更多的生命罢了。——你的脚，如今在水里也不会踩出洞来了。"
"您这话什么意思？"
"我这就指给你看。"
他们回到外洞，坐下谈了好久。终于大海老人站起来，对小苔生说——
"跟我来。"
他又领他顺着石阶上去，打开了另一扇门。他们站在怒涛汹涌的海面那一层高上，朝着东方望去。越过那片茫茫的大水，在一片凶猛的乌云的怀里，立着一道彩虹的根部，在黑暗里发着光。
"这果然是我的路。"小苔生一看见那彩虹，便说道，随即踏上了海面。他的脚在水上踩不出洞来。他一路和风搏斗，攀过一道道浪，朝着那彩虹走去。
风暴平息了。接下去是一个可爱的白天，和一个更可爱的夜晚。一阵凉风吹过那片平静的大洋的广阔平原。小苔生仍一路东行。可那彩虹已随风暴一同消失了。

===Original===
Day after day he held on, and he thought he had no guide. He did not see how a shining fish under the water directed his steps. He crossed the sea, and came to a great precipice of rock, up which he could discover but one path. Nor did this lead him farther than halfway up the rock, where it ended on a platform. Here he stood and pondered.﻿—It could not be that the way stopped here, else what was the path for? It was a rough path, not very plain, yet certainly a path.﻿—He examined the face of the rock. It was smooth as glass. But as his eyes kept roving hopelessly over it, something glittered, and he caught sight of a row of small sapphires. They bordered a little hole in the rock.
“The keyhole!” he cried.
He tried the key. It fitted. It turned. A great clang and clash, as of iron bolts on huge brazen cauldrons, echoed thunderously within. He drew out the key. The rock in front of him began to fall. He retreated from it as far as the breadth of the platform would allow. A great slab fell at his feet. In front was still the solid rock, with this one slab fallen forward out of it. But the moment he stepped upon it, a second fell, just short of the edge of the first, making the next step of a stair, which thus kept dropping itself before him as he ascended into the heart of the precipice. It led him into a hall fit for such an approach﻿—irregular and rude in formation, but floor, sides, pillars, and vaulted roof, all one mass of shining stones of every colour that light can show. In the centre stood seven columns, ranged from red to violet. And on the pedestal of one of them sat a woman, motionless, with her face bowed upon her knees. Seven years had she sat there waiting. She lifted her head as Mossy drew near. It was Tangle. Her hair had grown to her feet, and was rippled like the windless sea on broad sands. Her face was beautiful, like her grandmother’s, and as still and peaceful as that of the Old Man of the Fire. Her form was tall and noble. Yet Mossy knew her at once.

===Chinese===
他一天接一天地坚持着走，自以为并没有引路人。他没看见水下有一条发光的鱼，正在引着他的脚步。他渡过大海，来到一堵巨大的岩石峭壁前，他能寻到的上去的路只有一条。可这条路也只把他送到峭壁的半腰，便在一段石台上到了头。他站在那里沉思。——这条路不至于在这里就断了吧，否则这条路又是为着什么呢？这是一条崎岖的路，并不十分显眼，可终究是一条路。——他细细打量那岩壁的表面。它光滑如玻璃。可当他的目光在它上面无望地来回搜寻时，有一点什么闪了一闪，他瞥见了一排小小的蓝宝石。它们镶在一个岩壁上的小洞的边上。
"锁孔！"他喊道。
他把钥匙试了一试。正合适。一转，便转动了。里头响起一阵巨大的哐当哐当声，仿佛巨大的铜鼎上砸下了铁门栓，如雷一般回响着。他把钥匙抽出来。他面前的那块岩石开始往下坠。他便朝后退，直退到石台所容许的最远处。一大块石板落在他的脚边。前头仍是整块的岩石，只有这一块石板从里头朝前倒了出来。可他一踏上去，第二块又落了下来，恰好落在第一块的边沿之内，成了石阶的下一级，石阶就这样一路在他面前一级一级地落下去，引着他升进峭壁的心脏里去。它把他引到一座与此种走法相称的大厅里——结构参差不齐、粗犷古朴，可地面、四壁、廊柱、拱顶，全是一整块闪光的、各色各样的宝石组成，能映出光所能显现的每一种颜色。大厅中央立着七根柱子，从红色一直排到紫色。其中一根柱子的基座上，坐着一个一动不动的女子，脸低垂在膝上。她在那里等候了整整七年。小苔生走近时，她抬起头来。那正是缠儿。她的头发一直长到脚边，像无风的大海在宽阔的沙滩上泛起的层层涟漪。她的脸美得像她奶奶，又像烈火老人的脸那样宁静、安详。她的身姿高挑、高贵。然而小苔生一眼就认出了她。

===Original===
“How beautiful you are, Tangle!” he said, in delight and astonishment.
“Am I?” she returned. “Oh, I have waited for you so long! But you, you are like the Old Man of the Sea. No. You are like the Old Man of the Earth. No, no. You are like the oldest man of all. You are like them all. And yet you are my own old Mossy! How did you come here? What did you do after I lost you? Did you find the keyhole? Have you got the key still?”
She had a hundred questions to ask him, and he a hundred more to ask her. They told each other all their adventures, and were as happy as man and woman could be. For they were younger and better, and stronger and wiser, than they had ever been before.
It began to grow dark. And they wanted more than ever to reach the country whence the shadows fall. So they looked about them for a way out of the cave. The door by which Mossy entered had closed again, and there was half a mile of rock between them and the sea. Neither could Tangle find the opening in the floor by which the serpent had led her thither. They searched till it grew so dark that they could see nothing, and gave it up.
After a while, however, the cave began to glimmer again. The light came from the moon, but it did not look like moonlight, for it gleamed through those seven pillars in the middle, and filled the place with all colours. And now Mossy saw that there was a pillar beside the red one, which he had not observed before. And it was of the same new colour that he had seen in the rainbow when he saw it first in the fairy forest. And on it he saw a sparkle of blue. It was the sapphires round the keyhole.

===Chinese===
"缠儿，你多美啊！"他又惊又喜地说。
"我美吗？"她回道，"啊，我等了你这么久！可你，你像大海老人。不对。你像大地老人。不，不。你像那位最最年长的人。你像他们所有的人。可你仍旧是我从前的那个小苔生！你是怎么到这里来的？我走失你之后，你做了些什么？你找到锁孔了吗？那把钥匙你还留着吗？"
她有一百个问题要问他，他也有一百个问题要问她。他们把彼此一路上的遭遇一一相告，快活得世上再没有比他们更快活的男女了。因为他们比从前任何时候都更年轻、更好，更强壮、更有智慧。
天渐渐黑了下来。他们越发地想抵达影子所投下的那片国土了。于是他们四下里寻找一条出洞的路。小苔生进来的那扇门又关上了，他们和海之间隔着半英里厚的岩石。缠儿也找不到当初那条蛇引她进来的那个地上的开口。他们直找到天黑得什么也看不见，只好作罢。
然而过了一阵，洞穴又开始微微地亮起来。那光来自月亮，可它看着并不像月光，因为它是透过中间那七根柱子透进来的，把整个地方照得五颜六色。这时小苔生看见，那根红色柱子旁边，还有一根他先前没有注意到的柱子。它正是他初次在仙境森林里看见彩虹时所见到的那种新颜色。在它上面，他看见一点蓝色的闪光。那是镶在锁孔周围的蓝宝石。

===Original===
He took his key. It turned in the lock to the sound of Aeolian music. A door opened upon slow hinges, and disclosed a winding stair within. The key vanished from his fingers. Tangle went up. Mossy followed. The door closed behind them. They climbed out of the earth; and, still climbing, rose above it. They were in the rainbow. Far abroad, over ocean and land, they could see through its transparent walls the earth beneath their feet. Stairs beside stairs wound up together, and beautiful beings of all ages climbed along with them.
They knew that they were going up to the country whence the shadows fall.
And by this time I think they must have got there.

===Chinese===
他取出钥匙。钥匙在锁里一转，发出风神般的乐声。一扇门在缓缓的铰链声中打开了，露出里面一段蜿蜒的阶梯。钥匙从他指间消失了。缠儿走了上去。小苔生跟在后头。那扇门在他们身后关上了。他们从地底里攀了出来；仍一路往上攀，又升到了大地之上。他们已在彩虹之中了。透过它透明的四壁，他们能远远望见海洋和陆地，望见脚底下的那片大地。一道又一道的阶梯盘旋着一同上升，各种年纪的、美丽的生灵，也和他们一道一路向上攀着。
他们知道，自己正朝着影子所投下的那片国土上升。
而我想，到这时他们必定已经到了那里。

## The Gray Wolf / 灰狼

===Original===
One evening-twilight in spring, a young English student, who had wandered northwards as far as the outlying fragments of Scotland called the Orkney and Shetland Islands, found himself on a small island of the latter group, caught in a storm of wind and hail, which had come on suddenly. It was in vain to look about for any shelter; for not only did the storm entirely obscure the landscape, but there was nothing around him save a desert moss.

===Chinese===
一个春日的薄暮时分，一位年轻的英格兰学子一路北行，来到苏格兰那称作奥克尼与设得兰（Orkney and Shetland）的海外零星岛群，置身于设得兰群岛中一座小岛之上，猝然遭遇了一场风雹交加的风暴。他四下张望，欲觅一处遮蔽，却徒劳无功；那风暴不仅将眼前景象尽数遮蔽，他周身更是一片荒寒的苔原，再无他物。

===Original===
At length, however, as he walked on for mere walking’s sake, he found himself on the verge of a cliff, and saw, over the brow of it, a few feet below him, a ledge of rock, where he might find some shelter from the blast, which blew from behind. Letting himself down by his hands, he alighted upon something that crunched beneath his tread, and found the bones of many small animals scattered about in front of a little cave in the rock, offering the refuge he sought. He went in, and sat upon a stone. The storm increased in violence, and as the darkness grew he became uneasy, for he did not relish the thought of spending the night in the cave. He had parted from his companions on the opposite side of the island, and it added to his uneasiness that they must be full of apprehension about him. At last there came a lull in the storm, and the same instant he heard a footfall, stealthy and light as that of a wild beast, upon the bones at the mouth of the cave. He started up in some fear, though the least thought might have satisfied him that there could be no very dangerous animals upon the island. Before he had time to think, however, the face of a woman appeared in the opening. Eagerly the wanderer spoke. She started at the sound of his voice. He could not see her well, because she was turned towards the darkness of the cave.

===Chinese===
然而，他漫无目的地信步前行，终于来到了一处悬崖边沿；越过崖缘，他看见脚下数尺处有一道岩架，恰好可稍避从背后袭来的狂风。他双手攀援而下，落足时踩着咯吱作响之物，只见一个小岩洞前散落着许多小动物的骸骨——正是他所要寻觅的栖身之所。他走进洞中，在一块石头上坐下。风暴愈演愈烈，天色一寸寸暗下去，他心中渐生不安，实在不愿在这洞里熬过一夜。他原与同伴在小岛另一侧失散，此刻想到他们必为他忧心如焚，更添一分焦灼。终于风暴有了片刻的间歇，也就在这同一刹那，他听见洞口那堆骸骨上响起一阵脚步——轻而隐秘，恰似野兽的足音。他惊惧而起，其实稍一思量便该明白：这岛上断不会有甚么凶险的猛兽。可他尚来不及细想，洞口已浮现出一张妇人的面孔。游子急切地开口相问。她被他这一声惊得一颤。他看不真切她的容颜，因为她面朝洞内那一片幽暗。

===Original===
“Will you tell me how to find my way across the moor to Shielness?” he asked.

“You cannot find it tonight,” she answered, in a sweet tone, and with a smile that bewitched him, revealing the whitest of teeth.

“What am I to do, then?”

“My mother will give you shelter, but that is all she has to offer.”

“And that is far more than I expected a minute ago,” he replied. “I shall be most grateful.”

She turned in silence and left the cave. The youth followed.

===Chinese===
“可否告诉我，怎样穿过那片荒野，走到希尔内斯（Shielness）去？”他问。

“今夜你是寻不见路的了，”她答道，语声温婉，唇边漾起一笑，竟教他心神迷醉，那一笑之间，露出一口皓齿，白得耀眼。

“那我该怎么办？”

“我母亲会收留你歇息，可她所能相待的，也仅此而已。”

“这已远远胜过片刻之前我所敢望的了，”他答道，“我感激不尽。”

她默默转身，出了岩洞。那青年紧随其后。

===Original===
She was barefooted, and her pretty brown feet went catlike over the sharp stones, as she led the way down a rocky path to the shore. Her garments were scanty and torn, and her hair blew tangled in the wind. She seemed about five and twenty, lithe and small. Her long fingers kept clutching and pulling nervously at her skirts as she went. Her face was very gray in complexion, and very worn, but delicately formed, and smooth-skinned. Her thin nostrils were tremulous as eyelids, and her lips, whose curves were faultless, had no colour to give sign of indwelling blood. What her eyes were like he could not see, for she had never lifted the delicate films of her eyelids.

===Chinese===
她赤着双足，那秀美的褐色双脚如猫一般踏过锋利的石块，领他沿一条岩石小径走向海岸。她的衣衫单薄破旧，乱发在风中纠结飘拂。她瞧着约莫二十五岁年纪，身段纤小柔韧。行走之间，她那修长的手指始终不住地揪扯着自己的裙摆，似是不安。她面容灰败，憔悴不堪，轮廓却秀美，肌肤亦光洁。纤薄的鼻翼如眼睑般翕动；双唇曲线完美无瑕，却毫无血色，看不出内有血脉流转。至于她的眼眸是何模样，他却无从窥见——她始终不曾掀起那层柔薄如纱的眼睑。

===Original===
At the foot of the cliff, they came upon a little hut leaning against it, and having for its inner apartment a natural hollow within. Smoke was spreading over the face of the rock, and the grateful odour of food gave hope to the hungry student. His guide opened the door of the cottage; he followed her in, and saw a woman bending over a fire in the middle of the floor. On the fire lay a large fish broiling. The daughter spoke a few words, and the mother turned and welcomed the stranger. She had an old and very wrinkled, but honest face, and looked troubled. She dusted the only chair in the cottage, and placed it for him by the side of the fire, opposite the one window, whence he saw a little patch of yellow sand over which the spent waves spread themselves out listlessly. Under this window there was a bench, upon which the daughter threw herself in an unusual posture, resting her chin upon her hand. A moment after, the youth caught the first glimpse of her blue eyes. They were fixed upon him with a strange look of greed, amounting to craving, but, as if aware that they belied or betrayed her, she dropped them instantly. The moment she veiled them, her face, notwithstanding its colourless complexion, was almost beautiful.

===Chinese===
崖根之下，倚崖立着一间小屋，屋内的里间乃是崖壁上一处天然的凹洞。烟霭漫过崖面，饭菜的香气沁人心脾，给这位饥肠辘辘的学子带来一线希望。引他来的姑娘推开小屋的门，他随之入内，只见一位老妇人正俯身在地上的一堆火前。火上正架着一条大鱼，烤着。那女儿说了几句话，老妇人便转过身来，迎接这位不速之客。她脸庞苍老，皱纹密布，却是一副忠厚相，神色之间似有愁烦。她拂净了屋里唯一的一把椅子，安在火边请他落座，正对着那唯一的窗——透过窗，可以望见一小片黄沙，疲弱的海浪懒懒地铺展开去。窗下有一条长凳，那女儿便以一种古怪的姿势瘫坐其上，以手支颐。片刻之后，青年初次瞥见了她那双蓝眼睛。那目光定定地落在他身上，带着一种奇异的贪婪，近乎饥渴的渴慕；可她仿佛也察觉这眼神正泄露了、甚至出卖了她自己，立时将目光垂了下去。她将眼眸一遮的刹那，纵然面庞毫无血色，也几乎称得上美丽。

===Original===
When the fish was ready, the old woman wiped the deal table, steadied it upon the uneven floor, and covered it with a piece of fine table-linen. She then laid the fish on a wooden platter, and invited the guest to help himself. Seeing no other provision, he pulled from his pocket a hunting knife, and divided a portion from the fish, offering it to the mother first.

“Come, my lamb,” said the old woman; and the daughter approached the table. But her nostrils and mouth quivered with disgust.

The next moment she turned and hurried from the hut.

“She doesn’t like fish,” said the old woman, “and I haven’t anything else to give her.”

“She does not seem in good health,” he rejoined.

The woman answered only with a sigh, and they ate their fish with the help of a little rye bread. As they finished their supper, the youth heard the sound as of the pattering of a dog’s feet upon the sand close to the door; but ere he had time to look out of the window, the door opened, and the young woman entered. She looked better, perhaps from having just washed her face. She drew a stool to the corner of the fire opposite him. But as she sat down, to his bewilderment, and even horror, the student spied a single drop of blood on her white skin within her torn dress. The woman brought out a jar of whisky, put a rusty old kettle on the fire, and took her place in front of it. As soon as the water boiled, she proceeded to make some toddy in a wooden bowl.

===Chinese===
鱼烤好之后，老妇人抹了抹那张松木桌子，在高低不平的地上将它放稳，又铺上一块细软的桌布。她随后将鱼盛在一只木盘里，请客人自取。青年见没有别的餐具，便从衣袋里掏出一把猎刀，切下一块鱼肉，先递给那位母亲。

“来吧，我的小羊羔，”老妇人道。那女儿便走近桌前。然而她的鼻翼与嘴角却因厌恶而抽动起来。

下一刻，她转身匆匆奔出了小屋。

“她不爱吃鱼，”老妇人说，“我再没有别的什么可以给她了。”

“瞧她的气色，身子似乎不大爽健，”他接口道。

那妇人只以一声叹息作答，他们便就着一点黑麦面包吃起鱼来。正餐将毕之时，青年听见门外沙地上一阵声响，恍若犬足拍击之声；可他还来不及向窗外张望，门便开了，那年轻女子走了进来。她脸色比方才好些，许是刚洗过脸的缘故。她把一张矮凳拖到他对面火角的旁边。然而待她坐下，那学子怔住了，更生出几分惊惧——他瞥见她那破衣衫之内、白皙的肌肤上，凝着一滴鲜血。老妇人取出一坛威士忌，把一只生锈的旧水壶架在火上，自己在壶前坐定。水一开，她便动手在一只木碗里调起热威士忌酒来。

===Original===
Meantime the youth could not take his eyes off the young woman, so that at length he found himself fascinated, or rather bewitched. She kept her eyes for the most part veiled with the loveliest eyelids fringed with darkest lashes, and he gazed entranced; for the red glow of the little oil-lamp covered all the strangeness of her complexion. But as soon as he met a stolen glance out of those eyes unveiled, his soul shuddered within him. Lovely face and craving eyes alternated fascination and repulsion.

===Chinese===
这当儿，那青年的目光始终离不开这年轻女子，终至于发觉自己已被迷住——不，竟是被魇住了。她多半时候垂着眼帘，那眼帘秀美极了，镶着一圈最浓密乌黑的睫毛，他望着，如痴如醉；因为那盏小小油灯的红光，恰好将她肤色的种种诡异尽数掩去。可一旦他撞见她那双眼眸悄然掀起、投来一瞥偷视，他的灵魂便在体内打了个寒噤。可爱的容颜与渴慕的眼眸，交替着施魅与推拒。

===Original===
The mother placed the bowl in his hands. He drank sparingly, and passed it to the girl. She lifted it to her lips, and as she tasted﻿—only tasted it﻿—looked at him. He thought the drink must have been drugged and have affected his brain. Her hair smoothed itself back, and drew her forehead backwards with it; while the lower part of her face projected towards the bowl, revealing, ere she sipped, her dazzling teeth in strange prominence. But the same moment the vision vanished; she returned the vessel to her mother, and rising, hurried out of the cottage.

===Chinese===
母亲将那碗酒递到他手中。他浅饮一口，便把碗传给那姑娘。她举碗至唇边，方一沾唇——仅是尝了一尝——便抬眼看他。他疑心这酒里定是下了药，乱了心神：只见她的发丝自行向后展平，连带着把额头也往后牵去；而她脸的下半部却朝着碗口探将出来，未及啜饮，已露出那口耀眼的牙齿，突兀得诡异。可也就在同一瞬间，这幻象消失了；她把碗交还给母亲，起身匆匆奔出了小屋。

===Original===
Then the old woman pointed to a bed of heather in one corner with a murmured apology; and the student, wearied both with the fatigues of the day and the strangeness of the night, threw himself upon it, wrapped in his cloak. The moment he lay down, the storm began afresh, and the wind blew so keenly through the crannies of the hut, that it was only by drawing his cloak over his head that he could protect himself from its currents. Unable to sleep, he lay listening to the uproar which grew in violence, till the spray was dashing against the window. At length the door opened, and the young woman came in, made up the fire, drew the bench before it, and lay down in the same strange posture, with her chin propped on her hand and elbow, and her face turned towards the youth. He moved a little; she dropped her head, and lay on her face, with her arms crossed beneath her forehead. The mother had disappeared.

===Chinese===
于是老妇人指了指墙角一张铺着石楠枝的床铺，口中喃喃致歉；那学子白日里疲乏已极，此夜又这般诡谲，便将斗篷一裹，倒身其上。他刚一躺下，风暴又起了，风从小屋的缝隙间灌入，凛冽刺骨；他唯有将斗篷蒙在头上，才能稍稍挡住那股股寒流。他辗转难眠，卧听窗外狂乱之声愈演愈烈，直至浪花飞溅打在窗上。终于，门开了，那年轻女子走进来，拨旺了火，把长凳拖到火前，又以同样古怪的姿势躺下：下巴支在手肘之上，脸朝向那青年。他稍稍一动，她便低下头，俯脸而卧，双臂交叠在额下。那母亲已不知去向。

===Original===
Drowsiness crept over him. A movement of the bench roused him, and he fancied he saw some four-footed creature as tall as a large dog trot quietly out of the door. He was sure he felt a rush of cold wind. Gazing fixedly through the darkness, he thought he saw the eyes of the damsel encountering his, but a glow from the falling together of the remnants of the fire revealed clearly enough that the bench was vacant. Wondering what could have made her go out in such a storm, he fell fast asleep.

===Chinese===
困意悄然袭来。长凳一动，将他惊醒，他恍惚瞧见一只四足的生灵，身量如大犬一般高，悄悄地颠着步子出了门。他分明感到一股冷风灌了进来。他透过黑暗定睛凝望，似见那少女的眼眸正与他的目光相接；可余烬坍塌之间，火光一闪，已清清楚楚地照见长凳空空如也。他心中纳罕，这般风暴里她为何还要出去，想着想着，便沉沉睡去了。

===Original===
In the middle of the night he felt a pain in his shoulder, came broad awake, and saw the gleaming eyes and grinning teeth of some animal close to his face. Its claws were in his shoulder, and its mouth in the act of seeking his throat. Before it had fixed its fangs, however, he had its throat in one hand, and sought his knife with the other. A terrible struggle followed; but regardless of the tearing claws, he found and opened his knife. He had made one futile stab, and was drawing it for a surer, when, with a spring of the whole body, and one wildly contorted effort, the creature twisted its neck from his hold, and with something betwixt a scream and a howl, darted from him. Again he heard the door open; again the wind blew in upon him, and it continued blowing; a sheet of spray dashed across the floor, and over his face. He sprung from his couch and bounded to the door.

===Chinese===
夜半时分，他肩头一痛，登时彻底清醒，只见一张兽脸近在咫尺，双目荧荧，獠牙狰狞。那兽的利爪已扣进他的肩头，血口正要寻上他的咽喉。可未等它獠牙咬定，他一手已扼住了它的喉管，另一手去摸那把猎刀。一场殊死的搏斗随之而起；他不顾那撕裂皮肉的利爪，摸出了刀，打开了刃。他先是一刺落空，正抽刀要再扎得准些，那生灵却浑身一跃，发狂般地扭曲挣扎，从他掌中扭脱了脖颈，发出一声介于尖叫与嚎叫之间的怪响，从他身上窜了开去。他又一次听见门开之声；又一次风灌了进来，且兀自不止；一片浪花扫过地面，扑在他脸上。他从榻上跃起，奔到门边。

===Original===
It was a wild night﻿—dark, but for the flash of whiteness from the waves as they broke within a few yards of the cottage; the wind was raving, and the rain pouring down the air. A gruesome sound as of mingled weeping and howling came from somewhere in the dark. He turned again into the hut and closed the door, but could find no way of securing it.

The lamp was nearly out, and he could not be certain whether the form of the young woman was upon the bench or not. Overcoming a strong repugnance, he approached it, and put out his hands﻿—there was nothing there. He sat down and waited for the daylight: he dared not sleep any more.

===Chinese===
那是一个狂暴之夜——四野漆黑，唯有几码之外浪头拍岸时翻起的一抹白光；风在咆哮，雨自空中倾泻而下。黑暗之中，不知何处传来一种可怖的声响，像是啜泣与嚎叫交织一片。他转身回到屋里，掩上了门，却寻不出法子将它闩牢。

油灯将熄，他辨不清长凳之上究竟有没有那年轻女子的身影。他强压下一股强烈的厌恶，走上前去，伸出手去——凳上空无一物。他坐下来，静候天明：他再也不敢合眼了。

===Original===
When the day dawned at length, he went out yet again, and looked around. The morning was dim and gusty and gray. The wind had fallen, but the waves were tossing wildly. He wandered up and down the little strand, longing for more light.

At length he heard a movement in the cottage. By and by the voice of the old woman called to him from the door.

“You’re up early, sir. I doubt you didn’t sleep well.”

“Not very well,” he answered. “But where is your daughter?”

“She’s not awake yet,” said the mother. “I’m afraid I have but a poor breakfast for you. But you’ll take a dram and a bit of fish. It’s all I’ve got.”

===Chinese===
天终于破晓，他又走了出去，四下张望。这是一个昏暗、多风、灰沉沉的清晨。风已歇，海浪却仍在狂乱地翻腾。他在那片小沙滩上来回踱步，只盼天光更亮一些。

终于，他听见小屋里有了动静。不多时，老妇人的声音从门口向他唤道：

“先生，您起得好早。怕是昨夜没睡安稳吧。”

“睡得不大好，”他答道，“可您的女儿在哪里？”

“她还没醒呢，”那母亲说，“只怕我给您备的早饭太寒碜了。不过您且饮一小盅酒，吃一口鱼罢。我也就只有这些了。”

===Original===
Unwilling to hurt her, though hardly in good appetite, he sat down at the table. While they were eating, the daughter came in, but turned her face away and went to the farther end of the hut. When she came forward after a minute or two, the youth saw that her hair was drenched, and her face whiter than before. She looked ill and faint, and when she raised her eyes, all their fierceness had vanished, and sadness had taken its place. Her neck was now covered with a cotton handkerchief. She was modestly attentive to him, and no longer shunned his gaze. He was gradually yielding to the temptation of braving another night in the hut, and seeing what would follow, when the old woman spoke.

“The weather will be broken all day, sir,” she said. “You had better be going, or your friends will leave without you.”

===Chinese===
他不忍拂逆她一片心意，虽无甚胃口，仍在桌前坐了下来。他们正吃着，那女儿走了进来，却把脸别过一边，径直走到小屋的另一头去。过了一两分钟她才走上前来，青年看见她的头发湿淋淋的，脸色比先前更加苍白。她瞧着像是病了，又十分虚弱；待她抬起眼来，眸中那一派凶厉已荡然无存，取而代之的是一缕哀伤。她的颈项此刻围着一条棉布手帕。她对他殷勤而得体，也不再回避他的目光。他渐渐地屈从于一种诱惑，想在小屋里再熬上一个夜晚，看看其后会发生甚么——恰在这时，老妇人开了口：

“先生，这天气一整天都要坏下去了，”她说，“您还是动身的好，否则您的朋友们不等您就要离岛了。”

===Original===
Ere he could answer, he saw such a beseeching glance on the face of the girl, that he hesitated, confused. Glancing at the mother, he saw the flash of wrath in her face. She rose and approached her daughter, with her hand lifted to strike her. The young woman stooped her head with a cry. He darted round the table to interpose between them. But the mother had caught hold of her; the handkerchief had fallen from her neck; and the youth saw five blue bruises on her lovely throat﻿—the marks of the four fingers and the thumb of a left hand. With a cry of horror he darted from the house, but as he reached the door he turned. His hostess was lying motionless on the floor, and a huge gray wolf came bounding after him.

===Chinese===
他还来不及作答，便看见那姑娘脸上投来一缕如此哀恳的目光，登时心下踌躇，一片迷惘。他望向那位母亲，只见她脸上一道怒火闪过。她起身逼近女儿，扬起手便要打下去。那年轻女子一声惊叫，把头低了下去。他绕过桌子奔过去，要挡在她们母女之间。可那位母亲已一把抓住了女儿；那条手帕从她颈上滑落；青年这才看见，她那秀美的咽喉上有五道青紫的瘀痕——正是左手四指与拇指所掐下的印记。他惊叫一声，骇然冲出屋去，可一到门口，他又回过了头。他的女主人一动不动地倒在地上，而一头硕大的灰狼，正纵身追了出来。

===Original===
There was no weapon at hand; and if there had been, his inborn chivalry would never have allowed him to harm a woman even under the guise of a wolf. Instinctively, he set himself firm, leaning a little forward, with half outstretched arms, and hands curved ready to clutch again at the throat upon which he had left those pitiful marks. But the creature as she sprung eluded his grasp, and just as he expected to feel her fangs, he found a woman weeping on his bosom, with her arms around his neck. The next instant, the gray wolf broke from him, and bounded howling up the cliff. Recovering himself as he best might, the youth followed, for it was the only way to the moor above, across which he must now make his way to find his companions.

===Chinese===
手边没有兵刃；即便有，他天性中那份骑士的气度，也断不容他伤害一个女子——哪怕她披着狼的外形。他本能地稳住身形，略略前倾，双臂半张，两手虚握成爪，只待再去扼住那曾在其上留下那几道可怜伤痕的喉管。然而那生灵纵身一扑，便脱出了他的掌控；正当他以为要觉到她獠牙咬下之时，怀中却已伏着一个啼哭的女子，双臂正搂着他的颈项。下一瞬间，灰狼挣开他，嚎叫着纵身跃上悬崖。青年尽力定了定神，随之攀了上去——这是通往上方荒野唯一的路，他如今须穿过那片荒野，才能寻见他的同伴。

===Original===
All at once he heard the sound of a crunching of bones﻿—not as if a creature was eating them, but as if they were ground by the teeth of rage and disappointment; looking up, he saw close above him the mouth of the little cavern in which he had taken refuge the day before. Summoning all his resolution, he passed it slowly and softly. From within came the sounds of a mingled moaning and growling.

===Chinese===
蓦地，他听见一阵啃咬骸骨的声响——那声响不像是一只生灵在啃食骨头，倒像是有谁出于狂怒与失望，将骨头在齿间碾磨；他抬眼一望，看见就在头顶不远处，正是前一日他避难的那个小岩洞的洞口。他强抑心神，竭尽定力，缓缓地、轻轻地从洞口经过。洞里传出一阵呜咽与低吼交缠的声响。

===Original===
Having reached the top, he ran at full speed for some distance across the moor before venturing to look behind him. When at length he did so, he saw, against the sky, the girl standing on the edge of the cliff, wringing her hands. One solitary wail crossed the space between. She made no attempt to follow him, and he reached the opposite shore in safety.

===Chinese===
攀上崖顶之后，他在荒野上拼命地奔跑了一程，才敢回头张望。待他终于回过头去，只见以天为幕，那少女正立在悬崖边沿，绞着双手。一声孤零零的哀号，越过他们之间的空阔传了过来。她并无半分追来之意，而他则安然无恙地抵达了对岸。

## I What! No Children? / 一 什么！没有孩子？

===Original===
Once upon a time, so long ago that I have quite forgotten the date, there lived a king and queen who had no children.

And the king said to himself, “All the queens of my acquaintance have children, some three, some seven, and some as many as twelve; and my queen has not one. I feel ill-used.” So he made up his mind to be cross with his wife about it. But she bore it all like a good patient queen as she was. Then the king grew very cross indeed. But the queen pretended to take it all as a joke, and a very good one too.

===Chinese===
从前——那是多久以前的事，我早已忘了确切的年月——有一对国王和王后，膝下没有儿女。

国王暗自想道：“我认识的那些王后，谁都有孩子，有的三个，有的七个，还有的多到十二个；偏偏我的王后一个也没有。我可太亏了。”于是他打定主意，要为这件事跟妻子怄气。可王后就像她本来的好脾性那样，温顺地承受了一切。国王于是越发恼火了。王后却装作把这一切都当作一个笑话，而且还是个顶好笑的笑话。

===Original===
“Why don’t you have any daughters, at least?” said he. “I don’t say ‘sons’; that might be too much to expect.”

“I am sure, dear king, I am very sorry,” said the queen.

“So you ought to be,” retorted the king; “you are not going to make a virtue of *that*, surely.”

===Chinese===
“至少，你怎么不给我生几个女儿呢？”他说，“我还不说‘儿子’；那要求也许太过了。”

“亲爱的国王，我实在是过意不去。”王后说。

“你本来就该过意不去，”国王反唇相讥，“你总不至于把这当成什么美德吧。”

===Original===
But he was not an ill-tempered king, and in any matter of less moment would have let the queen have her own way with all his heart. This, however, was an affair of state.

The queen smiled.

“You must have patience with a lady, you know, dear king,” said she.

She was, indeed, a very nice queen, and heartily sorry that she could not oblige the king immediately.

===Chinese===
不过，他并不是个坏脾气的国王，换作旁的小事，他都会真心实意地依着王后的心意。可这一桩，却是关乎国体的大事。

王后微微一笑。

“您总得对一位女士有点耐心啊，您说是吧，亲爱的国王。”她说。

她确确实实是位极好的王后，只恨自己不能立刻遂了国王的心愿，心里着实过意不去。

===Original===
The king tried to have patience, but he succeeded very badly. It was more than he deserved, therefore, when, at last, the queen gave him a daughter—as lovely a little princess as ever cried.

===Chinese===
国王尽力耐着性子，却收效甚微。因此，当王后终于给他生下一位女儿——一个有史以来最可爱、最会啼哭的小公主——他所得到的，实在远超过他所应得的。

## The Shadows / 阴影

===Original===
Old Ralph Rinkelmann made his living by comic sketches, and all but lost it again by tragic poems. So he was just the man to be chosen king of the fairies, for in Fairyland the sovereignty is elective.
It is no doubt very strange that fairies should desire to have a mortal king; but the fact is, that with all their knowledge and power, they cannot get rid of the feeling that some men are greater than they are, though they can neither fly nor play tricks. So at such times as there happens to be twice the usual number of sensible electors, such a man as Ralph Rinkelmann gets to be chosen.
They did not mean to insist on his residence; for they needed his presence only on special occasions. But they must get hold of him somehow, first of all, in order to make him king. Once he was crowned, they could get him as often as they pleased; but before this ceremony there was a difficulty. For it is only between life and death that the fairies have power over grown-up mortals, and can carry them off to their country. So they had to watch for an opportunity.
Nor had they to wait long. For old Ralph was taken dreadfully ill; and while hovering between life and death, they carried him off, and crowned him king of Fairyland. But after he was crowned, it was no wonder, considering the state of his health, that he should not be able to sit quite upright on the throne of Fairyland; or that, in consequence, all the gnomes and goblins, and ugly, cruel things that live in the holes and corners of the kingdom, should take advantage of his condition, and run quite wild, playing him, king as he was, all sorts of tricks; crowding about his throne, climbing up the steps, and actually scrambling and quarrelling like mice about his ears and eyes, so that he could see and think of nothing else. But I am not going to tell anything more about this part of his adventures just at present. By strong and sustained efforts, he succeeded, after much trouble and suffering, in reducing his rebellious subjects to order. They all vanished to their respective holes and corners; and King Ralph, coming to himself, found himself in his bed, half propped up with pillows.
But the room was full of dark creatures, which gambolled about in the firelight in such a strange, huge, though noiseless fashion, that he thought at first that some of his rebellious goblins had not been subdued with the rest, but had followed him beyond the bounds of Fairyland into his own private house in London. How else could these mad, grotesque hippopotamus-calves make their ugly appearance in Ralph Rinkelmann’s bedroom? But he soon found out that although they were like the underground goblins, they were very different as well, and would require quite different treatment. He felt convinced that they were his subjects too, but that he must have overlooked them somehow at his late coronation﻿—if indeed they had been present; for he could not recollect that he had seen anything just like them before. He resolved, therefore, to pay particular attention to their habits, ways, and characters; else he saw plainly that they would soon be too much for him; as indeed this intrusion into his chamber, where Mrs. Rinkelmann, who must be queen if he was king, sat taking some tea by the fireside, evidently foreshadowed. But she, perceiving that he was looking about him with a more composed expression than his face had worn for many days, started up, and came quickly and quietly to his side, and her face was bright with gladness. Whereupon the fire burned up more cheerily; and the figures became more composed and respectful in their behaviour, retreating towards the wall like well-trained attendants. Then the king of Fairyland had some tea and dry toast, and leaning back on his pillows, nearly fell asleep; but not quite, for he still watched the intruders.

===Chinese===
老拉尔夫·林克尔曼（Ralph Rinkelmann）靠写滑稽小品谋生，又几乎被他的悲剧诗败光了家当。所以他正是个该被选为仙灵之王的人物——因为在仙境，王位是选举产生的。
仙灵竟想要一个凡人国王，这自然很奇怪；可事实是，他们尽管有种种本领和法力，却摆脱不了一个念头：有些人纵然既不会飞，也不会变戏法，却比他们更伟大。于是，每逢有识之士的数量恰好多出往常一倍的时候，拉尔夫·林克尔曼这样的人便会当选。
他们并不打算强留他长住；因为他们只在特殊场合才需要他出面。可要让他当国王，总得先把他弄到手。一旦加冕，他们便可随心意随时召唤他；但在典礼之前，却有一桩难处。因为仙灵只有在凡人处于生死之间时，才能管束成年的凡人，把他们掳到自己的国度去。于是他们只好守候时机。
他们倒也没等多久。因为老拉尔夫病得极重；就在他徘徊于生死之间时，他们把他带走了，加冕为仙境之王。可加冕之后，以他的健康状况而论，他在仙境的宝座上坐不大直，也就不足为奇了；也难怪那些侏儒（gnomes）和地精（goblins）、以及王国一切阴暗角落里栖身的丑陋残暴之物，会借他这副病弱之躯大肆猖獗，对这位国王百般戏弄；它们簇拥在宝座四周，攀上阶陛，竟像老鼠一般在他耳畔眼前你争我抢，闹得他看不见也想不成别的。但眼下我并不打算多讲他这段奇遇的这一节。他凭着坚毅持久的努力，经过许多艰难苦楚，到底把这群叛乱的臣民压服归顺了。它们尽数退回各自的洞穴角落；拉尔夫国王神志渐清，发觉自己躺在自家的床上，半靠着枕头。
可屋里满是幽暗的生灵，它们在炉火光里嬉闹跳跃，那姿态又怪又大，却毫无声息，起初他还以为自己那群叛党的地精有几个未曾与其余的一同被收服，竟跟着他越过仙境的边界，闯进了他在伦敦私邸的家中。不然，这些疯癫的、怪模怪样的"小河马犊子"，又怎会现身于拉尔夫·林克尔曼的卧房？但他很快便察觉，它们虽与地底的地精相像，却也十分不同，须得另眼相待。他确信它们也是他的臣民，只不过在方才那场加冕礼上自己不知怎的把它们漏掉了——倘若它们当时真在场的话；因为他想不起来从前见过与此完全相同的东西。他于是决意格外留心它们的习性、行止与脾性；否则他看得分明，它们不久便会叫他招架不住；事实上，它们闯进这间屋子——而林克尔曼太太，他若是国王她便是王后，此刻正坐在炉边喝茶——便已是明明白白的预兆了。可她见他四下打量的神情比许多日子以来都更加安详，便起身快而轻地走到他身旁，脸上喜气盎然。于是炉火烧得更欢；那些身影也变得规整而恭顺，像训练有素的侍从一般退向墙根。仙境之王用了些茶和干吐司，靠回枕上，几乎睡着；却又不曾真睡，因为他还在盯着这些不速之客。

===Original===
Presently the queen left the room to give some of the young princes and princesses their tea; and the fire burned lower, and behold, the figures grew as black and as mad in their gambols as ever! Their favourite games seemed to be “Hide and Seek;” “Touch and Go;” “Grin and Vanish;” and many other such; and all in the king’s bedchamber, too; so that it was quite alarming. It was almost as bad as if the house had been haunted by certain creatures which shall be nameless in a fairy story, because with them Fairyland will not willingly have much to do.
“But it is a mercy that they have their slippers on!” said the king to himself; for his head ached.
As he lay back, with his eyes half shut and half open, too tired to pay longer attention to their games, but, on the whole, considerably more amused than offended with the liberties they took, for they seemed good-natured creatures, and more frolicsome than positively ill-mannered, he became suddenly aware that two of them had stepped forward from the walls, upon which, after the manner of great spiders, most of them preferred sprawling, and now stood in the middle of the floor at the foot of his majesty’s bed, becking and bowing and ducking in the most grotesquely obsequious manner; while every now and then they turned solemnly round upon one heel, evidently considering that motion the highest token of homage they could show.
“What do you want?” said the king.
“That it may please your majesty to be better acquainted with us,” answered they. “We are your majesty’s subjects.”
“I know you are. I shall be most happy,” answered the king.
“We are not what your majesty takes us for, though. We are not so foolish as your majesty thinks us.”
“It is impossible to take you for anything that I know of,” rejoined the king, who wished to make them talk, and said whatever came uppermost;﻿—“for soldiers, sailors, or anything: you will not stand still long enough. I suppose you really belong to the fire brigade; at least, you keep putting its light out.”
“Don’t jest, please your majesty.” And as they said the words﻿—for they both spoke at once throughout the interview﻿—they performed a grave somerset towards the king.
“Not jest!” retorted he; “and with you? Why, you do nothing but jest. What are you?”
“The Shadows, sire. And when we do jest, sire, we always jest in earnest. But perhaps your majesty does not see us distinctly.”
“I see you perfectly well,” returned the king.
“Permit me, however,” rejoined one of the Shadows; and as he spoke he approached the king; and lifting a dark forefinger, he drew it lightly but carefully across the ridge of his forehead, from temple to temple. The king felt the soft gliding touch go, like water, into every hollow, and over the top of every height of that mountain-chain of thought. He had involuntarily closed his eyes during the operation, and when he unclosed them again, as soon as the finger was withdrawn, he found that they were opened in more senses than one. The room appeared to have extended itself on all sides, till he could not exactly see where the walls were; and all about it stood the Shadows motionless. They were tall and solemn; rather awful, indeed, in their appearance, notwithstanding many remarkable traits of grotesqueness, for they looked just like the pictures of Puritans drawn by Cavaliers, with long arms, and very long, thin legs, from which hung large loose feet, while in their countenances length of chin and nose predominated. The solemnity of their mien, however, overcame all the oddity of their form, so that they were very *eerie* indeed to look at, dressed as they all were in funereal black. But a single glance was all that the king was allowed to have; for the former operator waved his dusky palm across his vision, and once more the king saw only the fire-lighted walls, and dark shapes flickering about upon them. The two who had spoken for the rest seemed likewise to have vanished. But at last the king discovered them, standing one on each side of the fireplace. They kept close to the chimney-wall, and talked to each other across the length of the chimneypiece; thus avoiding the direct rays of the fire, which, though light is necessary to their appearing to human eyes, do not agree with them at all﻿—much less give birth to them, as the king was soon to learn. After a few minutes they again approached the bed, and spoke thus:﻿—

===Chinese===
不一会儿王后离了房，去给几位小王子小公主送茶；炉火低了下去，只见那些身影登时又黑又狂地嬉闹起来，与方才一般无二！它们最爱玩的游戏似乎有"捉迷藏"、"碰一下就跑"、"咧嘴一笑便消失"，诸如此类；而且都在国王的卧房里玩个不停；实在叫人惶恐。这情形简直同屋里闹了某种妖物一样糟糕——这类东西在童话里不便点名道姓，因为仙境也不情愿同它们多有瓜葛。
"幸亏它们穿着拖鞋！"国王自言自语道；因为他的头正疼着呢。
他仰躺着，双眼半开半阖，已疲乏得无心再瞧它们的把戏；不过总的说来，他对这些放肆之举与其说是恼怒，不如说是被逗乐了——它们看去都是好性子的生灵，与其说是当真没规矩，不如说是天性爱闹——忽然间他觉察到，有两个影子从墙边走了出来（大多数影子都喜欢学大蜘蛛的模样，摊手摊脚地趴在墙上），此刻正立在他御床前的屋子中央，以最滑稽的谄媚姿态点头、鞠躬、哈腰；还不时庄重地单脚原地转一圈，显然把这一转当作它们所能行的最高礼数。
"你们要什么？"国王问。
"恳请陛下多多垂顾，与我们更加熟识些，"它们答道，"我们是陛下的臣民。"
"这我知道。我很乐意，"国王答。
"可陛下并不把我们当作我们的本来面目。我们也不像陛下所想的那样蠢。"
"要把我所知的任何东西当作你们，都是不可能的，"国王应道，他正想逗它们开口，便信口说道——"当作兵士、水手，或别的什么都成：你们一刻也不肯站定。我看你们其实是消防队的吧；至少，你们老在把炉火的光灭掉。"
"请陛下莫开玩笑。"它们说这话时——因为整个会面中它们俩总是同声开口——便向国王郑重其事地翻了一个筋斗。
"不开玩笑！"他回嘴，"跟你们开玩笑？嗳，你们自己除了玩笑还干过别的吗？你们究竟是什么？"
"我们是阴影（the Shadows），陛下。可我们若要玩笑，陛下，我们总是郑重其事地玩。不过，或许陛下并未把我们看得真切。"
"我看你们再清楚不过了，"国王答。
"还请陛下允我，"其中一个阴影应道；它一边说，一边走近国王；举起一根幽暗的食指，轻而仔细地，从他的一个太阳穴划过眉骨，直到另一个太阳穴。国王感到那柔滑的触感如水一般，流进那思想山脉的每一道沟壑，又漫过每一座峰顶。他在这一动作间不由自主地阖上了眼，待那手指一抽开，他再睁眼时，便发觉自己"开了眼"，不止一重意义上开了眼。屋子仿佛四下里向外延展开去，直到他几乎看不见墙在何处；屋中处处立着阴影，一动不动。它们又高又庄重；神气甚至颇有几分骇人，尽管身上不乏许多古怪可笑之处；它们看上去活像保皇党人所画的清教徒（Puritans）漫画：长臂，又长又细的腿，垂着又大又松的脚，而面相上则以下巴和鼻子的长度最为突出。然而，它们神情的庄重压过了一切形体上的滑稽，于是它们望上去实在相当*诡谲*（eerie），何况一个个都身着丧服般的黑。可国王只被许看这么一眼；方才那位施术者把黝黑的手掌在他眼前一挥，他便又只能看见被炉火照亮的墙，和上面晃来晃去的黑色身形。那两个替众人发言的似乎也一并消失了。可国王终于又寻见它们，一个立在壁炉这边，一个立在壁炉那边。它们紧贴着烟囱的墙，隔着壁炉架的长度彼此交谈；这样便避开了炉火的直射光线——这光虽然为它们在人眼里显形所必需，却与它们全然不合——更不是它们的生身之父，这一点国王不久便会知晓。过了几分钟，它们重又走到床前，这样说道——

===Original===
“It is now getting dark, please your majesty. We mean, out of doors in the snow. Your majesty may see, from where he is lying, the cold light of its great winding-sheet﻿—a famous carpet for the Shadows to dance upon, your majesty. All our brothers and sisters will be at church now, before going to their night’s work.”
“Do they always go to church before they go to work?”
“They always go to church first.”
“Where is the church?”
“In Iceland. Would your majesty like to see it?”
“How can I go and see it, when, as you know very well, I am ill in bed? Besides, I should be sure to take cold in a frosty night like this, even if I put on the blankets, and took the featherbed for a muff.”
A sort of quivering passed over their faces, which seemed to be their mode of laughing. The whole shape of the face shook and fluctuated as if it had been some dark fluid; till by slow degrees of gathering calm, it settled into its former rest. Then one of them drew aside the curtains of the bed, and the window-curtains not having been yet drawn, the king beheld the white glimmering night outside, struggling with the heaps of darkness that tried to quench it; and the heavens full of stars, flashing and sparkling like live jewels. The other Shadow went towards the fire and vanished in it.
Scores of Shadows immediately began an insane dance all about the room; disappearing, one after the other, through the uncovered window, and gliding darkly away over the face of the white snow; for the window looked at once on a field of snow. In a few moments, the room was quite cleared of them; but instead of being relieved by their absence, the king felt immediately as if he were in a dead-house, and could hardly breathe for the sense of emptiness and desolation that fell upon him. But as he lay looking out on the snow, which stretched blank and wide before him, he spied in the distance a long dark line which drew nearer and nearer, and showed itself at last to be all the Shadows, walking in a double row, and carrying in the midst of them something like a bier. They vanished under the window, but soon reappeared, having somehow climbed up the wall of the house; for they entered in perfect order by the window, as if melting through the transparency of the glass.
They still carried the bier or litter. It was covered with richest furs, and skins of gorgeous wild beasts, whose eyes were replaced by sapphires and emeralds, that glittered and gleamed in the fire and snow light. The outermost skin sparkled with frost, but the inside ones were soft and warm and dry as the down under a swan’s wing. The Shadows approached the bed, and set the litter upon it. Then a number of them brought a huge fur robe, and wrapping it round the king, laid him on the litter in the midst of the furs. Nothing could be more gentle and respectful than the way in which they moved him; and he never thought of refusing to go. Then they put something on his head, and, lifting the litter, carried him once round the room, to fall into order. As he passed the mirror he saw that he was covered with royal ermine, and that his head wore a wonderful crown of gold, set with none but red stones: rubies and carbuncles and garnets, and others whose names he could not tell, glowed gloriously around his head, like the salamandrine essence of all the Christmas fires over the world.
