A sceptre lay beside him﻿—a rod of ebony, surmounted by a cone-shaped diamond, which, cut in a hundred facets, flashed all the hues of the rainbow, and threw coloured gleams on every side, that looked like Shadows too, but more ethereal than those that bore him. Then the Shadows rose gently to the window, passed through it, and sinking slowing upon the field of outstretched snow, commenced an orderly gliding rather than march along the frozen surface. They took it by turns to bear the king, as they sped with the swiftness of thought, in a straight line towards the north. The polestar rose above their heads with visible rapidity; for indeed they moved quite as fast as sad thoughts, though not with all the speed of happy desires. England and Scotland slid past the litter of the king of the Shadows. Over rivers and lakes they skimmed and glided. They climbed the high mountains, and crossed the valleys with a fearless bound; till they came to John-o’-Groat’s house and the Northern Sea. The sea was not frozen; for all the stars shone as clear out of the deeps below as they shone out of the deeps above; and as the bearers slid along the blue-gray surface, with never a furrow in their track, so pure was the water beneath that the king saw neither surface, bottom, nor substance to it, and seemed to be gliding only through the blue sphere of heaven, with the stars above him, and the stars below him, and between the stars and him nothing but an emptiness, where, for the first time in his life, his soul felt that it had room enough.

===Chinese===
"陛下，此刻天色将暗了。我们是说，屋外雪地上正是如此。陛下从躺着的地方，可以望见那雪的一整片冰冷的光，宛如一条巨大的裹尸布——正是阴影们跳舞的好地毯啊，陛下。此刻我们所有的兄弟姐妹都在教堂里了，随后便要去各自做一夜的工。"
"他们做工之前总要先去教堂吗？"
"他们总是先去教堂。"
"教堂在哪里？"
"在冰岛（Iceland）。陛下可愿一观？"
"我卧病在床，你们再清楚不过，怎能去看？再者，这般霜寒之夜，我便裹上毯子、把羽绒褥子当手筒，也保准要受凉。"
它们脸上掠过一种颤动，似乎是它们的笑法。整张脸的轮廓摇晃动荡起来，像是一泓黑色的流质；直到渐渐地聚拢了平静，才回到方才的安止。接着其中一个撩起床帷，因窗帘尚未拉拢，国王便望见窗外那白茫茫、幽光闪闪的夜，正与层层黑暗相抗，竭力不被它们扑灭；满天星斗，明灭闪耀，仿佛活着的珠宝。另一个阴影走向炉火，便在其中隐没了。
霎时间，数十个阴影在屋里狂舞起来；一个接一个地从那扇未掩的窗口消失，黑影绰绰地掠过白雪的表面而去；因为窗外正对着一片雪原。不消片刻，屋里便一个不剩了；可它们的离去并没叫他松一口气，国王反倒立刻觉得自己像置身于一间停尸房，那一阵空寂荒凉之感压得他几乎喘不过气来。可他躺着望向那片在眼前空空荡荡铺展开去的雪地，忽见远处有一道长长的黑线越来越近，最后显出原形——正是全体阴影，排成两行走来，中间抬着一副状如灵床的东西。它们在窗下消失了，却又很快出现，不知怎的已攀上了这屋子的墙；因为它们整整齐齐地从窗口进来，仿佛融化过了那透明的玻璃。
它们仍抬着那副灵床，或者说是担架。上面铺着最华贵的毛皮，和毛色绚烂的猛兽之皮，兽的双眼被蓝宝石和绿宝石所替代，在炉火与雪光里熠熠生辉。最外一层皮上凝着霜花闪光，里层却如天鹅翼下的绒羽一般柔软、温暖、干爽。阴影们走到床前，把担架放在床上。于是有几个抬来一件硕大的毛袍，把国王裹在其中，安放在担架上，卧于众皮之间。它们挪动他的举止再温柔恭顺不过；他也全然不曾起过拒绝之念。接着它们又在他头上戴了什么，抬起担架，抬着他绕屋一周，好整好队伍。他经过镜子时，看见自己身覆王家的貂袍，头上戴着一顶奇妙的金冠，上面镶的全是红色的宝石：红宝石、红玉、石榴石，还有几种他叫不出名字的，在他头上一齐熠熠生辉，仿佛把普天下圣诞炉火中的火蜥蜴精魂都聚在了一起。他身旁搁着一柄权杖——一根乌木杖，顶上镶着一颗锥形的钻石，钻石刻成一百个切面，闪出彩虹所有的颜色，向四下抛出五色光彩，那些光彩看上去也像阴影，却比抬他的这些更加飘渺。于是阴影们轻轻地升向窗口，穿了过去，缓缓地落到那一片平铺的雪原上，沿着冰封的面，与其说是行军，不如说是整齐地滑行。他们轮流抬着国王，以思想那般的迅疾，径直朝北方飞驰而去。北极星以肉眼可见的速度升上他们的头顶；因为他们的确跑得和悲愁的念头一样快，只是不及欢乐的渴望那般神速。英格兰和苏格兰从阴影国王的担架旁一一掠过。他们掠过江河湖泊，从上面轻轻滑过。他们攀上高山，又无所畏惧地一跃越过山谷；直来到约翰奥格罗茨（John-o'-Groat's）的小屋与北海之滨。海并未上冻；因为满天星辰，在水下的深渊里照得与水上的深渊里一般澄明；抬担架的们在蓝灰色的海面上滑行，身后不留一道波痕，底下水清得，国王竟分不出水面、水底和水体来，仿佛只是穿行在蓝色的天穹之中，头顶是星，脚下也是星，星辰与他之间别无一物，唯有空明——生平头一遭，他的灵魂觉得它有了足够的余地。

===Original===
At length they reached the rocky shores of Iceland. There they landed, still pursuing their journey. All this time the king felt no cold; for the red stones in his crown kept him warm, and the emerald and sapphire eyes of the wild beasts kept the frosts from settling upon his litter.
Oftentimes upon their way they had to pass through forests, caverns, and rock-shadowed paths, where it was so dark that at first the king feared he should lose his Shadows altogether. But as soon as they entered such places, the diamond in his sceptre began to shine and glow, and flash, sending out streams of light of all the colours that painter’s soul could dream of; in which light the Shadows grew livelier and stronger than ever, speeding through the dark ways with an all but blinding swiftness. In the light of the diamond, too, some of their forms became more simple and human, while others seemed only to break out into a yet more untamable absurdity. Once, as they passed through a cave, the king actually saw some of their eyes﻿—strange shadow-eyes; he had never seen any of their eyes before. But at the same moment when he saw their eyes, he knew their faces too, for they turned them full upon him for an instant; and the other Shadows, catching sight of these, shrank and shivered, and nearly vanished. Lovely faces they were; but the king was very thoughtful after he saw them, and continued rather troubled all the rest of the journey. He could not account for those faces being there, and the faces of Shadows, too, with living eyes.
But he soon found that amongst the Shadows a man must learn never to be surprised at anything; for if he does not, he will soon grow quite stupid, in consequence of the endless recurrence of surprises.
At last they climbed up the bed of a little stream, and then, passing through a narrow rocky defile, came out suddenly upon the side of a mountain, overlooking a blue frozen lake in the very heart of mighty hills. Overhead, the aurora borealis was shivering and flashing like a battle of ten thousand spears. Underneath, its beams passed faintly over the blue ice and the sides of the snow-clad mountains, whose tops shot up like huge icicles all about, with here and there a star sparkling on the very tip of one. But as the northern lights in the sky above, so wavered and quivered, and shot hither and thither, the Shadows on the surface of the lake below; now gathering in groups, and now shivering asunder; now covering the whole surface of the lake, and anon condensed into one dark knot in the centre. Every here and there on the white mountains might be seen two or three shooting away towards the tops, to vanish beyond them, so that their number was gradually, though not visibly, diminishing.

===Chinese===
他们终于抵达冰岛嶙峋的海岸。他们在那里登岸，仍继续赶路。这一路上，国王丝毫不觉得冷；因为冠冕上的红宝石替他取暖，而猛兽皮上翡翠与蓝宝石的眼睛，则使霜雪不得凝上他的担架。
途中他们常要穿过森林、洞穴和岩影幢幢的小径，那里暗得起初国王唯恐会把他的阴影们全丢了。可一踏进这样的地方，他权杖上的钻石便开始熠熠生辉，闪闪烁烁，射出画家灵魂所能梦见的一切色彩的光流；在这光里，阴影们比先前更欢跃、更有力，以几欲眩目的迅疾穿过那些幽暗的途径。在钻石的光里，它们有的形态变得更简洁、更近人形，有的却仿佛越发闹得无法无天。有一回穿过一个洞穴时，国王竟看见了它们之中几个的眼睛——怪异的阴影之眼；他从前从未见过它们的眼睛。可就在他看见它们眼睛的同一瞬间，他也认出了它们的面目，因为它们把脸正对他转了一转；而别的阴影一瞥见这几个，便瑟缩发颤，几乎消散了去。那都是些可爱的面容；可国王见了之后，心事重重，余下的路程一直颇为不安。他无法解释那些面孔为何会在那里——而且是阴影们的面孔，竟生着活生生的眼睛。
可他不久便明白，在阴影们中间，一个人须得学会对任何事都不再惊奇；否则，无穷无尽的惊异接踵而至，他不久便会变得十分迟钝。
他们终于攀上一条小溪的河床，随后穿过一道狭窄的石隙，忽然来到一座山的山腰，俯瞰着群山环抱正中一座冰封的蓝色湖泊。头顶上，北极光颤动闪烁，如同万矛交战。脚下，它的光带淡淡地掠过蓝色的冰面和雪山的山坡，山顶如一根根巨大的冰柱矗立四围，偶尔有一颗星子在其某一根尖端上灼灼闪耀。可天上的北极光怎样摇曳颤动、四面迸射，下方湖面上的阴影也就怎样摇曳颤动、四面迸射；时而聚作一堆，时而颤散开去；时而铺满全湖，时而又凝成中央的一团黑结。雪白的山坡上，处处可见两三个阴影朝山顶飞掠而去，在山顶的那一边消失不见，于是它们的数目虽不显眼，却是在渐渐地减少。

===Original===
“Please your majesty,” said the Shadows, “this is our church﻿—the Church of the Shadows.”
And so saying, the king’s bodyguard set down the litter upon a rock, and plunged into the multitudes below. They soon returned, however, and bore the king down into the middle of the lake. All the Shadows came crowding round him, respectfully but fearlessly; and sure never such a grotesque assembly revealed itself before to mortal eyes. The king had seen all kind of gnomes, goblins, and kobolds at his coronation; but they were quite rectilinear figures compared with the insane lawlessness of form in which the Shadows rejoiced; and the wildest gambols of the former were orderly dances of ceremony beside the apparently aimless and wilful contortions of figure, and metamorphoses of shape, in which the latter indulged. They retained, however, all the time, to the surprise of the king, an identity, each of his own type, inexplicably perceptible through every change. Indeed this preservation of the primary idea of each form was more wonderful than the bewildering and ridiculous alterations to which the form itself was every moment subjected.
“What are you?” said the king, leaning on his elbow, and looking around him.
“The Shadows, your majesty,” answered several voices at once.
“What Shadows?”
“The human Shadows. The Shadows of men, and women, and their children.”
“Are you not the shadows of chairs and tables, and pokers and tongs, just as well?”
At this question a strange jarring commotion went through the assembly with a shock. Several of the figures shot up as high as the aurora, but instantly settled down again to human size, as if overmastering their feelings, out of respect to him who had roused them. One who had bounded to the highest visible icy peak, and as suddenly returned, now elbowed his way through the rest, and made himself spokesman for them during the remaining part of the dialogue.
“Excuse our agitation, your majesty,” said he. “I see your majesty has not yet thought proper to make himself acquainted with our nature and habits.”
“I wish to do so now,” replied the king.
“We are the Shadows,” repeated the Shadow solemnly.
“Well?” said the king.
“We do not often appear to men.”
“Ha!” said the king.
“We do not belong to the sunshine at all. We go through it unseen, and only by a passing chill do men recognize an unknown presence.”
“Ha!” said the king again.
“It is only in the twilight of the fire, or when one man or woman is alone with a single candle, or when any number of people are all feeling the same thing at once, making them one, that we show ourselves, and the truth of things.”
“Can that be true that loves the night?” said the king.
“The darkness is the nurse of light,” answered the Shadow.
“Can that be true which mocks at forms?” said the king.
“Truth rides abroad in shapeless storms,” answered the Shadow.
“Ha! ha!” thought Ralph Rinkelmann, “it rhymes. The Shadow caps my questions with his answers. Very strange!” And he grew thoughtful again.
The Shadow was the first to resume.
“Please your majesty, may we present our petition?”
“By all means,” replied the king. “I am not well enough to receive it in proper state.”
“Never mind, your majesty. We do not care for much ceremony; and indeed none of us are quite well at present. The subject of our petition weighs upon us.”
“Go on,” said the king.
“Sire,” began the Shadow, “our very existence is in danger. The various sorts of artificial light, both in houses and in men, women, and children, threaten to end our being. The use and the disposition of gaslights, especially high in the centres, blind the eyes by which alone we can be perceived. We are all but banished from towns. We are driven into villages and lonely houses, chiefly old farmhouses, out of which, even, our friends the fairies are fast disappearing. We therefore petition our king, by the power of his art, to restore us to our rights in the house itself, and in the hearts of its inhabitants.”
“But,” said the king, “you frighten the children.”
“Very seldom, your majesty; and then only for their good. We seldom seek to frighten anybody. We mostly want to make people silent and thoughtful; to awe them a little, your majesty.”
“You are much more likely to make them laugh,” said the king.
“Are we?” said the Shadow.
And approaching the king one step, he stood quite still for a moment. The diamond of the king’s sceptre shot out a vivid flame of violet light, and the king stared at the Shadow in silence, and his lip quivered. He never told what he saw then; but he would say:
“Just fancy what it might be if *some* flitting thoughts were to persist in staying to be looked at.”
“It is only,” resumed the Shadow, “when our thoughts are not fixed upon any particular object, that our bodies are subject to all the vagaries of elemental influences. Generally, amongst worldly men and frivolous women, we only attach ourselves to some article of furniture or of dress; and they never doubt that we are mere foolish and vague results of the dashing of the waves of the light against the solid forms of which their houses are full. We do not care to tell them the truth, for they would never see it. But let the worldly man﻿—or the frivolous woman﻿—and then﻿—”
At each of the pauses indicated, the mass of Shadows throbbed and heaved with emotion; but they soon settled again into comparative stillness. Once more the Shadow addressed himself to speak. But suddenly they all looked up, and the king, following their gaze, saw that the aurora had begun to pale.

===Chinese===
"启禀陛下，"阴影们说，"这就是我们的教堂——阴影们的教堂。"
说着，国王的贴身卫队便把担架放在一块岩石上，一头扎进下面的人群中。可不久他们便回来了，把国王抬到湖心。所有的阴影都围拢过来，恭而有礼，却全无惧色；这样怪模怪样的一群，凡人的眼睛从前断未曾见过。加冕礼上国王见过各种各样的侏儒、地精和家神小精怪（kobolds）；可它们比起阴影们所沉醉的那种癫狂无忌的形态，简直算是方方正正的图形；前者的最狂的嬉闹，比起后者所乐此不疲的、显然全无目的、全凭性子的扭曲变形，也不过是规规矩矩的礼节之舞罢了。然而令国王诧异的是，它们始终各保其形——每一种各有自己的原型，不论怎样变幻，却莫名地辨识得出。原来，每一种形态所本的那个最初的理念之得以保持，比形体本身每一刻所承受的那种种令人目眩而可笑的变化，还要来得奇妙。
"你们是什么？"国王支着胳膊肘，环顾四周。
"是阴影，陛下，"几个声音一齐回答。
"什么的阴影？"
"人的阴影。男人、女人，和他们的孩子的阴影。"
"那你们不也是椅子桌子的阴影，火钳火夹的阴影么？"
这一问题一出，会场里顿时像挨了一震似的，起了一阵奇怪的骚动。好几个身影窜得同北极光一般高，却又立刻落回到人形大小，仿佛为了对这位惊动它们的人表示敬意，硬把自己的情绪压了下去。其中一个曾窜到目力所及最高的冰峰之上，又同样倏地回到地面，此刻推开众人挤上前来，在这一段对话的余下部分充当了它们的发言人。
"请原谅我们的激动，陛下，"他说，"我看陛下尚不曾屈尊，来了解我们的本性和习惯。"
"我这就想了解，"国王答。
"我们是阴影，"那阴影庄重地重复。
"怎样呢？"国王说。
"我们并不常向人显形。"
"哈！"国王道。
"我们压根儿不属于阳光。我们从它中间穿过而不被看见；人们只凭一阵掠过的寒意，才察觉有一个未知的存在经过。"
"哈！"国王又道。
"唯有在炉火的暮色里，或一个男人女人独对一盏烛火时，或许多人一同感受着同一件事、从而合而为一时，我们才显形，并显出万物的真相。"
"爱黑夜的，能是真的么？"国王问。
"黑暗是光明的乳母，"阴影答。
"嘲弄形相的，能是真的么？"国王问。
"真理乘无形的风暴出行，"阴影答。
"哈！哈！"拉尔夫·林克尔曼心想，"它押韵。这阴影拿答语来扣我的问语。妙极！"于是他又沉思起来。
那阴影先开了口。
"启禀陛下，我们可以呈上请愿书么？"
"当然，"国王答，"只是我贵恙未愈，不能以应有的仪节接下它。"
"无妨，陛下。我们并不爱许多礼节；其实眼下我们之中谁也不算十分康健。请愿之事压在我们心头。"
"说罢，"国王道。
"陛下，"阴影开始道，"我们的生存本身正岌岌可危。各种各样的人造光——屋子里有，男人女人孩子的心里也有——都威胁着要了我们的命。煤气灯的装置与排布，尤其是高悬在城中心的那些，弄瞎了唯以看见我们的那双眼睛。我们几乎被城镇尽数驱逐。我们被赶进村庄和孤屋，尤其是旧农舍，可就连那里，我们的朋友仙灵（fairies）也在迅速消失。所以我们恳请吾王，以陛下艺术的法力，恢复我们在人屋之内、在屋中人心之中的应得之权。"
"可，"国王说，"你们吓着孩子们。"
"极少吓着，陛下；即便吓着，也是为他们好。我们很少存心去惊吓任何人。我们大抵只想叫人安静下来、有所思量；对他们略略生出几分敬畏，陛下。"
"你们倒更可能把他们逗笑呢，"国王道。
"是么？"那阴影说。
于是它向国王近前一步，静立了片刻。国王权杖上的钻石射出一道鲜明的紫罗兰色火焰，国王默然凝视着那阴影，嘴唇颤抖起来。他从不告诉别人他当时看见了什么；可他会说：
"试想，*某些*掠过的念头，若竟执拗地停留下来任人端详，那将会怎样。"
"唯有，"那阴影接着说，"当我们的心思并不专注于任何特定对象时，我们的身体才会受制于各种元素的反复无常。一般说来，在世俗的男人和轻浮的女人那里，我们只是依附于某一件家具或衣饰之上；他们从不怀疑，我们不过是光波撞击他们屋里满盛的坚实形体所溅起的愚蠢而模糊的结果。我们也不屑告诉他们真相，因为他们永不会看见它。可让那世俗的男人——或那轻浮的女人——然后——"
在它所指出的每一处停顿上，阴影的群体都激动得起伏涌动；可它们不久又回到了相对的静止。那阴影又一次要开口。可忽然它们全都仰起脸，国王顺着它们的目光望去，看见北极光已开始变淡。

===Original===
“The moon is rising,” said the Shadow. “As soon as she looks over the mountains into the valley, we must be gone, for we have plenty to do by the moon; we are powerful in her light. But if your majesty will come here tomorrow night, your majesty may learn a great deal more about us, and judge for himself whether it be fit to accord our petition; for then will be our grand annual assembly, in which we report to our chiefs the things we have attempted, and the good or bad success we have had.”
“If you send for me,” returned the king, “I will come.”
Ere the Shadow could reply, the tip of the moon’s crescent horn peeped up from behind an icy pinnacle, and one slender ray fell on the lake. It shone upon no Shadows. Ere the eye of the king could again seek the earth after beholding the first brightness of the moon’s resurrection, they had vanished; and the surface of the lake glittered gold and blue in the pale moonlight.
There the king lay, alone in the midst of the frozen lake, with the moon staring at him. But at length he heard from somewhere a voice that he knew.
“Will you take another cup of tea, dear?” said Mrs. Rinkelmann.
And Ralph, coming slowly to himself, found that he was lying in his own bed.
“Yes, I will,” he answered; “and rather a large piece of toast, if you please; for I have been a long journey since I saw you last.”
“He has not come to himself quite,” said Mrs. Rinkelmann, between her and herself.
“You would be rather surprised,” continued Ralph, “if I told you where I had been.”
“I dare say I should,” responded his wife.
“Then I will tell you,” rejoined Ralph.
But at that moment, a great Shadow bounced out of the fire with a single huge leap, and covered the whole room. Then it settled in one corner, and Ralph saw it shaking its fist at him from the end of a preposterous arm. So he took the hint, and held his peace. And it was as well for him. For I happen to know something about the Shadows too; and I know that if he had told his wife all about it just then, they would not have sent for him the following evening.
But as the king, after finishing his tea and toast, lay and looked about him, the Shadows dancing in his room seemed to him odder and more inexplicable than ever. The whole chamber was full of mystery. So it generally was, but now it was more mysterious than ever. After all that he had seen in the Shadow-church, his own room and its Shadows were yet more wonderful and unintelligible than those.
This made it the more likely that he had seen a true vision; for instead of making common things look commonplace, as a false vision would have done, it had made common things disclose the wonderful that was in them.
“The same applies to all arts as well,” thought Ralph Rinkelmann.

===Chinese===
"月亮升起来了，"那阴影说，"她一从山头望进这山谷，我们便得走，因为月下我们有许多事要做；我们在她的光里是大有能为的。可陛下若明夜肯再来，陛下便可多知许多关于我们的事，自行裁度是否该准我们的请愿；因为明夜是我们一年一度的大集会，在会上我们要向诸位首领禀报自己尝试过的事，以及成败如何。"
"你们若来叫我，"国王答，"我便来。"
不等那阴影回答，新月尖尖的角尖已从一座冰峰背后探出，一道纤纤的光落在湖上。它照不到一个阴影。国王的眼睛刚观赏过月亮复生之初的那一派清辉，再要回头寻地面时，它们已经消失了；湖面在淡淡的月光里金蓝相间地闪烁。
国王就那样躺在冰湖的中央，孤身一人，月亮瞪眼望着他。可他终于从某处听见了一个熟悉的声音。
"亲爱的，再喝一杯茶好么？"林克尔曼太太说。
拉尔夫慢慢醒转，发觉自己躺在自家的床上。
"好，我喝，"他答道，"再要一大片吐司，劳驾；因为自上次见你之后，我走了好长一段路。"
"他还没全醒过来呢，"林克尔曼太太自言自语道。
"你怕要吃惊呢，"拉尔夫继续说，"要是我告诉你我方才去了哪里。"
"那是自然，"妻子应道。
"那我就告诉你，"拉尔夫说。
可就在这一刻，一个巨大的阴影从炉火里纵身一跃跳了出来，罩住了整间屋子。接着它落在角落里，拉尔夫看见它从一条荒唐的长胳膊尽头朝他直挥拳头。他领了这示意，便住了口。他这一住口倒是对的。因为我恰好也知道些阴影们的事；我知道，他那时若是把前前后后都对妻子讲了，第二天晚上它们便不会再来叫他了。
可国王用完茶和吐司之后，躺着四下张望，觉得屋里舞着的那些阴影比先前越发古怪、越发不可解。整间屋子满是神秘。它平日便是如此，可此刻比往日更神秘。他在阴影的教堂里见了那一切之后，自己屋里和屋里的阴影竟比那些还要奇妙、还要费解。
这倒越见得他看见的是一场真幻象；因为，它并不像一场假幻象那样把寻常之物看得更寻常，它反而叫寻常之物显出了它们里头藏着的奇妙。
"这道理于一切艺术也都一样呢，"拉尔夫·林克尔曼想。

===Original===
The next afternoon, as the twilight was growing dusky, the king lay wondering whether or not the Shadows would fetch him again. He wanted very much to go, for he had enjoyed the journey exceedingly, and he longed, besides, to hear some of the Shadows tell their stories. But the darkness grew deeper and deeper, and the shadows did not come. The cause was, that Mrs. Rinkelmann sat by the fire in the gloaming; and they could not carry off the king while she was there. Some of them tried to frighten her away by playing the oddest pranks on the walls, and floor, and ceiling; but altogether without effect; the queen only smiled, for she had a good conscience. Suddenly, however, a dreadful scream was heard from the nursery, and Mrs. Rinkelmann rushed upstairs to see what was the matter. No sooner had she gone than the two warders of the chimney-corners stepped out into the middle of the room, and said, in a low voice﻿—
“Is your majesty ready?”
“Have you no hearts?” said the king; “or are they as black as your faces? Did you not hear the child scream? I must know what is the matter with her before I go.”
“Your majesty may keep his mind easy on that point,” replied the warders. “We had tried everything we could think of to get rid of her majesty the queen, but without effect. So a young madcap Shadow, half against the will of the older ones of us, slipped upstairs into the nursery; and has, no doubt, succeeded in appalling the baby, for he is very lithe and long-legged.﻿—Now, your majesty.”
“I will have no such tricks played in my nursery,” said the king, rather angrily. “You might put the child beside itself.”
“Then there would be twins, your majesty. And we rather like twins.”
“None of your miserable jesting! You might put the child out of her wits.”
“Impossible, sire; for she has not got into them yet.”
“Go away,” said the king.
“Forgive us, your majesty. Really, it will do the child good; for that Shadow will, all her life, be to her a symbol of what is ugly and bad. When she feels in danger of hating or envying anyone, that Shadow will come back to her mind and make her shudder.”
“Very well,” said the king. “I like that. Let us go.”
The Shadows went through the same ceremonies and preparations as before; during which, the young Shadow before-mentioned contrived to make such grimaces as kept the baby in terror, and the queen in the nursery, till all was ready. Then with a bound that doubled him up against the ceiling, and a kick of his legs six feet out behind him, he vanished through the nursery door, and reached the king’s bedchamber just in time to take his place with the last who were melting through the window in the rear of the litter, and settling down upon the snow beneath. Away they went as before, a gliding blackness over the white carpet. And it was Christmas-eve.

===Chinese===
第二天下午，暮色渐浓渐暗，国王躺着，心里纳闷阴影们是否还会来接他。他极想去，因为昨夜那一程走得极是畅快，此外他还盼着听几个阴影讲它们的故事。可天色越沉越深，阴影们却迟迟不来。缘故是，林克尔曼太太正坐在炉边的暮色里；她在那里，它们便不能把国王带走。有几个便试着在墙上、地上、天花板上玩最最古怪的把戏，想把她吓走；可全然无效；王后只是微笑，因为她良心无亏。然而，育儿室里忽然传来一声可怕的尖叫，林克尔曼太太赶忙冲上楼去看出了什么事。她前脚刚走，壁炉两角的两个守卫便走到屋子中央，低声说道——
"陛下准备好了么？"
"你们没有心么？"国王说，"还是说，你们的心也和你们的面孔一样黑？你们没听见孩子尖叫？走之前我非得弄明白她出了什么事不可。"
"陛下在这上头尽可放心，"那两个守卫答道，"我们想尽了法子要把王后陛下打发走，都无效。于是我们里头一个冒失的小阴影，半违着年长些的几位的意思，溜上楼进了育儿室；想必已经成功把那婴孩吓着了，因为他身段极软、腿又极长。——陛下，走吧。"
"不许在我的育儿室玩这种把戏，"国王相当恼怒地说，"你们会把孩子吓得丢了魂的。"
"那陛下便有双胞胎了。我们倒顶喜欢双胞胎。"
"少来这套没出息的玩笑！你们会把孩子吓疯的。"
"不可能，陛下；因为她的神志还没进到她里头去呢。"
"去吧，"国王说。
"请陛下见谅。说真的，这事对孩子有好处；因为那个阴影会终生成为她心中丑与恶的象征。每当她觉得自己快要恨上谁、或是妒忌上谁的时候，那阴影便会重回她心头，使她不寒而栗。"
"很好，"国王道，"这个我喜欢。走吧。"
阴影们便照旧举行了同样的仪式和准备；这其间，方才所说的那个小阴影拼命做鬼脸，把孩子吓得直哭，也把王后绊在了育儿室里，直到一切就绪。然后他往上一纵，身子在半空对折着撞上天花板，两条腿又朝身后踢出六尺远，便从育儿室门口消失了，恰好赶上与最后几个正从窗口化入、跟在担架后面落到下方雪地上的同伴会合。他们又像昨夜一样出发了，一片滑行的黑色掠过白色的地毯。那一晚，是圣诞前夜。

===Original===
When they came in sight of the mountain-lake, the king saw that it was crowded over its whole surface with a changeful intermingling of Shadows. They were all talking and listening alternately, in pairs, trios, and groups of every size. Here and there large companies were absorbed in attention to one elevated above the rest, not in a pulpit, or on a platform, but on the stilts of his own legs, elongated for the nonce. The aurora, right overhead, lighted up the lake and the sides of the mountains, by sending down from the zenith, nearly to the surface of the lake, great folded vapours, luminous with all the colours of a faint rainbow.
Many, however, as the words were that passed on all sides, not a shadow of a sound reached the ears of the king: the shadow-speech could not enter his corporeal organs. One of his guides, however, seeing that the king wanted to hear and could not, went through a strange manipulation of his head and ears; after which he could hear perfectly, though still only the voice to which, for the time, he directed his attention. This, however, was a great advantage, and one which the king longed to carry back with him to the world of men.
The king now discovered that this was not merely the church of the Shadows, but their news exchange at the same time. For, as the shadows have no writing or printing, the only way in which they can make each other acquainted with their doings and thinkings, is to meet and talk at this word-mart and parliament of shades. And as, in the world, people read their favourite authors, and listen to their favourite speakers, so here the Shadows seek their favourite Shadows, listen to their adventures, and hear generally what they have to say.
Feeling quite strong, the king rose and walked about amongst them, wrapped in his ermine robe, with his red crown on his head, and his diamond sceptre in his hand. Every group of Shadows to which he drew near, ceased talking as soon as they saw him approach; but at a nod they went on again directly, conversing and relating and commenting, as if no one was there of other kind or of higher rank than themselves. So the king heard a good many stories. At some of them he laughed, and at some of them he cried. But if the stories that the Shadows told were printed, they would make a book that no publisher could produce fast enough to satisfy the buyers. I will record some of the things that the king heard, for he told them to me soon after. In fact, I was for some time his private secretary.

===Chinese===
他们望见那山中湖泊时，国王看见湖面上密密层层挤满了阴影，彼此交错变幻。它们成双成对、三五成群，或大或小地聚着，一会儿说，一会儿听。这里那里，一大群正出神地听其中一个高出众人的发言——他并不站在什么讲坛或台上，而是凭着自己两条腿作高跷，临时拉长了身形。北极光正当头顶，从天顶直垂而下，几乎落到湖面，垂下一道道巨大的褶皱蒸汽，泛着淡淡彩虹的全部颜色，照亮了湖面和山坡。
然而，四下里说的话虽多，国王耳畔却没有一丝声音的影子：阴影的话语进不了他的肉耳。不过他的一个向导看出他想听却听不见，便在他头和耳朵上做了一阵奇怪的动作；这之后他便听得一清二楚了，只是仍只能听见他此刻专心去听的那一个声音。这却已是大便利，国王恨不得把它一并带回人间的世上。
国王这时才发觉，这里不仅是阴影们的教堂，同时也是它们的新闻交易所。因为阴影们没有文字也没有印刷，它们彼此通晓所作所为所思所想的唯一办法，便是在这话语集市、这阴影的议会上相会交谈。正如世人读自己心爱的作家、听自己心爱的演说家一样，在这里，阴影们也去寻自己心爱的阴影，听它们的奇遇，听它们大体上想说的话。
国王觉得身上有了力气，便起身披着貂袍、头戴红冠、手执钻石权杖，在它们中间走来走去。每一群阴影见他走近，一见他来便住口不言；可他一点头，它们便立刻又说起来，交谈、叙述、评论，仿佛现场并无异类、也无高于自己的存在。于是国王听了许多故事。有些叫他笑，有些叫他落泪。可若把阴影们所讲的故事印出来，那将是一本令任何出版商都印不及、赶不上买者需求的书。我将记下国王听到的一些话，因为不久之后他把它们讲给了我。事实上，我曾一度做过他的私人秘书。

===Original===
“I made him confess before a week was over,” said a gloomy old Shadow.
“But what was the good of that?” rejoined a pert young one. “That could not undo what was done.”
“Yes, it could.”
“What! bring the dead to life?”
“No; but comfort the murderer. I could not bear to see the pitiable misery he was in. He was far happier with the rope round his neck, than he was with the purse in his pocket. I saved him from killing himself too.”
“How did you make him confess?”
“Only by wallowing on the wall a little.”
“How could that make him tell?”
“*He* knows.”
The Shadow was silent; and the king turned to another, who was preparing to speak.

===Chinese===
"我让他不出一星期便认了罪，"一个阴郁的老阴影说。
"可那又有什么用呢？"一个冒失的小阴影应道，"已经做了的事，是收不回的。"
"收得回。"
"什么！能让死者复生？"
"不能；可却能叫那杀人犯心里好受。我不忍看他那副可怜巴巴的苦况。他颈上套着绞索的时候，远比他兜里揣着钱袋的时候快活。我也免了他把自己也杀了。"
"你怎么让他认罪的？"
"不过是在墙上打了个滚儿。"
"那怎么就能让他招？"
"他自己心里明白。"
那阴影住口了；国王转向另一个正要开口的。

===Original===
“I made a fashionable mother repent.”
“How?” broke from several voices, in whose sound was mingled a touch of incredulity.
“Only by making a little coffin on the wall,” was the reply.
“Did the fashionable mother confess too?”
“She had nothing more to confess than everybody knew.”
“What did everybody know then?”
“That she might have been kissing a living child, when she followed a dead one to the grave.﻿—The next will fare better.”
“I put a stop to a wedding,” said another.
“Horrid shade!” remarked a poetic imp.
“How?” said others. “Tell us how.”
“Only by throwing a darkness, as if from the branch of a sconce, over the forehead of a fair girl.﻿—They are not married yet, and I do not think they will be. But I loved the youth who loved her. How he started! It was a revelation to him.”
“But did it not deceive him?”
“Quite the contrary.”
“But it was only a shadow from the outside, not a shadow coming through from the soul of the girl.”
“Yes. You may say so. But it was all that was wanted to make the meaning of her forehead manifest﻿—yes, of her whole face, which had now and then, in the pauses of his passion, perplexed the youth. All of it, curled nostrils, pouting lips, projecting chin, instantly fell into harmony with that darkness between her eyebrows. The youth understood it in a moment, and went home miserable. And they’re not married *yet*.”

===Chinese===
"我让一位时髦的母亲悔过了。"
"怎么做的？"几个声音一齐发问，话音里掺着一丝不信。
"不过是在墙上弄出一口小棺材来罢了，"它答。
"那位时髦的母亲也认了罪么？"
"她已无更多可认，胜过人人皆知的事。"
"那人人皆知的是什么事？"
"便是：她原可以亲吻一个活着的孩子，却去追随一个死的进了坟。——下一个孩子会过得好些。"
"我拦下了一桩婚事，"另一个说。
"可恶的影子！"一个带诗意的捣蛋鬼评道。
"怎么拦的？"旁人道，"讲给我们听。"
"不过是把一片幽暗——像是从一盏壁灯的灯枝上投下的——投到了一位美丽姑娘的额上。——他们至今没有成婚，我想他们也不会成了。可我爱那个爱她的青年。他吃了一惊呢！这对他是天启。"
"可那不是骗了他么？"
"恰恰相反。"
"可那不过是外面投上去的一片阴影，不是从那姑娘灵魂里透出来的阴影啊。"
"是的。可以这么说。可那已足以叫她额头的含义显露出来——是的，叫她整张脸的含义显露出来；那张脸，在他热恋的间歇里，曾屡屡叫他困惑。这一切——翘起的鼻翼、撅起的嘴唇、突出的下巴——霎时间全都与眉宇间那片幽暗协调了起来。青年立刻懂了，满怀愁苦地回了家。他们*至今*没有成婚。"

===Original===
“I caught a toper alone, over his magnum of port,” said a very dark Shadow; “and didn’t I give it him! I made delirium tremens first; and then I settled into a funeral, passing slowly along the length of the opposite wall. I gave him plenty of plumes and mourning coaches. And then I gave him a funeral service, but I could not manage to make the surplice white, which was all the better for such a sinner. The wretch stared till his face passed from purple to grey, and actually left his fifth glass only, unfinished, and took refuge with his wife and children in the drawing-room, much to their surprise. I believe he actually drank a cup of tea; and although I have often looked in since, I have never caught him again, drinking alone at least.”
“But does he drink less? Have you done him any good?”
“I hope so; but I am sorry to say I can’t feel sure about it.”
“Humph! Humph! Humph!” grunted various shadow throats.

===Chinese===
"我逮着一个酒鬼独个儿对着他那一大瓶波特酒，"一个极黑的阴影说，"我好好收拾了他一顿！我先弄出震颤性谵妄来；接着稳稳化成一支出殡的队伍，沿着对面的墙缓缓行过。我给了他好多羽毛饰、好多辆丧车。然后我给他来了一场葬礼仪式，可我无论如何没法把那件白罩袍弄成白色——这对这样一个罪人倒更好。那可怜虫直勾勾瞪着眼，脸上由紫转灰，竟把第五杯酒只喝了一半就放下了，躲到他妻子和孩子们所在的客厅里去了，叫他们都吃了一惊。我信他真的喝了一杯茶；自那以后我虽常去张望，却再没逮着过他——至少没逮着他独自喝酒。"
"可他喝得少了么？你对他有好处么？"
"我希望是；可说来遗憾，我没法确定。"
"哼！哼！哼！"好几个阴影的喉咙哼哼道。

===Original===
“I had such fun once!” cried another. “I made such game of a young clergyman!”
“You have no right to make game of anyone.”
“Oh yes, I have﻿—when it is for his good. He used to study his sermons﻿—where do you think?”
“In his study, of course. Where else should it be?”
“Yes and no. Guess again.”
“Out amongst the faces in the streets.”
“Guess again.”
“In still green places in the country?”
“Guess again.”
“In old books?”
“Guess again.”
“No, no. Tell us.”
“In the looking glass. Ha! ha! ha!”
“He was fair game; fair shadow game.”
“I thought so. And I made such fun of him one night on the wall! He had sense enough to see that it was himself, and very like an ape. So he got ashamed, turned the mirror with its face to the wall, and thought a little more about his people, and a little less about himself. I was very glad; for, please your majesty,”﻿—and here the speaker turned towards the king﻿—“we don’t like the creatures that live in the mirrors. You call them ghosts, don’t you?”
Before the king could reply, another had commenced. But the story about the clergyman had made the king wish to hear one of the shadow-sermons. So he turned him towards a long Shadow, who was preaching to a very quiet and listening crowd. He was just concluding his sermon.

===Chinese===
"有一回我玩得可真痛快！"另一个叫道，"我狠狠地耍了一个年轻的牧师！"
"你无权捉弄任何人。"
"哦，我有——当是为他好时。他从前总是琢磨他的布道文——你们猜在哪里？"
"当然在书房里。还能在哪里？"
"也是也不是。再猜。"
"在外面街上，众人脸中间。"
"再猜。"
"在乡间幽静的青翠处？"
"再猜。"
"在古书里？"
"再猜。"
"不不，告诉我们罢。"
"在镜子里。哈！哈！哈！"
"他是该被捉弄的；该被阴影捉弄的。"
"我也这么想。我便有一夜在墙上狠狠捉弄了他一番！他还有点脑子，看得出那正是他自己，而且很像一只猿。于是他羞愧起来，把镜子翻过去面朝墙，多想着了他的会众一些，少想着了自己一些。我很高兴；因为，陛下，"——说到这里那发言者转向国王——"我们不喜欢住在镜子里的那些东西。你们管它们叫鬼，是么？"
国王还没来得及回答，另一个已经开了口。可那关于牧师的故事，倒叫国王想听一段阴影的布道。于是他转向一个长长的阴影，那阴影正对一群安安静静、屏息聆听的听众讲道。他正要作结。

===Original===
“Therefore, dear Shadows, it is the more needful that we love one another as much as we can, because that is not much. We have no such excuse for not loving as mortals have, for we do not die like them. I suppose it is the thought of that death that makes them hate so much. Then again, we go to sleep all day, most of us, and not in the night, as men do. And you know that we forget everything that happened the night before; therefore, we ought to love well, for the love is short. Ah! dear Shadow, whom I love now with all my shadowy soul, I shall not love thee tomorrow eve, I shall not know thee; I shall pass thee in the crowd and never dream that the Shadow whom I now love is near me then. Happy Shades! for we only remember our tales until we have told them here, and then they vanish in the shadow-churchyard, where we bury only our dead selves. Ah! brethren, who would be a man and remember? Who would be a man and weep? We ought indeed to love one another, for we alone inherit oblivion; we alone are renewed with eternal birth; we alone have no gathered weight of years. I will tell you the awful fate of one Shadow who rebelled against his nature, and sought to remember the past. He said, ‘I *will* remember this eve.’ He fought with the genial influences of kindly sleep when the sun rose on the awful dead day of light; and although he could not keep quite awake, he dreamed of the foregone eve, and he never forgot his dream. Then he tried again the next night, and the next, and the next; and he tempted another Shadow to try it with him. But at last their awful fate overtook them; for, instead of continuing to be Shadows, they began to cast shadows, as foolish men say; and so they thickened and thickened till they vanished out of our world. They are now condemned to walk the earth, a man and a woman, with death behind them, and memories within them. Ah, brother Shades! let us love one another, for we shall soon forget. We are not men, but Shadows.”
The king turned away, and pitied the poor Shadows far more than they pitied men.

===Chinese===
"所以，亲爱的阴影们，我们更当尽力彼此相爱，因为那也实在爱不了多少。我们没有凡人们那种不相爱的借口，因为我们并不像他们那样会死。我想，正是对那死亡的念头，使他们恨得那样厉害。再者，我们大多数白天睡觉，而不像人那样夜里睡。你们又知道，我们把昨夜发生的事忘得一干二净；所以，我们应当爱得恳切，因为这爱是短的。啊！亲爱的阴影，此刻我用我全部的阴影之魂爱着你，明夜我便不再爱你，不再认得你；我将在人群中与你擦肩而过，做梦也想不到我此刻所爱的那个阴影就在我身旁。幸福的众影啊！因为我们只把我们的故事记得讲到这里为止，然后它们便消失在阴影的墓园里——在那里我们埋葬的不过是已死的自己。啊！弟兄们，谁愿意做一个人、而又记得呢？谁愿意做一个人、而又哭泣呢？我们实在应当彼此相爱，因为唯有我们继承了遗忘；唯有我们以永恒的诞生而日新；唯有我们没有岁月积攒的重负。我要告诉你们一个阴影的可怕命运——他背叛了自己的本性，妄图记取过去。他说，'我*偏要*记得今宵。'当太阳在那可怕的、死寂的白昼之光上空升起时，他与那抚慰人的好睡的温柔力量相抗；虽然他不能完全醒着，他却梦见了已逝的昨宵，而且从未忘记他的梦。于是下一夜他又试，再下一夜又试，再下一夜又试；他还诱惑了另一个阴影与他一同尝试。可终于他们可怕的命数追上了他们；因为，他们不再继续是阴影，却开始像愚人所言投起影子来了；于是他们越变越厚、越变越厚，终于从我们这个世界里消失了。他们如今被判在地上行走，一男一女，身后是死亡，心中是记忆。啊，阴影弟兄们！让我们彼此相爱罢，因为我们不久便会忘记。我们不是人，我们是阴影。"
国王转过身去，可怜这些可怜的阴影们，远比它们可怜人类可怜得深。

===Original===
“Oh! how we played with a musician one night!” exclaimed a Shadow in another group, to which the king had first directed a passing thought, and then had stopped to listen.﻿—“Up and down we went, like the hammers and dampers on his piano. But he took his revenge on us. For after he had watched us for half an hour in the twilight, he rose and went to his instrument, and played a shadow-dance that fixed us all in sound forever. Each could tell the very notes meant for him; and as long as he played, we could not stop, but went on dancing and dancing after the music, just as the magician﻿—I mean the musician﻿—pleased. And he punished us well; for he nearly danced us all off our legs and out of shape into tired heaps of collapsed and palpitating darkness. We won’t go near him for some time again, if we can only remember it. He had been very miserable all day, he was so poor; and we could not think of any way of comforting him except making him laugh. We did not succeed, with our wildest efforts; but it turned out better than we had expected, after all; for his shadow-dance got him into notice, and he is quite popular now, and making money fast.﻿—If he does not take care, we shall have other work to do with him by and by, poor fellow!”

===Chinese===
"哦！有一夜我们跟一位音乐家玩得可真痛快！"另一群里一个阴影叫道，那群是国王先漫不经心地想了一想、又驻足倾听的。——"我们上上下下地跳，像他钢琴上的琴槌和制音器。可他倒向我们报了仇。因为他在暮色里瞧了我们半个钟头之后，便起身走到他的乐器前，弹了一曲阴影舞，把我们全体永远地凝固在声音里了。每一个都能听出哪几个音符是为自己而作的；他弹着的时候，我们便停不下来，只管跟着音乐一舞再舞，恰如那魔术师——我是说那音乐家——所乐意的那样。他可好好惩罚了我们；因为他几乎把我们全都跳出腿去、跳出形来，化作一堆堆疲软而悸动的黑暗。今后好一阵子我们再也不敢靠近他了——只要我们记得住的话。他那一整天都愁苦不堪，因为他太穷了；我们也想不出什么法子安慰他，除了逗他笑。我们用尽最最放诞的手段也没成功；可到头来结果竟比我们所料的还好；因为他那曲阴影舞引起了人们的注意，如今他颇受欢迎，钱也赚得很快。——他若不当心，迟早我们还有别的活儿要找他做呢，可怜的人！"

===Original===
“I and some others did the same for a poor play-writer once. He had a Christmas piece to write, and [not] being an original genius, it was not so easy for him to find a subject as it is for most of his class. I saw the trouble he was in, and collecting a few stray Shadows, we acted, in dumb-show of course, the funniest bit of nonsense we could think of; and it was quite successful. The poor fellow watched every motion, roaring with laughter at us, and delight at the ideas we put into his head. He turned it all into words, and scenes, and actions; and the piece came off with a splendid success.”
“But how long we have to look for a chance of doing anything worth doing,” said a long, thin, especially lugubrious Shadow. “I have only done one thing worth telling ever since we met last. But I am proud of that.”
“What was it? What was it?” rose from twenty voices.
“I crept into a dining-room, one twilight, soon after Christmas-day. I had been drawn thither by the glow of a bright fire shining through red window-curtains. At first I thought there was no one there, and was on the point of leaving the room, and going out again into the snowy street, when I suddenly caught the sparkle of eyes. I found that they belonged to a little boy who lay very still on a sofa. I crept into a dark corner by the sideboard, and watched him. He seemed very sad, and did nothing but stare into the fire. At last he sighed out﻿—‘I wish mamma would come home.’ ‘Poor boy!’ thought I, ‘there is no help for that but mamma.’ Yet I would try to while away the time for him. So out of my corner I stretched a long shadow arm, reaching all across the ceiling, and pretended to make a grab at him. He was rather frightened at first; but he was a brave boy, and soon saw that it was all a joke. So when I did it again, he made a clutch at me; and then we had such fun! For though he often sighed and wished mamma would come home, he always began again with me; and on we went with the wildest games. At last his mother’s knock came to the door, and starting up in delight, he rushed into the hall to meet her, and forgot all about poor black me. But I did not mind that in the least; for when I glided out after him into the hall, I was well repaid for my trouble by hearing his mother say to him﻿—‘Why, Charlie, my dear, you look ever so much better since I left you!’ At that moment I slipped through the closing door, and as I ran across the snow, I heard the mother say﻿—‘What shadow can that be, passing so quickly?’ And Charlie answered with a merry laugh﻿—‘Oh! mamma, I suppose it must be the funny shadow that has been playing such games with me all the time you were out.’ As soon as the door was shut, I crept along the wall and looked in at the dining-room window. And I heard his mamma say, as she led him into the room, ‘What an imagination the boy has!’ Ha! ha! ha! Then she looked at him, and the tears came in her eyes; and she stooped down over him, and I heard the sounds of a mingling kiss and sob.”

===Chinese===
"我和另外几个，从前也替一个可怜的剧作家做过同样的事。他有一出圣诞戏要写，而他[并]不是个有原创才华的人，所以找题材不像他那一行大多数人那么容易。我看他正犯愁，便纠集了几个游荡的阴影，把所能想到的最滑稽的一段胡闹——当然是哑剧——演了出来；竟大获成功。那可怜虫瞧着每一个动作，对着我们哄笑不止，又为我们塞进他脑中的念头而欣喜若狂。他把这一切都化作了文字、场景和动作；那出戏上演时大获成功。"
"可我们得等多久，才能碰上一个做点值得一做的事的机会啊，"一个又长又瘦、特别愁苦的阴影说。"自上次一别，我只做过一件值得一讲的事。可我倒颇以此自豪。"
"什么事？什么事？"二十个声音一齐问。
"圣诞日刚过不久，有一天暮色里，我溜进了一间餐室。我是被明亮炉火透过红窗帘射出的光引到那里去的。起初我以为里头没人，正要离开那屋子，重新回到下雪的街上去，忽然我捕捉到一双眼睛的闪光。我发现那是一双属于一个小男孩的眼睛，他一动不动地躺在沙发上。我溜进餐具柜旁一个阴暗的角落，瞧着他。他似乎很伤心，什么都不做，只管盯着炉火。终于他叹出一口气——'妈妈要是回家来就好了。' '可怜的孩子！'我心想，'这事除了妈妈，谁也帮不了。'可我仍要替他打发时光。于是我从角落里伸出一只长长的阴影胳膊，一直伸过天花板，假装要抓他一把。他起初颇为害怕；可他是个勇敢的孩子，不久便看出这不过是玩笑。于是我再来一下时，他也朝我抓了一把；这下我们玩得可真痛快！因为尽管他时常叹气、盼着妈妈回家，可他总是又跟我从头玩起；我们便一直玩着最最疯的游戏。终于他妈妈的敲门声到了门前，他欢天喜地一跃而起，冲进过道去迎她，把我这个可怜的黑影忘得一干二净。可我一点也不在意；因为当我随他滑进过道时，我听见他妈妈对他说——'哎呀，查理，亲爱的，你比我出门时气色可好多了！'——这已足够报偿我的辛劳了。就在那一刻，我从正要合上的门缝里溜了出去，当我跑过雪地时，听见那位母亲说——'那是什么影子，跑得这么快？'查理欢笑着答道——'哦！妈妈，那准是一直在陪我玩的那个滑稽的影子，您不在家的时候它一直在陪我玩。'门一关上，我便顺着墙溜到餐室的窗外往里瞧。我听见他妈妈领他进屋时说，'这孩子的想象力可真了不得！'哈！哈！哈！然后她看着他，眼里涌上了泪；她俯身向他，我听见了吻与啜泣交织在一起的声音。"

===Original===
“I always look for nurseries full of children,” said another; “and this winter I have been very fortunate. I am sure children belong especially to us. One evening, looking about in a great city, I saw through the window into a large nursery, where the odious gas had not yet been lighted. Round the fire sat a company of the most delightful children I had ever seen. They were waiting patiently for their tea. It was too good an opportunity to be lost. I hurried away, and gathering together twenty of the best Shadows I could find, returned in a few moments; and entering the nursery, we danced on the walls one of our best dances. To be sure it was mostly extemporized; but I managed to keep it in harmony by singing this song, which I made as we went on. Of course the children could not hear it; they only saw the motions that answered to it; but with them they seemed to be very much delighted indeed, as I shall presently prove to you. This was the song:﻿—

> ‘Swing, swang, swingle, swuff,
 Flicker, flacker, fling, fluff!
 Thus we go,
 To and fro;
 Here and there,
 Everywhere,
 Born and bred;
 Never dead,
 Only gone.
> ‘On! Come on.
 Looming, glooming,
 Spreading, fuming,
 Shattering, scattering,
 Parting, darting,
 Settling, starting,
 All our life
 Is a strife,
 And a wearying for rest
 On the darkness’ friendly breast.
> ‘Joining, splitting,
 Rising, sitting,
 Laughing, shaking,
 Sides all aching,
 Grumbling, grim, and gruff.
 Swingle, swangle, swuff!
> ‘Now a knot of darkness;
 Now dissolved gloom;
 Now a pall of blackness
 Hiding all the room.
 Flicker, flacker, fluff!
 Black, and black enough!
> ‘Dancing now like demons;
 Lying like the dead;
 Gladly would we stop it,
 And go down to bed!
 But our work we still must do,
 Shadow men, as well as you.
> ‘Rooting, rising, shooting,
 Heaving, sinking, creeping;
 Hid in corners crooning;
 Splitting, poking, leaping,
 Gathering, towering, swooning.
 When we’re lurking,
 Yet we’re working,
 For our labour we must do,
 Shadow men, as well as you.
 Flicker, flacker, fling, fluff!
 Swing, swang, swingle, swuff!’
“ ‘How thick the Shadows are!’ said one of the children﻿—a thoughtful little girl.
“ ‘I wonder where they come from,’ said a dreamy little boy.
“ ‘I think they grow out of the wall,’ answered the little girl; ‘for I have been watching them come; first one and then another, and then a whole lot of them. I am sure they grow out of the walls.’
“ ‘Perhaps they have papas and mammas,’ said an older boy, with a smile.
“ ‘Yes, yes; and the doctor brings them in his pocket,’ said another, a consequential little maiden.
“ ‘No; I’ll tell you,’ said the older boy: ‘they’re ghosts.’
“ ‘But ghosts are white.’
“ ‘Oh! but these have got black coming down the chimney.’
“ ‘No,’ said a curious-looking, white-faced boy of fourteen, who had been reading by the firelight, and had stopped to hear the little ones talk; ‘they’re body ghosts; they’re not soul ghosts.’
“ ‘A silence followed, broken by the first, the dreamy-eyed boy, who said﻿—
“ ‘I hope they didn’t make me;’ at which they all burst out laughing. Just then the nurse brought in their tea, and when she proceeded to light the gas, we vanished.”

===Chinese===
"我总爱找些满是孩子的育儿室，"另一个说，"今年冬天我运气很好。我敢说孩子们尤其属于我们。有一天傍晚，我在一座大城里四下张望，从一扇窗望进一间大育儿室，那可憎的煤气灯还没点上。炉火周围坐着一群我从未见过的最最讨人喜欢的孩子。他们正耐心地等着吃茶。这机会太好了，错过不得。我赶忙走开，纠集了我所能找到的二十个最好的阴影，一会儿便回来了；进了育儿室，我们在墙上跳了一支我们最拿手的舞。当然，那大半是即兴的；可我一边跳一边编了这首歌，一边唱，借此把舞步串得谐调。孩子们当然听不见；他们只看见与歌声相和的舞姿；可他们似乎确实喜欢得不得了，这一点我马上便可向诸位证明。歌词是这样——

> "摇呀摆，扭呀甩，呼呼，
 闪闪忽忽，飘飘扑扑！
 我们就这样，
 来来回回逛；
 这儿那儿，
 无处不在，
 生来如此；
 从不曾死，
 只是走开。
> "来呀！快来呀。
 阴阴沉沉，
 四散扬纷，
 碎裂迸射，
 分开飞射，
 落定又起，
 我们一生
 便是相争，
 又是倦极求安息，
 扑在黑暗的慈怀。
> "聚了又裂，
 起了又坐，
 笑了又颤，
 两肋都酸，
 嘟囔、阴沉、又粗鲁。
 摇呀摆呀，呼呼！
> "忽而结成一团黑；
 忽而化作一片阴；
 忽而蒙上一层幕，
 把满屋子都遮尽。
 闪闪忽忽，飘飘扑！
 黑呀，黑得真够数！
> "时而跳得像群魔；
 时而倒下像死尸；
 巴不得我们就停下，
 下去上床睡个够！
 可是我们还得做工，
 阴影人，同你们一样。
> "扎根，升起，猛抽，
 起伏，沉落，潜行；
 躲在角落里哼唱；
 裂开，戳弄，纵跃，
 聚拢，高耸，昏晕。
 我们潜伏之时，
 仍然未曾闲适，
 因为我们的工总要作，
 阴影人，同你们一样。
 闪闪忽忽，飘飘扑！
 摇呀摆，扭呀甩，呼呼！"
"'影子真多呀！'孩子们里有一个说——一个爱动心思的小女孩。
"'它们从哪儿来呢？'一个爱做梦的小男孩说。
"'我看它们是从墙上长出来的，'小女孩答道，'因为我一直瞧着它们来；先是一个，再是一个，然后便是一大群。我准知道它们是从墙上长出来的。'
"'它们怕也有爸爸妈妈吧，'一个大些的男孩微笑着说。
"'是啊是啊；大夫把它们装在口袋里带来呢，'另一个、一个挺拿腔拿势的小姑娘说。
"'不；我告诉你们，'那个大些的男孩说：'它们是鬼。'
"'可鬼是白的呀。'
"'哦！可这些是从烟囱下来的，是黑的。'
"'不，'一个面色苍白、模样好奇的十四岁男孩说——他一直在炉光下读书，停下来听小家伙们说话；'它们是身体的鬼；不是灵魂的鬼。'
"一阵沉默，被头一个、那个爱做梦的男孩打破，他说——
"'但愿它们不是把我造出来的；'孩子们一听哄堂大笑。正在这时，保姆端来了他们的茶，她动手点煤气灯时，我们便消失了。"

===Original===
“I stopped a murder,” cried another.
“How? How? How?”
“I will tell you. I had been lurking about a sickroom for some time, where a miser lay, apparently dying. I did not like the place at all, but I felt as if I should be wanted there. There were plenty of lurking-places about, for the room was full of all sorts of old furniture, especially cabinets, chests, and presses. I believe he had in that room every bit of the property he had spent a long life in gathering. I found that he had gold and gold in those places; for one night, when his nurse was away, he crept out of bed, mumbling and shaking, and managed to open one of his chests, though he nearly fell down with the effort. I was peeping over his shoulder, and such a gleam of gold fell upon me, that it nearly killed me. But hearing his nurse coming, he slammed the lid down, and I recovered.
“I tried very hard, but I could not do him any good. For although I made all sorts of shapes on the walls and ceiling, representing evil deeds that he had done, of which there were plenty to choose from, I could make no shapes on his brain or conscience. He had no eyes for anything but gold. And it so happened that his nurse had neither eyes nor heart for anything else either.
“ ‘One day, as she was seated beside his bed, but where he could not see her, stirring some gruel in a basin, to cool it from him, I saw her take a little phial from her bosom, and I knew by the expression of her face both what it was and what she was going to do with it. Fortunately the cork was a little hard to get out, and this gave me one moment to think.
“The room was so crowded with all sorts of things, that although there were no curtains on the four-post bed to hide from the miser the sight of his precious treasures, there was yet but one small part of the ceiling suitable for casting myself upon in the shape I wished to assume. And this spot was hard to reach. But having discovered that upon this very place lay a dull gleam of firelight thrown from a strange old dusty mirror that stood away in some corner, I got in front of the fire, spied where the mirror was, threw myself upon it, and bounded from its face upon the oval pool of dim light on the ceiling, assuming, as I passed, the shape of an old stooping hag, who poured something from a phial into a basin. I made the handle of the spoon with my own nose, ha! ha!” And the shadow-hand caressed the shadow-tip of the shadow-nose, before the shadow-tongue resumed.
“The old miser saw me: he would not taste the gruel that night, although his nurse coaxed and scolded till they were both weary. She pretended to taste it herself, and to think it very good; but at last retired into a corner, and after making as if she were eating it, took good care to pour it all out into the ashes.”
“But she must either succeed, or starve him, at last,” interposed a Shadow.
“I will tell you.”
“And,” interposed a third, “he was not worth saving.”
“He might repent,” suggested another who was more benevolent.
“No chance of that,” returned the former. “Misers never do. The love of money has less in it to cure itself than any other wickedness into which wretched men can fall. What a mercy it is to be born a Shadow! Wickedness does not stick to us. What do we care for gold!﻿—Rubbish!”
“Amen! Amen! Amen!” came from a hundred shadow-voices.
“You should have let her murder him, and so you would have been quit of him.”
“And besides, how was he to escape at last? He could never get rid of her, you know.”
“I was going to tell you,” resumed the narrator, “only you had so many shadow-remarks to make, that you would not let me.”
“Go on; go on.”
“There was a little grandchild who used to come and see him sometimes﻿—the only creature the miser cared for. Her mother was his daughter; but the old man would never see her, because she had married against his will. Her husband was now dead, but he had not forgiven her yet. After the shadow he had seen, however, he said to himself, as he lay awake that night﻿—I saw the words on his face﻿—‘How shall I get rid of that old devil? If I don’t eat I shall die; and if I do eat I shall be poisoned. I wish little Mary would come. Ah! her mother would never have served me so.’ He lay awake, thinking such things over and over again, all night long, and I stood watching him from a dark corner, till the dayspring came and shook me out. When I came back next night, the room was tidy and clean. His own daughter, a sad-faced but beautiful woman, sat by his bedside; and little Mary was curled up on the floor by the fire, imitating us, by making queer shadows on the ceiling with her twisted hands. But she could not think how ever they got there. And no wonder, for I helped her to some very unaccountable ones.”

===Chinese===
"我阻止了一桩谋杀，"另一个叫道。
"怎么阻止的？怎么？怎么？"
"我告诉你们。我在一间病室附近潜藏了好一阵子，那里躺着一个守财奴，看样子已命在旦夕。我一点也不喜欢那地方，可我总觉得那里用得着我。那屋里藏身之处多得很，因为屋里堆满了各种旧家具，尤其是橱柜、箱子和立柜。我相信他一辈子敛来的财产，每一星半点都堆在那间屋里了。我发现那些家什里头是一坛坛的金子；因为有一夜，趁看护不在，他从床上爬下来，嘴里嘟囔着、浑身发抖，硬撑着打开了一只箱子，差点为此摔倒。我从他肩头偷看过去，一道金光扑到我身上，险些把我当场弄死。可他听见看护来了，砰地把箱盖一摔，我才缓过劲来。
"我很费了些力气，可对他毫无益处。因为尽管我在墙上天花板上投出各种各样的形状，演的尽是他做过的恶事——其多简直挑不过来——我却没法在他脑子里或良心上投出任何形状。除了金子，他眼里别无一物。偏巧，他的看护眼里、心里也除金子之外别无他物。
"'有一天，她坐在他床边——她坐的地方他看不见——在一个盆里搅着麦片粥，好替他晾凉；我看见她从怀里摸出一个小药瓶来，凭她脸上的神情我便既知那是什么，又知她要拿它做什么。幸而那软木塞有点难拔，我便得了一刻思考的工夫。
"屋里塞满了各色物件，所以，那张四柱床虽无床帷遮住那守财奴看他那些宝贝的眼光，天花板上却仍只有一小块地方，适宜我投上自己、扮出我想扮的形状。而那一处又难以够得着。可我发现，正有一道昏暗的炉火反光落在那一处，那光是从立在某个角落里的一面古怪蒙尘的旧镜子里投来的；我便走到火前，瞅准镜子所在，把自己投到它面上，又从镜面一跃，跳到天花板那一小块椭圆形的昏光池上，跳过时便化成一个佝偻老妪的形状，正从小药瓶里把什么东西倒进盆里。我用我自己的鼻子做了那把勺子的柄，哈！哈！"那只阴影的手爱抚地摸了摸阴影鼻子的尖端，阴影的舌头才又接着讲下去。
"那老守财奴看见了我：那一夜他不肯尝那粥，任凭看护又哄又骂，直到两人都精疲力竭。她假装自己尝了一口、又连夸味道好；可末了退到角落里，做出一副在吃的样子之后，却小心翼翼地把粥全倒进了灰里。"
"可她要么得手，要么便把他饿死，到底难免其一吧，"一个阴影插嘴道。
"我告诉你们。"
"再者，"第三个插嘴道，"他也不值得救。"
"他也许会悔过呢，"另一个较为仁厚的主张道。
"休想，"前者反驳，"守财奴从来不悔过。贪财这一桩，比可怜人能陷入的任何别的恶习都更少自医之药。生而为阴影，可真是天大的福气！恶习沾不上我们。我们何在乎金子！——废铜烂铁！"
"阿门！阿门！阿门！"上百个阴影的声音应道。
"你本该让她把他谋杀了，那样你也便摆脱了他。"
"再者，他到底又怎能逃脱？他永远甩不掉她，你知道。"
"我正要告诉你们呢，"那叙述者接着说，"只怪你们阴影的议论太多，不让我说下去。"
"往下讲；往下讲。"
"有一个外孙女，从前常来看他——是这守财奴唯一在乎的生灵。她母亲是他的女儿；可这老人从不肯见她，因为她违背他的意思嫁了人。她丈夫如今已死，他仍不肯宽恕她。然而，自从他见了那个阴影之后，那一夜他躺着睡不着，自言自语——我从他脸上读出了这些话——'我怎样才能摆脱那个老魔鬼？我不吃就得饿死；吃了就得被毒死。要是小玛丽来了就好了。唉！她母亲是绝不会这样待我的。'他彻夜未眠，翻来覆去想这些，我站在一个暗角里看着他，直到晨光升起，把我抖了出去。第二夜我回来时，屋里已整洁干净。他自己的女儿——一个面带愁容却十分美丽的妇人——坐在他床边；小玛丽蜷在炉边的地板上，学着我们，用扭来扭去的双手在天花板上投出种种奇形怪状的阴影。可她怎么也想不通那些影子是怎么上去的。也难怪，因为我帮她投出了几个极难解释的影子呢。"

===Original===
“I have a story about a granddaughter, too,” said another, the moment that speaker ceased.
“Tell it. Tell it.”
“Last Christmas-day,” he began, “I and a troop of us set out in the twilight to find some house where we could all have something to do; for we had made up our minds to act together. We tried several, but found objections to them all. At last we espied a large lonely country-house, and hastening to it, we found great preparations making for the Christmas dinner. We rushed into it, scampered all over it, and made up our minds in a moment that it would do. We amused ourselves in the nursery first, where there were several children being dressed for dinner. We generally do go to the nursery first, your majesty. This time we were especially charmed with a little girl about five years old, who clapped her hands and danced about with delight at the antics we performed; and we said we would do something for her if we had a chance. The company began to arrive; and at every arrival we rushed to the hall, and cut wonderful capers of welcome. Between times we scudded away to see how the dressing went on. One girl about eighteen was delightful. She dressed herself as if she did not care much about it, but could not help doing it prettily. When she took her last look at the phantom in the glass, she half smiled to it.﻿—But *we* do not like those creatures that come into the mirrors at all, your majesty. We don’t understand them. They are dreadful to us.﻿—She looked rather sad and pale, but very sweet and hopeful. So we wanted to know all about her, and soon found out that she was a distant relation and a great favourite of the gentleman of the house, an old man, in whose face benevolence was mingled with obstinacy and a deep shade of the tyrannical. We could not admire him much; but we would not make up our minds all at once: Shadows never do.
“The dinner-bell rang, and down we hurried. The children all looked happy, and we were merry. But there was one cross fellow among the servants, and didn’t we plague him! and didn’t we get fun out of him! When he was bringing up dishes, we lay in wait for him at every corner, and sprang upon him from the floor, and from over the banisters, and down from the cornices. He started and stumbled and blundered so in consequence, that his fellow-servants thought he was tipsy. Once he dropped a plate, and had to pick up the pieces, and hurry away with them; and didn’t we pursue him as he went! It was lucky for him his master did not see how he went on; but we took care not to let him get into any real scrape, though he was quite dazed with the dodging of the unaccountable shadows. Sometimes he thought the walls were coming down upon him; sometimes that the floor was gaping to swallow him; sometimes that he would be knocked to pieces by the hurrying to and fro, or be smothered in the black crowd.

===Chinese===
"我也有个关于外孙女的故事，"那一位话音才落，另一个便说。
"讲罢。讲罢。"
"去年圣诞日，"他开头道，"我和一群同伴在暮色里出门，想找一户人家，让我们大家都有点事做；因为我们已下定决心要一同登台。我们试了好几家，家家都有不妥之处。终于我们望见一幢孤零零的大乡间宅子，赶到那里，正赶上圣诞大宴的种种筹备。我们冲了进去，满屋乱窜，一刹那便拿定了主意：就是它了。我们先在育儿室里玩了一阵，那里有几个孩子正被打扮着准备入席。我们一般总是先到育儿室，陛下。这一回我们尤其喜欢一个约莫五岁的小姑娘，她见我们做的种种把戏便拍手欢跳；我们便说，若有机会，定要为她做点什么。客人陆续到来了；每来一位，我们便冲到过道，跳出一通花样百出的欢迎蹦跳。间歇之时，我们便溜过去瞧梳妆进行得如何。有一个约莫十八岁的姑娘最讨人喜欢。她打扮自己时仿佛并不大在意，却又忍不住要打扮得漂漂亮亮。她朝镜中那个幻影最后投去一瞥时，对它微微一笑。——可是，陛下，*我们*一点也不喜欢那些进到镜子里来的东西。我们不懂它们。它们在我们看来可怖极了。——她看上去有些忧愁、面色苍白，可又十分温柔、满怀希望。于是我们想知道她的一切，不久便打听到她是宅主——一位老人——的远亲，又是他最宠爱的人物；那位老人的脸上，仁慈之中掺着执拗，还有一层深深的专横之色。我们对他颇难恭维；可我们也不肯一下子就把他看死：阴影们从不这样。
"开饭的铃响了，我们便急急下楼。孩子们个个喜气洋洋，我们也跟着欢天喜地。可仆人里有一个脾气乖戾的家伙，我们没少折磨他！我们没少从他身上寻开心！他端菜上来时，我们在每个角落埋伏，从地板上、从楼梯扶手上、从檐口上向他扑去。他因此一惊一绊、连连出错，同僚们都以为他喝醉了。有一回他摔了一只盘子，只得捡起碎片，匆匆抱走；我们没少在他身后追杀！幸亏主人没瞧见他这副德行；可我们倒小心着不叫他真惹上什么祸，尽管他已被这些来无影去无踪的阴影折腾得晕头转向。他有时以为墙要朝他压下来；有时以为地要张口吞了他；有时又以为自己会被这来来回回的奔忙撞得粉碎，或被这一群黑压压的家伙闷死。

===Original===
“When the blazing plum-pudding was carried in we made a perfect shadow-carnival about it, dancing and mumming in the blue flames, like mad demons. And how the children screamed with delight!
“The old gentleman, who was very fond of children, was laughing his heartiest laugh, when a loud knock came to the hall-door. The fair maiden started, turned paler, and then red as the Christmas fire. I saw it, and flung my hands across her face. She was very glad, and I know she said in her heart, ‘You kind Shadow!’ which paid me well. Then I followed the rest into the hall, and found there a jolly, handsome, brown-faced sailor, evidently a son of the house. The old man received him with tears in his eyes, and the children with shouts of joy. The maiden escaped in the confusion, just in time to save herself from fainting. We crowded about the lamp to hide her retreat, and nearly put it out; and the butler could not get it to burn up before she had glided into her place again, relieved to find the room so dark. The sailor only had seen her go, and now he sat down beside her, and, without a word, got hold of her hand in the gloom. When we all scattered to the walls and the corners, and the lamp blazed up again, he let her hand go.
“During the rest of the dinner the old man watched the two, and saw that there was something between them, and was very angry. For he was an important man in his own estimation, and they had never consulted him. The fact was, they had never known their own minds till the sailor had gone upon his last voyage, and had learned each other’s only this moment.﻿—We found out all this by watching them, and then talking together about it afterwards.﻿—The old gentleman saw, too, that his favourite, who was under such obligation to him for loving her so much, loved his son better than him; and he grew by degrees so jealous that he overshadowed the whole table with his morose looks and short answers. That kind of shadowing is very different from ours; and the Christmas dessert grew so gloomy that we Shadows could not bear it, and were delighted when the ladies rose to go to the drawing-room. The gentlemen would not stay behind the ladies, even for the sake of the well-known wine. So the moody host, notwithstanding his hospitality, was left alone at the table in the great silent room. We followed the company upstairs to the drawing-room, and thence to the nursery for snapdragon; but while they were busy with this most shadowy of games, nearly all the Shadows crept downstairs again to the dining-room, where the old man still sat, gnawing the bone of his own selfishness. They crowded into the room, and by using every kind of expansion﻿—blowing themselves out like soap-bubbles﻿—they succeeded in heaping up the whole room with shade upon shade. They clustered thickest about the fire and the lamp, till at last they almost drowned them in hills of darkness.

===Chinese===
"当那冒着蓝焰、烈火熊熊的李子布丁被端进来时，我们在它周围办起了一场地道的阴影狂欢，在蓝焰里又跳又作哑剧，像一群疯魔。孩子们叫得多欢哪！
"那位极爱孩子的老先生正笑得开怀，忽听大门上一声重重的叩门。那美丽的少女一惊，脸更白了，随即又红得像圣诞的炉火。我看得分明，便伸手把她的脸一遮。她满心感激，我知道她心里说了一句：'你这好心的影子！'这话已够报偿我了。接着我随众人涌到过道，见那里有一个乐呵呵、英俊、脸膛棕黑的水手，显然是这家的儿子。老人含泪迎接他，孩子们欢呼雀跃。那少女趁乱溜走，恰好免去当场晕倒。我们簇拥到灯前掩护她退走，几乎把灯扑灭；管家一时没法把灯拨亮，她已经悄悄回到自己座上，见屋里这般昏暗，松了一口气。只有那水手瞧见她走开，此刻便在她身边坐下，一言不发，在昏暗里握住了她的手。等我们全散到墙上和角落里，灯又旺起来时，他才放开她的手。
"席间余下的时光里，老人一直注视着这两人，看出他们之间有点什么，便动了怒。因为他在自己眼里是个举足轻重的人，他们竟从未与他商量。其实，那两人直到水手出这最后一趟海才摸清自己的心，又直到此刻才摸清彼此的心。——这一切都是我们盯着他们看、然后彼此议论才打听出来的。——老人又看出，他那位受他厚爱、理应感恩的宠侄女，竟爱他的儿子胜过爱他；他便渐渐妒忌起来，阴沉的脸色和简慢的答话把满桌人都罩上了一层阴霾。这种'罩阴霾'与我们的笼罩可大不相同；圣诞的甜点席变得如此沉闷，连我们阴影也受不了，巴不得太太们赶快起身去客厅。先生们为了那出名的美酒也不肯在女士走后独留。于是这位好生气的东家——尽管他待客殷勤——竟被独自留下，在那间巨大而寂静的屋里、桌前。我们随众人上楼到客厅，又跟到育儿室去看抢龙涎戏；可当他们忙着玩这最最富于阴影气息的游戏时，几乎所有的阴影又都偷偷溜下楼回到餐室，那里老人仍坐着，啃着他自私的骨头。它们挤进屋里，使出各种各样的膨胀法——像肥皂泡似的把自己吹大——终于把满屋子堆得影上加影。它们最密集地簇拥在炉火和灯的周围，到末了几乎把火和灯都淹没在重重黑暗的山峦之下。

===Original===
“Before they had accomplished so much, the children, tired with fun and frolic, had been put to bed. But the little girl of five years old, with whom we had been so pleased when first we arrived, could not go to sleep. She had a little room of her own; and I had watched her to bed, and now kept her awake by gambolling in the rays of the night-light. When her eyes were once fixed upon me, I took the shape of her grandfather, representing him on the wall as he sat in his chair, with his head bent down and his arms hanging listlessly by his sides. And the child remembered that that was just as she had seen him last; for she had happened to peep in at the dining-room door after all the rest had gone upstairs. ‘What if he should be sitting there still,’ thought she, ‘all alone in the dark!’ She scrambled out of bed and crept down.
“Meantime the others had made the room below so dark, that only the face and white hair of the old man could be dimly discerned in the shadowy crowd. For he had filled his own mind with shadows, which we Shadows wanted to draw out of him. Those shadows are very different from us, your majesty knows. He was thinking of all the disappointments he had had in life, and of all the ingratitude he had met with. And he thought far more of the good he had done, than the good others had got. ‘After all I have done for them,’ said he, with a sigh of bitterness, ‘not one of them cares a straw for me. My own children will be glad when I am gone!’﻿—At that instant he lifted up his eyes and saw, standing close by the door, a tiny figure in a long nightgown. The door behind her was shut. It was my little friend, who had crept in noiselessly. A pang of icy fear shot to the old man’s heart, but it melted away as fast, for we made a lane through us for a single ray from the fire to fall on the face of the little sprite; and he thought it was a child of his own that had died when just the age of her child-niece, who now stood looking for her grandfather among the Shadows. He thought she had come out of her grave in the cold darkness to ask why her father was sitting alone on Christmas-day. And he felt he had no answer to give his little ghost, but one he would be ashamed for her to hear. But his grandchild saw him now, and walked up to him with a childish stateliness, stumbling once or twice on what seemed her long shroud. Pushing through the crowded shadows, she reached him, climbed upon his knee, laid her little long-haired head on his shoulders, and said﻿—‘Ganpa! you goomy? Isn’t it your Kissy-Day too, ganpa?’
“A new fount of love seemed to burst from the clay of the old man’s heart. He clasped the child to his bosom, and wept. Then, without a word, he rose with her in his arms, carried her up to her room, and laying her down in her bed, covered her up, kissed her sweet little mouth unconscious of reproof, and then went to the drawing-room.
“As soon as he entered, he saw the culprits in a quiet corner alone. He went up to them, took a hand of each, and joining them in both his, said, ‘God bless you!’ Then he turned to the rest of the company, and ‘Now,’ said he, ‘let’s have a Christmas carol.’﻿—And well he might; for though I have paid many visits to the house, I have never seen him cross since; and I am sure that must cost him a good deal of trouble.”

===Chinese===
"在它们办成这桩大事之前，玩累了的孩子们已被送上床去。可我们初到时最喜欢的那位五岁小姑娘，却怎么也睡不着。她有一间自己的小屋；我一直目送她上了床，此刻又在夜灯的光里蹦跳嬉闹，叫她睡不着。她一双眼睛一盯住我，我便化作她祖父的模样，在墙上投出他坐在椅中、垂着头、双臂无力地垂在身侧的样子。孩子记得，这正是她上次见他时的模样；因为众人上楼之后，她碰巧朝餐室门里偷看过一眼。'他要是一直坐在那里，'她想，'一个人在黑漆漆的屋里，那可怎么办！'她爬下床，蹑手蹑脚地下了楼。
"与此同时，楼下那间屋子已被其余的阴影弄得很暗，影影绰绰之中，只能依稀辨出老人的脸和他一头白发。因为他自己的心里已塞满了阴影，而我们这些阴影，正是要把这些阴影从他心里引出来。陛下知道，那些阴影与我们大不相同。他正想着平生种种失意，和所遭遇的种种忘恩负义。而他所想自己做过的善事，远多于别人由此受的惠。'我替他们做了那么多，'他苦涩地叹道，'却没有一个把我放在心上。我自己的孩子们，巴不得我早点死呢！'——就在这一瞬，他抬起眼，看见紧挨门口立着一个穿长睡袍的小小身影。她身后的门已经关上。正是我的小朋友，她蹑手蹑脚地溜了进来。一阵冰冷的恐惧射进老人的心，可它又霎时化去，因为我们在自己中间让出一条道来，让炉火的一缕光落在那小精灵的脸上；他便以为那是他自己的一个孩子——当年恰与如今这位寻祖父的小侄女同一年纪便夭折了的那个。他以为她从寒冷的黑暗的坟里出来，要问他：为什么她的父亲圣诞日独坐在这里。他觉得他没有答复他这个小鬼魂的话，唯有一句他羞于让她听见。可他的外孙女这时已看见了他，便带着孩子气的端庄朝他走去，在那条仿佛是她的长殓衣上绊了一两跤。她挤过簇拥的众影，走到他跟前，爬上他的膝头，把她那长头发的小脑袋搁在他肩上，说道——'外公！你不开心吗？今天也是你的亲亲日呀，外公？'
"一脉新的爱似乎从老人那颗泥土的心里迸了出来。他把孩子紧紧抱在胸前，哭了。然后他一言不发，抱着她站起身，把她抱回她的小屋，放进床里，替她盖好被，亲了亲她那张浑然不觉责备的小嘴，便到客厅去了。
"他一进门，便瞧见那两个'罪人'独自在一个安静的角落。他走到他们跟前，各拉起一只手，把两只手一齐合在自己两手之中，说：'上帝祝福你们！'然后他转向其余的宾客，说：'来罢，咱们唱一首圣诞颂歌。'——他这样说是理所当然的；因为自那以后我虽常去那宅子拜访，却从未见他再发过脾气；我敢说，那一定费了他好大的劲儿呢。"

===Original===
“We have just come from a great palace,” said another, “where we knew there were many children, and where we thought to hear glad voices, and see royally merry looks. But as soon as we entered, we became aware that one mighty Shadow shrouded the whole; and that Shadow deepened and deepened, till it gathered in darkness about the reposing form of a wise prince. When we saw him, we could move no more, but clung heavily to the walls, and by our stillness added to the sorrow of the hour. And when we saw the mother of her people weeping with bowed head for the loss of him in whom she had trusted, we were seized with such a longing to be Shadows no more, but winged angels, which are the white shadows cast in heaven from the Light of Light, so as to gather around her, and hover over her with comforting, that we vanished from the walls, and found ourselves floating high above the towers of the palace, where we met the angels on their way, and knew that our service was not needed.”

===Chinese===
"我们刚从一座宏伟的王宫里来，"另一个说，"那里我们知道有许多孩子，本想能听见欢声、看见王家般欢乐的容颜。可我们一进去，便觉察有一重巨大的阴影笼罩着一切；那阴影越沉越深，直到聚集在一位睿智亲王安息的遗体四周。我们一看见他，便动弹不得，沉沉地贴在墙上，以我们的静默加深了那一个时辰的哀伤。当我们看见那位子民之母正为失去她所信赖的他而低头哭泣时，我们忽然生出一种渴望，巴不得不再是阴影，而是化作生翼的天使——天使便是众光之光在天上投下的白色阴影——好去聚集在她周围、盘旋在她头上给她安慰；于是我们从墙上消失，发觉自己已高高漂浮在那王宫的塔楼之上，在那里遇见了正在前来的天使们，便知道我们的效力已无需了。"

===Original===
By this time there was a glimmer of approaching moonlight, and the king began to see several of those stranger Shadows, with human faces and eyes, moving about amongst the crowd. He knew at once that they did not belong to his dominion. They looked at him, and came near him, and passed slowly, but they never made any obeisance, or gave sign of homage. And what their eyes said to him, the king only could tell. And he did not tell.
“What are those other Shadows that move through the crowd?” said he to one of his subjects near him.
The Shadow started, looked round, shivered slightly, and laid his finger on his lips. Then leading the king a little aside, and looking carefully about him once more﻿—
“I do not know,” said he in a low tone, “what they are. I have heard of them often, but only once did I ever see any of them before. That was when some of us one night paid a visit to a man who sat much alone, and was said to think a great deal. We saw two of those sitting in the room with him, and he was as pale as they were. We could not cross the threshold, but shivered and shook, and felt ready to melt away. Is not your majesty afraid of them too?”
But the king made no answer; and before he could speak again, the moon had climbed above the mighty pillars of the church of the Shadows, and looked in at the great window of the sky.
The shapes had all vanished; and the king, again lifting up his eyes, saw but the wall of his own chamber, on which flickered the Shadow of a Little Child. He looked down, and there, sitting on a stool by the fire, he saw one of his own little ones, waiting to say good night to his father, and go to bed early, that he might rise early too, and be very good and happy all Christmas-day.
And Ralph Rinkelmann rejoiced that he was a man, and not a Shadow.

===Chinese===
这时已有一线月光将临的微光，国王开始看见好几个那类陌生的阴影——生着人的面孔和眼睛的——在人群中走动。他立刻便知它们不属于他的疆土。它们望着他，走近他，缓缓走过，可它们从不躬身行礼，也不作臣服之态。它们的眼睛对他说了什么，唯有国王自己知道。而他并不告诉。
"那些穿行于人群之间的别些阴影是什么？"他问身旁一个臣民。
那阴影一惊，四下望望，微微一颤，把手指按在唇上。然后把国王领到稍远处，又仔细地把周围看了一遍——
"我不知道，"他低声说，"它们是什么。我常听人说起它们，可从前只见过它们一回。那一回，我们里头有几个，有一夜去拜访过一个常独坐、据说极爱深思的人。我们看见那屋里有两个这样的影子同他坐在一起，而他脸白得同它们一样。我们过不了门槛，只抖得摇摇晃晃，险些化掉。陛下不也怕它们么？"
可国王没有作答；他还来不及再开口，月亮已爬上了阴影教堂那些巨柱之上，从天空那扇大窗望了进来。
所有的形象都消失了；国王再抬眼，看见的只是自己卧房的墙，墙上晃动着一个小孩的阴影。他低下头，便见炉边一张小凳上坐着他的一个孩子，等着跟父亲道晚安，好早早去睡，明日也好早起，整一日圣诞节都乖乖的、快快活活。
拉尔夫·林克尔曼庆幸自己是个人，而不是一个阴影。

===Original===
But as the Shadows vanished they left the sense of song in the king’s brain. And the words of their song must have been something like these:﻿—

> “Shadows, Shadows, Shadows all!
 Shadow birth and funeral!
 Shadow moons gleam overhead;
 Over shadow-graves we tread.
 Shadow-hope lives, grows, and dies.
 Shadow-love from shadow-eyes
 Shadow-ward entices on
 To shadow-words on shadow-stone,
 Closing up the shadow-tale
 With a shadow-shadow-wail.
> “Shadow-man, thou art a gloom
 Cast upon a shadow-tomb
 Through the endless shadow air,
 From the shadow sitting there,
 On a moveless shadow-throne,
 Glooming through the ages gone;
 North and south, and in and out,
 East and west, and all about,
 Flinging Shadows everywhere
 On the shadow-painted air
 Shadow-man, thou hast no story;
 Nothing but a shadow-glory.”

===Chinese===
可阴影们消失之际，却把一支歌的感觉留在了国王脑中。那歌的歌词，想必约莫是这样的——

> "阴影、阴影、尽是阴影！
 阴影的生，阴影的葬！
 阴影的月在头顶闪光，
 阴影的坟上我们走过。
 阴影的盼，生、长、又亡。
 阴影的眼投出阴影的爱，
 朝着阴影之处引诱向前，
 直到阴影之石上的阴影之言，
 用一声阴影—阴影的悲啼，
 收束了这阴影的故事。
> "阴影之人啊，你是一团幽暗，
 投在一座阴影的坟上，
 穿过无尽的阴影之气，
 来自坐在那里的那个阴影，
 端坐于不动的阴影之座，
 幽暗地穿过已逝的岁月；
 南北上下、出出入入，
 东西四方、四面八方，
 把阴影四处投洒，
 洒在这阴影描绘的空气上——
 阴影之人啊，你没有故事；
 唯有一抹阴影的荣耀。"

===Original===
But Ralph Rinkelmann said to himself﻿—
“They are but Shadows that sing thus; for a Shadow can see but Shadows. A man sees a man where a Shadow sees only a Shadow.”
And he was comforted in himself.

===Chinese===
可拉尔夫·林克尔曼对自己说——
"这样唱着的，不过是阴影；因为阴影所能看见的，只是阴影。一个人看见的是一个人，而阴影看见的，只是一个阴影。"
于是他心中得了安慰。

## The Wow o' Rivven / The Wow o' Rivven（鲁斯文之哀钟）

===Original===
Elsie Scott had let her work fall on her knees, and her hands on her work, and was looking out of the wide, low window of her room, which was on one of the ground floors of the village street. Through a gap in the household shrubbery of fuchsias and myrtles filling the windowsill, one passing on the foot pavement might get a momentary glimpse of her pale face, lighted up with two blue eyes, over which some inward trouble had spread a faint, gauze-like haziness. But almost before her thoughts had had time to wander back to this trouble, a shout of children’s voices, at the other end of the street, reached her ear. She listened a moment. A shadow of displeasure and pain crossed her countenance; and rising hastily, she betook herself to an inner apartment, and closed the door behind her.
Meantime the sounds drew nearer; and by and by an old man, whose strange appearance and dress showed that he had little capacity either for good or evil, passed the window. His clothes were comfortable enough in quality and condition, for they were the annual gift of a benevolent lady in the neighbourhood; but, being made to accommodate his taste, both known and traditional, they were somewhat peculiar in cut and adornment. Both coat and trousers were of a dark grey cloth; but the former, which, in its shape, partook of the military, had a straight collar of yellow, and narrow cuffs of the same; while upon both sleeves, about the place where a corporal wears his stripes, was expressed, in the same yellow cloth, a somewhat singular device. It was as close an imitation of a bell, with its tongue hanging out of its mouth, as the tailor’s skill could produce from a single piece of cloth. The origin of the military cut of his coat was well known. His preference for it arose in the time of the wars of the first Napoleon, when the threatened invasion of the country caused the organisation of many volunteer regiments. The martial show and exercises captivated the poor man’s fancy; and from that time forward nothing pleased his vanity, and consequently conciliated his goodwill more, than to style him by his favourite title﻿—the Colonel. But the badge on his arm had a deeper origin, which will be partially manifest in the course of the story﻿—if story it can be called. It was, indeed, the baptism of the fool, the outward and visible sign of his relation to the infinite and unseen. His countenance, however, although the features were not of any peculiarly low or animal type, showed no corresponding sign of the consciousness of such a relation, being as vacant as human countenance could well be.
The cause of Elsie’s annoyance was that the fool was annoyed; he was followed by a troop of boys, who turned his rank into scorn, and assailed him with epithets hateful to him. Although the most harmless of creatures when left alone, he was dangerous when roused; and now he stooped repeatedly to pick up stones and hurl them at his tormentors, who took care, while abusing him, to keep at a considerable distance, lest he should get hold of them. Amidst the sounds of derision that followed him, might be heard the words frequently repeated﻿—“*Come hame, come hame.*” But in a few minutes the noise ceased, either from the interference of some friendly inhabitant, or that the boys grew weary, and departed in search of other amusement. By and by, Elsie might be seen again at her work in the window; but the cloud over her eyes was deeper, and her whole face more sad.

===Chinese===
埃尔西·斯科特（Elsie Scott）任由手中的活计滑落在膝头，双手又落在活计上，正望着她那间屋子又宽又矮的窗外出神——那屋子正开在村街上一溜底楼的当中。窗台上满满当当地栽着倒挂金钟和桃金娘，像是屋里养的一丛矮树篱；打人行道上经过的人，透过这丛花木的缝隙，偶尔能瞥见她那张苍白的面庞，亮着两只蓝眼睛，只是某种内心的隐忧在眼波之上蒙了一层薄纱似的轻雾。然而，她的心思几乎还没来得及回到那桩隐忧上去，街那一头便传来一阵孩子们的喧嚷，钻进了她的耳朵。她凝神听了一瞬。一道不悦与痛楚的阴影像掠过她的脸庞；她匆匆站起身，退进了里屋，随手把门掩上。
这时那喧声渐渐近了；不多时，一个老头儿打窗外走过，他那古怪的形貌与衣着，显出他无论为善为恶，都难有多大的能耐。他的衣裳在质地与成色上都算体面，原是邻近一位善心太太每年周济他的；只是这些衣裳都照着他那口味——无论是人所共知的，还是世代相传的——裁制出来，故而在式样与装饰上未免有些古怪。上衣与长裤都是深灰的呢料；只是那上衣，形制上带着几分军装的味道，领子是直直的黄色，袖口也镶着同样黄色的窄边；而两边衣袖上，约莫在下士佩戴军衔条纹的位置，用同样的黄呢缀着一个颇为别致的图样。那是裁缝凭一块呢料所能做出的、最近乎逼真的一口钟——钟舌正从钟口垂挂出来。他这上衣的军装式样由来已久，人尽皆知。他偏爱这身打扮，始于拿破仑第一帝国战争年间，那时国家受入侵之威胁，遂编练了许多义勇团。那威武的排场与操练迷住了这可怜人的心窍；自那以后，再没有什么比用他最得意的头衔来称呼他——"上校"（the Colonel）——更能讨好他的虚荣、从而博取他好感的了。然而他臂上那徽记却另有更深的来历，在故事——倘若这也算得一个故事——的进展中，自会略显端倪。那其实是这痴人的受洗之印，是他与那无穷无尽、肉眼不见的世界彼此关联的外在可见的标记。不过他的面容，纵使五官并非特别低劣粗野的那一类，却全然没有流露出他意识到这种关联的痕迹，只是一张人情所能有的、最为空漠的脸。
埃尔西恼怒的缘由，是那痴人正受人欺负；一帮男孩跟在他身后，把他的"军衔"化作笑柄，用种种他最憎厌的恶名辱骂他。这痴人无人招惹时本是最无害的生灵，一旦被激怒却也十分凶险；此刻他屡屡弯下腰去捡石头，朝折磨他的人掷去，而那些孩子一边辱骂，一边却小心地保持着相当的距离，唯恐被他抓在手里。在追随他的哄笑声中，时时可闻一句话翻来覆去地响起——"回家来，回家来"（Come hame, come hame）。可不过几分钟，喧声便止息了，或是有哪位好心的住户出来干预，或是孩子们自己也腻了，散开去另寻别的玩耍。不多时，埃尔西又可见她坐回窗前做活计了；只是她眼底的阴云更深，整张脸也更添了几分哀戚。

===Original===
Indeed, so much did the persecution of this poor man affect her, that an onlooker would have been compelled to seek the cause in some yet deeper sympathy than that commonly felt for the oppressed, even by women. And such a sympathy existed, strange as it may seem, between the beautiful girl (for many called her *a bonnie lassie*) and this “tatter of humanity.” Nothing would have been farther from the thoughts of those that knew them, than the supposition of any correspondence or connection between them; yet this sympathy sprang in part from a real similarity in their history and present condition.
All the facts that were known about “Feel Jock’s” origin were these: that seventy years ago, a man who had gone with his horse and cart some miles from the village, to fetch home a load of peat from a desolate moss, had heard, while toiling along as rough a road on as lonely a hillside as any in Scotland, the cry of a child; and, searching about, had found the infant, hardly wrapt in rags, and untended, as if the earth herself had just given birth﻿—that desert moor, wide and dismal, broken and watery, the only bosom for him to lie upon, and the cold, clear night-heaven his only covering. The man had brought him home, and the parish had taken parish-care of him. He had grown up, and proved what he now was﻿—almost an idiot. Many of the townspeople were kind to him, and employed him in fetching water for them from the river or wells in the neighbourhood, paying him for his trouble in victuals, or whisky, of which he was very fond. He seldom spoke; and the sentences he could utter were few; yet the tone, and even the words of his limited vocabulary, were sufficient to express gratitude and some measure of love towards those who were kind to him, and hatred of those who teased and insulted him. He lived a life without aim, and apparently to no purpose; in this resembling most of his more gifted fellow-men, who, with all the tools and materials necessary for building a noble mansion, are yet content with a clay hut.
Elsie, on the contrary, had been born in a comfortable farmhouse, amidst homeliness and abundance. But at a very early age she had lost both father and mother; not so early, however, but that she had faint memories of warm soft times on her mother’s bosom, and of refuge in her mother’s arms from the attacks of geese, and the pursuit of pigs. Therefore, in after-times, when she looked forward to heaven, it was as much a reverting to the old heavenly times of childhood and mother’s love, as an anticipation of something yet to be revealed. Indeed, without some such memory, how should we ever picture to ourselves a perfect rest? But sometimes it would seem as if the more a heart was made capable of loving, the less it had to love; and poor Elsie, in passing from a mother’s to a brother’s guardianship, felt a change of spiritual temperature too keen. He was not a bad man, or incapable of benevolence when touched by the sight of want in anything of which he would himself have felt the privation; but he was so coarsely made that only the purest animal necessities affected him, and a hard word, or unfeeling speech, could never have reached the quick of his nature through the hide that enclosed it. Elsie, on the contrary, was excessively and painfully sensitive, as if her nature constantly portended an invisible multitude of half-spiritual, half-nervous antenna, which shrank and trembled in every current of air at all below their own temperature. The effect of this upon her behaviour was such that she was called odd; and the poor girl felt she was not like other people, yet could not help it. Her brother, too, laughed at her without the slightest idea of the pain he occasioned, or the remotest feeling of curiosity as to what the inward and consistent causes of the outward abnormal condition might be. Tenderness was the divine comforting she needed; and it was altogether absent from her brother’s character and behaviour.

===Chinese===
埃尔西为这可怜人所受的迫害而动容，竟到了这般地步：旁观的人若要寻其根由，必得到一种比世人对受压迫者——哪怕是女子所怀的——更深一层的同情里去找。而这种同情，奇虽奇哉，确乎存在于这美丽的姑娘（许多人都唤她"一个标致的小姑娘"［a bonnie lassie］）与那"人世的破布片"之间。但凡认识他们的人，做梦也不会想到他们之间会有什么往来或瓜葛；然而这同情，部分正是从他们身世与处境中某种真实的相似里生发出来的。
关于"痴汉乔克"（Feel Jock）的来历，人所知道的不过这些：七十年前，有个人赶着马车离村几英里，到一处荒凉的泥炭沼去拉一车泥炭回家，在苏格兰境内最崎岖的一条山路、最荒僻的一面山坡上艰难跋涉时，听见了一声婴儿的啼哭；他四下寻觅，便找见了那婴孩，几乎只裹着几片破布，无人照管，仿佛是大地母亲刚刚生下他来——那一片荒漠的沼地，辽阔而凄清，坑洼积水，是他唯一可以偎依的胸膛，而那清冷澄明的夜空，是他唯一的遮盖。那人把他抱回了家，教区也照教区的规矩收养了他。他长大成人，便成了他如今这副模样——几乎是个白痴。镇上许多人待他都不错，差他去河边或附近的井里替他们打水，拿饭食或威士忌——他极爱喝——作他跑腿的酬劳。他极少开口；能说出口的句子寥寥无几；然而他那有限的词库里，话的口气甚至字眼，已足够表达他对善待他的人的感激和几分情意，以及对作弄侮辱他的人的憎恨。他过着一种漫无目的、看来毫无意义的一生；这一点倒像极了他那些天分更高的同类——他们手里握着建造一座华厦所需的全部工具与材料，却甘心住在一间土坯小屋里。
埃尔西则相反，她生在一户安逸的农舍里，家常温暖，丰衣足食。可她很小就父母双亡；不过那时她已有了些许记忆，记得在母亲胸前那温软柔软的时光，记得躲进母亲怀里逃避鹅群的攻击和猪只的追逐。因此，后来每当她向往天堂，那既像是回到童年与母爱那段旧日天堂般的时光，也像是期盼着某种尚待揭示的事物。说真的，若没有这样一段记忆，我们又怎能为自己描摹出一种完美的安息呢？可有时候，一颗心仿佛越是被造就得能爱，便越是无物可爱；可怜的埃尔西从母亲的监护落到兄长的监护之下，只觉这精神气温的落差太过凛冽。他不是个坏人，见到别人缺了自己也深知其苦的必需之物，他并非不能动恻隐之心；只是他生得太粗粝，唯有最纯粹的肉体需求才能打动他，一句硬话、一番无情的话语，绝无可能透过他那层厚皮，触到他性情的要害。埃尔西则相反，敏感到了痛苦的地步，仿佛她的天性里时刻悬着无数看不见的、半属精神半属神经的触须，遇到一丝低于它们自身温度的气流，便瑟缩战栗。这在她的举止上的影响，是使她被人称作古怪；这可怜的姑娘自己也觉得与旁人不同，却又无可奈何。她兄长也嘲笑她，全然不知自己惹她多痛，也丝毫不好奇那外在的异常状况背后，究竟藏着怎样一贯而内在的根由。温柔，正是她所需要的那属天的安慰；而在她兄长的性情与举止里，这种东西丝毫也无。

===Original===
Her neighbours looked on her with some interest, but they rather shunned than courted her acquaintance; especially after the return of certain nervous attacks, to which she had been subject in childhood, and which were again brought on by the events I must relate. It is curious how certain diseases repel, by a kind of awe, the sympathies of the neighbours: as if, by the fact of being subject to them, the patient were removed into another realm of existence, from which, like the dead with the living, she can hold communion with those around her only partially, and with a mixture of dread pervading the intercourse. Thus some of the deepest, purest wells of spiritual life, are, like those in old castles, choked up by the decay of the outer walls. But what tended more than anything, perhaps, to keep up the painful unrest of her soul (for the beauty of her character was evident in the fact that the irritation seldom reached her *mind*), was a circumstance at which, in its present connection, some of my readers will smile, and others feel a shudder corresponding in kind to that of Elsie.
Her brother was very fond of a rather small, but ferocious-looking bulldog, which followed close at his heels, wherever he went, with hanging head and slouching gait, never leaping or racing about like other dogs. When in the house, he always lay under his master’s chair. He seemed to dislike Elsie, and she felt an unspeakable repugnance to him. Though she never mentioned her aversion, her brother easily saw it by the way in which she avoided the animal; and attributing it entirely to fear﻿—which indeed had a great share in the matter﻿—he would cruelly aggravate it, by telling her stories of the fierce hardihood and relentless persistency of this kind of animal. He dared not yet further increase her terror by offering to set the creature upon her, because it was doubtful whether he might be able to restrain him; but the mental suffering which he occasioned by this heartless conduct, and for which he had no sympathy, was as severe as many bodily sufferings to which he would have been sorry to subject her. Whenever the poor girl happened inadvertently to pass near the dog, which was seldom, a low growl made her aware of his proximity, and drove her to a quick retreat. He was, in fact, the animal impersonation of the animal opposition which she had continually to endure. Like chooses like; and the bulldog *in* her brother made choice of the bulldog *out of* him for his companion. So her day was one of shrinking fear and multiform discomfort.
But a nature capable of so much distress, must of necessity be *capable* of a corresponding amount of pleasure; and in her case this was manifest in the fact that sleep and the quiet of her own room restored her wonderfully. If she were only let alone, a calm mood, filled with images of pleasure, soon took possession of her mind.

===Chinese===
邻里们看她，多少带着几分关切，可他们宁可躲着她，也不愿与她亲近；尤其是在某种神经性的病症复发之后——这病她小时候就常犯，后来又因我必须讲述的那桩变故而被重新引发。说来也怪，有些病症竟能凭着一种近乎敬畏的力量，把邻人的同情推开：仿佛一个人一旦患上这种病，便被移入了另一种存在之境，从此她与周遭之人的交往，便如同死者与生人一般，只能断断续续，且满含一种渗入每次往来的恐惧。于是，一些最深最纯的性灵生命之井，便如古堡中的水井一样，被外层墙壁的倾颓淤塞了。然而，比任何事都更使她灵魂不得安宁的——她性情的美丽，恰恰显在这样一桩事实上：这种刺激很少能侵到她的"心智"——也许要数下面这件事了；就其与眼下的关联而言，它会使有些读者发笑，而另一些读者，则会感到一种与埃尔西同出一类的战栗。
