I do believe it was only one of those creaks in the timbers which announce the torpid, age-long, sinking flow of every house back to the dust—a motion to which the flow of the glacier is as a torrent, but which is no less inevitable and sure. Day and night it ceases not; but only in the night, when house and heart are still, do we hear it. No wonder it should sound fearful! for are we not the immortal dwellers in ever-crumbling clay? The clay is so near us, and yet not of us, that its every movement starts a fresh dismay. For what will its final ruin disclose? When it falls from about us, where shall we find that we have existed all the time?

===Chinese===
"我刚捧起书坐下，便听见莉蒂希娅正用颇不和软的口气斥责管家，怪他把花园那扇门整夜敞着。我一听见这话，那桩我接下来要讲的怪事，便开始在记忆里浮现出来了。那扇门，万圣节（All Saints）与万灵节之间的那一整夜都敞着。那一夜的中宵，我猛地醒了。我凭身上的感觉便知那不是早晨，因为夜的感觉与晨是截然两回事。四下里一片漆黑。我的心跳得厉害，我几乎不能、抑或几乎不敢呼吸。一种无名的恐怖压在我身上，而我的听觉则仿佛被它那一份期待之力撩拨得格外敏锐。响起来了——房间里有一声轻响！——虽轻，却分明，还带着一种我无从索解的意味！一想到它还会再来，便叫人不寒而栗。我现在相信，那不过是木料的一声吱呀，宣告着每一栋房子正以迟缓的、亘古长久的沉陷之流，缓缓地回归尘土——这沉降之慢，连冰川的流动与之相比都算得上洪流，可它却同样无可避免、同样确凿。日日夜夜它不曾停歇；唯有夜里，房与心都静下来之时，我们才听得见它。它听起来可怖，又有什么好奇怪的呢！因为我们岂不正是住在永在剥落的泥土里的、不朽的住客么？那泥土与我们如此贴近，却又非我们自身之物，以致它每一下动静都要勾起一阵新的惊惶。因为它最终的倾毁，将会露出什么来呢？当它从我们周身崩落，我们又将发现自己这一向究竟是在何处存身呢？

===Original===
“My skin tingled with the bursting of the moisture from its pores. Something was in the room beside me. A confused, indescribable sense of utter loneliness, and yet awful presence, was upon me, mingled with a dreary, hopeless desolation, as of burnt-out love and aimless life. All at once I found myself sitting up. The terror that a cold hand might be laid upon me, or a cold breath blow on me, or a corpse-like face bend down through the darkness over me, had broken my bonds!—I would meet halfway whatever might be approaching. The moment that my will burst into action the terror began to ebb.

===Chinese===
"我的皮肤因毛孔里渗出的冷汗而一阵阵刺痛。屋里除了我之外，还有些什么。一种说不清、道不明的，极度的孤独、却又分明有某种可怖之物在场的意绪压在我心头，其间夹杂着一种阴郁、无望的荒凉，像极了爱已燃尽、生已无的滋味。猛然间我发觉自己已坐了起来。唯恐一只冰冷的手会按上我身、唯恐一阵冰冷的气息会扑上面颊、唯恐一张死尸般的脸会穿过黑暗俯到我脸上来——正是这份恐惧，挣断了我的束缚！——无论有什么正在逼近，我都要迎上去一半。我意志一起而行动，那恐惧便开始退潮。

===Original===
“The room in which I slept was a large one, perfectly dreary with tidiness. I did not know till afterwards that it was Laetitia’s room, which she had given up to me rather than prepare another. The furniture, all but one article, was modern and commonplace. I could not help remarking to myself afterwards how utterly void the room was of the nameless charm of feminine occupancy. I had seen nothing to wake a suspicion of its being a lady’s room. The article I have excepted was an ancient bureau, elaborate and ornate, which stood on one side of the large bow window. The very morning before, I had seen a bunch of keys hanging from the upper part of it, and had peeped in. Finding however, that the pigeonholes were full of papers, I closed it at once. I should have been glad to use it, but clearly it was not for me. At that bureau the figure of a woman was now seated in the posture of one writing. A strange dim light was around her, but whence it proceeded I never thought of inquiring. As if I, too, had stepped over the bourne, and was a ghost myself, all fear was now gone. I got out of bed, and softly crossed the room to where she was seated. ‘If she should be beautiful!’ I thought—for I had often dreamed of a beautiful ghost that made love to me. The figure did not move. She was looking at a faded brown paper. ‘Some old love-letter,’ I thought, and stepped nearer. So cool was I now, that I actually peeped over her shoulder. With mingled surprise and dismay I found that the dim page over which she bent was that of an old account-book. Ancient household records, in rusty ink, held up to the glimpses of the waning moon, which shone through the parting in the curtains, their entries of shillings and pence!—Of pounds there was not one. No doubt pounds and farthings are much the same in the world of thought—the true spirit-world; but in the ghost-world this eagerness over shillings and pence must mean something awful! I To think that coins which had since been worn smooth in other pockets and purses, which had gone back to the Mint, and been melted down, to come out again and yet again with the heads of new kings and queens—that dinners, eaten by men and women and children whose bodies had since been eaten by the worms—that polish for the floors, inches of whose thickness had since been worn away—that the hundred nameless trifles of a life utterly vanished, should be perplexing, annoying, and worst of all, interesting the soul of a ghost who had been in Hades for centuries! The writing was very old-fashioned, and the words were contracted. I could read nothing but the moneys and one single entry—‘Corinths, Vs.’

===Chinese===
"我睡的那间屋子很大，洁净得叫人无比发愁。直到事后我才晓得，那原是莉蒂希娅的房间，她宁可把它让与我，也不愿另收拾一间。屋里家具除却一件，都是新式的、寻常的货色。我事后不禁暗自感叹：这屋子竟如此全然不见一丝女子栖居所特有的、说不出的意趣。我没看见任何足以令我疑心这是女子之房的物事。我所除开的那一件，是一张古旧的写字台，做工繁复考究，立于那扇大弓形凸窗的一侧。头天早晨我还看见它上半截挂着一串钥匙，曾探头往里瞧过。可一见那分格里塞满了纸，我便立刻合上了它。我本乐意使用它，可它显然并非为我而设。此刻，这张写字台前正坐着一个女子的身影，作伏案书写之姿。她周身笼着一层古怪的微光，那光从何处来，我却从未想去追问。仿佛我也已越过了那条界限，自己成了一个鬼魂，一切恐惧此刻都已烟消云散。我下了床，蹑足走过房间，到她坐处跟前。'她若是美丽的该多好！'我暗想——因为我常梦见一个向我倾诉爱意的美丽女鬼。那身影一动不动。她正瞧着一张褪了色的褐色纸页。'是封旧情书罢，'我心想，又凑近一步。此刻的我竟冷静到当真从她肩头上探望过去。怀着又惊又惶的心情，我发现她俯身凝视的那一页昏暗的纸，竟是一本旧账簿。古老的家用账目，用锈色的墨水写下，借着透过帘缝洒下的将残之月的微光，一一展示出来——记的全是先令和便士的出入！——英镑的账目一条也没有。在思想之界——那真正的灵魂之界——里，英镑也好、四分之一便士也好，原无分别；可在鬼魂之界里，这般对先令和便士的热衷，必意味着某种可怖之事！想想看：那些此后又在别人的口袋和钱袋里磨得光滑的硬币，那些此后又回了造币厂、被熔化、一次次重新铸出来、带着新的国王和王后的头像的硬币——那些此后被男人、女人和孩子吃下肚、而他们的身子又被虫吃了的晚餐——那些此后被磨损掉好几英寸的地板蜡——那一种早已彻底消逝的人生里上百件无名的琐事——竟仍在困扰、惹恼、更糟的是牵动着一个在冥府里待了几个世纪的鬼魂的灵魂！那字迹非常古旧，字也多用缩写。除了金额之外，我只认得出单独的一行——'葡萄干，五先令（Corinths, Vs.）'。

===Original===
“Currants for a Christmas pudding, most likely!—Ah, poor lady! the pudding and not the Christmas was her care; not the delight of the children over it, but the beggarly pence which it cost. And she cannot get it out of her head, although her brain was ‘powdered all as thin as flour’ ages ago in the mortar of Death. ‘Alas, poor ghost!’ It needs no treasured hoard left behind, no floor stained with the blood of the murdered child, no wickedly hidden parchment of landed rights! An old account-book is enough for the hell of the housekeeping gentlewoman!
“She never lifted her face, or seemed to know that I stood behind her. I left her, and went into the bow window, where I could see her face. I was right. It was the same old lady I had met in Russell Square, walking in front of James Hetheridge. Her withered lips went moving as if they would have uttered words had the breath been commissioned thither; her brow was contracted over her thin nose; and once and again her shining forefinger went up to her temple as if she were pondering some deep problem of humanity. How long I stood gazing at her I do not know, but at last I withdrew to my bed, and left her struggling to solve that which she could never solve thus. It was the symbolic problem of her own life, and she had failed to read it. I remember nothing more. She may be sitting there still, solving at the insolvable.

===Chinese===
"多半是为圣诞布丁（Christmas pudding）备的葡萄干罢！——唉，可怜的夫人！她在乎的是那布丁，而不是那圣诞节；不是孩子们对着布丁的欢欣，而是它所费的那么几个可怜的便士。而她竟始终把这事搁不下心，尽管她的脑浆早在千百年前便已在死亡（Death）的臼里被'研得如面粉一般细'了。'呜呼，可怜的鬼魂！'原来无需身后留有秘藏的积蓄，无需地板上染着被谋害孩童的血迹，无需什么阴毒藏匿的地契羊皮纸！一本旧账簿，便足以构作一个当家主妇的地狱！
"她始终未抬起脸来，也仿佛并不知晓我立在她身后。我离开她，走到那凸窗处，从那里可以看见她的脸。我没认错。正是我在罗素广场遇见、走在我和詹姆斯·赫瑟里奇之间的那位老妇人。她干瘪的嘴唇翕动着，仿佛只要有一口气被遣到那里，便能吐出字句来；她的眉头在薄薄的鼻梁上方紧锁着；她那闪着光的食指偶尔又抬到太阳穴边，仿佛在琢磨某个深奥的人性问题。我在那儿凝视了她多久，我也不知道，可我终究退回到床上，留下她还在那里苦苦求解——她那样是永远解不出来的。那是她一生那个象征性的难题，而她生前没能读懂它。此后的我便记不得什么了。也许她至今仍坐在那里，对着那无解之题冥思苦想。

===Original===
“I should have felt no inclination, with the broad sun of the squire’s face, the keen eyes of James, and the beauty of Laetitia before me at the breakfast table, to say a word about what I had seen, even if I had not been afraid of the doubt concerning my sanity which the story would certainly awaken. What with the memories of the night and the want of my spectacles, I passed a very dreary day, dreading the return of the night, for, cool as I had been in her presence, I could not regard the possible reappearance of the ghost with equanimity. But when the night did come, I slept soundly till the morning.

===Chinese===
"早饭桌上，有乡绅那张脸如朗朗旭日，有詹姆斯那双锐利的眼睛，有莉蒂希娅的美貌——单是这些，便已叫我半点没有要提起我所见之物的兴致了，更何况我还怕这故事一讲出来，必会惹人对我的神志起疑呢。夜里那些记忆，再加上没了眼镜，我这一日过得极为愁闷，只怕夜晚再来——因为虽说在她面前我镇定自若，我却仍不能泰然处之那女鬼可能再度现身之事。然而夜真来时，我倒是一觉睡到了天明。

===Original===
“The next day, not being able to read with comfort, I went wandering about the place, and at length began to fit the outside and inside of the house together. It was a large and rambling edifice, parts of it very old, parts comparatively modern. I first found my own window, which looked out of the back. Below this window, on one side, there was a door. I wondered whither it led, but found it locked. At the moment James approached from the stables. ‘Where does this door lead?’ I asked him. ‘I will get the key,’ he answered. ‘It is rather a queer old place. We used to like it when we were children.’ ‘There’s a stair, you see,’ he said, as he threw the door open. ‘It leads up over the kitchen.’ I followed him up the stair. ‘There’s a door into your room,’ he said, ‘but it’s always locked now.—And here’s Grannie’s room, as they call it, though why, I have not the least idea,’ he added, as he pushed open the door of an old-fashioned parlour, smelling very musty. A few old books lay on a side table. A china bowl stood beside them, with some shrivelled, scentless rose-leaves in the bottom of it. The cloth that covered the table was riddled by moths, and the spider-legged chairs were covered with dust.

===Chinese===
"次日，我既读不成书，便在这宅子四周漫无目的地游荡，末了开始把这房子的里外两面对照着想凑起来。这是一座占地甚广、布局散乱的建筑，有些部分很古老，有些则相对新些。我先找到了我自家那扇窗户，它是朝后开的。这窗下、偏一侧，有一扇门。我纳闷它通向何处，却发觉它锁着。正这时，詹姆斯从马厩那边走来。'这扇门通到哪儿？'我问他。'我去取钥匙，'他答，'这地方是个挺怪的旧宅子。我们小时候挺喜欢它的。''你看，这儿有一道楼梯，'他把门一推开便说，'它从厨房顶上通上去。'我跟在他身后上了楼。'这儿有一扇门通你那间屋子，'他说，'不过现在总锁着。——这儿便是大家所说的奶奶屋（Grannie's room）了，至于为何这样叫，我可毫无主意。'他一边说，一边推开一间老式小客厅的门，里头一股浓重的霉味。一张边几上摊着几本旧书。旁边摆着一只瓷碗，碗底盛着几片干瘪无香的玫瑰花瓣。罩着桌子的台布被蛀虫蛀得尽是洞眼，那几张蛛脚般的椅子则蒙了一层灰。

===Original===
“A conviction seized me that the old bureau must have belonged to this room, and I soon found the place where I judged it must have stood. But the same moment I caught sight of a portrait on the wall above the spot I had fixed upon. ‘By Jove!’ I cried, involuntarily, ‘that’s the very old lady I met in Russell Square!’
“‘Nonsense!’ said James. ‘Old-fashioned ladies are like babies—they all look the same. That’s a very old portrait.’
“‘So I see,’ I answered. ‘It is like a Zucchero.’
“‘I don’t know whose it is,’ he answered hurriedly, and I thought he looked a little queer.
“‘Is she one of the family?’ I asked.
“‘They say so; but who or what she was, I don’t know. You must ask Letty,’ he answered.
“‘The more I look at it,’ I said, ‘the more I am convinced it is the same old lady.’
“‘Well,’ he returned with a laugh, ‘my old nurse used to say she was rather restless. But it’s all nonsense.’
“‘That bureau in my room looks about the same date as this furniture,’ I remarked.
“‘It used to stand just there,’ he answered, pointing to the space under the picture. ‘Well I remember with what awe we used to regard it; for they said the old lady kept her accounts at it still. We never dared touch the bundles of yellow papers in the pigeonholes. I remember thinking Letty a very heroine once when she touched one of them with the tip of her forefinger. She had got yet more courageous by the time she had it moved into her own room.’
“‘Then that is your sister’s room I am occupying?’ I said.
“‘Yes.’
“‘I am ashamed of keeping her out of it.’
“‘Oh! she’ll do well enough.’
“‘If I were she though,’ I added, ‘I would send that bureau back to its own place.’
“‘What do you mean, Heywood? Do you believe every old wife’s tale that ever was told?’
“‘She may get a fright some day—that’s all!’ I replied.
“He smiled with such an evident mixture of pity and contempt that for the moment I almost disliked him; and feeling certain that Laetitia would receive any such hint in a somewhat similar manner, I did not feel inclined to offer her any advice with regard to the bureau.

===Chinese===
"我忽地生出一种确信：那张古旧的写字台，原本必是这间屋子的。我很快便找到了一处地方，我断定它当年必是立在那里的。可就在那一瞬，我瞧见我认定的那个位置的上方墙上，挂着一幅画像。'天哪！'我情不自禁地叫出来，'这不正是我在罗素广场遇见的那位老妇人么！'
"'胡说！'詹姆斯说，'旧时的夫人就像婴儿——长得全都一个样。那是一幅很古旧的画像。'
"'这我看得出来，'我答道，'它像是祖卡罗（Zucchero）的手笔。'
"'我不晓得画的是谁，'他匆匆答道，我看他的神色有些古怪。
"'她是本家的人么？'我问。
"'据说是；可她究竟是谁、是怎样一个人，我不知道。你得去问莉蒂。'他答。
"'我越看这画，'我说，'就越相信这是同一位老妇人。'
"'好啦，'他笑着回我，'我那老保姆从前常说，她有点儿坐不住。不过那全是无稽之谈。'
"'我屋里那张写字台，看起来和这屋的家具是同一个年头的。'我道。
"'它从前就放在那儿。'他答，用手指着画下面那块空地。'我清楚地记得，我们当年是怀着何等敬畏之心望着它的；因为人家说，那位老夫人至今还在那儿记账呢。分格里那一捆捆发黄的纸，我们从来不敢碰一下。我记得有一回，莉蒂用她食指尖碰了其中一张，我便觉得她真是个了不起的女英雄。后来她竟胆大到把它搬进了自己的房里。'
"'那么我占用的正是你妹妹的房间？'我说。
"'是啊。'
"'占她的屋子，真叫人过意不去。'
"'嗳！她将就着也行。'
"'若我是她，'我又道，'我就把那张写字台搬回它原该在的地方去。'
"'你这话什么意思，海伍德？难道但凡有过的老婆子的故事，你都信？'
"'她不定哪天会吓一跳——不过如此而已！'我答。
"他那一笑里显然是怜悯与轻蔑兼而有之，一时之间我几乎对他生出反感来；我又确信莉蒂希娅若听见这类暗示，反应也差相仿佛，便无心再就那张写字台向她进一言了。

===Original===
“Little occurred during the rest of my visit worthy of remark. Somehow or other I did not make much progress with Laetitia. I believe I had begun to see into her character a little, and therefore did not get deeper in love as the days went on. I know I became less absorbed in her society, although I was still anxious to make myself agreeable to her—or perhaps, more properly, to give her a favourable impression of me. I do not know whether she perceived any difference in my behaviour, but I remember that I began again to remark the pinched look of her nose, and to be a little annoyed with her for always putting aside my book. At the same time, I daresay I was provoking, for I never was given to tidiness myself.

===Chinese===
"此后我在那儿逗留的日子里，可记之事甚少。不知怎的，我和莉蒂希娅并没多大进展。我想，我已开始略微看穿她的品性，因此日子一天天过去，我并未陷得更深。我知道自己对她那份陪伴不再那么全神贯注了，尽管我仍一心想讨她的欢心——或者更准确地说，想给她留下个好印象。我不知道她是否察觉出我言行上的什么不同，但我记得我又开始留意起她鼻子上那副被捏紧的模样，并因她老把我那本书收走而有些恼她。同时，我想我也挺招人烦的，因为我这个人向来与整洁无缘。

===Original===
“At length Christmas Day arrived. After breakfast, the squire, James, and the two girls arranged to walk to church. Laetitia was not in the room at the moment. I excused myself on the ground of a headache, for I had had a bad night. When they left, I went up to my room, threw myself on the bed, and was soon fast asleep.
“How long I slept I do not know, but I woke again with that indescribable yet well-known sense of not being alone. The feeling was scarcely less terrible in the daylight than it had been in the darkness. With the same sudden effort as before, I sat up in the bed. There was the figure at the open bureau, in precisely the same position as on the former occasion. But I could not see it so distinctly. I rose as gently as I could, and approached it, after the first physical terror. I am not a coward. Just as I got near enough to see the account book open on the folding cover of the bureau, she started up, and, turning, revealed the face of Laetitia. She blushed crimson.
“‘I beg your pardon, Mr. Heywood,’ she said in great confusion; ‘I thought you had gone to church with the rest.’
“‘I had lain down with a headache, and gone to sleep,’ I replied. ‘But—forgive me, Miss Hetheridge,’ I added, for my mind was full of the dreadful coincidence—‘don’t you think you would have been better at church than balancing your accounts on Christmas Day?’
“‘The better day the better deed,’ she said, with a somewhat offended air, and turned to walk from the room.
“‘Excuse me, Laetitia,’ I resumed, very seriously, ‘but I want to tell you something.’

===Chinese===
"终于到了圣诞节那天。早饭之后，乡绅、詹姆斯和两个姑娘张罗着要步行去教堂。莉蒂希娅那会儿不在屋里。我借口头痛推辞了——我那夜确实睡得不好。他们一走，我便上楼回房，往床上一倒，很快睡死了过去。
"我睡了多久我不知道，可再醒过来时，又是那种说不清却再熟悉不过的、并非独自一人的感觉。这感觉在大白天里，竟与在黑暗中一样令人胆寒。我像前次那般猛一使劲，从床上坐了起来。那张敞开的写字台前，正是那个身影，姿势与上回如出一辙。可这回我看不大真切。待那头一阵本能的惊惶过去，我尽可能轻手轻脚地起身，朝她走去。我不是个懦夫。正当我走近到可以看见摊在写字台翻盖上的账簿之时，她猛地一惊而起，回过身来，露出的却是莉蒂希娅的脸。她涨得满脸通红。
"'对不住，海伍德先生，'她极其慌乱地说，'我以为您和他们一同去教堂了呢。'
"'我因头痛躺下，便睡着了，'我答道，'不过——赫瑟里奇小姐，请恕我冒昧，'我又道，因为我满脑子都是这可怖的巧合——'圣诞节这天，您不去教堂，倒来对账，您不觉得您若去了教堂会更好么？'
"'日子好，做的事也就好。'她带着几分被冒犯的神气说，转身便要走出房间。
"'且慢，莉蒂希娅，'我极其郑重地接着说，'我有话要跟您讲。'

===Original===
“She looked conscious. It never crossed me, that perhaps she fancied I was going to make a confession. Far other things were then in my mind. For I thought how awful it was, if she too, like the ancestral ghost, should have to do an age-long penance of haunting that bureau and those horrid figures, and I had suddenly resolved to tell her the whole story. She listened with varying complexion and face half turned aside. When I had ended, which I fear I did with something of a personal appeal, she lifted her head and looked me in the face, with just a slight curl on her thin lip, and answered me. ‘If I had wanted a sermon, Mr. Heywood, I should have gone to church for it. As for the ghost, I am sorry for you.’ So saying she walked out of the room.
“The rest of the day I did not find very merry. I pleaded my headache as an excuse for going to bed early. How I hated the room now! Next morning, immediately after breakfast, I took my leave of Lewton Grange.”

===Chinese===
"她神色有几分不自在。我压根儿没想到，她也许以为我这是要向她有所表白。那时我心里想的，完全是另一回事。因为我想到，倘若她也像那位祖辈的鬼魂一样，不得不在那张写字台和那些可憎的数字上作一桩旷日持久的赎罪游荡，那将是何等可怖——我于是突然下定决心，要把整桩事原原本本告诉她。她脸上一阵红一阵白地听着，半侧着脸。待我讲完——我怕我讲到最后是带了几分私情的恳求的——她抬起头来，直视我的脸，薄薄的嘴唇上挂着一丝冷笑，答我道：'海伍德先生，我若想听人讲道，自会到教堂里去听。至于那鬼嘛，我替您感到惋惜。'说罢，她便走出房间去了。
"那一天的余下时光，我过得并不快活。我借口头痛，早早回房歇了。如今我多么厌恶那间屋子啊！次日清晨，一吃过早饭，我便向卢顿庄园告辞了。"

===Original===
“And lost a good wife, perhaps, for the sake of a ghost, uncle!” said Janet.
“If I lost a wife at all, it was a stingy one. I should have been ashamed of her all my life long.”
“Better than a spendthrift,” said Janet.
“How do you know that?” returned her uncle. “All the difference I see is, that the extravagant ruins the rich, and the stingy robs the poor.”
“But perhaps she repented, uncle,” said Kate.
“I don’t think she did, Katey. Look here.”
Uncle Cornelius drew from the breast pocket of his coat a black-edged letter.
“I have kept up my friendship with her brother,” he said. “All he knows about the matter is, that either we had a quarrel, or she refused me;—he is not sure which. I must say for Laetitia, that she was no tattler. Well, here’s a letter I had from James this very morning. I will read it to you.

===Chinese===
"说不定您就为一个鬼，错过了一位好太太呢，叔叔！"珍妮特说。
"我若当真错过了一位太太，那也是个吝啬的太太。我这一生都会为她羞愧的。"
"总比一个败家子强。"珍妮特说。
"这你怎么知道？"叔叔回她，"我能看出的区别不过是：挥霍的人毁的是富人，吝啬的人抢的是穷人罢了。"
"可说不定她悔改了呢，叔叔。"凯特说。
"我看她没有，凯蒂。你瞧这个。"
科尼利厄斯叔叔从外衣胸袋里抽出一封黑边的信来。
"我和她哥哥一直维持着友谊，"他说，"关于这事，他所知道的，无非是我们是吵了一场，还是她拒绝了我——他也不清楚是哪一种。我得替莉蒂希娅说句话，她绝不多嘴多舌。好，这是我今早刚收到的詹姆斯的一封信。我念给你们听。

===Original===
“‘My dear Mr. Heywood—We have had a terrible shock this morning. Letty did not come down to breakfast, and Lizzie went to see if she was ill. We heard her scream, and, rushing up, there was poor Letty, sitting at the old bureau, quite dead. She had fallen forward on the desk, and her housekeeping-book was crumpled up under her. She had been so all night long, we suppose, for she was not undressed, and was quite cold. The doctors say it was disease of the heart.’
“There!” said Uncle Cornie, folding up the letter.

===Chinese===
"'亲爱的海伍德先生——今早我们受了一场可怕的惊吓。莉蒂没下来吃早饭，莉齐（Lizzie）上去看她是不是病了。我们听见她一声尖叫，冲上楼去，可怜的莉蒂正坐在那张旧写字台前，已经咽了气。她前倾着扑在台面上，她的家用账本被揉皱了压在身下。我们猜她整一夜都是这副模样，因为她衣裳未换，身子已冰凉。大夫说，是心脏病发作。'
"瞧！"科尼叔叔说着，把信折好。

===Original===
“Do you think the ghost had anything to do with it, uncle?” asked Kate, almost under her breath.
“How should I know, my dear? Possibly.”
“It’s very sad,” said Janet; “but I don’t see the good of it all. If the ghost had come to tell that she had hidden away money in some secret place in the old bureau, one would see why she had been permitted to come back. But what was the good of those accounts after they were over and done with? I don’t believe in the ghost.”
“Ah, Janet, Janet! but those wretched accounts were not over and done with, you see. That is the misery of it.”
Uncle Cornelius rose without another word, bade them good night, and walked out into the wind.

===Chinese===
"叔叔，您觉得那鬼与这事有什么干系么？"凯特几乎是悄声地问。
"这我怎么晓得呢，亲爱的？也许是有的。"
"太叫人难过了，"珍妮特说，"可我看不出这整件事有什么用处。倘若那鬼是来报信说，她在那张旧写字台的某处秘藏了钱财，那她被准许回来一趟，倒还说得通。可那些账既已了结，还有什么用处呢？我才不信什么鬼。"
"哎呀，珍妮特，珍妮特！可你瞧，那些可悲的账目并未了结呀。这正是可悲之处。"
科尼利厄斯叔叔一言不发地站起身来，与他们道过晚安，便走进风里去了。
